Anselm of Laon, the Glossa Ordinaria,  and the Tangled Web of Twelfth-Century Psalms-Exegesis 
 by  
Ellen Annika Margareta Ekman 
A thesis submitted in conformity with the requirements for the degree of Doctor of Philosophy Centre for Medieval Studies University of Toronto 
© Copyright by Ellen Annika Margareta Ekman 2019   

--- [p.2] ---

 ii 
 Anselm of Laon, the Glossa Ordinaria, and the Tangled Web  of Twelfth-Century Psalms-Exegesis Ellen Annika Margareta Ekman Doctor of Philosophy Centre for Medieval Studies University of Toronto 2019 Abstract This thesis studies the textual relationships between a group of related early scholastic commentaries on the Psalms. At the centre of the discussion stands the commentary which is often said to epitomize the developments in teaching which emerged within the cathedral schools in the twelfth century, namely the so-called Glossa ordinaria on the Bible, and its association with Anselm of Laon, one of the most celebrated theologians of the period. Despite its central place in twelfth-century intellectual culture, relatively little has been able to be conclusively established regarding the origins of the Gloss. Likewise, the authorship of many of the early scholastic Psalms-commentaries related in some way to the Gloss remains uncertain. A great many suggestions have been put forward by modern scholars, but rather than looking only at one or two of the attributions, this thesis broadens the scope of the question and takes a comprehensive view of a larger group of these commentaries, showing that this is necessary if we want to be able to say anything conclusive about their authorship.  The first chapter examines the latest scholarship on the Gloss on some other books of the Bible, showing how this bears on the question of Anselm’s authorship of the Gloss on the Psalms. The second chapter analyses the relationship of the Gloss on the Psalms to its two closest 

--- [p.3] ---

 iii 
relatives and attempts to settle conclusively the question of the direction of influence. The third chapter examines the evidence and arguments for the attribution of one of the other Psalms-commentaries to Anselm, arguing, on the basis of the expanded scope of the examination, against the attribution. Chapter Three also begins to examine the relationships that exist within the larger group and introduces a new hypothesis for the place of the Gloss in the family tree and for Anselm’s involvement in its creation. The fourth chapter analyses the relationships of the group as a whole, demonstrating how they are all related to one another, but that none can be the sole source of the rest of the group, and arguing further for the hypotheses introduced in Chapter Three.

1 
INTRODUCTION  The Psalms in the Twelfth-Century Schools   With the Book of Psalms in combination with the twelfth-century schools as our object of study, we are dealing with two central and much studied aspects of the Middle Ages—the Psalms because it is one of the biblical books most central to the medieval Christian liturgy,1 and the twelfth-century schools because they are the birthplace of what is known as scholasticism and the precursors of the universities.2  The twelfth century was a period of great political, economic, social, and religious change. Some of the defining characteristics of the so-called Twelfth-Century Renaissance3 are an increased urbanization, an institutionalization of government, and a specialization and professionalization of trades which brought with them a demand for clerks and notaries educated to handle a growing legal and administrational bureaucracy.4 At the same time, the monastic                                                 1 See, for example, James W. McKinnon, “The Book of Psalms, Monasticism, and the Western Liturgy,” in The Place of the Psalms in the Intellectual Culture of the Middle Ages, ed. Nancy Van Deusen, 43–58 (Albany: State University of New York Press, 1999). As a point of interest, McKinnon mentions that the Psalter is the Old Testament text most frequently quoted in the New. (McKinnon, “The Book of Psalms,” 45.) 2 A good introduction to the topic of the schools in the twelfth century is Gérard M. Paré, Adrien Brunet, and Pierre Tremblay, La renaissance du XIIe siècle: Les écoles et l’enseignement (Paris: J. Vrin, 1933). For a more concise overview also dealing with the universities, see Ulrich Köpf, “The Institutional Framework of Christian Exegesis in the Middle Ages,” in Hebrew Bible / Old Testament. The History of Its Interpretation. From the Beginnings to the Middle Ages (Until 1300), ed. Magne Sæbø, vol. 1:2, 148–179 (Göttingen: Vanderhoeck & Ruprecht, 2000). For a concise but comprehensive overview of the development of the schools and the societal changes that drove it, see John W. Baldwin, The Scholastic Culture of the Middle Ages, 1000–1300 (Lexington, MA: D. C. Heath and Company, 1971). On the birth and history of scholasticism, see Richard W. Southern, Scholastic Humanism and the Unification of Europe, 2 vols (Oxford: Blackwell, 1995), and Martin Grabmann, Die Geschichte der scholastischen Methode, 2 vols (Berlin: Akademie-Verlag et Darmstadt, 1956). 3 The term gained currency with Charles Homer Haskins’s now classic work on the period, The Renaissance of the Twelfth Century (Cambridge: Harvard University Press, 1927).  4 On the political, economic, and social changes of the twelfth century, see the many excellent contributions in Thomas F. X. Noble and John Van Engen (eds), European Transformations: The Long Twelfth Century (Notre Dame, IN: Notre Dame University Press, 2012); for example, David Nicholas, “Lords, Markets, and Communities,” in European Transformations: The Long Twelfth Century, ed. Thomas F. X. Noble and John Van Engen, 229–258 (Notre Dame, IN: Notre Dame University Press, 2012), on the mechanisms and consequences of twelfth-century 

--- [p.9] ---

 
 
2 reform movement of the eleventh century had led to many monasteries closing their schools to the outside world and returning to what they believed was their true mission: praying and mourning.5 This meant that the task of educating any non-monastic students fell to individual secular masters and to the cathedral schools who educated them. The latter consequently grew in importance and attracted larger and larger numbers of masters and students to the cities.  The increased demand for education put a new pressure on the cathedral schools to develop some form of standardized curricula, which, in turn, required a systematic approach to the material to be taught. Systematization, therefore, became one of the key features defining the developments in education which took place in the twelfth century: the various literary and scientific texts of antiquity, and, above all, the commentaries on these texts that had been produced in the centuries after, were placed side by side, compared and evaluated, and new syntheses were produced. This phenomenon is what is referred to as scholasticism, and it had particularly far-reaching effects on the study of the Bible and theology: it is often said that the twelfth century is when “theology” as a systematic, academic discipline was born.6                                                 urbanization, and Anders Winroth, “The Legal Revolution of the Twelfth Century,” in European Transformations: The Long Twelfth Century, ed. Thomas F. X. Noble and John Van Engen, 338–353 (Notre Dame, IN: Notre Dame University Press, 2012) on the development of a modern legal system. For a discussion of the ways in which the Twelfth-century Renaissance can, in fact, be seen as a renaissance, see Robert N. Swanson, The Twelfth-Century Renaissance (Manchester: Manchester University Press, 1999). For the developments in religious life, see Giles Constable, The Reformation of the Twelfth Century (Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 1996). 5 On monastic reform in the eleventh century, see Gerd Tellenbach, Church, State and Christian Society at the Time of the Investiture Contest, translated by Ralph F. Bennett (Oxford: Blackwell, 1940).  6 This is when it begins to model itself on the study and teaching of the liberal arts. On this subject, see Gillian R. Evans, Old Arts and New Theology: The Beginnings of Theology as an Academic Discipline (Oxford: Clarendon Press, 1980). One of the novelties was to provide also biblical texts with a so-called accessus, an introduction to the work in question, addressing specific topics, such as the author, title, and purpose of the work, which had long been standard practice for the classical works of pagan antiquity. For the history of the accessus, see Edwin A. Quain, “The Medieval Accessus ad Auctores,” Traditio 3 (1945): 215–264; for the development of the accessus in the twelfth century, see Richard W. Hunt, “The Introduction to the ‘Artes’ in the Twelfth Century,” in Studia Mediaevalia in Honorem admodum Reverendi Patris R. J. Martin, 85–112 (Bruges: De Tempel, 1948). A more recent discussion is found in Alastair J. Minnis, Medieval Theory of Authorship: Scholastic Literary Attitudes in the Later Middle Ages, 2nd ed. (Philadelphia, PA: University of Pennsylvania Press, 1988), 9–72. The development of systematic theology from Anselm of Laon to Peter Lombard is also traced by Marcia L. Colish, “Systematic Theology and Theological Renewal in the Twelfth Century,” Journal of Medieval and Renaissance Studies 18:2 (1988): 135–156. 

--- [p.10] ---

 
 
3 Since antiquity and the appearance of the earliest desert monks, the Psalms had stood at the centre of Christian worship in the West:7 seeking to fulfil the Pauline exhortation to pray without ceasing,8 the anchorites of the Egyptian desert chose the Psalms as their text of continuous prayer. While these ascetics would sometimes recite the entire Psalter in a single night, and some early monastic rules placed a similarly demanding focus on psalms-praying,9 a more modest requirement was found in the mid-sixth-century Rule of St Benedict, which gained a wide influence in the later Middle Ages: this rule prescribed that the singing of the 150 psalms be completed within the course of one week.10 Finally, due to their central place in the liturgy, the Psalms were even used to teach schoolboys elementary Latin.11                                                  7 According to McKinnon, “the process whereby the Psalter achieved its extraordinary status [in the medieval Western liturgy] was complete by the end of the fourth century.” (McKinnon, “The Book of Psalms,” 43.) The history of the Latin Psalter is itself a complex story. The text of the earliest translation of the Old Testament into Latin, the so-called Vetus Latina, the Old Latin Bible, was based on the Greek Septuagint translation and varied greatly from manuscript to manuscript. This was why pope Damasus, around the year 383, commissioned Jerome with the revision of the text of the Vetus Latina which ultimately resulted in the so called Vulgate version of the Bible. In the course of this process, Jerome produced three versions of the Psalter, the first of which is believed to have been a now lost revision of the Vetus Latina based on the Septuagint. (It was once believed that the so-called Roman Psalter represented this original revision, but the general consensus is now that the Roman Psalter is simply a version of the Vetus Latina. See Scott Goins, “Jerome’s Psalters,” in The Oxford Handbook of the Psalms, ed. William P. Brown, 185–198 [Oxford: Oxford University Press, 2014], 188.) For the second version, Jerome relied on Origen’s edition of the Old Testament known as the Hexapla, which compared the texts of several Greek translations with the original Hebrew; for this version, Jerome used also a number of newer Greek translations as well as the Hebrew text itself. (Goins, “Jerome’s Psalters,” 188.) As he ultimately doubted the reliability of the manuscripts of the Septuagint, Jerome finally translated the Psalms entirely from the Hebrew. This version, the so-called Hebrew Psalter, was the accepted text until the ninth century, when Alcuin, as part of his revision of the Vulgate, replaced it with Jerome’s second version, which was the one commonly used in Gaul (see Raphael Loewe, “The Medieval History of the Latin Vulgate,” in The Cambridge History of the Bible, vol. 2, The West from the Fathers to the Reformation, ed. Geoffrey W. H. Lampe, 102–154 [Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 1969], 111 and 137). This thus became known as the Gallican Psalter and eventually replaced the Hebrew Psalter as the standard text of the Vulgate. 8 1 Thess. 5:17. 9 See Joseph Dyer, “The Psalms in Monastic Prayer,” in The Place of the Psalms in the Intellectual Culture of the Middle Ages, ed. Nancy Van Deusen, 59–89 (Albany: State University of New York Press, 1999), 60. 10 RB 18.23: “… ut omni ebdomada psalterium ex integro numero centum quinquaginta psalmorum psallatur et dominico die semper a caput reprendatur ad uigilias, …” (“… that every week the Psalter is sung in its entirety of 150 psalms and is always resumed from the beginning on Sunday at matins, …”) As for the secular clergy, the Second Council of Nicaea (787) prescribed that each bishop had to know the entire Psalter by heart in order to be able to examine the praying of the Psalms of his clergy. (Köpf, “The Institutional Framework,” 156). 11 On this topic, see Pierre Riché, “Le Psautier, livre de lecture élémentaire d’après les vies des saints mérovingiens,” in Études merovingiennes: Actes des journées de Poitiers 1er–3 Mai 1952, 253–256 (Paris: Picard, 1953), and James J. Murphy, “The Teaching of Latin as a Second Language in the 12th Century,” Historiographia Linguistica 7:1 (1980): 159–175. See also Evelyn Birge Vitz, “Liturgy as Education in the Middle Ages,” in 

--- [p.11] ---

 
 
4 As the Psalms, furthermore, together with the Pauline Epistles were believed to contain the totality of sacred doctrine, acquiring an adequate knowledge and understanding of these ancient Jewish, sometimes contradictory and often obscure texts was imperative for anyone who wanted to devote his or her life to the practice and study of the Christian faith. Consequently, these books, for centuries, held a central place in biblical studies, both in the monastery and the cathedral school, and thus became the objects of many a commentary by the theologians and teachers of Christianity as they strove to unveil for their audience the deeper meanings of the Sacred Page.12  Given their central place in Christian worship, it is thus not surprising that when, in the twelfth century, a magister sacrae paginae (a master of the sacred page) wanted to establish his reputation as a religious scholar and a teacher of Scripture, the Psalms and the Pauline Epistles were the texts that he would generally lecture upon,13 and it is no wonder, therefore, that there survives a particularly large number of commentaries and glosses on the Psalms dating from this period. Many of these have been printed in the Patrologia Latina; others are available only in manuscript. Common to both categories is the fact that the commentaries in question are often anonymous or of spurious attribution. The situation is aptly described by Valerie Flint, who calls the textual tradition of these twelfth-century Psalms-commentaries “extremely difficult to 
                                                Medieval Education, ed. Ronald B. Begley and Joseph W. Koterski, 20–34 (New York: Fordham University Press, 2005) for an account of how the laity was educated through the liturgy, where the Psalms, of course, feature prominently. 12 On the topic of biblical exegesis and the four senses of Scripture, see Henri de Lubac, Medieval Exegesis: The Four Senses of Scripture, translated by Mark Sebanc, 3 vols (Grand Rapids, MI: Eerdmans, 1998–2000). See also Gilbert Dahan, Lire la Bible au Moyen Age: Essais d’herméneutique médiévale (Geneva: Droz, 2009), and idem, L’exégèse chrétienne de la Bible en Occident médiéval: XIIe–XIVe siècle, Patrimoines Christianisme (Paris: Les Éditions du Cerf, 1999). 13 Beryl Smalley, “Some Gospel Commentaries of the Early Twelfth Century,” Recherches de théologie ancienne et médiévale 45 (1978): 147–180 (at p. 148).  

--- [p.12] ---

 
 
5 unravel,” as “the ground is strewn with doubtful ascriptions and inadequately established dates.”14 The accuracy of this characterization shall become evidently clear in the following. Several of the attributions of these commentaries have been the subject of much debate by modern scholars and shall be the topic also of this thesis. Many of these previous discussions have, however, been of limited scope, focusing only on one or two of the commentaries. The most comprehensive approach is taken by Damien Van den Eynde, who in two articles written in the 1950s discusses a larger group of related twelfth-century Psalms-commentaries;15 his group forms the basis of the selection of commentaries discussed in this thesis. One of the aims of this dissertation is to explore what happens to the arguments made by previous scholars, including Van den Eynde, when one takes a more comprehensive approach, and to show that this is, indeed, a necessary requirement if one wants to say anything at all about the relationships of these commentaries to one another and, hence, about their potential authorship. The relationships between the commentaries as a group will be the main topic of Chapter Four. Before we address the group as a whole, however, we shall treat in detail the commentary which is the centrepiece of twelfth-century scholastic Psalms-exegesis, namely the so-called Glossa ordinaria (the Gloss) on the Psalms and its attribution to the early twelfth-century theologian and cathedral school master Anselm of Laon (d. 1117). The attribution to Anselm of the Gloss on the Psalms, and of the instigation of the Gloss-project as a whole, is accepted by more or less all modern scholars. Recent research is, however, beginning to complicate the idea of Anselm’s direct involvement in the creation of the Gloss. This research and its implications for the Gloss on the Psalms is the topic of Chapter One.                                                 14 Valerie I. J. Flint, “Some Notes on the Early Twelfth Century Commentaries on the Psalms,” Recherches de théologie ancienne et médiévale 38 (1971): 80−88 (at p. 80). 15 Damien Van den Eynde, “Literary Note on the Earliest Scholastic Commentarii in Psalmos,” Franciscan Studies 14 (1954): 121−154; idem, “Complementary Note on the Early Scholastic Commentarii in Psalmos,” Franciscan Studies 17 (1957): 149−172. 

--- [p.13] ---

 
 
6 Chapter Two deals with the Gloss’s relationship to its closest relatives, the so-called Media and Magna glosatura of Gilbert of Poitiers and Peter Lombard, attempting to settle this question conclusively. In Chapter Three, we turn to examine an alternative hypothesis for the nature of Anselm’s contribution to the Gloss, and finally, in Chapter Four, we place the Gloss side by side with the other commentaries in our group, widening the scope of our analysis further. By examining the relationship of the Gloss to the commentaries in the group as a whole, I hope to reach some new answers regarding the nature of Anselm’s involvement in the creation of one of the greatest achievements of the twelfth-century cathedral schools, the Glossa ordinaria on the Bible.  THE COMMENTARIES Our group consists of eight twelfth-century Psalms-commentaries, including the Gloss.16 For purposes of easy reference, each commentary has been given a shorthand name based on its proposed (or assumed) authorship; these names are not, however, indicative of any consensus regarding the authenticity of their attribution. As they are used as titles of works, these names have been italicized to distinguish them from the names of the actual persons to whom the works have been attributed. Nevertheless, these italicized names shall also be used variably as author’s names in the way these are used metonymically to refer to a work; I shall thus be speaking of, for example, “Pseudo-Haimo” both as a commentary and as an author. In Chapter Three, Gilbert of Poitiers’s Media glosatura will be referred to as Gilbert P (both as a commentary and as an author) in order to distinguish it from the commentary attributed to Gilbert the Universal, 
                                                16 Although closely related, the Psalms-commentary of Peter Lombard is not included in the group as such; its relationship to the Gloss and the Psalms-commentary of Gilbert of Poitiers is, however, addressed in Chapter Two. 

--- [p.14] ---

 
 
7 designated Gilbert U. The commentaries not belonging to our group of eight shall be referred to either by their given title or by the name of their author. Our group consists of the following commentaries: Pseudo-Bede. This is the commentary printed in volume 93 of the PL among the works of Bede but long since recognized to be spurious.17 It has received relatively little attention by modern scholars. The first to treat it in any greater detail was J. Douglas Bruce, who noted its connection to the Anglo-Saxon version of the so-called Paris Psalter, with which it shares certain portions of text.18 In Migne’s text, the commentary on each psalm is divided into three parts, namely argumentum, explanatio, and commentarius; what Bruce discovered is that a large part of the argumenta found in Migne’s Pseudo-Bede are also present in the Paris Psalter, and that they stem from the Psalms-commentary of the fifth-century theologian Theodore of Mopsuestia (which does not survive in any Latin translation).19 Bruce gives a brief history of the attribution of Pseudo-Bede to the Venerable Benedictine, concluding that Bede’s early editors simply pulled it out of thin air.20 While Bruce was not able to find any manuscript containing the text of Pseudo-Bede (none of its editors reveal what manuscript[s] they’ve used),21 Germain Morin, the second modern scholar to take an interest in Pseudo-Bede, found one manuscript of the text, namely Einsiedeln, SB, 175.22 Morin found, however, that the manuscript contains only the commentarius and not the argumenta nor the explanationes, the latter of which he identified as 
                                                17 PS. BEDA psalm. 18 J. Douglas Bruce, “The Anglo-Saxon Version of the Book of Psalms Commonly Known as the Paris Psalter,” in Publications of the Modern Language Association of America, vol. 9, edited by James W. Bright, 43–164 (Baltimore: John Murphy & Company, 1894). 19 Bruce, “The Paris Psalter,” 94–97 and 99. 20 Bruce, “The Paris Psalter,” 120–122. 21 Bruce, “The Paris Psalter,” 120. 22 Germain Morin, “Le Pseudo-Bède sur les Psaumes, et l’Opus super Psalterium de maître Manegold de Lautenbach,” Revue bénédictine 28 (1911): 331–40 (at p. 331). 

--- [p.15] ---

 
 
8 excerpts from Cassiodorus. He thus concluded that the argumenta and the explanationes are later additions by someone other than the author of the commentary proper. As the author of the commentary, Morin suggests the late eleventh-century theologian Manegold of Lautenbach (d. 1103).23 This hypothesis shall be challenged below. Bruno. This is the commentary printed in volume 152 of the PL under the name of Bruno the Carthusian (d. 1101), who was a schoolmaster at Rheims before he founded the Carthusian order in 1084.24 The commentary is attributed (in a sixteenth-century hand) to Bruno in its only surviving manuscript, Grenoble, BM, 341 (210), the oldest part of which dates to the first third of the twelfth century.25 Its authenticity has been the subject of much scholarly discussion, and there is, as yet, no consensus on the matter. Scholars who have argued for Bruno’s authorship include Artur Landgraf,26 Anselme Stoelen,27 and, more recently, Constant Mews28 and Andrew 
                                                23 His arguments include the fact that Pseudo-Bede uses Pseudo-Haimo—which we shall see in the following is a relationship that is not so easily proven—the fact that Pseudo-Bede uses ‘quasi dicat’ and ‘ac si dicat’ as transitional formulae—which is something more or less every other commentator in our group does—and the fact that Pseudo-Bede quotes many of the same Classical authors as does Manegold in his authentic works. None of these facts are, however, conclusive evidence of authorship. 24 (PS.) BRUNO CARTHUS. psalm. Mews’s argument for Bruno’s authenticity is based on his belief that the commentary was used for a Psalms-commentary by Roscelin of Compiègne, who studied at Rheims, and on the fact that Bruno is commended for his knowledge of the Psalms in his funerary rolls. The latter is, however, no proof of having written a commentary, and as for Roscelin’s authorship, no other evidence is advanced except that the commentary displays a similarity to his thinking.

Gilbert P. This is the Psalms-commentary of Gilbert of Poitiers (d. 1154);32 it is often referred to in the literature as the Media glosatura.33 It is the only commentary in our group of which the authenticity is certain. Gilbert wrote it sometime before he became bishop of Poitiers in 1141 or 1142, and perhaps as early as by 1117.34 To date, only the prologue and the commentary on the first three psalms have been printed,35 but the full commentary is being edited by Theresa Gross-
                                                29 Andrew B. Kraebel, “Grammatica and the Authenticity of the Psalms-Commentary Attributed to Bruno the Carthusian,” Medieval Studies 71 (2009): 63–97. Kraebel focuses on demonstrating that the commentator uses specifically what he calls a grammatical hermeneutic to interpret the Psalms—that is, that he uses his particular knowledge of grammar and rhetoric, including some rare rhetorical terms, to illustrate the poetic qualities of the Psalms. He then seeks to tie this to Bruno by means of certain evidence that Bruno taught grammar at Rheims. He also argues that several of the other commentators in our group use this commentary, and, since he accepts the suggested attributions of these commentaries (that is, their attributions to Roscelin of Compiègne, Anselm of Laon, and Gilbert the Universal), he concludes that Bruno must predate these authors. Both these relationships of dependence and these attributions will, however, be contested in the following. 30 Van den Eynde, “Complementary Note.” See Chapter Four, 196n52 for the details of Van den Eynde’s argument.  31 Morard, “Le Commentaire des Psaumes.” As opposed to the other scholars, Morard focuses mainly on external criticism. He notes, first, that no decorative elements nor any of the four scribal hands can be traced to the Grande Chartreuse in the first third of the twelfth century (at p. 24). In addition, he notes that Bruno is most often styled “father” in contemporary Carthusian sources, not “master,” as the attribution has it (at p. 25), and that the four references to Bruno’s knowledge of the Psalms in his funerary rolls is no proof of having written a commentary; he points out (at p. 28) that the same is said of Abelard, who did not produce any commentary on the Psalms. Finally, he points to the lack of references in Carthusian sources to any commentary by Bruno prior to the appearance of the attribution in the sixteenth century, and that in those cases where Psalms-commentaries are attributed to Bruno of Chartreux, they turn out to be that of Bruno of Segni (d. 1123). (He questions the attribution to Roscelin of the commentary discussed by Mews.) All in all, there is thus very little that speaks for the authenticity of the attribution of Grenoble, BM, 341 (210) to Bruno of Rheims. 32 GILB. PICT. in psalm. 33 Although this title is often used to include also Gilbert’s commentary on Paul, it will be used here only to refer to his Psalms-commentary. 12 Eynde, however, who revisits this question, believes that Remi A is the shared source of both Bruno, Remi M, and Gilbert U.48 This question will be one of the main topics of Chapter Four. Remi M. This is the commentary spuriously printed in volume 131 of the PL under the name of Remigius of Auxerre.49 Remigius’s true Psalms-commentary was discovered in 1931 by Wilmart;50 although Wilmart did not himself address the attributions of Remi A and Remi M to Remigius, his discovery indirectly disproved the attributions of these two commentaries to the ninth-century Benedictine. The implication of Wilmart’s discovery was explored in detail and reinforced by Alberto Vaccari, who, among other things, traces the attribution of Remi M to Remigius, showing how the caveats stated by its first editor, Eucarius Hirzhorn (Cologne, 1537), regarding this attribution were dropped from the text by Migne,51 thus allowing the spurious attribution to gain traction among later scholars. The manuscript used by Hirtzhorn, purportedly from the abbey of Altenberg, has yet to be identified. In the following chapters, the relationships between these commentaries will be analysed, and many small details regarding their textual agreements will be pointed out. It will, however, never be claimed that any of these details in and of itself proves the argument being made; it is instead the totality of these details which I claim makes a strong case for the thesis that I shall propose regarding the complex nature of the web of twelfth-century Psalms-exegesis.                                                  48 This is the main argument of Van den Eynde, “Complementary Note.” 49 (PS.) --- [p.20] ---

 
 
13 EDITORIAL PRINCIPLES None of the commentaries in our group exists in any critical edition; some of them are printed in the PL, and some are available only in manuscript, which means that they display a wide variety of spellings and punctuation practices. As such, a number of choices have had to be made regarding the presentation of the texts. For my own transcriptions, I have, for the sake of readability, aimed to conform to Classical spelling, hence expanding all diphthongs and using ‘ti’ rather than ‘ci’, ‘c’ rather than ‘k’, and ‘i’ and ‘y’ according to Classical practice. Following the most common practice for Classical editions, I have used consonantal ‘i’ rather than ‘j’, and ‘v’ for consonantal ‘u’. For the sake of internal consistency, and since they are not critical editions, I have applied these principles also to Migne’s printed texts, thus sometimes changing their spelling somewhat. In those cases where I have used a critical edition (namely, in the cases of Psalms-authorities like Augustine, Cassiodorus, and Jerome), or where I cite a text from another secondary source, I have, however, followed the spelling of that edition or source. For the purpose of consistency, I have also allowed myself to change the punctuation of Migne’s texts in certain contexts, or in cases where I believe it is simply incorrect. Furthermore, as different languages employ different punctuation practices, I have, for certain elements, such as, for example, adverbs, punctuated the Latin differently from the English translations. The same goes for capitalization: while English capitalizes, for instance, adjectives and every word in a title, I have chosen, for the Latin text, to capitalize only proper names and nomina sacra; for titles in Latin, I have used sentence style. I have, furthermore, tried to maintain a distinction between the use and the mention of a word: the latter is signalled by single inverted commas: when a Latin word is mentioned in the English 

--- [p.21] ---

 
 
14 text rather than used, it will thus both be italicized and have single inverted commas.52 For words or information to be supplied, I have used square brackets for both the English and the Latin text; pointed brackets are used in the Latin text to signal that the word(s) in question should be omitted. As we are dealing with exegetical texts, italics have—in addition to their normal use for emphases, titles, and non-English words in the body text—been used for all Biblical quotations, both in the Latin text and in the English translations; in those cases where a lemma is mentioned and discussed as such rather than used, it has been set off with single inverted commas.53  For quotations of the Vulgate, I have, as a starting point, followed the Douay-Rheims translation; I have, however, modernized some of its vocabulary—mainly its pronouns. I have also modified the translation in those cases where a more literal translation is needed to make sense of our commentators’ exegetical discussion of the lemma in question. Finally, translations of the Latin text other than the Vulgate are, unless otherwise indicated, my own.   
                                                52 The principle I have tried to apply with consistency is that, if you can understand the word ‘verbum’/‘verba’ in front of a word in the text, then I have put this word in single inverted commas, for example, “Ponit [verba] ‘non stetit’…” (“He puts [the words] ‘has not stood’…”), or “In [verbum] ‘cathedra’ enim magisterium denotamus, in [verbum] ‘sedere’ moram.” (“For in [the word] ‘teacher’s seat’ we signify instructorship, in [the word] ‘to sit’ [we signify] delay.”) 53 I have not changed the typography or punctuation of any critical editions, but I have followed my own editorial principles in my translations of these passages. 

--- [p.22] ---

 
15 
Chapter One ANSELM OF LAON AND THE GLOSSA ORDINARIA ON THE BIBLE  Theories of the Origins of the Biblical Gloss   Glossing—the method of elucidating a literary or scientific text by means of inserting explanatory comments in its margins or in-between its lines—was by no means new in the twelfth century; the practice has its roots in antiquity, when the desire to establish accurate texts and standard versions of already ancient Greek and Roman authors, such as Homer and Plautus, led to great developments in textual criticism.1 Originally, the fruit of these scholarly activities—such as discussions of corrupt or spurious passages, or explanations of archaic words and grammar—took the form of separate commentaries.2 In these commentaries, the segments of text singled out for explanation—the so-called, lemmata—are copied from the work in question—known as the littera—and expounded upon, one after another, in a continuous prose text. This method of commentary continued to be used throughout the Middle Ages, but, from as early as the seventh century, compilers would also often excerpt passages from these late antique commentaries and import them into a copy of the littera itself.3 These excerpts were inserted in the margins and (in the case of Bibles, only later) in-between the lines of the littera,4 thus creating an apparatus of                                                 1 For an account of the development of literary criticism in antiquity, see Leighton D. Reynolds and Nigel G. Wilson, Scribes and Scholars: A Guide to the Transmission of Greek and Latin Literature, 2nd ed. (Oxford: Clarendon Press, 1974), 5–15 and 18–30. 2 Reynolds, Scribes and Scholars, 10. But there are also early examples of brief comments or interpretations being inserted into a copy of the text itself (see Reynolds’s comment on Varro in Scribes and Scholars, 21). 3 See Margaret T. Gibson, “Carolingian Glossed Psalters,” in The Early Medieval Bible: Its Production, Decoration and Use, ed. Richard Gameson, 78–100 (Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 1994), 98. 4 Gibson differentiates between marginal and interlinear glosses based on the type and function of the littera in question: originally, interlinear glosses were used predominantly in the texts of pagan authors employed in the teaching of grammar and rhetoric, while early glossed biblical books would include only a marginal apparatus. Gibson associates the introduction of interlinear glosses into biblical books (“double glossing”) with the use of the 

--- [p.23] ---

 
 
16 explanatory material accompanying the literary or scientific work in question. The individual excerpts of explanatory material accompanying a text are what is referred to in modern scholarly literature as glosses, and a text accompanied by a set of glosses is often referred to as a gloss on that text (as opposed to a continuous commentary).5 In the Middle Ages, the Bible, perhaps not surprisingly, became such a littera in need of interpretation, and this same method of expounding texts began to be applied to it.6 
                                                Bible for classroom teaching, saying that they are not found in Carolingian glossed Bibles before 1050 (see Margaret T. Gibson, “The Place of the Glossa ordinaria in Medieval Exegesis,” in Ad Litteram: Authoritative Texts and Their Medieval Readers, ed. Mark D. Jordan and Kent Emery Jr., 5–27 [Notre Dame, IN: University of Notre Dame Press, 1992], 5–6, 12–13, and 16–17; see also eadem, “Carolingian Glossed Psalters,” 79). Jean de Blic, however, mentions a marginal and interlinear gloss on four of the Prophets (St. Gallen, SB, Cod. Sang. 41) dated to the 9th or 10th century (“near contemporary with Strabo [d. 849].” Jean de Blic, “L’oeuvre exégétique de Walafrid Strabon et la Glossa ordinaria,” Recherches de théologie ancienne et médiévale 16 [1949]: 5–28 [at p. 6]). 5 From a medieval perspective, there is a certain ambiguity in the term ‘glos[s]a’ or ‘glos[s]ula’: Guy Lobrichon notes that, from the time of Isidore of Seville (d. 636) to Hugh of St Victor (d. 1141), ‘glosa’ had been understood simply as a single explanatory word—more or less just a synonym—but that a new definition appeared at the beginning of the twelfth century. (Guy Lobrichon, “Une nouveauté: les gloses de la Bible,” in Le Moyen Âge et la Bible, ed. Pierre Riché and Guy Lobrichon, 95–114, Bible de Tous Les Temps 4 [Paris: Beauchesne, 1984], 96–7.) In support of this claim, Lobrichon cites the testimony of William of Conches: according to William, “… the collection of many things that are held in the mind through study or learning into one work is called a commentary. And although, according to this definition, any book could be called a commentary, we nowadays do not call anything a commentary except the exposition on someone else’s work. It is different from a gloss; for a commentary expounds only the deeper meaning [sententia], not dealing with the larger context [continuatio] or the explanation of the actual wording [littera] of the text. But a gloss covers all of this.” (“… commentum dicitur plurium studio uel doctrina in mente habitorum in unum collectio. Et quamuis secundum hanc diffinitionem commentum possit dici quislibet liber, tamen non hodie uocamus commentum nisi alterius libri expositorium. Quod differt a glosa. Commentum enim, solam sententiam exequens, de continuatione uel expositione litterae nichil agit. Glosa uero omnia illa exequitur.”) GUILL. CONCH. in Plat. Tim. 19.37–43. William’s conception of a glosa as a full, often continuous commentary is evidenced in the manuscripts (as well as in somewhat later library catalogues), where it is used synonymously with terms like ‘enarrationes’ or ‘expositio’. We may note, however, that it appears, in the cases of full commentaries, that the plural, ‘glosae’/‘glosulae’, is more often used. (Although an exception to this is Laon, BM, 17 (containing Gilbert U), which calls the work glosula, in the singular.) ‘Glosatura’ also seems to be applied only to commentaries as a whole. On the different types of medieval commentaries, see Nikolaus Häring, “Commentary and Hermeneutics,” in Renaissance and Renewal in the Twelfth Century, ed. Robert Benson, Giles Constable, and Carol Lanham, 173–200 (Toronto: University of Toronto Press in association with the Medieval Academy of America, 1991). Häring also points out here the difficulty of determining the exact meaning of the term ‘sententia’; he himself translates it as “doctrine” (see Häring, “Commentary and Hermeneutics,” 179n72). As the word seems, however, to cover a larger sematic field, I have opted to translate it variably as “deeper meaning,” “interpretation,” or “held position” (as in, the position held by an authority—either ancient or “modern”—on an exegetical or doctrinal question). 6 The practice of glossing biblical books appears to have been exported to the continent from Ireland or Northumbria towards the end of the eighth century or the beginning of the ninth. As opposed to what the Carolingians began producing, however, the Irish glossators had not applied the technique in a systematic way. (Gibson, “Carolingian Glossed Psalters,” 98). Lobrichon agrees with Gibson in his assessment that double glossing (see 15n4) did not take root on the continent until the third quarter of the eleventh century. (Lobrichon, “Une nouveauté”, 99.) 

--- [p.24] ---

 
 
17 In the centuries spanning from the first Church Fathers until the time of Bede (d. 735), the Bible received a vast body of orthodox, authoritative interpretation in the form of continuous commentaries. By the time the Carolingians began their efforts to reform religious life, the amount of exegetical material had accumulated beyond what any single person could possibly master. As part of their efforts to place the study of the Bible at the centre of their reform program,7 the Carolingians applied themselves to making this material more accessible and manageable by collecting, selecting, and abridging the patristic commentaries, and gathering these excerpts together in so-called florilegia, “collections of flowers.”8  Although they also produced some biblical glosses—mainly on the Psalter9—the predominant format of Carolingian exegesis was still, however, the continuous, lemmatized text.                                                 7 John Contreni stresses the prominence given, as a matter of deliberate policy, to the study of the Bible in Charlemagne’s reform program. As an illustration of this, he contrasts the number of biblical commentaries produced in the two centuries preceding the Carolingians with that of the amount produced by them: between 500 and 750, only 35 biblical commentaries are recorded—14 of which were written by Bede—while at least 150 biblical commentaries were written in the 125-year period stretching from the end of the eighth century to the beginning of the tenth (see John J. Contreni, “Carolingian Biblical Culture,” in Iohannes Scottus Eriugena: The Bible and Hermeneutics. Proceedings of the Ninth International Colloquium of the Society for the Promotion of Eriugenian Studies Held at Leuven and Louvain-la-Neuve June 7-10, 1995, ed. Gerd Van Riel, Carlos Steel, and James McEvoy, 1–23 [Leuven: Leuven University Press, 1996], 3–4 and 7). 8 According to Contreni, the study of the Bible in the ninth century meant the study of the Fathers: the Carolingian commentators were keenly aware of their own exegetical methodology when composing their florilegia, and, “in the process … defined a new kind of exegesis in which the biblical text itself receded into the background, leaving the patristic authorities prominently in the foreground. … The biblical verses themselves served only to organize the exposition.” The biblical text is brought back into focus in the third quarter of the ninth century with Paschasius Radbertus and Johannes Scotus Eriugena. (Contreni, “Carolingian Biblical Culture,” 9–10.) Regarding the patristic material, Gibson observes that, if given a choice between the original patristic work and a later, Carolingian recension of it, the Carolingian commentators preferred the latter when compiling their glosses. (Margaret T. Gibson, “The Twelfth-Century Glossed Bible,” in Studia Patristica 23, ed. Elizabeth A. Livingstone, 232–244 [Leuven: Peeters, 1989], 237, and eadem, “The Glossa ordinaria in Medieval Exegesis,” 10.) This seems to be true for some early as well as some later commentators. Contreni notes, however, that there was also a realization for the need to re-establish direct contact with the Fathers. (Contreni, “Carolingian Biblical Culture,” 8.) 9 According to Gibson, the Carolingians rarely applied the practice of including a gloss-apparatus in the copying of a biblical book other than to the Psalter. (Gibson, “Carolingian Glossed Psalters, 99.) As examples of non-Psalter glosses, she identifies only one mid-ninth-century group of four volumes—three containing different prophets, and the fourth containing Acts, the Catholic Epistles, and the Apocalypse—which displays a marginal apparatus. (Gibson, “The Glossa ordinaria in Medieval Exegesis,” 9.) The Carolingian glossed Psalters are almost all luxury manuscripts with what Gibson calls a systematic annotation—that is, glosses which are a planned part of the production, as opposed to spontaneous notes by individual readers. Initially, these marginal apparatuses were often based on Cassiodorus, but there are also examples of Augustine-based glossed Psalters. The few interlinear glosses that are found are later readers’ notes. (Margaret T. Gibson, “The Glossed Bible,” in Biblia Latina cum Glossa Ordinaria: Facsimile Reprint of the Editio Princeps. Adolph Rusch of Strassburg 1480/81, ed. Karlfried Froehlich 

--- [p.25] ---

 
 
18 Furthermore, before the twelfth-century and the arrival of the Glossa ordinaria, the production of biblical commentaries was, for the most part, sporadic, meaning that most theologians commented only on selected books of their choice (favourites included Genesis, Job, the Psalms, Canticles, the Gospels, and the Pauline Epistles). The exception is Hrabanus Maurus (d. 856), who commented on all of the Old Testament except Proverbs, the Psalms, and the Minor Prophets, as well as on Matthew and the Pauline Epistles.10 By the twelfth century, many of these Carolingian commentators had become authorities in their own right, adding to the mass of exegetical material to be considered in theological education.  THE GLOSSA ORDINARIA ON THE BIBLE The Glossa ordinaria is often described as a product of the surge of pedagogical activity that occurred in the cathedral schools at the beginning of the twelfth-century;11 it is the first exegetical work to cover the entire Bible, and it is believed to be the first concerted effort to provide this comprehensiveness. The Gloss is made up of the complete Bible text presented integrally in a central column and surrounded by an apparatus of authoritative, mostly patristic and Carolingian exegetical excerpts; on either side of the biblical text we find the longer, so-called marginal glosses, and in-between the lines the shorter, so-called interlinear glosses, all of 
                                                and Margaret T. Gibson, vii–ix [Turnhout: Brepols, 1992], viii.) Gibson surmises that the clear division in these luxury manuscripts between the Bible-text and its marginal apparatus would have discouraged readers from inserting their own comments, as was otherwise common. (Gibson, “Carolingian Glossed Psalters,” 79). 10 See William Schipper, “‘Unpublished’ Commentaries by Hrabanus Maurus,” The Journal of Medieval Latin 27 (2017): 223–301 (at p. 225).  11 The designation of the Gloss as ordinaria is not contemporary but dates to the fourteenth century (see Beryl Smalley, “Glossa ordinaria,” in Theologische Realenzyklopädie, vol. 13, ed. Christian Uhlig, 452–457 [Berlin, New York: Walter de Gruyter, 1984], 452). Originally, it was referred to simply as glosa, glosatura, or expositor. Later in the twelfth century it began to be designated as the parva glosatura, the “little gloss,” to distinguish it from its two successors (see Chapter Two). As the former designation is, however, the one by which the work is generally known in modern scholarship, I have chosen to follow this nomenclature. 

--- [p.26] ---

 
 
19 which serve to interpret and explain individual words, phrases, or extended passages of the biblical text.12  As Christopher de Hamel notes, everyone who taught or read the Bible added their own marginal notations of patristic readings to it. What was new about the Gloss was that the Bible now came with a complete ready-to-copy and guaranteed-to-be-orthodox apparatus,13 from which anyone could choose on what to elaborate in, for example, a lecture or a sermon.14 While it may now seem self-evident that the conception of such a work fits perfectly in the scholastic culture of the early twelfth century, the Gloss was long believed (seemingly based on an 
                                                12 According to Lobrichon, the three-column format, with interlinear glosses inserted into the space of the sacred text itself, is unique to Latin Christendom. (Lobrichon, “Une nouveauté,” 98.) The general view is that the text of the Gloss (that is, the specific set of glosses that make up the Gloss) displays a relatively high degree of stability, even if it is possible to see a certain development between the early and the mid-twelfth century (see Smalley, Study [1978], 65). In support of this assessment, Gibson makes the point that “odd little interlinear notes, easily overlooked and of no great value are scrupulously copied in manuscript after manuscript.” (Gibson, “The Glossed Bible,” vii; eadem, “The Glossa ordinaria in Medieval Exegesis,” 19. The fact that these seemingly insignificant interlinear notes do get copied out so carefully should, however, indicate to us that they were, in fact, considered to be of great value or, at least, integral to the work.) 13 Christopher de Hamel, Glossed Books of the Bible and the Origins of the Paris Book Trade (Woodbridge: D. S. Brewer, 1984), 1. 14 Despite its central place in the history of medieval intellectual culture, the exact purpose of the Gloss has never been conclusively established. Smalley believes that it was a classroom text, used by masters of the sacred page in their lecturing on Scripture (see Beryl Smalley, “Les commentaires bibliques de l’époque romane: glose ordinaire et gloses périmées,” Cahiers de civilisation médiévale 4 [1961]: 15–22 [at p. 16]; her basis for this theory is most likely the fact that Peter Comestor lectured on the Gloss itself later in the twelfth century). Gibson, however, believes that the Gloss was meant for the library rather than the classroom (Gibson, “The Glossed Bible,” vii;)—in essence, a reference work, “as much the convenient record of patristic opinion as the analysis of grammar and syntax.” (Gibson, “The Glossa ordinaria in Medieval Exegesis,” 19; also stated in eadem, “The Twelfth-Century Glossed Bible,” 243–244.) The two theories are not necessarily mutually exclusive; we may remind ourselves of Peter the Chanter’s testimony to the fact that it was also the duty of a cathedral school master to preach to the chapter: “In tribus igitur constitit exercicium sacre Scripture: circa lectionem, disputationem et praedicationem, …” (“The practice of sacred Scripture, therefore, consists of three things: lecturing, disputation and preaching, …”) PETR. CANTOR abbrev. A, 1, 38–39 (CCCM 196 A, 14), and it is easy to see how a work such as the Gloss would have been useful both as a reference work when putting together a sermon and as the basis of a lecture on the Bible. The advantage of the gloss-arrangement is, unmistakably, that the user, while having the Biblical text fully and clearly in front of him, is instantly directed to, and quickly gets the gist of, a pre-approved selection of orthodox interpretation without having to peruse pages and pages of commentary material or search for lemmata in a dense continuous text. Thus, at the same time as being comprehensive (albeit terse), it gives the user a good overview of a large body of information—two otherwise often opposed metrics. The Gloss is, in a word, efficient. That it was not the place to advance new and daring theological interpretations is probably a correct assessment—presumably, this is not what “Biblical Studies 101” was about—so when Gibson says that the Gloss remained available and consulted but unchallenged (Gibson, “The Glossa ordinaria in Medieval Exegesis,” 21), we should perhaps instead say that it remained available and consulted, and unchallenged.

20 attribution by the sixteenth-century German humanist Johannes Trithemius, who is responsible for a number of spurious attributions of medieval works to German authors) to be the work of the Carolingian abbot Walafrid Strabo (d. 849);15 this attribution appears for the first time in the Gloss itself in the 1590 Paris edition, which also names Anselm as a later co-author.16 This double authorship became, in the 1617 Douay/Antwerp edition, a division between the authorship of the marginal and the interlinear glosses, the former being attributed to Strabo and the latter to Anselm.17 These attributions were accepted by Jacques Paul Migne, who, as he was only interested in printing the “original” form of the Gloss, consequently printed in the Patrologia Latina only the marginal glosses among Strabo’s works.18  The attribution to Strabo was first brought into question in 1893 by Samuel Berger, who noted that there are almost no manuscripts of the Gloss that can be dated to before the twelfth century.19 (There are, in fact, none.) Berger’s lingering reservations—based on some (perceived) similarities between the Gloss and certain works ascribed to Strabo—were finally dismissed in 1949 by Jean de Blic, who established that the similarities in question are exaggerated and are better explained by a mutual dependence on the same patristic sources.20 As for the idea that the                                                 15 “Hic denique Strabus glosam quae ordinaria nunc dicitur super totam Bibliam ex dictis sanctorum patrum primus comportasse memoratur, quam alii multis postmodum adiunctis sententiis patrum ampliaverunt.” (“Lastly, this Strabo is said to have compiled from the sayings of the Holy Fathers the gloss on the whole Bible which is now called the Ordinaria, which others later expanded by adding many patristic interpretations.”) JOH. TRITH. script. eccl., fol. 65r. 16 Karlfried Froehlich, “The Printed Gloss,” in Biblia Latina cum Glossa Ordinaria: Facsimile Reprint of the Editio Princeps. Adolph Rusch of Strassburg 1480/81, ed. Karlfried Froehlich and Margaret T. Gibson, xii–xxvi (Turnhout: Brepols, 1992), xxiii–xxiv. Froehlich speculates that the ascription of one of the first glosses on Genesis 1:1 to Strabus was enough to satisfy Trithemius of the German abbot’s authorship. On Strabo’s commentary on Genesis, see 20n20. 17 Froehlich, “The Printed Gloss,” xxv. 18 PL Database vol. 113, cols 67–1316, and vol. 114, cols 9–752. 19 Samuel Berger, Histoire de la Vulgate pendant les premiers siècles du Moyen Age (Paris: 1893. Reprint, New York: Burt Franklin, 1958), 133–36. Those few eighth- to ninth-century manuscripts that he does find contain texts that have only served as sources for what later became the Gloss (namely, on the Prophets and Catholic Epistles) and can still not be attributed to Strabo. 20 De Blic, “L’oeuvre exégétique,” 5–9. De Blic does, however, show that there is material in the Gloss on the Pentateuch—attributed to Strabo already in the twelfth century—that is authentic. He also points out that, apart from 

--- [p.28] ---

 
 
21 “Glossa interlinearis” and the “Glossa ordinaria” are two separate works, Hans Glunz had already in 1933 been the first to point out that we know of no twelfth-century manuscript that contains one without the other,21 and Smalley dispensed with the notion altogether by observing that, quite often, the same gloss may appear in the margin in one manuscript but in-between the lines in another.22  Although he believed that many generations of earlier compilers had contributed to gathering the material, Glunz attributed the execution of the Gloss as a whole to Peter Lombard (d. 1160). This theory was, in turn, disproven by Smalley, who showed that there are parts of the Gloss that predate Lombard.23 It was her work that gave rise to the now generally accepted view that the Glossa ordinaria was a unified project, both envisioned and begun as such by Anselm at his cathedral school at Laon in the late eleventh century.24  THE LAONNOISE CONNECTION Anselm of Laon is supposed to have begun teaching at the cathedral of that city in ca. 1080. He continued his ecclesiastical career there until his death in 1117, assuming consecutively the offices of chancellor, dean, and archdeacon.25 Plenty of sources indicate that he was regarded by                                                 being only one of the Gloss’s many sources, Strabo’s authentic commentaries are continuous and thus cannot have been the model for the format of the Gloss. (“L’oeuvre exégétique,” 22.) 21 Hans H. Glunz, History of the Vulgate in England: From Alcuin to Roger Bacon (Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 1933), 202. Glunz, nevertheless, still treats the “interlinearis” and the “ordinaria” as two distinct works. 22 Smalley, “Gilbertus Universalis II,” 27. 23 She does this by demonstrating that Gilbert the Universal compiled the Gloss on Lamentations and was most likely also involved in putting together the Gloss on the Pentateuch and the Minor Prophets (see Beryl Smalley, “Gilbertus Universalis, Bishop of London [1128–34], and the Problem of the ‘Glossa Ordinaria,’” Recherches de théologie ancienne et médiévale 7 [1935]: 235−261). 24 See Smalley, “Gilbertus Universalis II.” 25 Anselm is attested as chancellor in 1095, as dean from at least 1109 (but possibly already from 1098; for a discussion and arguments in favour of this date, see Cédric Giraud, Per verba magistri: Anselme de Laon et son école au XIIe siècle [Turnhout: Brepols, 2010], 52–53), and, finally, as archdeacon in 1114. 22 his contemporaries as one of the most distinguished theologians of his time. Cédric Giraud, who has produced the most comprehensive study of Anselm to date, cites a substantial number of epitaphs and chronicles which extol Anselm and his teaching to the skies: he is described as the first among teachers (princeps doctorum), most renowned (nominatissimus), most distinguished master (celeberrimus magister), brilliant and outstanding teacher of teachers (praeclarus and egregius doctor doctorum), star of the Franks (sidus Francorum), and glory of Laon (gloria Lauduni). If one of the chronicles is to be believed, even a pope (namely, Eugene III, 1145–1153) commented on Anselm’s important contribution to the understanding of Scripture.26  From some genres that are less intentionally laudatory in nature, we may cite two different passages by John of Salisbury (d. 1180): the first is from his Metalogicon; here, he calls Anselm and his brother Ralph (d. ca. 1134) “the brightest lights of Gaul, the glory of Laon ... whose memory is held in delight and blessedness.”27 The second is found in his Historia pontificalis and illustrates even more clearly Anselm’s status as an authority on sacred doctrine: here, John recounts an episode from the 1148 trial of Gilbert of Poitiers (who had been Anselm’s student at Laon), where one of the masters present  describes Anselm and Ralph as the “the most illustrious teachers” and uses their rejection of a certain theological interpretation (dismissed also by Gilbert the Universal and Alberic of Rheims, both of whom had either worked with or studied under Anselm at Laon) as a warning to Bernard of Clairvaux and his supporters against endorsing said doctrine.28                                                  ecclesiastical career are found in the episcopal acts of Laon, edited by Annie Dufour-Malbezin and cited in Giraud, Per verba magistri, 52–53. For the full account of Anselm’s life, see Giraud, Per verba magistri, 35–78. 26 This according to the chronicler Robert of Auxerre: “De quo dixisse fertur Eugenius papa quia Deus spiritum eius suscitaverit, ne scriptura periret.” (“About whom pope Eugene is told to have said that God stirred up his spirit in order that Scripture not perish.”) ROB. 23 Testimonies of Anselm’s magisterial authority can also be found in more unexpected contexts: as Andrew Kraebel astutely points out, the explicit of the manuscript Oxford, Balliol College, 36, which contains the Psalms-commentary of Gilbert of Poitiers, demonstrates “the degree to which Laonnois exegesis of the Psalter was expected to conform to Anselm’s interpretation”;29 the colophon reads: “Here ends master Gilbert Porreta’s gloss on the Psalter, which he himself read aloud before his master Anselm in order for it to be corrected.”30 These testimonies together make it clear that Anselm was regarded by his contemporaries with exceptional esteem for his abilities as a teacher and theologian.31                                                 Gislebertus Universalis qui post fuit episcopus Lundoniensis, nec Albericus Remensis qui post in archiepiscopum Bituris sublimatus est, hoc ob eandem causam admittere uoluerunt.” (“He had heard, he said, that this [interpretation] had been addressed in the schools of the most renowned teachers, the brothers Anselm and Ralph of Laon, but not at all accepted by them because they feared going beyond the definitions that the Fathers had set. For the same reason, neither Gilbert the Universal, who later became bishop of London, nor Alberic of Rheims, afterwards promoted to archbishop of Bourges, wanted to accept it.”) JOH. SARISB. hist. pont. 18–19. The passage bespeaks a certain conservativeness in Anselm’s approach to theology noted by several scholars. For a summary of the different assessments of Anselm’s originality by modern scholars, see Alexander Andrée, “Anselm of Laon Unveiled: The Glosae Super Iohannem and the Origins of the Glossa Ordinaria on the Bible,” Medieval Studies 73 (2011): 217–260 (at pp. 219–220). For Gilbert the Universal’s connection to Laon, see Andrée, Glossa ordinaria in Lamentationes, 40–45; for Alberic, see John R. Williams, “The Cathedral School of Rheims in the Time of Master Alberic, 1118–1136,” Traditio 20 (1964): 93–114 (at p. 96). 29 Andrew B. Kraebel, “The Education of Anselm of Laon: Some Evidence from the Psalms-Commentaries” (paper, Annual Meeting of the Medieval Academy of America, Chicago, IL, March 2009). 30 “Explicit glosatura magistri giliberti porretani super psalterium, quam ipse recitavit coram suo magistro anselmo, causa emendationis.” Cited in Gross-Diaz, From Lectio Divina, 32n26. 31 It should be noted, however, that the testimony best remembered in modern times—and therefore the one that seems to have had the greatest influence on modern scholarship—is not a positive one at all: it is the scathingly critical account given by Anselm’s most famous student, namely Peter Abelard (d. 1142), in his Historia calamitatum. Here, Abelard recounts how he went to Laon to seek Anselm out, as he was the greatest authority on theology, but that he found him to be an old windbag, capable of nothing but entertaining an unquestioning audience with elegant speech, which, to anyone looking more closely, revealed itself to lack any sensible meaning or reason. (Even though Abelard thus acknowledges Anselm’s status, he attributes this to the master’s age and time in the profession rather than to any talent; he seems also to be critical of his fellow students, who listen, but apparently do not question.) Disappointed to find no fruits of wisdom in Anselm’s teaching, Abelard likens him to the barren fig tree that Christ cursed (cf. Mark 11:13–14) and elaborates with a metaphor of someone lighting a fire in his house but managing to fill it only with smoke rather than light; to those seeking clarification, Anselm was, according to Abelard, able to provide only confusion: “Quo completo reuersus sum in Franciam, maxime ut de diuinitate addiscerem, quando iam sepefatus magister noster Guillhelmus in episcopatu Catalunensi pollebat. In hac autem lectione magister eius Anselmus Laudunensis maximam ex antiquitate auctoritatem tunc tenebat. … Accessi igitur ad hunc senem, cui magis longeuus usus quam ingenium uel memoria nomen comparauerat. Ad quem si quis de aliqua questione pulsandum accederet incertus, redibat incertior. Mirabilis quidem in oculis erat auscultantium, sed nullus in conspectu questionantium. Verborum usum habebat mirabilem, sed sensu contemptibilem et ratione uacuum. Cum ignem accenderet, domum suam fumo implebat, non luce illustrabat. Arbor eius tota in foliis aspicientibus a longe conspicua uidebatur, sed propinquantibus et diligentius intuentibus infructuosa reperiebatur. 

--- [p.31] ---

 
 
24 The Middle Ages also clearly associated the Gloss with Anselm. We have, for example, the chronicle of the Cistercian brother Alberic of Trois-Fontaines, written sometime between 1227 and 1240. For the year 1115, Alberic writes: “At this time … lived at Laon, in France, that most renowned master Anselm, who was the first to produce a non-continuous marginal and interlinear gloss, and his brother Ralph … .”32 Similarly, according to a late twelfth-century appendix to a thirteenth-century catalogue of “illustrious authors of the Church,” “Anselm of Laon clarified, in a new kind of exposition, the scriptures of both Testaments in an interlinear and marginal gloss excerpted from the writings of the Fathers.”33 As a final example, we have also the twelfth-century theologian Peter the Chanter (d. 1197), who in his Psalms-commentary laments the fact that Anselm was prevented from finishing his gloss on the whole Bible due to the squabbling and shenanigans of his canons.34  
                                                Ad hanc itaque cum accessissem, ut fructum inde colligerem, deprehendi illam esse ficulneam, cui maledixit Dominus, seu illam ueterem quercum cui Pompeium Lucanus comparat dicens: / Stat, magni nominis umbra, / Qualis frugifero quercus sublimis in agro, / et cetera.” ABELARD. Hist. cal. 9.140–10.161. To be sure, Abelard’s description is teeming with disdain, but, for that very reason, it can perhaps be taken as a confirmation of Anselm’s standing as a theologian and teacher, for outshining such a pathetic creature as is portrayed here would be no achievement worth bragging about, as Abelard does. 32 “Hoc tempore … florebant in Francia apud Laudunum nominatissimus ille magister Anselmus, qui glosaturam interlinearem seu marginalem interruptam primus exhibuit, et frater eius Radulfus …” ALBER. TRI. chron., 820. 33 “Anselmus Laudunensis … nouo expositionis genere utriusque Testamenti Scripturas glosa interlineari et marginali de Patrum scriptis elucidauit.” Nikolaus Häring (ed.), “Two Catalogues of Medieval Authors,” Franciscan Studies 26 [1966]: 195–211 (at p. 208). (Häring edits here the Appendix.) Even though, as noted above, glossing per se was not a new phenomenon when it came to biblical exegesis in the twelfth century, we may note that this testimony perceives of the method of the Gloss as a novelty, as does Alberic in the preceding testimony. 34 “Unde lugere adhuc debemus super hoc quod magister Anselmus non permittebatur perfecte glosare omnem sacram paginam, sicut incepit, quia canonici quorum erat decanus et alii plures eum ab illo studio amoverunt saepe, vel litigando, scilicet propter lites suas, vel honorando quasi per adulationem, vel pauperes opprimendo, quorum necessitatibus exigebatur interesse, vel persequendo, quando scilicet compellebant eum interesse negotiis capituli sui.” (“Wherefore we ought still to lament the fact that master Anselm was not allowed to completely gloss the whole Bible, as he had begun to, because the canons of whom he was dean, and many others, often took him away from this pursuit, either by litigating due to their squabbles, or by fawning over him as if with flatteries, or by oppressing the poor, whose needs he was required to attend to, or by pursuing him, namely when they forced him to take part in the business of their chapter.”) PETR. CANTOR psalt. 118,115. (Paris, BnF, lat. 12011, fol. 173r.) Alexander Andrée, however, does not believe that the Chanter is referring here to the Gloss (see Andrée, “Anselm Unveiled,” 234). In reference to the question of the timespan of Anselm’s teaching activities, we may note that Anselm was apparently still teaching in 1113, the time of Abelard’s visit (see 23n31).

25 But Anselm was also associated specifically with the Gloss on the Pauline Epistles and—most importantly for our present purposes—the Psalms: for example, as we shall see below, both Peter the Chanter and Peter of Poitiers (d. 1215) refer in their respective Psalms-commentaries to Anselm’s Psalms-exegesis—references that agree in content with the Gloss.35 Other examples are found in various chronicles: one is the so-called Continuatio Praemonstratensis, which represents a continuation of a chronicle begun by Sigebert of Gembloux (d. 1111), redacted around 1160 by a Premonstratensian canon of St. Martin at Laon;36 it reads under the year 1117: Anselm, most renowned master of the city of Laon, famous for his knowledge of letters, passed away—a man laudable for his impeccable character and sound judgement, who, among his other works, with gainful devotion and expert industry also arranged marginal and interlinear glosses on the Psalter abridged from the original expounders.37   This testimony is among those that Smalley musters against Glunz’s argument that the Gloss as a whole should be attributed to Peter Lombard. She presents a slew of contemporary (or near-contemporary) evidence associating Anselm with the Gloss, the majority of which mentions specifically the Gloss on the Psalms. We have, for example, Maurice, prior of the Augustinian house of Kirkham from 1169 to 1177,38 who in a treatise sent to Gilbert of Sempringham (d. 1189)

(Paris, BnF, lat. 15009, fol. 77r.) 38 Janet E. Burton, Kirkham Priory from Foundation to Dissolution (York: University of York, Borthwick Institute of Historical Research, 1995), 16.

26 mentions that he has, at the time of writing, had in his possession Anselm’s glosses on the Psalter for nearly 44 or 45 years.39 Another, oft-cited example is that of Herbert of Bosham, a student of Peter Lombard’s, who, in the preface to his edition of Lombard’s Psalms-commentary, written sometime between 1173 and 1176, comments on his master’s purpose for compiling the work: according to Herbert, Lombard had told him in person (as an explanation as to why his glosses may have not been executed with irreproachable precision) that he only compiled his commentary in order to clarify “the obscure brevity of the former glossator, Anselm of Laon.”40 These accounts, together with several medieval library catalogue entries and contemporary attributions in the manuscripts, lead Smalley to conclude that “we can hardly deny to Anselm the authorship of the Gloss on the Psalter and St. Paul.”41 This is where the general consensus now stands.                                                 39 “… quam [sententiam] et uos olim sicut dixistis ab illo magno et insigni doctore uestro beato Anselmo habuistis, cuius apud nos in psalterio tractatus et glosule ab annis ferme quadraginta quinque habentur.” (“… which [interpretation] also you, as you said, once received from your great and distinguished teacher, blessed Anselm, whose treatise and glosses on the Psalter have been with us for nearly 45 years.”) Cited in Montague R. James, “The Salomites,” Journal of Theological Studies 35 [1934]: 287–297 [at p. 289].) Maurice’s treatise is extant in two manuscripts—Oxford, Lincoln College, lat. 27, and Oxford, Bodleian Library, Hatton 92—the second of which gives the number of years as 44. The Hatton manuscript (cited by Smalley in “Gilbertus Universalis II,” 31) also places the specification ‘in psalterio’ differently: “… cuius apud nos tractatus et glosule ab annis ferme quadraginta quattuor in psalterio habentur.” The reading of the Lincoln College manuscript is, therefore, interesting, as it states less ambiguously than does the Hatton manuscript that both the tractatus and the glosule of Anselm expounded the Psalms; the significance of this implication will become clear below. As Maurice wrote his treatise during his tenure as prior, and presuming his estimate is accurate, this gives us a date of acquisition of between 1124 and 1132, which is quite well before the appearance of the earliest datable manuscript in 1145 (see below). Some time after the tract had been written and sent to Gilbert, Maurice sent another copy to Roger of Pont L’Evêque, archbishop of York between 1154 and 1181; the letter of address mentions his prior exchange with Gilbert (see James, “The Salomites,” 288). This should mean that Maurice was in possession of the works in question by 1136 at the latest. 40 “Nam cum haec opera scriberet, nequaquam sicut ipsomet referente didici, ipsi venit in mentem, quod in scolis publicis legerentur, solum ob id facta, ut antiquioris glosatoris, magistri videlicet Anselmi Laudunensis, brevitatem elucidarent obscuram.” (“For when he wrote these works, it did not in the least enter into his mind, as I learned from his own account, that they would be read in the public schools; he said that they had only been made for the purpose of elucidating the obscure brevity of the older glossator, namely master Anselm of Laon.”) Cambridge, Trinity College, B.5.4, fol. 2r. 41 Smalley, “Gilbertus Universalis II,” 34. The Psalms-Gloss manuscripts that carry an attribution to Anselm are Hereford, Cathedral Library, O. 6. XII, and Cambridge, University Library, Dd. IV. 25; the first belonged to bishop Robert Foliot (1174–86) and calls the Gloss Parva glosatura; the second manuscript adds the attribution in a different but contemporary hand and identifies Anselm as Sanctum Anselmum, confusing him with Anselm of Canterbury. (Smalley comments, however, that this confusion of the two Anselms need not surprise us. [“Gilbertus Universalis II,” 32.] Nevertheless, the mistake shows that the medievals were not necessarily always one hundred percent clear on who this Anselm-author of the Gloss actually was.)
