--- [p.34] ---

 
 
27 ORALITY AND THE TRANSMISSION OF KNOWLEDGE IN THE TWELFTH CENTURY Of great significance to the question of the origins and authorship of our twelfth century Psalms-commentaries is the recognition that the main mode of the transmission of knowledge in the twelfth-century cathedral school was the spoken lecture rather than, as in our day, the written word. The central activity of the early medieval classroom was the master’s lectio, the reading aloud to the class of one of the primary texts of the subject to be studied.42 Every branch of the so-called trivium—which consisted of grammar, rhetoric, and dialectic—and the quadrivium—arithmetic, geometry, astronomy, and music—had its canonical texts to be lectured upon: for grammar, Donatus and Priscian, for rhetoric, Cicero’s De inventione and the Rhetorica ad Herennium, for dialectic, Porphyry’s Isagoge, Aristotle’s Categories, and later the Prior and Posterior Analytics, for arithmetic and music Boethius, and for theology, of course, the Bible. While Paré, Brunet, and Tremblay press the point that a good memory was of the utmost importance to the student, as books were rare and expensive,43 later scholars have concluded that 
                                                psalterium glosatum Anselmi/secundum magistrum Anselmum are Bury St. Edmund’s, Christ Church, Canterbury, and Durham Cathedral, the last of which is recorded in a list of books that belonged to prior Lawrence, who occupied this position from 1149 to -53. In addition to this, Smalley also mentions some thirteenth-century marginal notes in the chronicle of Robert of Auxerre (d. 1212), which lay out what has become the standard account of the progression of the three major Paul- and Psalms-glosses; they add to the year 1138: “Clarebat Gislebertus doctor eximius … Hic post magistrum Anselmum super psalterium et super epistolas Pauli ex dictis sanctorum patrum compactam utilem satis edidit glosaturam.” (“It was the heyday of the outstanding teacher Gilbert … Following master Anselm, he produced a very useful gloss put together from the sayings of the Holy Fathers.”) ROB. AUTISS. chron., 234. And under 1154: “Hic etiam [Petrus Lombardus] glosaturam super psalterium et epistolas Pauli ab Anselmo per glosulas interlineales marginalesque distinctam, post a Gisleberto continuative productam, latius apertiusque explicuit.” (“[Peter Lombard] also expounded more extensively and clearly the gloss on the Psalter and the Pauline Epistles furnished by Anselm with interlinear and marginal glosses and later made continuous by Gilbert.”) Ibid., 237. 42 The following account draws on Paré, Brunet, and Tremblay, Les écoles, 109–137. We may note here that ‘lectio’ could mean three different things: the master’s reading aloud to his students, the students listening to their master’s reading, and the individual reading of any person (see Paré, Brunet, and Tremblay, Les écoles, 115). 43 Paré, Brunet, and Tremblay, Les écoles, 114. 

--- [p.35] ---

 
 
28 students must also have had their own copies of the littera in front of them in order to be able to follow along in the lecture.44  The lectio consisted of three parts: first, the linguistic aspects of the text (the littera) were explained; then, its literal meaning (the sensus), and lastly, its deeper meaning (the sententia).45 In the course of these explications, questions would arise out of the various difficulties and statements found in the text which the master would pause to allow for the discussion of, consider various solutions to, and, finally, declare his own position on (also known as his sententia). Out of these discussions was later born the literary device known as the quaestio and the academic practice of disputatio.46 Important for our purposes is the fact that the students would take notes on these discussions, so-called reportationes, which could then be copied by others. In his Historia calamitatum, Peter Abelard gives a testimony of this process: For that lecture was so enjoyable to everyone who was there, that they praised it with exceptional acclamation and compelled me to gloss [the text] in the same vein as this lecture. Indeed, when those who had not been there heard this, they started jostling to get to the second and third lecture, and everyone began equally to greatly concern themselves with copying the glosses which I had begun on the first day, at their very beginning.47  
                                                44 On this topic, see Alexander Andrée and Mark J. Clark, “The School of Paris: the Bible and Theology in the Twelfth-Century Classroom” (Toronto: PIMS, manuscript submitted for review), 18–19, and 19n31 of typescript.  45 See 16n5 for the meaning(s) of the term ‘sententia’. Compare also how these activities relate to the genres of commentary discussed by William of Conches. (Ibid.) 46 See Paré, Brunet, and Tremblay, Les écoles, 113. 47 “Omnibus tamen, qui affuerunt, in tantum lectio illa grata extitit, ut eam singulari preconio extollerent et me secundum hunc nostre lectionis tenorem ad glosandum compellerent. Quo quidem audito hii, qui non interfuerant, ceperunt ad secundam et terciam lectionem certatim concurrere, et omnes pariter de transcribendis glosis, quas prima die inceperam in ipso earum initio, plurimum solliciti esse.” ABELARD, Hist. cal. 12.192-198. Paré, Brunet, and Tremblay do not believe that Abelard is speaking here about students’ notes, since he later refers to them as his written work (see Paré, Brunet, and Tremblay, Les écoles, 117n1). This does not, however, seem to be a good reading of the passage: the students are asking for the glosses from the first day of Abelard’s lecturing; if he had been lecturing from a finished commentary, there would have been no reason to specify the day on which the desired glosses had been read. This indicates rather, as we shall discuss below, that Abelard’s finished commentary was produced as a result of his teaching. 

--- [p.36] ---

 
 
29 Another witness to the practice of students taking notes on their master’s lectures is Lawrence of Westminster, a student of Hugh of St Victor’s in the 1130s. His only surviving work is the Sententiae de divinitate, which he describes in his prefatory letter as a reportatio of Hugh’s lectures, referring to himself not as the author of the work but only as its craftsman, artifex.48 Lawrence explains, furthermore, how his fellow students persuaded him, ultimately with the help of Hugh himself, to take down these notes on the master’s sententiae. Once a week, Lawrence then showed his notes to Hugh, who, clearly conscious of the dangers involved in having one’s teachings misrepresented by a negligent note-taker, would correct them:  When [Hugh] had begun to teach his sentences on divinity, I was asked by several of my fellow students … to commend to writing and memory those same sentences for the common good of myself and others. When I, since I knew myself better than they, did not agree to their entreaties after having been asked two or three times, they finally brought master Hugh with them in this petition. He also imposed this task of writing upon me and offered his trust [that I would] perform [it] with rather a certain readiness. … And so that no opportunity for false accusation would lay open either to justified critics or spiteful detractors, I would once a week bring my tablets to master Hugh, and if something had been added, it was, on his decision, cut out; if something had been omitted, it was supplied; if something had been written incorrectly, it was changed, and if at some point something had by fortuitous accident been written well, it was confirmed by the authority of the great man.49  An important consideration for the modern scholar dealing with a reportatio is the circumstances under which such notes were taken down, as these are the cause of several textual problems. One such circumstance is that the stenographic so-called tironian notes of antiquity had disappeared from use, and that note-takers consequently were not able to write at the same                                                 48 “Non enim me huius operis auctorem, sed quodammodo artificem profiteor.” (“For I do not profess myself to be the author of this work, but in a way its craftsman.”) LAUR. WEST. sent. div., praef. (p. 250.) 49 “Qui cum sentencias de divinitate dicere incepisset, rogatus sum a plerisque sociorum … quatenus ad communem tam mei quam aliorum utilitatem easdem sentencias scripto et memoriae commendarem. Quorum precibus cum iam bis vel ter commonitus non acquiescerem, ut qui me ipsum melius quam ipsi noveram, demum magistrum Hugonem in eadem peticione secum adhibuerunt. Qui et hoc onus scribendi nobis iniunxit et fiduciam perficiendi magna quadam alacritate promisit. … Et ne quis vel iuste reprehendentium vel invide mordencium calumpnie pateret introitus, semel in septimana ad magistrum Hugonem tabellas reportabam et eius arbitrio, si quid superfluum esset, resecaretur, si quid pretermissum, suppleretur, si quid viciose positum, mutaretur, si quid vero quandoque forte fortuitu bene dictum, tanti viri auctoritate comprobaretur.” LAUR. WEST. sent. div., praef. (p. 250.) 

--- [p.37] ---

 
 
30 pace as lectures were delivered, so that the reportatio can only ever be a summary of the main points of a lecture—and this, furthermore, according to the point of view of the particular note-taker; the text of a different note-taker would inevitably differ to some degree.50 Another consideration is the physical constraints of sitting uncomfortably in a packed and noisy classroom, and a third is the fact that a student may misunderstand or disagree with his master and change his text accordingly. The processes by which lectures were transformed and preserved as written records—in this case, by Abelard and his students—is also the topic of an article by Constant Mews.51 Mews points out here that, because the defining characteristic of scholasticism is seen as “the quest to frame answers to philosophical or theological quaestiones within a systematic framework,”52 it has tended to be discussed in literary terms. In these discussions, a large focus is often placed on the new types of systematic educational texts which were produced during the century, and especially on their earliest form, namely the collections of positions held by the various masters on theological and doctrinal questions known (once again) as sententiae, “sentences,”53 but also                                                 50 These circumstances are described in detail by Jacqueline Hamesse, “‘Reportatio’ et transmission de textes,” in The Editing of Theological and Philosophical Texts from the Middle Ages: Acts of the Conference Arranged by the Department of Classical Languages, University of Stockholm, 29–31 August, 1984, ed. Monika Asztalos, 11–34, Studia Latina Stockholmiensis 30 (Stockholm: Almqvist & Wiksell International, 1986), at pp. 12–16. 51 Constant J. Mews, “Orality, Literacy and Authority in the Twelfth-Century Schools,” Exemplaria 2 (1990): 475–500. On Abelard’s reluctance to discuss his doctrinal positions based on the collections of these circulated by his students, see 490–91. 52 Mews, “Orality, Literacy and Authority” 475. 53 Later genres are the so-called distinctiones, quaestiones, and summae, which are all different types of systematic treatises. Briefly speaking, distinctiones are dictionaries of the various spiritual meanings of biblical words, quaestiones are treatises on scriptural and theological questions arising from the text of the Bible, and summae are comprehensive, systematic magisterial treatises on theology. For the various forms and genres of medieval exegesis, see Gilbert Dahan, “Genres, Forms and Various Methods in Christian Exegesis of the Middle Ages,” in Hebrew Bible / Old Testament. The History of Its Interpretation. From the Beginnings to the Middle Ages (Until 1300), ed. Magne Sæbø, vol. 1:2 196–236 (Göttingen: Vanderhoeck & Ruprecht, 2000), especially pp. 220–224. (Quaestiones on the Bible were composed already in antiquity but became significantly more complex in the twelfth century.) For the development from sentence collections to summae, see Marcia L. Colish, “From the Sentence Collection to the Sentence Commentary and the Summa: Parisian Scholastic Theology, 1130–1215,” in Manuels, programmes de cours et techniques d’enseignement dans les universités médiévales, ed. Jaqueline Hamesse, 9–29 (Louvain-la-Neuve: Institut d’Études Médiévales, 1994). On the new ways of organizing written expositions and on these new literary genres as finding devices in themselves, see Richard H. Rouse and Mary A. Rouse, “Statim invenire: 

--- [p.38] ---

 
 
31 on the exegetical commentaries, which began to feature new (or updated) ways of introducing and approaching the various litterae, as well as of organizing the material.54  Being one of the principal surviving artefacts of the nascent twelfth-century shift in the methods of teaching theology which signals the beginning of scholasticism, scholars have, understandably, been very interested in the origins and authorship of these different works. The assumption has been that they were originally conceived as literary products to which a specific author can be assigned. Mews argues, however, that this is a misconception. Pointing to the still inherently oral nature and face-to-face context of the transmission of knowledge in the twelfth-century, Mews asks whether “the difficulty scholars have had in attributing specific writings to either [here, Anselm of Laon or William of Champeaux] may result from pursuing a distinction between ‘author’ and ‘disciple of his school’ irrelevant to their teaching activities.”55 Thus, they may have dismissed as spurious or pseudonymous works which are, in fact, essentially representative of the teachings of these masters.  Using the main example of Abelard’s three theological sentence collections, Mews shows how these texts underwent a transition from the first version (the Theologia “summi boni”), which has a colloquial tone and comparatively casual attitude to quoting its sources verbatim, to its successors (the Theologia Christiana and the Theologia scholarium), which display much more attention to justifying arguments by means of authoritative sources and greater care in quoting them—and this as a result of the first work having been challenged and publicly 
                                                Schools, Preachers, and New Attitudes to the Page,” in Renaissance and Renewal in the Twelfth Century, ed. Robert L. Benson, Giles Constable, and Carol D. Lanham, 201–223 (Toronto: University of Toronto Press in association with the Medieval Academy of America, 1991). 54 Two such literary devices are the accessus (see Introduction, 2n6 and Chapter Four, 198n55) and the quaestio, by which the commentator introduces, discusses and, hopefully, solves questions raised by the littera in light of established doctrine or the conflicting testimony of another authority on the matter in question. (The quaestio can thus both be a literary device and a literary genre; see 30n53.) 55 Mews, “Orality, Literacy and Authority,” 477. 

--- [p.39] ---

 
 
32 debated.56 According to Mews, the context in which “scholastic literature” was first produced was thus the face-to-face discussion and debate between individuals, the main forum of which was, of course, the master’s classroom. This was only later codified into what we would define as deliberately literary works. That it is a grave error to understand many of our extant medieval exegetical texts as commentaries, or books in the modern sense of the word, is also one of the central theses of a forthcoming work by Alexander Andrée and Mark Clark.57 The authors stress heavily the fact that teaching always began with the lectio and that the written record(s) of this lecturing was, in the majority of cases, the product rather than the basis of this activity. Glossing, they point out, is not necessarily a writing-exercise but can also be an oral one—an explanation from the master’s mouth to the student’s ear; it is not, they point out, called a glossa (literally, “tongue”) by accident!58 Many of our surviving exegetical “commentaries” should thus, they maintain, be understood as clean copies of students’ notes taken at different points in time and existing and extant in various degrees of elaboration and distance from the master’s actual lecture.59 Their content and form can therefore vary due to the fact that they represent different instances or stages of the master’s lecture course, which he would give many times during his teaching career, but the core is the same.60 These students’ notes were subsequently copied and spread, adding to the variety in form of the preserved written records of the master’s oral teaching, and 
                                                56 Mews, “Orality, Literacy and Authority,” 480–82. Mews traces a similar development over time, from the early sentence book to Lombard. When the medieval sources say, however, that Anselm glossed the Psalter and the Pauline Epistles, we should probably imagine that they are referring to the written work, since Anselm must surely have lectured on more books than just these two. 59 Andrée and Clark, “The School of Paris,” 201 of typescript. Morard speculates that this is, in fact, the way in which the teaching of Bruno of Rheims lived on. (Morard, “Le Commentaire des Psaumes,” 33.) 60 Andrée and Clark, “The School of Paris,” 52 of typescript. 

--- [p.40] ---

 
 
33 meaning that the “author” of the “work” was not necessarily in control of how this was represented. In other words, from one and the same master can stem a profusion of representations of his teachings. As we shall see, these considerations will be of great import to the question of the authorship of our group of early twelfth-century Psalms-commentaries.  RECENT DEVELOPMENTS IN THE GLOSS-SCHOLARSHIP Smalley’s initial study of the Glossa ordinaria was a response to Glunz’s attempt to attribute the entire work to Peter Lombard. What Glunz had discovered is that there is a number of continuous twelfth-century commentaries which appear to have served as major sources for the Gloss. He thus wanted to attribute these texts to Anselm and instead credit Lombard with putting together the Gloss using material he had mined from Anselm’s continuous commentaries. But what Smalley then showed is, as mentioned, that there are parts of the Gloss that must predate Lombard. None of the Gloss-books she refers to, however, also predates Anselm’s death in 1117, nor is the Gloss on the Psalms among them. Admittedly, as we have seen, there is clearly a very strong medieval tradition associating Anselm with the Gloss, and the Gloss on Psalms in particular, but none of these testimonies can be dated to before the mid-twelfth century.61 Nevertheless, Smalley’s conclusion that Anselm was the original author of the Gloss has long remained the prevailing perception.62 However, 
                                                61 The testimony that may be giving the earliest date is Maurice of Kirkham’s, as he could have acquired his copy as early as the late 30s (see 26n39). 62 For example, of the post-Smalley scholars mentioned so far, de Blic, de Hamel, Häring, and Lobrichon (as well as the majority of those introduced below) all accept Smalley’s attribution of the original creation of the Gloss to Anselm. Only Gibson has some reservations; she thinks it more plausible that the Gloss originated somewhat later in the century, perhaps at St Victor at Paris, or, at least, at an institution like it. (Gibson, “The Twelfth-Century Glossed Bible,” 244; eadem, “The Glossed Bible,” xi; eadem, “The Glossa ordinaria in Medieval Exegesis,” 20–21.) Lesley Smith, the author of the most recent monograph on the Gloss does not question Smalley’s attribution (see Lesley Smith, The ‘Glossa Ordinaria’: The Making of a Medieval Bible Commentary [Leiden: Brill, 2009], 19). 

--- [p.41] ---

 
 
34 recent scholarship has begun to question this picture, breathing new life into the question of the validity of the scenario first envisioned by Glunz. To begin with, there is, as several scholars have noted, a conspicuous lack of manuscripts of the Gloss—on any biblical book—that can be dated to the first quarter of the twelfth century. According to Patricia Stirnemann, the three earliest manuscripts of the Gloss should be dated to 1120–1135.63 All three of these were produced and are currently found at Laon; they contain the Gloss on Job (Laon, BM, 51), on the Gospel of Matthew and Canticles (Laon, BM, 74), and on the Gospel of John and the Catholic Epistles (Laon, BM, 78). In addition to these codices, there are nineteen other manuscripts that Stirnemann dates to before 1135–1140, which, in addition to the books already mentioned, contain also the Gloss on Genesis, Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, and the Apocalypse.64 Out of these twenty-two manuscripts, ten were, according to Stirnemann, produced at Laon.65 But while at least three of these early Gloss-books (on Matthew, John, and Canticles) are associated by the tradition with Anselm and his school at Laon, there is no manuscript of the Gloss on the Psalms that is datable to before the mid-1140s.  The earliest datable manuscript of the Gloss on the Psalms is Troyes, BM, 511, which was part of a library donated to Clairvaux by prince Henry (d. 1175), son of Louis VI (d. 1137), upon his entry into the monastery in 1145.66 As it happens, however, some recent studies on three of                                                 63 Patricia Stirnemann, “Où ont été fabriqués les livres de la glose ordinaire dans la première moitié du XIIe siècle,” in Le XIIe siècle. Méditations et renouveau en France dans la première moitié du XIIe siècle, ed. Françoise Gasparri, 257–301, Cahiers du léopard d’or 3 (Paris: Léopard d’or, 1994), 259–260. According to Andrée, however, the earliest datable manuscript of any Gloss-book (namely, Lamentations), Kassel, Universitätsbibliothek, 2o, Theol. 6, was copied in 1131. (Andrée, Glossa ordinaria in Lamentationes, 23.) 64 It should be noted that two of the manuscripts listed by Stirnemann (Oxford, Trinity College, D.20 and Oxford, Bodleian Library, Rawl. G.17) are categorized by Smalley as gloses perimées—that is, not as the Glossa ordinaria itself, but as forerunners of it. (Smalley, “Les commentaires bibliques,” 16–17.) 65 See Stirnemann, “Où ont été fabriqués,” 260–262. She also identifies here another seven manuscripts—on Mark, Luke and the Pauline Epistles—that may also date to before 1140. 66 On Henry’s donation, see André Wilmart, L’Ancienne bibliothèque de Clairvaux, Mémoires de la Société académique d’agriculture, des sciences, arts et belles-lettres du Département de l’Aube vol. 54, 3rd series (Troyes: J.-L. Paton, 1917), 175–177. Stirnemann is among those who comment that the lack of early manuscripts of the Gloss on the Psalms and Paul is puzzling. (“Où ont été fabriqués,” 264. The only manuscript of the Gloss on the Psalms that can be 

--- [p.42] ---

 
 
35 the books first to be glossed at Laon—namely, Canticles, Matthew, and John—point to some conclusions regarding the compilation of the Gloss that could be relevant also for the Psalms: two of the studies have revealed the same pattern in the compilation-history of the Gloss, and the third suggests a similar scenario, namely that, for each of these books, there exists (or existed) a continuous commentary that has served as a main source for the Gloss, and that, in each case, this commentary can be linked to Anselm.  I. The Gloss on Canticles In line with the evidence put forward by Stirnemann, Mary Dove, who has edited the Gloss on Canticles,67 is of the opinion that everything points to early twelfth-century Laon as the place of compilation for the Gloss on this book.68 She notes, for one, that, whereas the Gloss on Canticles begins, after 1170, to travel with the other two books ascribed to Solomon (Proverbs and Ecclesiastes), the early versions are found traveling together with other Gloss-texts associated by the tradition with Laon.69 She identifies two pre-Ordinaria glosses—both dating to ca. 1100—                                                traced to Laon [Laon, BM, 29] dates, according to her, to the third quarter of the twelfth century. [Ibid.]) The fact that Henry is styled “son of the king” (regis filius) in the ex libris of Troyes 511 and Troyes 871 (containing the Gloss on Mark) has led some scholars to believe that the two manuscripts were copied before 1137—the year of Louis VI’s death. (See, for example, Wilmart, L’Ancienne bibliothèque, 178, and Smalley, “Gilbertus Universalis II,” 44–45. Wilmart thinks that the two ex libris, which are written in the same hand, may be Henry’s autographs.) The argument for the dating has, however, recently been challenged: de Hamel points out that a necrology from Rheims styles Henry in the same way at his death in 1175. (De Hamel, Glossed Books, 6.) Part of Wilmart’s argument for the dating is the fact that the word ‘regis’ has been erased in Troyes 871. This circumstance, however, points rather in the opposite direction, for if this erasure had been made while the books were still in Henry’s possession—and especially if he himself had written the ex libris—then one would expect that he would had done the same in Troyes 511. But however this may be, Troyes, BM, 511 remains the earliest—more or less—securely datable manuscript of the Gloss on the Psalms. 67 Mary Dove, Glossa Ordinaria in Cantica Canticorum, CCCM 170 (Turnhout: Brepols, 1997). 68 For the following account, see Dove, Glossa Ordinaria, 28–40. 69 A good example is Laon, BM, 74—one of the three manuscripts believed by Stirnemann to have been produced at Laon 1120–35—in which the earliest Gloss on Canticles is accompanied by the Gloss on Matthew, which is traditionally attributed to Ralph. (Dove, however, only goes so far as to date the manuscript to before 1140. [Dove, Glossa Ordinaria, 24 and 38.] On Ralph’s association with the Gloss on Matthew, see below.) Other examples of early Canticles-companions include the Gloss on Genesis, the Catholic Epistles and the Apocalypse. (Dove, Glossa

36 that belong to the same tradition of Canticles-glosses as does the Ordinaria;70 the Gloss itself differs from these earlier versions in two significant ways: first, it includes a number of direct quotations from Origen’s Homiliae in Cantica canticorum, a manuscript of which was present at Laon from the ninth century;71 second, it shares more than 200 glosses with one or more of the three versions—designated A, B, and E—of a continuous commentary attributed in one manuscript each of the three versions to Anselm, and in two manuscripts of E, as well as in one (now lost) fragment, to Ralph.72 Jean Leclercq, who also studied the three versions of this commentary,73 finds that A and B give the impression of being two redactions springing from the same source of oral teaching, with B, in fact, being an abridgement of A: they display, he maintains, a number of stylistic features and grammatical infelicities which lead him to believe that they are reportationes. E is related to A and B in substance but presents a more polished text. 37 brother, Leclercq ventures that A and B are reportationes of Anselm’s lectures and that E is Ralph’s redaction of Anselm’s teaching.  Dove, in turn, envisions that the text of E is derived from two sources: a reportatio of Anselm’s lectures (an abridged version closer to B than to A), and the Gloss on Canticles, with which E alone shares 122 glosses (presumably out of the total 200) not traceable to any other source.75 These circumstances lead her to believe that E is Ralph’s conflation of Anselm’s commentary (by which she must either mean the reportatio at the base of E—that is to say, that Anselm himself did not necessarily produce any written commentary—or an actual commentary written by Anselm—presumably for his lectures—but preserved only through the various reportationes) and his contribution to the Gloss on Canticles.76 Dove’s belief in Anselm’s involvement in compiling the Gloss on Canticles is strengthened by the marginal references to an anonymous magister found in one late twelfth-/early thirteenth-century manuscript of the Gloss on Canticles (Oxford, New College, 21), nine of which she has identified in one or more of A, B, and E.77 Her conclusion is that Anselm began by lecturing on the Song of Songs in the first decade of the twelfth century (despite the administrative duties which some scholars, on the Chanter’s testimony, believe kept him from this work) and then moved on, possibly with Ralph’s help, to compiling the Gloss on Canticles from the sources he had used for his lectures (in other words the “commentary”-source of E that gave rise also to A and B).78 What is important for our purposes is that Dove believes that Anselm produced a

Since no one disagrees with the assessment that A and B are reportationes, the idea must be that these reflect a commentary produced by Anselm for his lectures and later used by him to compile the Gloss on Canticles. 

--- [p.45] ---

 
 
38 source—either only in the form of oral lecturing taken down by students, or of his own writing—which he then used when compiling what became the Gloss.  II. The Gloss on John Whether or not Anselm was himself responsible for compiling the Gloss on Canticles, as Dove believes, or whether this was done later by his students and successors, the significance of Dove’s study for our present purposes is the textual relationships that she establishes between the Gloss and the three continuous commentaries (if A, B, and E can, indeed, be called that) which can be linked to Anselm and his school, and which appear to have provided some of the Gloss’s material. This conclusion is significant because a similar situation applies to the Gloss on the Gospel of John.  Glunz was the first to note that the two main sources for the Gloss on John are the commentary of John Scotus Eriugena and a continuous commentary with the incipit Verbum substantiale intelligitur.79 Even though the two manuscripts of this commentary known to Glunz are anonymous, he attributed the work to Anselm based on its similarity to a number of other continuous commentaries which he believed to be Anselm’s.80 As the Verbum substantiale lacks precisely the excerpts of Eriugena’s commentary that have been incorporated into the Gloss, 
                                                79 Glunz, History of the Vulgate, 305–312 and 322–328. 80 Glunz, History of the Vulgate, 322–323, 203–206 and 203n2. One of these is the continuous commentary on Canticles discussed above (see 36n73). Another is the commentary on Matthew discussed below (see 36n74 above). The arguments for the authenticity of these commentaries seem, however, to be somewhat circular, as they rest on similarities—such as the use of the “standard expression” ‘enarrationes’ in a few manuscripts—to other commentaries assumed to be authentic based on similarities to the other commentaries assumed to be authentic. Ultimately, the arguments rest on these commentaries’ similarity to the Gloss, assumed to be by Anselm. As this claim has, however, now been brought into question, Glunz’s arguments regarding Anselm’s authorship cannot be said to be conclusive. 

--- [p.46] ---

 
 
39 Glunz draws the conclusion that someone must have compiled the Gloss from these two sources after Anselm’s time.81 Smalley, who followed up on this suggestion, was made aware of two manuscripts in which the Verbum substantiale is ascribed to Anselm (Cambridge, Trinity College, B.I.10 and Durham, Dean and Chapter Library, B.III.17). She also found some additional parallels between the Verbum substantiale and the Gloss on John: one of these is a gloss attributed in one manuscript of the Ordinaria (Cambridge, Pembroke College, 76) to a magister Laudunensis and found word for word in the Verbum substantiale; another Gloss-manuscript (York, Cathedral Library, XVI.M.9) contains several glosses that are attributed by a thirteenth-century hand to Anselmus and that are, again, found in the Verbum substantiale.82 Even though Smalley, at the time, did not consider the evidence to be conclusive, she did admit the possibility that the attribution of the Verbum substantiale to Anselm is authentic.83 Based on the rest of her findings, she still, however, wanted to see Anselm as the compiler of the Gloss as well (that is, on the Psalms, the Pauline Epistles, and John), which, in the case of John, would make him responsible for both works: the continuous commentary as well as the Gloss. (Dove envisions the same scenario applying to Canticles.) Andrée, however, who has edited the Verbum substantiale under the title Anselmi Laudunensis Glosae super Iohannem, questions the ascription of the Gloss on John to Anselm made simply on the basis of the attribution of a few individual glosses;84 he proposes instead an amendment to the standard account.                                                   81 Glunz, History of the Vulgate, 312. As mentioned, Glunz wants to attribute the Gloss to Peter Lombard and thus argues that the Gloss postdates Anselm. 82 Smalley, “Gilbertus Universalis II,” 40–42. 83 See Smalley, “Gilbertus Universalis II,” 42–43. Andrée notes that she appears to have become more convinced of its authenticity over the course of her career. (Alexander Andrée (ed.), Anselmi Laudunensis Glosae super Iohannem, CCCM 267 [Turnhout: Brepols, 2014], xin11, with references to Beryl Smalley, The Study of the Bible in the Middle Ages, 3rd ed. (Oxford: Blackwell, 1983), 60 and eadem, “Some Gospel Commentaries,” 3–4.) 84 Andrée, Glosae super Iohannem, xxin46.  

--- [p.47] ---

 
 
40 Adding to Glunz’s observations—and with the support of Stirnemann’s conclusion that John was one of the first books to receive the Glossa ordinaria, and that this happened at Laon—Andrée shows convincingly that the Glosae super Iohannem (the Glosae) must have been produced at Laon and must predate the Gloss.85 As for the author of the Glosae, he argues that there is good reason to believe the two attributions of the work to Anselm that we find in the manuscripts.86 Based mainly on the lack of manuscripts of the Gloss dating before the late 1120s, however, Andrée thinks it unlikely that Anselm was directly responsible for the Gloss on John, or, indeed, for any part of it, let alone the entire work,87 thus coming to the opposite conclusion of Smalley and Dove. He believes instead that Anselm—most likely unknowingly—supplied the material for some of the Gloss in the form of continuous commentaries, such as the Glosae, which were the products of his lectures. If these were then (possibly after his death) mined, abridged, and re-arranged by his students in double gloss format (see 15n4) in order to preserve his much-celebrated teaching, this would explain why he was posthumously credited for parts, and even the whole, of the enterprise.88                                                 85 See Alexander Andrée, “The Glossa Ordinaria on the Gospel of John: A Preliminary Survey of the Manuscripts with a Presentation of the Text and its Sources,” Revue Bénédictine 118 (2008), 109–134. See also idem, “Anselm Unveiled,” 234–245), and idem, Glosae super Iohannem, xvi–xxii. 86 He points out 1) that, in light of the fact that the works of such famous authors as William of Champeaux, William of Conches, and Gilbert of Poitiers are often published anonymously, the attributions to Anselm constitute striking evidence, and 2) that we really only have two Anselms to choose from: Anselm of Laon and St. Anselm of Canterbury, the latter of whom is not credited with any commentary on John. (Andrée, “Anselm Unveiled,” 232–233; repeated in idem, Glosae super Iohannem, xvi–xviii.) 87 Cf. his comment “that an entire hypothetical first generation of glossed books should have been completely annihilated without leaving any trace seems rather unlikely.” (Andrée, “Anselm Unveiled,” 227–28.) 88 See Andrée, Glosae super Iohannem, xii-xiii. Andrée lists Ralph, Gilbert the Universal, and Gilbert of Poitiers as possible successors. (Andrée, “Anselm Unveiled,” 228n47.) If this is the case, it still, however, remains to be explained why Anselm’s successors chose the Gloss-format to preserve his teaching instead of simply reproducing his continuous commentaries; it seems reasonable to infer that the format must have been an end in and of itself. We may note that the appearance of the Gloss in no way erased the demand for continuous commentaries, which continued to be produced and copied. Furthermore, the creation of direct expansions of the Gloss (Gilbert of Poitiers’s Media and Peter Lombard’s Magna glosatura—the subjects of the next chapter) containing largely the same exegetical material (with some additions), implies that the two modes of presentation each had a different function. We may also note that the modus operandi suggested independently by the above-mentioned scholars agrees with the one case in which we know for certain how the Gloss was compiled, namely that of Gilbert the Universal and the Gloss on Lamentations: Gilbert used one continuous commentary—that of Paschasius 

--- [p.48] ---

 
 
41 III. The Gloss on Matthew The third study, carried out by Adrian Ballentyne, is less comprehensive than the previous two but nevertheless significant, as it concerns another of the early books of the Gloss associated by the tradition—even if indirectly—with Anselm, namely the Gloss on the Gospel of Matthew.89 The main reason for modern scholars’ association of Anselm with the Gloss on Matthew is a couple of remarks made by Peter Comestor in his lectures on the Gloss on Matthew and Mark. Regarding the second gospel, Comestor states that “Mark is almost untouched, as it follows after Matthew; even the ancients left it alone due to its difficulty, nor did master Anselm or his brother, master Ralph, lecture on it.”90 This comment has led scholars to infer that Anselm and Ralph, between them, lectured on the other three gospels.91  Comestor’s second remark concerns a specific gloss on Matthew 1:12, about which he says: “Here you have Rabanus’s gloss. You do not, however, have this gloss identified as to its author, and it is therefore uncertain from where master Anselm’s brother master Ralph, who arranged the 
                                                Radbertus—as the main source for his Gloss-book, abridging it and making it more readable. (The prologue and first book of his commentary are edited in Andrée, Glossa ordinaria in Lamentationes.) In this case, the purpose can clearly not have been to preserve the teaching of a personally beloved master, but rather the production of the Gloss itself as such. If so, the situation is perhaps analogous to Gibson’s theory about the early Carolingian theologians—that is, that the compilers of the Gloss (for whatever reason they were compiling it) used the best recensions of the best authoritative biblical commentaries they had available—as Gilbert the Universal used Paschasius for the Gloss on Lamentations (see Andrée, Glossa ordinaria in Lamentationes, 61–75)—and that Anselm’s work was their preference in the case of the books he was famous for lecturing on, and in which there remained some written trace of his teaching. (Cf. the question raised by Smalley: “Ceux du cercle de Laon choisissaient parmi les recueils qui précédaient les leurs. Le faisaient-ils entièrement au hasard, ou se souciaient-ils de chercher au loin pour trouver les plus propres à leurs besoins?” [Smalley, “Les commentaires bibliques,” 22.] It would not be surprising if the records of the teaching of the most celebrated master Anselm—for which they did not have to look far at all—was exactly what fit their needs best.) 89 Adrian Ballentyne, “A Reassessment of the Exposition on the Gospel according to St Matthew in Manuscript Alençon 26,” Recherches de théologie ancienne et médiévale 52 (1986): 19–57. 90 “Marcum pene intactum, quia pedissecus est Mathei; etiam pro difficultate eum reliquerunt antiqui, nec legit eum magister Anselmus nec magister Radulfus frater eius.” Oxford, Bodleian Library, Laud. Misc. 291, fol. 93r. 91 See Smalley, “Some Gospel Commentaries,” 151. 

--- [p.49] ---

 
 
42 gloss, took it.”92 The inference is that Ralph compiled the Gloss on Matthew.93 The reason for linking the Gloss on Matthew also to Anselm is its close textual relationship to a group of continuous commentaries, two of which bear an attribution to him in one or more of their witnesses. The first of these is the so-called Enarrationes in Evangelium Sancti Mattaei, attributed in five of its ca. 40 surviving witnesses to Geoffrey Babion (d. 1158) but in two manuscripts to Anselm, and printed under his name in the PL (see 36n74).94  The second commentary is found in the manuscript Alençon, BM, 26, designated in the literature as A, or Cum post ascensionem from its incipit; its title reads, “Here begins the exposition on the Gospel of our Lord Christ according to Matthew gathered from the different authorities by the most excellent philosopher master Anselm of Laon,”95 and it shares much of its material with the Enarrationes, as well as with the Gloss.96 The third is an expanded version of A preserved in an early twelfth-century manuscript at Durham (DM),97 and the fourth is an abridgement of a commentary on Matthew by Paschasius Radbertus (d. 865), referred to in the 
                                                92 “De hoc habes glosam Rabani. Non tamen habes hanc glosam intitulatam cuius aucoris sit, et ideo incertum est unde magister Radulfus, frater magistri Anselmi, qui glosaturam ordinavit, eam assumpsit.” Cited in Smalley, “Some Gospel Commentaries,” 151. One must conclude here that the ‘qui’ refers back to Ralph. 93 See, for example, Smalley, “Gilbertus Universalis II,” 39. 94 Smalley, “Some Gospel Commentaries,” 167. The attribution of the Enarrationes is discussed in detail by Van den Eynde, who finds that, due to its theological content, the commentary cannot be ascribed to Anselm. He believes the attribution to Babion to be possible if the work is dated to after his teaching career at Anger (1096–1110) but remains highly sceptical of this as well. (Damien Van den Eynde, “Autour des Enarrationes in Evangelium S. Matthaei attribuées à Geoffroi Babion,” Recherches de théologie ancienne et médiévale 26 [1959]: 50–84 [at p. 80–85].) Smalley accepts Van den Eynde’s conclusions, and describes Anselm’s authorship of this commentary as “long discredited.” Smalley, “Some Gospel Commentaries,” 166. She claims, however, that the text, on palaeographical evidence, cannot be later than 1150. Ibid. 168. 95 “Incipit expositio ex diversis auctoribus a domino Ansello Laudunensi philosopho exquisitissimo collecta super evangelium domini nostri Jesu Christi secundum Mathaeum.” (Cited in Ballentyne, “A Reassessment of Alençon 26,” 22.) We may note here the spelling ‘Ansellus’, as Wilmart uses this as one of his arguments for authenticity in the case of Pseudo-Haimo (see Chapter Three). 96 Ballentyne, “A Reassessment of Alençon 26,” 21, 24, and 28. 97 Durham, Dean and Chapter Library, A.1.10 (see Ballentyne, “A Reassessment of Alençon 26,” 28 and 33).

43 literature as V for the Valenciennes manuscript in which it was first found, or as Nomen libri evangelium from its incipit.98 V was first discovered by Odon Lottin, but its material was identified as coming from Paschasius by Heinrich Weisweiler.99 Lottin noticed that V had much in common with the Gloss and, not yet knowing that it is an abridgement of Paschasius, suggested that it might be Anselm’s compilation.100 Weisweiler, however, based on his observation that V is often closer to Paschasius’s original, drew the opposite conclusion—that is, that the Gloss must have borrowed material from V.101 Ballentyne, in turn, makes the opposite observation regarding the Gloss’s relationship to A: he finds that, in the material that they share, A is often freer in its handling of the original patristic and Carolingian sources than is the Gloss, which he calls a “respecter of his authorities.”102 As they also often borrow different excerpts from the same source, he does not think it likely that the two works have the same compiler.103  The details of the various arguments for which commentary can or cannot be Anselm’s need not concern us here: we may simply note that this is yet another instance where the evidence indicates that there might be a continuous commentary serving as the base of one of the books of

Duculot, 1942–1960), 5:162–169. He insists on this attribution despite having been made aware of Van den Eynde’s argument that V depends on A and the Gloss. (Ibid. 168–169.) Weisweiler, in fact, discovered an early redaction of the Gloss on Matthew which differs from the final version in that it lacks the material excerpted from V. He thus ventures that the Gloss on Matthew as we have it may be Ralph’s redaction of Anselm’s earlier Gloss. (Ibid., 401.) Smalley concurs with this hypothesis. 44 the Gloss, and that this commentary seems to be associated by the tradition with Anselm. The one point that is noteworthy, however, is Smalley’s conclusion that the author of the Enarrationes borrowed material from both A and V, as well as from the Gloss:104 the question that presents itself in the face of such a suggestion is why a compiler would have excerpted material from three different commentaries which, although they are not the exact same text, nevertheless share such a considerable amount of material—in other words, when the compiler of the Gloss would be likely to find in both of his additional sources more of the same material that he had already excerpted from their relative. The question is thus if there is not a more reasonable scenario that explains the agreements. The significance of Ballentyne’s study for our purposes is that he proposes just such a reasonable scenario explaining the textual relationships of the four continuous commentaries. What he demonstrates is that the four commentaries cannot, in fact, be fitted into a straightforward chronological hierarchy. For example, as mentioned above, A and the Gloss sometimes excerpt different passages from a shared patristic source—that is to say, one cannot be dependent simply on the other for access to that source. At the same time, he also points to signs that their adaptations of the original sources are sometimes clearly related, meaning that there also exists some sort of relationship of dependence between them.  As another illustration of his thesis, Ballentyne shows that DM both reproduces all of A but, at the same time, also borrows independently from their mutual source (in this case, Rabanus),105 and, furhermore, that it also shares passages with the Enarrationes that are not found in either A or the Gloss.106 The solution he proposes to this web of connections is that the three                                                 104 Smalley, “Some Gospel Commentaries,” 167. Van den Eynde before her argues that the Enarrationes borrows from both A and the Gloss, as well as from a few other collections. (Van den Eynde, “Autour des Enarrationes,” 54–69.) 105 Ballentyne, “A Reassessment of Alençon 26,” 28–29. 106 Ballentyne, “A Reassessment of Alençon 26,” 32–33. --- [p.52] ---

 
 
45 commentaries A, DM and the Enarrationes all mined a now lost commentary on Matthew compiled by Anselm.107 We shall see in the following that the same pattern of agreements applies to our Psalms-commentaries.  We should also remind ourselves here of Andrée’s and Clark’s thesis that these continuous “commentaries” are not necessarily the product of an author creating his exegetical work at the writing-desk but may be the written records of someone’s lectures. The shared, now lost commentary which Ballentyne posits may thus be lost exactly because it never existed as a written text but only as the transient words of the master, preserved for posterity by his students.108  CONCLUSION The findings of the above cited studies raise new questions regarding Anselm’s involvement in the compilation of the Gloss on the Psalms. On the one hand, there is the lack of Psalms-Gloss manuscripts predating the mid-1140s—all the more puzzling, considering the number of extant early manuscripts on several other biblical books. On the other, there is the particularly strong medieval association of the Gloss on the Psalms with Anselm, which, in light of the relative scarcity of contemporary testimonies to Anselm’s involvement in glossing Canticles and John, ought to be all the more telling.109 Andrée makes the very reasonable point that                                                  43n101) as well as directly with V—another indication that the relationship between these commentaries is not unilateral and linear. (Smalley, “Some Gospel Commentaries,” 161.) 107 Ballentyne, “A Reassessment of Alençon 26,” 33. 108 As a final point of interest, we may note that Giraud reaches the same conclusion regarding the sentence-literature as the above-mentioned studies do—that is, that the works preserved and associated with Anselm are not his original creations but were compiled by his successors in the scholastic environment that was keeping his legacy alive. See Giraud, Per verba magistri, 405. The idea was first suggested by Franz Bliemetzrieder in his “L’oeuvre d’Anselme de Laon et la littérature théologique contemporaine II: Hugues de Rouen,” Recherches de théologie ancienne et médiévale 6 (1934): 261–283, continued in Recherches de théologie ancienne et médiévale 7 (1935): 28–51. 109 Cf. Andrée’s argument that the comparatively scanty evidence for any contemporary associations of the Gloss on John—namely, the two manuscript attributions—are strikingly eloquent, considering that the works of such 

--- [p.53] ---

 
 
46 it is not only plausible but likely that [Anselm] wrote glosses on the texts which he regularly taught at Laon, or at least that notes taken during his lectures later resulted in some kind of written commentary. A man does not gain a reputation as the foremost exegete of his day without having provided commentaries—oral or written—on some of the biblical books central to doctrine and theology, such as the Psalms, the Epistles of Paul, and the Gospel of John.110  At the same time—possibly apart from the claims of the various manuscript attributions—there appear to be no testimonies to Anselm’s authorship of any part of the Gloss that date to his lifetime: the 1140s seem to be when these begin to appear.111 The question that thus presents itself is if the same scenario that has been found to hold for the Canticles, the Gospel of John, and possibly the Gospel of Matthew might apply to the Psalms as well: could it be that Anselm produced a continuous commentary, either as a base for his lectures or as a result of them, and that this commentary served as a source of the Gloss on the Psalms? And if so, was the Gloss compiled by Anselm himself or by one of his students or successors? Could the case perhaps be analogous to what seems to hold for the Canticles, namely that his teaching was only preserved as various reportationes, from which the Gloss was later compiled? These are the questions which the following chapters aim to contribute to answering. We shall begin by examining the relationship of the Gloss on the Psalms to its two closest relatives, namely the respective Psalms-commentaries of Gilbert of Poitiers and Peter Lombard, and the status quaestionis of the recent scholarship on these texts.
                                                celebrated masters as William of Champeaux, William of Conches, and Gilbert of Poitiers were published anonymously (see 40n86). 110 Andrée, “Anselm Unveiled,” 228. 111 Prior Lawrence, who owned the Durham manuscript carrying an attribution, occupied this position between 1149 and 1153, and even if we posit that Maurice of Kirkham’s correspondence with Gilbert of Sempringham took place several years before Gilbert’s death in 1189, we do not seem justified in moving it back far enough that Maurice would have acquired his glosses before the 1130s. All the other testimonies are decidedly later.

47 
Chapter Two THE PARVA, MEDIA, AND MAGNA GLOSATURA  The “Standard Account” and the Question of Priority   In the second quarter of the twelfth century, two particular rivals to the Gloss on the Psalms appeared on the scene, namely the respective Psalms-commentaries of Gilbert of Poitiers—who had been a student of Anselm’s—and Peter Lombard, both of which are found in the manuscripts as lemmatized, continuous texts. Once all three works were in circulation, the Gloss began to be distinguished as the Parva glosatura, and its two successors as the Media and Magna glosatura respectively.1 The Magna had soon outcompeted its two predecessors in popularity: while the Gloss on the Psalms survives in some 50 manuscripts and the Media glosatura in 75, Lombard’s commentary is extant in close to 500 manuscripts spread out all across Europe.2 The general modern consensus is that the Media and the Magna are both expansions of the Gloss itself. This was the perception already in the Middle Ages: we saw it, for example, in Herbert of Bosham’s explanation of Lombard’s reasons for composing his commentary.3 We have also the thirteenth-century marginal notes found in the chronicle of Robert of Auxerre, which are often invoked to attest to what has become the standard account of the genealogy of these three works: at the entry for the year 1138, the notes add that “it was the heyday of the                                                 1 Smalley, “Gloses périmées,” 16.  2 De Hamel describes a scenario where Lombard’s Magna glosatura eventually replaced the Gloss and the Media. Based on the fact that prince Henry (see Chapter One, 34 and 34n66) owned copies of both the Media and the Gloss, de Hamel imagines that Gilbert’s work was produced, with Anselm’s approval, as a companion volume to the Gloss on the Psalms (see de Hamel, Glossed Books, 9). Gibson, however, objects to the idea that Lombard’s commentary replaced the Parva glosatura as an element of the Glossed Bible. However this may be, one has to agree, based on the numbers of extant manuscripts, that the Gloss on the Psalms must have taken a step back in favour of the Magna. 3 See Chapter One, 26n40. 

--- [p.55] ---

 
 
48 outstanding teacher Gilbert … Following master Anselm, he produced a very useful gloss put together from the sayings of the Holy Fathers,”4 and at the year 1153 a different hand5 adds that “[Peter Lombard] also expounded more extensively and clearly the gloss on the Psalter and the Pauline Epistles furnished by Anselm with interlinear and marginal glosses and later made continuous by Gilbert.”6 Additional support for this scenario is found in the Appendix to the catalogue of authors edited by Häring: For having considered the method of glossing of master Anselm of Laon, which due to its excessive brevity cannot be understood by anyone but those versed in the works of the Fathers, [Gilbert] made the gloss more prolix and thus clearer. He expounded the Psalter, the Pauline Epistles and the Gospel of John in continuous fashion.7  But even though this remains the generally accepted account of the progression from the Gloss to the Magna, there are some, like Glunz, who have proposed revised scenarios, and, even among those scholars who accept the general outline of the standard account, some have suggested a number of amendments to its exact details. These suggestions are the topic of this chapter.  
                                                4 “Clarebat Gislebertus doctor eximius … Hic post magistrum Anselmum super psalterium et super epistolas Pauli ex dictis sanctorum patrum compactam utilem satis edidit glosaturam.” ROB. AUTISS. chron., 234. 5 According to Gross-Diaz, From Lectio Divina, 122. 6 “Hic etiam glosaturam super psalterium et epistolas Pauli ab Anselmo per glosulas interlineales marginalesque distinctam, post a Gisleberto continuative productam, latius apertiusque explicuit.” ROB. AUTISS. chron., 237. 7 “Considerato quippe magistri Anselmi Laudunensis glosandi modo, quod uidelicet nimia breuitate non nisi ab exercitatis in expositionibus Patrum posset intelligi, glosam prolixiorem eoque euidentiorem fecit. Exposuit continuatim psalterium, epistolas Pauli et Euangelium Iohannis.” Häring (ed.), “Two Catalogues,” 210. This is the only contemporary indication we have that Gilbert of Poitiers should have written a commentary on John (see Andrée, Glosae super Iohannem, xixn37). We may also note that the first sentence of this passage can be interpreted in two different ways: either as “Gilbert made the (i.e. Anselm’s) gloss more prolix and thus more clear,” or “Gilbert made a more prolix and thus more clear gloss.” Indeed, Gross-Diaz argues that the scribe of the catalogue is indicating two separate activities here—that “fecit glosam prolixiorem” is one thing, and “exposuit continuatim” another—that is, that Gilbert both edited Anselm’s gloss (a pre-Ordinaria version), and wrote his own, continuous commentary. (Gross-Diaz, From Lectio Divina, 130.) The possibility of this scenario shall be discussed in detail below. 

--- [p.56] ---

 
 
49 THE PATTERNS OF AGREEMENT BETWEEN THE PARVA, MEDIA, AND MAGNA GLOSATURA The very close affinity that exists between these three Psalms-commentaries is obvious even upon the most superficial comparison: they simply share a very large number of more or less verbatim passages. Upon closer examination, the pattern which appears is that, while the verbal and grammatical agreement between the Gloss and the Media is somewhat looser, the Magna contains more or less every single word that occurs in the Gloss. What we also see is that every one of the three commentaries has its independent relationship to the patristic originals, meaning that, sometimes the Gloss contains the most faithful reproduction of the original (in which cases Lombard usually follows the Gloss), and sometimes it is the Media that does so. All three also contain patristic material which does not occur in the others. In Lombard’s case, what we often see is that it is when the Gloss and the Media diverge in their representation of the patristic source that he appears to have gone directly to the original; when he does, this also often results in the incorporation of additional patristic material into his account. A couple of examples will illustrate these patterns.8 We begin with three introductory glosses to Psalm 50, Have mercy on me, O God (Miserere mei, Deus). The agreements between the three commentaries are illustrated typographically: words occurring in the Gloss are in bold, words occurring in the Media are underlined, and words occurring in the Magna are in SMALL CAPS. This means that, from the perspective of the Gloss, words occurring also in both the Media and the Magna look like THIS; from the perspective of the Media, words occurring also in both the Gloss and the Magna look like THIS; 
                                                8 It is true that there is no critical edition of either of the three commentaries, but we can still look at the manuscripts we have available, and if they agree across the three traditions, this is strong evidence that the readings in question are more than variants, and that the agreement across the traditions is not coincidental; for example, if all three commentaries are quoting, say, Augustine, and display certain shared adaptations of the original, in wording, and/or syntax, and/or word order, this is very unlikely to be a coincidence, but rather a sign of close kinship. The same argument applies to the agreements between the commentaries that will be analysed in the following chapters. 

--- [p.57] ---

 
 
50 and from the perspective of the Magna, words occurring also in both the Gloss and the Media look like this. (Where only the stem of a word is marked, this means that the word (or phrase) occurs in the work so signalled, but that the grammatical structure has been changed. Where only the ending is marked, this means that the grammatical structure is the same, but that a synonym has been used.) The purpose of this formatting is not that the reader keep track of every single detail of the agreements, but rather to easily illustrate that there are, in fact, substantial agreements, and that there is a significant difference in how the Media agrees with the Gloss versus how the Magna agrees with it. Since it happens that any individual gloss may in one manuscript be placed in a different position than in the others, glosses that are in general found above the line of the psalm-verse will be headed by the relevant lemma followed by a slash (/), whereas glosses that are in general found in the margin will be headed by a pilcrow (¶). Finally, the individual snippets of each text have been numbered, and, where relevant, passages also contain the snippet-numbers of one or more of the other commentaries for the purpose of easy comparison:9  Glossa O (GO1a) ¶ DAVID proPONITUR HIC EXEMPLUM, NON CADENDI, (GO1b) SED, SI CECIDERIS, RESURGENDI, NE DESPERES.  ((GO1) ¶ HERE, DAVID IS OFFERED AS AN EXAMPLE, NOT OF FALLING, BUT OF GETTING BACK UP, SO THAT, IF YOU HAVE FALLEN, YOU WILL NOT DESPAIR.)  (GO2a) ¶ HIC PAENITENTIALIS PSALMUS PLUS FREQUENTATUR, (GO2b) QUIA HIC MAGIS HUMILITAS OSTENDITUR, (GO2c) ET QUIA HOC EST TEMPERATUM SUPPLICATIONIS GENUS, (GO2d) QUOD ET INFIRMI POSSUNT IMPLERE, (GO2e) QUIA HIC NIHIL DIFFICILE DICITUR, UT IN ALIIS dicitur: LAVABO PER SINGULAS NOCTES LECTUM MEUM,10 … (GO2f) ALIBI ERGO DISTRICTIORA FORTIORIBUS; HIC TEMPERATA INFIRMIS PROVIDIT.  
                                                9 As always, there is a certain amount of individual variation between the manuscripts in terms of choice of synonyms, word order, individual omissions and additions, and so on. Only variations which are of interest to the argument being made, or where the manuscript(s) differ from the PL will, however, be recorded in the notes. 10 Ps. 6:7.

51 ((GO2a) ¶ THIS PENITENTIAL PSALM IS USED MORE (GO2b) SINCE HUMILITY IS SHOWN MORE HERE, (GO2c) AND SINCE THIS IS A TEMPERED FORM OF SUPPLICATION, (GO2d) WHICH ALSO THE WEAK ARE ABLE TO FULFIL, (GO2e) SINCE NOTHING DIFFICULT IS SAID HERE, AS is said IN THE OTHERS [i.e. the penitential psalms]: EVERY NIGHT I WILL WASH MY BED [with tears], … (GO2f) ELSEWHERE, ACCORDINGLY, [he provides] STERNER [words] FOR THE STRONGER; HERE, HE PROVIDES TEMPERED [words] FOR THE WEAK.) 52 BED [with tears], … (GP4/GO2b) Nevertheless, the HUMILITY of the penitent is commended HERE, … (GP5/GO2f) ACCORDINGLY, STERNER [words] are PROVIDEd FOR THE STRONGER in the others [i.e. the penitential psalms]; HERE, TEMPERED [words are provided] FOR THE WEAK. (GP6/GO2a) Wherefore [this psalm] IS also USED MORE in the Church. … (GP7a/GO3) For it is A PSALM FOR THE MANNER OF THE JUBILEE; (GP7b) through this HUMILITY FULL REMISSION is understood.  Lombard (PL1a/GO1a) David autem hic apPONITUR in exemplum, non cadendi, (PL1b/GP1f) non prosperitatis otia affectandi, (PL1c/GO1b) sed, si cecideris resurgendi, et ne desperes de peccati immanitate, … (PL2a/GO2a) Inde est quod hic paenitentialis psalmus plus aliis frequentatur in Ecclesia, (PL2b/GO2b) quia hic magis humilitas ostenditur (PL2c/GO2c) et quia hoc est temperatum supplicationis genus, (PL2d/GO2d) quod etiam implere infirmi possunt, (PL2e/GO2e) quia hic nihil difficile dicitur, ut in aliis: Lavabo per singulas noctes lectum, … (PL3/GO2f) Alibi ergo districtiora fortioribus; hic temperata providit infirmis. … (PL4a/GP1a) Et est iste psalmus quartus in ordine paenitentialium, (PL4b) et in ordine psalmorum quinquagesimus est, (PL4c/GO3) pro modo iubilaei, quia hic est psalmus remissionis. … (PL5/GP7b) Ita per paenitentialem humilitatem, de qua hic agitur, plena his praestatur peccatorum remissio.14  ((PL1a/GO1a) For David is set forth here as an example, not of falling, (PL1b/GP1e) not of striving for the leisure of prosperity, (PL1c/GO1b) but, if you have fallen, [as an example] of getting back up, and so that you will not despair about the immensity of the sin, … (PL2a/GO2a) This is why this penitential psalm is used more in the Church than the others, (PL2b/GO2b) since humility is shown more here, (PL2c/GO2c) and since this a tempered form of supplication, (PL2d/GO2d) which also the weak are able to fulfil, (PL2e/GO2e) since nothing difficult is said here, as in the other ones: Every night I will wash my bed [with tears], … (PL3/GO2f) Elsewhere, accordingly, [he provides] sterner [words] for the stronger; here, he provides tempered [words] for the weak. … (PL4a/GP1a) And this psalm is the fourth in the series of penitential [psalms], (PL4b) and it is the fiftieth in the series of the psalms, (PL4c/GO3) for the manner of the jubilee, since this is a psalm of remission. … (PL5/GP7b) Thus, through penitential humility, which is dealt with here, full remission of sins is offered to these people.  What we immediately see is that more or less every single word of the Gloss is in small caps, meaning that they all occur, in the same form, in Lombard. (Lombard uses ‘apponitur’ instead of 
                                                14 PETR. LOMB. psalm. 50, acces. (PL Database vol. 191, cols 483D–484B.) 

--- [p.60] ---

 
 
53 ‘proponitur’ and leaves out the superfluous ‘dicitur’ in GO2e.) While it is clear that the Media and the Gloss are also related, we see that far fewer words of the latter are underlined, and, in several cases, only the stem of the word, meaning that the Media presents the same material as the Gloss, but in rather a different way, such as giving the subject of ‘proponitur’ as the psalm itself rather than David and using “the weak” found in GO2d as the indirect object of this verb. Lombard, on the other hand, agrees with the Gloss in more or less every detail: if we, for example, look only at the snippet-numbers of the Gloss and the order in which are presented in the Magna, we see that the latter follows the former exactly, with GO1a–GO1b–GO2a–GO2b–GO2c–GO2d–GO2e–GO2f–GO3, while the Media has GO1a–GO2d–GO1b–GO1a–GO2e–GO2b–GO2f–GO2a–GO3, leaving out GO2c. We shall return to the significance of this state of affairs below. If we again compare the Gloss to the Magna, we see from what is in plain text that Lombard offers here and there a few specifications that are not present in the Gloss, such as the despair being “about the immensity of the sin” (“de peccati immanitate”) and the addition that this psalm is used more than the other penitential psalms (“aliis”). Such differences would be completely in line with his stated mission of clarifying the obscure brevity of the Gloss. In contrast, there is significantly more plain text in the Media, meaning that Gilbert includes more material which is not found in any of the other two commentaries.  As there is no doubt that the Magna postdates the Media, we can reliably say that the many close agreements which we find between these two commentaries show that Lombard used the Media as one of his sources. His comparatively relaxed attitude towards this commentary is demonstrated here by, for example, the fact that he uses Gilbert’s idea of people striving for the leisure of prosperity (“(GP1f) prosperitatis otia non affectent”—which does not derive from any of the Fathers) but excerpts it from its Gilbertine context and inserts it into GO1, “David 

--- [p.61] ---

 
 
54 proponitur hic exemplum non cadendi, sed, si cecideris, resurgendi…”, changing Gilbert’s sentence structure (a subordinate clause) to fit that of the Gloss (gerunds).15 We see also that the largest amount of plain text—in other words, unique adaptations—is found in connection with Lombard’s Gilbertine excerpts in PL4a–b and PL5, demonstrating that it is when working with Gilbert’s text that Lombard feels the most free to elaborate and adapt: at the end of the passage, we see how Lombard first reproduces the exact words of GO3, “a psalm of remission for the manner of the jubilee,” but with a more elaborate syntax (the corresponding Ordinaria-sentence is, in the Magna, divided into two parts with a causal relationship expressed between the two: “(PL3c) for the manner of the jubilee, because this is [quia hic est] a psalm of remission”), but then also includes Gilbert’s version of GO3 at the end of his account, resulting in a repetition of the word ‘remissio’ (“Thus, through penitential humility, which is dealt with here, full remission of sins is offered to these people”).16 In contrast to the agreements with the Gloss, however, the Magna displays some significant modifications of Gilbert’s version of GO3, showing that Lombard does not feel obliged to quote Gilbert verbatim. It should also be noted that these glosses all stem from the Fathers: GO1 comes from Augustine, and GO2 and GO3 come from Cassiodorus. A comparison with the original passages will demonstrate further the close relationship that exists between the three twelfth-century commentaries. We begin with Augustine; once again, words occurring in the Gloss are in bold, words occurring in the Media are underlined, and words occurring in the Magna are in SMALL CAPS. This means that a snippet borrowed by all three commentaries looks like THIS; something                                                 15 Already here we thus have an indication that Lombard must be using the Gloss, as the standard account holds, rather than vice versa: it seems unlikely that a compiler of the Gloss would have identified the Gilbertine phrase and, for reasons unclear, decided to excise it to produce the gloss we see in the Ordinaria. Several instances of this type of pattern will be presented and discussed below. 16 The repetition of the word ‘remission’, once in agreement with the Gloss and once in agreement with the Media, is another sign that Lombard is excerpting from these two sources, as the standard account holds, rather than any other scenario. Similar instances of repetition as a sign of excerpting from a variety of sources will be discussed below. 

--- [p.62] ---

 
 
55 borrowed only by Gilbert and the Gloss looks like this; something borrowed only by Gilbert and LOMBARD looks like THIS; and something borrowed only by the Gloss and LOMBARD looks like THIS. The Augustinian snippets have also been numbered and the snippet-numbers of the twelfth-century commentaries included for reference: (A1) Sed nec DE talibus [uanitatibus] DESPERari debere, iste psalmus ostendit. …  (A2) Multi enim cadere uolunt cum Dauid, et nolunt surgere cum Dauid. (A3a/GO1a/GP1b/GP2/PL1a) NON ergo CADENDI EXEMPLUM PROPOsitum est, (A3b/GO1b/PL1c) SED SI CECIDERIS, RESURGENDI. … (A4/GO2a/GP6/PL2a) … [A]d hoc IN ECCLESIA saepe lectum atque cantatum; audiant qui non ceciderunt, ne cadant, audiant qui ceciderunt, ut surgant. (A5) Tanti uiri peccatum non tacetur, praedicatur in ecclesia. (A6/GP1d) Audiunt male audientes, et quaerunt sibi patrocinia peccandi; … (A7a/GP1d) Alii uero audientes salubriter, in casu fortis (A7b/GO2d/GP1c/PL2d) metiuntur INFIRMItatem suam; … (A8/GP1d) Proponunt enim sibi casum Dauid, et ad hoc illum magnum uident cecidisse, ut parui nolint uidere unde possint cadere. (A9/GP1g) Reprimunt enim oculos a petulantia, non se facile adiungunt, non miscent se mulieribus alienis.17 (But this psalm shows that it ought not to be DESPAIRed ABOUT such [vanities]. …  For many wish to fall with David, [but] do not wish to rise with David. Accordingly, the EXAMPLE is NOT GIVEN [as one] OF FALLING, BUT, IF YOU HAVE FALLEN, [as one] OF GETTING BACK UP. … For this reason, it is often read and sung IN THE CHURCH: may those who have not fallen hear, lest they fall; may those who have fallen hear, that they may rise. The sin of such a great man is not silenced; it is preached in the Church. Those who listen poorly hear and seek for themselves justifications for sinning; … But others, who listen well, measure their WEAKness in the fall of a strong man; … For they set the fall of David before themselves, and they see that this great man fell in order that lesser men should not want to see where they might fall. For they restrain their eyes from wantonness, they do not easily join [or] associate themselves with the wives of others.)  From this passage—which is quite a bit longer than here represented—there is only one full snippet that has been borrowed by all three commentaries, namely A3a–b, “Non ergo exemplum … resurgendi.” This circumstance alone is indicative of the direct relationship that exists between our three commentaries, as it is unlikely that they would have happened to excerpt this particular snippet independently of one another. The Gloss and Lombard cleave closer to Augustine’s original construction, keeping the gerunds ‘cadendi’ and ‘resurgendi’, but all three 
                                                17 AVG in psalm. 50,1–3. (CCSL 38, 599–601.) 

--- [p.63] ---

 
 
56 commentaries change the form ‘propositum est’ to ‘proponitur’ (or ‘apponitur’ for Lombard), again, indicating a dependence on each other rather than on the patristic original independently.  Two other details demonstrate the direct relationship that exists between the three in terms of their use of the patristic originals: the first is the fact that they all incorporate Augustine’s point in A1 about not despairing and adapt it in the exact same way, incorporating the verb ‘desperari’ (“to be despaired”) in finite form into their rendering of A3a–b. (Gilbert only changes the subject from the second person singular to the third person plural.) The second detail is the fact that they all incorporate a form of Augustine’s ‘infirmitatem’ (“weakness”) from A7b, changing it to the adjective, ‘infirmi’, used substantively to denote those people to whom Psalm 50 is directed. We see, however, that Gilbert, once more, deviates slightly from the other two: the Gloss and Lombard use the adjective as the subject of the verb ‘possunt’ (“are able”) in GO2d/PL2d—which gloss as a whole, as mentioned, comes from Cassiodorus—while Gilbert, as also mentioned, makes it the indirect object of the verb ‘proponitur’ in GP1c. We also see that Gilbert incorporates additional Augustinian material not picked up by the others (GP1d–g), demonstrating that he cannot have gotten his patristic material from the Gloss alone. That our three twelfth-century commentaries must depend in large degree on each other for their use of the patristic originals is even more clear from the Cassiodorean material; in his Psalms-commentary we read: (C1a) … [I]n nullo psalmorum quae paenitentibus maxime necessaria est, (C1b/GO2b/GP4/PL2b) tanta uirtutis HUMILITAS inuenitur, ut rex potens et in prophetali culmine constitutus, tamquam extremus hominum sua festinauerit peccata deflere. … (C2a/GO2c/GP5/PL2c) Electum est plane TEMPERATUM et expeditum SUPPLICATIONIS GENUS, (C2b/GO2d/PL2d) QUOD omnis aetas uere sapiens debeat appetere, et ad subitum festinanter POSSiT IMPLERE. (C3/GO2de/GP3/PL2e) Non enim HIC UT IN ceterIS paenitentibus aliquid DIFFICILE DICITUR, quale est illud in sexto psalmo: LAVABO PER SINGULAS NOCTES LECTUM MEUM, … (C4/GO3/GP7a/PL3c/PL5) Huius autem nec numerus uacat; pertinet enim ad annum IUBILAEum, qui apud 

--- [p.64] ---

 
 
57 Hebraeos contractus ueteres obligationesque soluebat, quem in Leuitico Dominus annum REMISSIONIS uniuersos habitatores terrae uocare praecepit.18 (In none of the psalms is such great HUMILITY of virtue—which is most necessary for the penitent—found, that a great king, set upon the summit of prophecy, hastens like the last of men to bewail his sins. … A quite TEMPERED and easy KIND OF SUPPLICATION has been chosen, which every truly wise age should strive for, and is ABLE TO FULFIL quickly and at a moment’s notice. For nothing DIFFICULT is said HERE, as IN the rest of the penitential [psalms], like this [phrase] in the sixth psalm: EVERY NIGHT I WILL WASH MY BED [with tears], … Nor is the number of this [psalm] void [of meaning]; for it pertains to the year of the JUBILEE, which having been agreed upon with the Hebrews released [them] form their old obligations, [and] which the Lord in Leviticus mandates that all inhabitants of the earth call the year of REMISSION.)  As in the Augustinian example above, the fact that our three commentaries have picked out many of the exact same words and snippets from Cassiodorus indicates that they did not do this independently of one another. A clear example of a shared adaptation is the gloss GO3, “a psalm of remission for the manner of the jubilee,” where the Gloss and Lombard display the exact same rendering of Cassiodorus, and the Media, although its adaptation goes further, can be linked to the others through the use of the phrase “a psalm for the manner of the jubilee” (GP7a). Another clear indication is that all three have exchanged Cassiodorus’s phrase “every truly wise age” for Augustine’s “the weak” (discussed above). We see also, once more, that Gilbert strays from the other two in his renderings, as evidenced, for example, by his adaptation of Cassiodorus’s phrase “nothing difficult is said”—retained as such by the other two—as “no difficult work of penitence from God is described.” This passage thus clearly demonstrates the particular affinity that exists between the Parva, the Media, and the Magna glosatura: we see that they contain the same adaptations of the patristic material, and that Lombard includes more or less every single word found in the Gloss, while Gilbert’s version often differs in various degrees from the other two. We also see that 
                                                18 CASSIOD. in psalm. 50,21. (CCSL 97, 469–470.)
