58 Lombard uses the Media but does not follow it slavishly, and that, despite their clear dependence on each other, the commentators also display independent connections to the patristic sources themselves. An analysis of the exegesis of Psalm 129:4, For with you there is merciful forgiveness: and by reason of your law, I have endured you, O Lord (Quia apud te propitiatio est; et propter legem tuam sustinui te, Domine), reveals the same patterns. Here, we begin with the patristic original, once again Augustine, and the typographical key is the same: Augustine (A1) Quis est ergo qui clamat? Peccator. (A2) Et QUA SPE CLAMAt? (A3) Quia qui uenit SOLUere PECCATA, dedit spem etiam in profundo posito peccatori. …  … (A4) Et quae est ista propitiatio, nisi sacrificium? (A5a) Et quod est SACRIFICIUM, (A5b) nisi QUOD PRO NOBIS OBLATUM EST? (A6) SANGUIS INNOCENS FUSUS DELEuit OMNIA PECCATA NOCENTIUM: (A7) pretium tantum datum REDEMit omnes CAPTIUOS DE MANU CAPTIUANTIS INIMICI. Ergo est apud te propitiatio. (A8) NAM si non esset apud te propitiatio, SI IUDEX SOLUM ESSE UELLES ET MISERICORS ESSE NOLLES, OBSERUARES OMNES INIQUITATES NOSTRAS, et quaereres eas, quis sustineret? (A9) QUIS ANTE TE STARET, et diceret: Innocens sum? (A10) QUIS STARET IN IUDICIO TUO? (A11) SPES ERGO UNA EST: Quoniam est apud te propitiatio. Propter legem tuam sustinui te, Domine. (A12) Quam LEGEm? Quae REOS FeCit? (A13) Data est enim lex sancta, iusta, bona Iudaeis; sed reos eos potuit facere. (A14) Non data est lex QUAE posset UIUIFICare, sed quae ostenderet peccata peccatori. … (A15) Est ergo lex MISERICORDIAE Dei, lex propitiationis Dei. Illa timoris fuit, est alia lex CARITATIS. … (A16) HANC LEGEM statuISTI mihi, ut quomodo dimitto, dimittatur mihi. Propter hanc legem sustinui te, Domine. (A17a) EXSPECTAUI QUANDO UENIAS ET LIBERES AB OMNI NECESSITATE, (A17b) quia IN IPSA NECESSITATE non deseruisti legem misericordiae.  … (A18) Deinde quod commemoraui, subiunxit: ALTER ALTERIUS ONERA PORTATe, et sic adimplebitis legem Christi.19 …  (A19a) HAnC autem LEGEm qui non seruat, Nec SUSTINET Dominum; nec, si uelit sustinere, causa est quare sustineat; (A19b) INANITER SUSTINET.20 ((A1) And who is it therefore that cries? The sinner. (A2) And DUE TO WHAT HOPE does he CRY? (A3) Because the one who came to ABSOLVE OUR SINS gave hope also to the sinner placed in the depths. …  
                                                19 Gal. 6:2. 20 AVG in psalm. 129,2–5. (CCSL 40, 1890–1893.) 

--- [p.66] ---

 
 
59 … (A4) And what is this forgiveness if not the sacrifice? (A5a) And what is the SACRIFICE (A5b) if not that WHICH WAS OFFERED FOR OUR SAKE? (A6) THE INNOCENT BLOOD SPILLED WIPEd AWAY ALL THE SINS OF THE EVILDOERS: (A7) such a great price given REDEEMed all THE CAPTIVES FROM THE HAND OF THE IMPRISONING ENEMY. Therefore, with you there is merciful forgiveness. (A8) FOR if forgiveness were not with you—IF YOU WISHED ONLY TO BE A JUDGE AND DID NOT WANT TO BE MERCIFUL—YOU WOULD MARK ALL OUR INIQUITIES, and you would examine them: who would sustain it? (A9) WHO WOULD STAND BEFORE YOU and say, “I am innocent”? (A10) WHO WOULD STAND IN YOUR JUDGEMENT? (A11) THERE IS, THEREFORE, ONE HOPE: For with you there is merciful forgiveness. By reason of your law, I have endured you, O Lord. (A12) What LAW? The one that MAde [them] GUILTY? (A13) For a holy, just, [and] good law was given to the Jews; but it was able to make them guilty. (A14) A law THAT could GIVE LIFE was not given, but one that showed the sinner his sins, … (A15) The law is, therefore, OF God’s MERCY—a law of God’s forgiveness. That [other law] was [a law] of fear; there is another [law] of CHARITY. … (A16) YOU HAVE instituted THIS LAW for me that, just as I forgive, I may be forgiven. For the reason of this law, I have endured you, O Lord. (A17a) I HAVE WAITED [for] WHEN YOU COME AND SET [me] FREE FROM ALL NECESSITY, (A17b) because IN NECESSITY ITSELF you have not abandoned the law of mercy. …   … (A18) Then, as I have said, he adds: BEAR ye ONE ANOTHER’S BURDENS, and so you shall fulfil the law of Christ. … (A19a) For NEITHER DOES he who does not serve THIS LAW ENDURE the Lord, nor is there a reason, if he should want to endure, why he should endure; (A19b) HE ENDURES IN VAIN.)  Glossa O (GO1/A2) Quia apud te propitiatio/21 QUA SPE CLAMAS? QUIA IN EO MISERICORDIA.  ((GO1/A2) For with you there is merciful forgiveness/ DUE TO WHAT HOPE DO YOU CRY? BECAUSE [THERE IS] MERCY IN HIM.)  (GO2) Quia/ SED  ((GO2) For/ BUT)   (GO3/A11) Quia apud te propitiatio/ IN HOC EST SPES. ((GO3/A11) For with you there is merciful forgiveness/ THERE IS HOPE IN THIS.)

From the context of Augustine’s text, however, it is clear that it belongs at the beginning of verse 4.

(GO7e/A15/A19a) THEREFORE, NO ONE ENDURES WITHOUT THIS LAW OF MERCY AND CHARITY, (GO7f/A18) BY WHICH ONE BEARS ANOTHER’S BURDENS, (GO7g/A19b) OR THEY ENDURE IN VAIN.)  Gilbert P (GP1) Quia etc. QUASI: Ideo confidenter clamo, quia apud te propitiatio, (GP2a/A5a/GO4a) id est SACRIFICIUM (GP2b/A6) efFUSi SANGUInis tui, (GP2c/A3/GO4b) QUO PECCATA quaecumque SOLVuntur. (GP3a/A12/GO6) Et CUM PREMERER SUB LEGE quae REOS FACIt, sustinui te (GP3b/A17b/GO7c) IN NECESSITATE (GO7d/A17a) EXPECTANS QUANDO VENIAS,                                                 22 All of the manuscripts except Paris, BnF, lat. 106 and Reims, BM, 150 attach this gloss to the previous one, making “misericordiae, cum premerer …” one gloss, but the second part only makes sense once one has read the continuation of the lemma ‘sustinui te’; it seems, therefore, more likely that the snippets should be divided into two separate glosses. It is easier to see how the two glosses may have melded together due to their close proximity above the psalm-text, or, perhaps, through influence from Lombard, than that they should have separated to find their proper place above ‘legem’ and ‘sustinui’ respectively. In Lombard, the immediate succession of the second snippet works since he inserts a repetition of the lemma before ‘misericordiae’. Gilbert, for his part, does not include ‘misericordiae’, but goes straight to “cum premerer …” 23 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 129,4. 62 there is merciful forgiveness, etc. (PL3a) Or connect [the verses] like this: No one can endure if you will mark iniquities, (PL3b/GO2) but, nevertheless, I do not despair, for with you there is merciful forgiveness. (PL4a/A11/GO3) In this there is hope for me, for with you there is merciful forgiveness for me, namely, (PL4b/A5a/GO4a) the sacrifice (PL4b/A5b) which was offered for our sake, (PL4c/A3/GO4b) by which he absolved our sins, (PL4d/A6) namely, the blood of Christ, spilled and offered for us, by which all the sins of the guilty are washed away; (PL4e/A7) by which the captives are redeemed from the hand of the imprisoning enemy. (PL5/A11/GO3) Therefore, there is hope for me in this. (PL6/A8) For if you wanted only to be a judge, and did not want to be merciful, you would mark all iniquities. (PL7/A9) And who would stand before you? (PL8/A10) Who would stand at your judgement? (PL9a/A11/GO3) Therefore, there is one hope, for with you there is merciful forgiveness, and for the reason of your law, (PL9b/A15/GO5) namely, of mercy. (PL9c/A12/GO6) When, once, I was oppressed under the old law making [people] guilty, I have endured you, O Lord, (PL9d/A15/GO7a) for the reason of your law, namely, of mercy and charity, (PL9e/A14) which gives life, … (PL10a/A16/GO7b) And because you provided this law, (PL10b/A17b/GO7c) I have endured you in necessity itself, (PL10c/A17a/GO7d) waiting [for] when you come and set [me] free from all necessity. (PL11a/A19/GO7e) Accordingly, no one endures without this law of mercy and charity, (PL11b/A18/GO7f) by which one bears another’s burdens, (PL11c/A19/GO7g) or he endures in vain.)  If we look at Augustine’s passage, we see that there are four places where all three twelfth-century commentaries borrow material: they are A3/A5a, A12, A15, and A17a–b. As in the previous example, and as this Augustinian passage is even lengthier than the previous one (a total of 581 words which are not excerpted by our commentaries have been left out, with the smallest gap amounting to 69 words), the fact that all three of them excerpt precisely these snippets is another strong indication of their close relationship to each other.  They also reproduce essentially the same adaptations of the patristic source: all three contain the same combination of A3 with A5a (the statement on the sacrifice which absolved all sins); all of them have made the two separate questions of A12 into one statement (on the law that makes people guilty); all of them have extracted only the words ‘misericordia’ and ‘caritas’ from A15; and all of them have inverted the order of A17a and b. However, while Lombard, as above, agrees with the Gloss in every detail and includes its every word (once again seen from the fact 

--- [p.70] ---

 
 
63 that every word of the Gloss is in small caps), Gilbert leaves out several Ordinaria-glosses as well as displays several differences in grammatical structure: in A3, he has the sins as the subject of the verb put in the passive (“peccata solvuntur” instead of “peccata solvit”); in A12, he uses a clause with a finite verb for the Gloss’s and Lombard’s participial construction (“quae facit” instead of “faciente”);28 and in A15 he changes “the law of mercy and of charity” into “the law of mercy, which is in charity”; in A17 a he has ‘ut’ instead of ‘et’.29  In the one case where Gilbert includes some Augustine not reproduced by the Gloss—namely, the comment about spilled blood (GP2b/A6)—we see that when Lombard takes this snippet over this, he also ends up adding additional Augustinian material to his account, namely A7–A10. This is typical of Lombard: whenever the Gloss and the Media display differences, Lombard goes to check the original account (nevertheless, often still choosing to follow the rendering of the Gloss rather than the original authority).30 Finally, we may note the amount of repetition displayed by the Magna: in order to accommodate all of the Ordinaria-glosses for the first lemma, ‘For with you there is merciful forgiveness’, Lombard is obliged to repeat the lemma no fewer than five times. (Gilbert, on the other hand, seems to consciously limit himself to one mention of each lemma.) Lombard also repeats the adaptation of A15, “law, of mercy and charity,” twice. This repetition corresponds in some degree to the Ordinaria, which also includes A15 twice: once between the lines, in incomplete form, at GO5, “law/ of mercy,” and once in full, as part of a marginal gloss, namely GO7e. In addition to this, Lombard repeats the phrase corresponding to GO3 three times.                                                 28 We may note here that, in this, Gilbert is closer to Augustine, meaning that Lombard choses to follow the Gloss over the patristic original. It also means that Gilbert is most likely not relying solely on the Gloss for his Augustine, as also evidenced by his incorporation of the reference to spilled blood in A6, which is not found in the Gloss. 29 This difference is small and may simply be a variant. 30 We also see a couple of instances of what could—if one posits the standard account—be interpreted as Lombard glossing the gloss, namely the additions “for me” (mihi) to GO3, and “once” (olim) to GO6; his version of GO7a is also more “complete,” including the original phrase “and of charity” (et caritatis) from A15. 

--- [p.71] ---

 
 
64 The patterns described above are all typical of the agreements that exist between the Gloss, the Media, and the Magna glosatura: while the Media is clearly closely related to the Gloss, the most prominent pattern is the complete inclusion of the words and structure of the Gloss in Lombard’s Magna, but examples of Lombard “glossing the gloss,” of him using and adapting the Media, and of the Magna displaying additional patristic material at precisely those points where the Gloss and the Media diverge could easily be multiplied. The question for this chapter is whether these patterns are best explained by the standard account or by some other scenario. We shall, therefore, turn to an examination of a couple of theories which challenge the validity of the standard account.  CHALLENGES TO THE STANDARD ACCOUNT:                                                                                                   GLUNZ AND WOODWARD ON THE MAGNA GLOSATURA The very close verbatim agreement between the Gloss and the Magna is what led Glunz to the conclusion that the Gloss on the Psalms and the Pauline Epistles should be attributed to Lombard.31 In his view, “it is extremely unlikely that each individual word of so disconnected a gloss as the Interlinearis appears to be … should have been adopted by the Lombard in precisely the same form for his larger commentaries.”32 That is to say, only if he was the author of both works could such an exceptional degree of textual agreement be explained.33                                                  31 As mentioned in Chapter One, there are other reasons for why he wanted to attribute the Gloss as a whole to Lombard. 32 Glunz, History of the Vulgate, 215. 33 It is not entirely clear what Glunz means by “same form” here: it could refer either to the internal (grammatical) form and wording of the individual glosses, or to the sequence of different glosses; both interpretations raise their respective problems. When it comes to the grammatical structure of individual glosses, Lombard’s versions of the glosses are often, in fact, not in the same form: a common difference is that Lombard’s versions are less elliptical; they often include many words that are only implied in the Gloss, like forms of esse, pronouns, objects etc. (Indeed, expansion of ellipsis would be a large part of the task of clarifying the obscura brevitas of the Gloss!) When it comes to the sequence of different glosses, it is not surprising that they appear in the same order in the two commentaries, as they follow the progression of the exposition of the lemmata. It is thus only in wording that the 

--- [p.72] ---

 
 
65 Indeed, the degree of verbatim agreement between the two commentaries in combination with the more or less complete inclusion of the Ordinaria’s material within the Magna, as well as the lack of Psalms-Gloss manuscripts that can be dated to before the 1140s, makes this conclusion appear almost irresistible: we may note, for example, that Mark Zier does not dismiss the possibility entirely even though he argues in his 2007 article that Lombard’s commentary on Romans had a dramatic influence on the Gloss as it was undergoing development (which, of course, assumes that the Gloss predates the Magna).34  Even more recent than this is Michael Woodward’s argument for the Magna’s priority over the Gloss—again, on the Pauline Epistles—made on similar grounds as Glunz’s.35 Woodward finds that over 80 percent of the Ordinaria’s glosses are also found in Lombard’s Magna glosatura on Paul’s letters; he thinks that such extensive parallels “can only be accounted for by 
                                                exegesis of the Ordinaria and the Magna can be said to have the same form. However, as Glunz is speaking of the “Interlinear Gloss” and its disjointedness—still treating these glosses as forming a work separate from the Ordinaria—the logic he seems to be invoking is the idea that a coherent whole is more easily broken up into disconnected pieces than disconnected pieces are put together into a coherent whole. (This logic could, in fact, be applied to both interpretations of Glunz’s argument—that is, it can be applied both to the internal structure of the individual “grammatically disconnected” glosses of the Ordinaria and to its sequencing of separate glosses.) Both the sequencing of individual glosses and the internal form of individual glosses will be discussed in the following. 34 Mark Zier, “Peter Lombard and the Glossa Ordinaria: A Missing Link?” in Pietro Lombardo: Atti del XLIII Convegno storico internazionale, Todi, 8–10 ottobre 2006, 361–409, Atti dei Convegni del Centro italiano di studi sul basso medioevo 20, ed. Enrico Menestò (Spoleto: Fondazione Centro italiano di studi sull’alto medioevo, 2007), 380. Although Zier himself believes it most likely that the Gloss was used by Lombard for his commentary, he notes that the relationship between the two texts is complex and therefore does not dismiss entirely the two other possibilities, namely that Lombard and the compiler of the Gloss were using the same florilegium (which he, however, deems less likely), or that the Gloss is an abridgement of the Magna (which he deems least likely; see Zier, “Missing Link,” 374). His main conclusion is that Lombard’s commentary on Romans exerted a decisive influence on the Gloss as it developed into its final form, which is what we see in the Rusch edition. Zier notes, however, that we have no manuscript witnesses to the full text of the Rusch edition of the Gloss on Romans, and that it is therefore not possible to determine the time at which Lombard’s commentary began to exert this purported influence. (Zier, “Missing Link,” 381.) As we shall see, a similar state of affairs holds true for the Psalms: in the printed editions, we find material which is not present in any of the fifteen manuscripts that have been consulted for this project (except in one, but in the form of later additions), but which can be found in Lombard’s Magna. At the same time, we shall also see evidence that the version of the Gloss used by some of the masters at Paris in the later twelfth century appears to have contained these Lombardian additions. The lack of manuscript witnesses to this interpolated version of the Gloss suggests that Lombard’s influence was perhaps very local, even though it appears to have become the point of departure for the printed editions (which are all based on Rusch). 35 Michael Scott Woodward, The Glossa Ordinaria on Romans: Translated with an Introduction and Notes by Michael Scott Woodward (Kalamazoo, MI: Medieval Institute Publications, 2011). 

--- [p.73] ---

 
 
66 systematic abridgement rather than expansion.”36 Unlike Glunz, however, Woodward does not appeal simply to sheer amount of verbatim agreement for his conclusion but analyses also the sequencing of the excerpts from the different authorities shared by the Gloss and the Magna; he finds here that, where the Ordinaria on Romans has a number of separate glosses excerpted from different authorities—say, first a gloss taken from Augustine, then a gloss from Jerome, then one from Bede—the Magna follows this order in its exposition. As his example, he uses the exposition of Romans 1:1, where the Magna and the Gloss as it is printed in Rusch’s 1480/81 edition do, indeed, follow the same sequence of authorities: they both present the opinions of Bede, Augustine, Ambrose, Jerome, and Origen in that order.37 In his view, the occurrence of such a pattern is a sure indication that Peter’s continuous exposition must be prior to the Gloss; he argues that  if Lombard were borrowing from the Gloss, he would have to be taking these pithy marginal and interlinear glosses and interweaving them into a larger whole. And he would be doing this over and over again throughout his commentary, following the same sequence of authorities as the Gloss. Such an endeavour would be strange and unprecedented.38   Such agreement as we see in the examples above is certainly not coincidental, and the move to look at the sequencing of shared authoritative excerpts is a clever one, as it adds another dimension to the criterion of textual agreement which makes this even more binding. But even so, Woodward’s analysis, as it stands, does not prove the priority of the Magna over any part of the Gloss, not even on Romans.  
                                                36 Woodward, Glossa Ordinaria on Romans, x. Woodward seems to make this claim for all of the Pauline Epistles even though his analysis covers only Romans. 37 Woodward, Glossa Ordinaria on Romans, xvii–xxi. 38 Woodward, Glossa Ordinaria on Romans, x–xi. 

--- [p.74] ---

 
 
67 The objection that could be immediately raised is that the scenario described by Woodward as being unlikely is, in fact, exactly what one would expect from someone who was using the Gloss to put together a continuous commentary: the simplest thing would be to connect the snippets of the different authorities in the same order that they appear in one’s expositor. This order would, furthermore, be determined to a large extent by the progression of the lemmata according to the text of the Bible, even though there would, of course, still be some room for variation in the sequencing of the different glosses, at least for those which expound the same lemma. The real problem with Woodward’s argument, however, is that he uses the Rusch edition of the Gloss, which has been shown to be interpolated with material from Lombard39 and is therefore also likely to have been influenced by the Magna in its ordering of the glosses. For the Gloss on the Psalms, it becomes immediately obvious when we look at the manuscripts that the placement of individual glosses often varies between them, and that there is thus no authoritative sequence to be followed.40 Without evidence for any “correct” order of the glosses from the manuscripts of the Gloss on Romans, Woodward’s argument can bear no significant weight. At the same time, there is something to be said for the logic behind Glunz’s and Woodward’s arguments, which rests on our notion of the relationship between order and chaos: it is easier to imagine order disintegrating into disorder than it is to imagine order being created out of disorder. The idea seems intuitively sound, and we may therefore wish to accept it. The problem with Woodward’s argument, from this point of view, is, however, that he looks at the sequencing 
                                                39 See Zier “Missing Link.” Zier examines the relationship between a number of twelfth- and thirteenth-century manuscripts of the Gloss on Romans, Lombard’s Magna on Romans, and the Rusch edition of the Gloss on the same book, and finds that the Rusch edition contains extra material—that is, material not found in the Gloss-manuscripts—that appears to have been excerpted directly from Lombard’s Magna. In the following, we shall see that the same state of affairs holds true for the Gloss on the Psalms. 40 It is not that the same gloss is found in a different position in each manuscript, but rather that any given gloss will often be found in the same position—in the margin or in-between the lines of the Psalm-text—in the majority of the manuscripts, while one or two manuscripts will have placed the gloss in question differently. 

--- [p.75] ---

 
 
68 of different glosses when we do not, in fact, have an authoritative order for the Gloss giving us a key to the “correct” answer. (And even if we did, an agreement in the sequencing of different glosses would not necessarily prove the priority of either commentary, as explained above.) If we were, however, to look at the internal structuring of individual glosses, where the authoritative order is given by the original patristic source, we would have a criterion by which to judge which commentary contains more “disorder.”41 We shall, therefore, turn to examine a recent theory which rests on a version of this criterion but uses it to argue for the priority of Gilbert of Poitiers’s Media glosatura over the Gloss on the Psalms rather than for the Magna’s.  CHALLENGES TO THE STANDARD ACCOUNT: GROSS-DIAZ ON THE MEDIA GLOSATURA In her study of Gilbert of Poitiers’s Psalms-commentary, Theresa Gross-Diaz examines, among other things, its relationship to the Gloss. The results of her analysis lead her to believe that Gilbert did, indeed, base his commentary on his master’s work, as the standard account holds, and that he composed it while studying under Anselm at Laon,42 but, like Glunz, she questions the assumption that Anselm’s work is to be identified with the Gloss on the Psalms as we have it. Gross-Diaz argues instead that Anselm’s Parva glosatura was a primitive stage of the Gloss, and that Gilbert, by elaborating on Anselm’s true “little gloss,” and using it during his own time as 
                                                41 Interestingly, Woodward also gives an example of how anonymous marginal and interlinear glosses are represented in the Magna, and here we can see that the order is clearly jumbled: there are instances where different elements of one and the same Ordinaria-gloss do not appear in the same sequence in the Magna, but mixed in with parts of other glosses. In other words, Lombard could easily have picked apart these already disconnected Gloss-elements and reorganized them to form a running exposition, while we would have to imagine that a compiler using Lombard’s commentary would have been taking bits from completely different sections of Lombard’s continuous text and brought them together into one Ordinaria-gloss. 42 In support of this, she cites, among other things, the explicit of Oxford, Balliol College, 36, which states that Gilbert read his commentary to Anselm in order for it to be corrected (see Chapter One, 23n30). 

--- [p.76] ---

 
 
69 schoolmaster, contributed to the development and popularisation of what later became the Glossa ordinaria on the Psalms as we have it.  Just as Glunz’s does for Lombard’s Magna, Gross-Diaz believes that the coherence in both form and content which she sees in Gilbert’s commentary could not possibly have been created through a process of joining together such disjointed elements as the glosses of the Ordinaria; she states: Whatever guidance Gilbert may ultimately have had from Anselm, an attentive reading confirms that his commentary had to have developed directly out of his integral sources, and that it would have been patently impossible for Gilbert to have arrived at his well integrated, programmatic patristic-Carolingian synthesis from the strings of extracts which make up the Glossa ordinaria.43  The reasoning is similar to that of Glunz and Woodward and therefore seems intuitively sound; both sides cannot, however, be right: the Gloss as we have it cannot both be the product of Gilbert’s continued teaching and glossing activities and at the same time be an abridgement of the Magna.44 But if we can answer the question of the direction of influence between the Gloss and the Media, we will automatically answer the same question for the Magna. We shall, therefore, examine Gross-Diaz’s argument for Gilbert’s influence on the final form of the Gloss.  Gross-Diaz’s case for the formative influence of the Media on the Gloss is based on the more strongly binding criterion of looking not at the Media’s sequencing of different Ordinaria-glosses, but at the internal structure of individual glosses appearing in both commentaries.                                                 43 Gross-Diaz, From Lectio Divina, 123–124. 44 Even if Gross-Diaz does not claim that the Gloss is an abridgement of Gilbert’s Media, but that his contribution simply consists of an overlay on a “more ‘pure’ Laonnoise gloss” (Gross-Diaz, From Lectio Divina, 136), the two hypotheses are still very difficult to reconcile into one coherent picture: it would mean that there would have to be three stages of the Gloss: first, Anselm’s purely Augustinian Laonnoise original, then the “overlaid” version which came out of Gilbert’s teaching; these two would then have had to have been used by Lombard to produce his commentary, and, finally, his commentary be excerpted to form the Gloss on the Psalms as we have it. The problem is that we do not have any manuscript evidence for these stages. The fifteen manuscripts here consulted all present more or less exactly the same text. There are small variations in the sense that one manuscript may omit a certain gloss found in all the others, or add a gloss of its own, but other than that, it is all the same text. 

--- [p.77] ---

 
 
70 However, she still focuses less on “harder,” formalist criteria and more on the “softer,” literary qualities of the shared material: in her assessment, Gilbert’s text displays a very deliberate and thought-through exegetical program that is entirely lacking from the corresponding material in the Ordinaria. Her main argument for the Media’s priority in those instances of shared material that she analyses is thus the greater amount of contextualization and theological coherence which she finds in the former work as compared to the Gloss. However, as these arguments are largely dependent on how one interprets the glosses in relation to their sources, many of them can be interpreted differently, resulting in the opposite conclusion—that is, that the Media also in these cases depends on the Gloss rather than vice versa. This shall be demonstrated in the following. Gross-Diaz does, however, also look at some formalist aspects of the two texts,45 but, like Woodward, she bases her analysis on the Rusch edition of the Gloss, and when one examines instead the manuscripts, even these arguments in favour of Gilbert’s priority turn out not to hold. Furthermore, the picture also changes when one takes Lombard’s Magna into account. So even though Gross-Diaz is, as we shall see, certainly correct on many points, there are a few ways in which her analysis—and consequently her conclusion—needs adjustment.  First of all, we need to look at the manuscripts instead of the Rusch edition of the Gloss. Once this crucial criterion is fulfilled, we are in a position to analyse, once again, the internal structure of the glosses, but with a view purely to their formalist features, which have the advantage of not being susceptible to differing judgements of such things as topical coherence. Finally, it will be fruitful also to compare the glosses with the corresponding material in Lombard’s Magna, for, as it 
                                                45 I shall speak below of the “surgical precision”-argument, which Gross-Diaz formulates in the following way, commenting on Gilbert’s exegesis of Psalm 138: “It is patently impossible to imagine that Gilbert would have been able (or willing) meticulously to distinguish the Cassiodoran interlinear fragments in the Glossa on Psalm 138—often only a disconnected word or two—from the Augustinian ones, then reconstruct the original, integral patristic context from such disiecta membra.” (From Lectio Divina, 145.) This mechanism has been touched upon above (see 54n15) and will be discussed further below. 

--- [p.78] ---

 
 
71 turns out, an explanation for the appearance of the Rusch edition can often be found here. We shall, therefore, examine how taking these aspects into account affects the outcome of Gross-Diaz’s analysis. Her first example compares the Ordinaria’s and Gilbert’s use of Augustine and Cassiodorus on Psalm 20:2–3, In your strength, O Lord, the king shall joy; and in your salvation he shall rejoice exceedingly. You have given him his heart’s desire and have not withheld from him the will of his lips (Domine in virtute tua laetabitur rex, et super salutare tuum exsultabit vehementer. Desiderium cordis eius tribuisti ei, et voluntate labiorum eius non fraudasti eum). Gross-Diaz shows typographically which parts of Augustine and Cassiodorus have been reproduced in Gilbert’s account, illuminating how the two patristic sources have been woven together into larger wholes.46 She then compares Gilbert’s passage to the corresponding glosses in the Ordinaria. The passages are reproduced below as they appear in Gross-Diaz’s text (that is to say, the Ordinaria-glosses are taken from the Rusch edition), but with the same typographical key as above: words that are present in the Gloss are thus in bold; words that are present in Gilbert’s Media are underlined, and words or ideas that are present in Lombard’s Magna are in SMALL CAPS; something borrowed by all three commentaries thus looks like THIS; something borrowed only by Gilbert and the Gloss looks like this; something borrowed only by Gilbert and LOMBARD looks like THIS; and something borrowed only by the Gloss and LOMBARD looks like THIS. Once again, snippets have been numbered, and, where relevant, passages also contain the snippet-numbers of one or more of the other commentaries for the purpose of easy comparison:

We see here the patterns typical of the three commentaries: Lombard more often follows the Gloss’s structure and wording closely, but at the same time also borrows extra material from Gilbert. So, for example, he takes over verbatim the Ordinaria’s adaptation of Cassiodorus’s interpretation of ‘vehementer’ (“ut quanta est copia in beneficio, tanta sit et gaudii magnitudo”), namely “(PL5e/GO6) “ut res magna est.” Gilbert also adapts this, but keeps closer to the original Cassiodorus, using the phrase “quantitatem beneficii” (GP4). Lombard also follows precisely the Gloss’s heavy adaptation of Cassiodorus’s explanation of the result of the fulfilment of God’s Word (“quando spiritibus imperabat immundis, languores diversos sermonis sui sanabat imperio, praedicationes quoque suas devotis mentibus inserebat”), namely “(PL10/GO11) Verbum quoque eius impletum est, fugando daemones, sanando infirmos, praedicatio eius quoque recepta est,” whereas Gilbert uses a slightly different grammatical structure: “(GP6c) scilicet daemones fugati, infirmi sanati, predicatio recepta.” 

--- [p.82] ---

 
 
75 (PL6a) And this [is] because you have given him his heart’s desire, (PL6b/A4) namely, to eat the Passover meal with his disciples, which he desired, (PL6e) and, when he wanted, to lay down his life, and to take it up again (PL6f/C6) and redeem the world.  (PL7a) And [you] have not withheld from him the will of his lips. (PL8/A5/C8) because whatever he said was done: (PL9/A5) for he said, My peace I leave with you, and it was done. (PL10a/C8) And his word also was fulfilled, (PL10b/C7) by putting demons to flight, by curing the sick, and his preaching was also received.  An analysis of the structure of Gilbert’s account reveals a true weave of the chunks of authoritative material: GP1a and b come from Cassiodorus (C1 and C4), and GP1c from Augustine (A2); GP2b comes from Augustine (A1, which is omitted from the Gloss), and GP2c from Cassiodorus (C2); GP3c and d look like a chunk of Cassiodorus (C3a–b), although the sentence ends in some Augustine (GP3e/A3b—though Gilbert appears to have changed ‘vivificas’ to ‘iustificas’);56 GP4 is a somewhat more extensive adaptation of Cassiodorus’s “ut quanta est copia in beneficio, tanta sit et gaudii magnitudo” (C5); in-between GP5b and d, which correspond to A4 (GP5d being slightly adapted), Gilbert has inserted GP5c, taken from Cassiodorus (C6); at GP6b we have another snippet that may be either from Cassiodorus or Augustine (Gross-Diaz thinks Augustine; the question will be discussed below), but GP6c is certainly from Cassiodorus (C7), although it is heavily adapted (it also appears in a clearly related but not identical adaptation in the Gloss); GP7 is from Augustine (A5). If we display only the numbers of the authoritative snippets as they occur in the Media, we get the following sequence: C1–C4–A2–A1–C2–A3/C3a–C3b–A3b–C5–A4–C6–A4–(A5/)C8–C7–A5, showing clearly that the two authorities have been truly intertwined. The corresponding Ordinaria-glosses as printed in Rusch are:57

(Gross-Diaz, From Lectio Divina, 133n31.) 57 GO2 is not included by Gross-Diaz, but it is present in the Rusch edition and has been included here simply for comprehensiveness. GO9 is omitted by both Rusch and (hence) Gross-Diaz.

Gross-Diaz points here to those glosses that (appear to) consist of a combination of Augustine and Cassiodorus (GO5, GO8, GO11a–b), arguing that Gilbert is the one who must be responsible for these fusions rather than the compiler of the Gloss, and that the latter thus depends here on the former. The general claim she makes in support of this argument is that Gilbert’s version reveals a deliberate effort to “make explicit the necessary relationship between the redemption of humankind and the united natures of Christ,” while, in her assessment, “no such theological program is manifest in the sketchy phrases which constitute the Glossa on these verses.”69 This argument is problematic as it stands, as it is based on the interpretation of what constitutes a manifestation of a theological program: someone else might say that the exact same theological program is manifest in the phrases that make up the Gloss—after all, the two commentaries

Reims, BM, 150 67 Aug./Cas.] sic Rusch; Cass. Paris, BnF, 17213, BAV, Vat. lat. 85; om. ceteri 68 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 20,2–3. (Laon, BM, 29, fol. 28r; Lund, UB, Med. 4, fols 19v–20r; Paris, BnF, lat. 105, fol. 21v; Paris, BnF, lat. 106, fol. 24r; Paris, BnF, lat. 442, fol. 22r; Paris, BnF, lat. 8846, fol. 35r; Paris, BnF, lat. 14403, fol. 25r; Paris, BnF, lat. 17213, fol. 28rv; Reims, BM, 150, fol. 33r; Troyes, BM, 511, fol. 23v; Troyes, BM, 881, fol. 25v; Troyes, BM, 924, fol. 21r; Troyes, BM, 925, fols 31v–32r; BAV, Vat. lat. 85, fol. 26rv; BAV, Vat. lat. 86, fol. 26r.) 69 Gross-Diaz, From Lectio Divina, 135. 

--- [p.85] ---

 
 
78 reproduce more or less the exact same phrases, and make more or less the exact same points, and it is quite easy to see how Gilbert could have created his continuous account simply by stringing the Gloss’s snippets together in succession. The argument that I wish to put forward here does not, on the other hand, rely on any such interpretations, but is based on purely formalist criteria.  We begin with the gloss GO8, “Comedere pascha, et cum vellet ponere animam et sumere et mundum redimere” (“To eat the Passover meal, and, when he wanted, to lay down his life and take it up again and redeem the world”) explaining ‘desiderium’ (“desire”), the first part of which, “Comedere pascha … sumere,” comes from Augustine (A4), and the rest from Cassiodorus (C6). Expressed in patristic snippet-sequence, the corresponding Media-snippets have the structure A4–C6–A4 (“(GP5b/A4) scilicet comedere pascha, quod desideravit, et animam ponere, et iterum sumere, (GP5c/C6) et mundum redimere, (GP5d/A4) pro ut voluit”). Gross-Diaz makes here the argument that “it is difficult to imagine the reasons a compiler might have melded Augustine and Cassiodorus in this fashion, unless, of course, he was extracting from a source which had already done so,”70 but, in fact, it is not. If we imagine a compiler putting together a gloss on the Psalms, it would hardly be surprising if we found him excerpting from the two main patristic authorities on this book. Owing to the fact that Cassiodorus used Augustine for his Psalms-commentary, our compiler would sometimes find that the two authorities offer similar interpretations of a particular lemma, making his job easy; sometimes he would find that they offer two different but non-conflicting interpretations, allowing him to excerpt both, and sometimes, that they offer two more or less completely different interpretations, and he that has to make a decision as to how to handle this: include both, harmonize the two, or exclude one of them. In this case, Augustine and Cassiodorus present 
                                                70 Gross-Diaz, From Lectio Divina, 135.  

--- [p.86] ---

 
 
79 different but non-conflicting interpretations of the psalm verse, and it would therefore not be strange if the compiler excerpted from them both. An even more compelling, formalist argument for the priority of the Gloss here is, however, the fact that the Gloss presents two “pure” chunks of the original sources, while the Media displays both an adaptation of the wording and a “weave” of the two: while the Gloss preserves Augustine’s “cum vellet” (“when he wanted”), Gilbert has recast this as “pro ut voluit” (“just as he wanted”) and placed it after the Cassiodorean snippet. If the compiler of the Gloss were using the Media, he would be making the effort to untangle the snippets and restore the Augustine to its original form, which seems like a far less likely scenario.71  Gross-Diaz’s argument for the second combination-gloss, GO5, “super hoc quod per eum vivificas omnia” (“in the fact that you give life to everything through him”) explaining ‘salutare’ (“salvation”), is similarly based on contextual grounds. The first part, “super hoc quod,” could be either Augustine (A3a) or Cassiodorus (C3a), “per eum” is Cassiodorus (C3b), and “vivificas omnia” is Augustine (A3b). The corresponding Media-snippets are GP3c–e, “id est in hoc quod per se salvatorem iustificas omnia” (“that is, in the fact that you justify everything through him [who is] the saviour”). Gross-Diaz invokes here the qualitative difference between what she describes as Gilbert’s apt use of the Cassiodorean phrase “id est in hoc quod per se salvatorem” and its “entirely unessential” counterpart “per eum” in the Gloss.72 She notes, furhermore, that the gloss is (mis-)identified in the Ordinaria as coming from Cassiodorus even though it is largely Augustinian, but that the mid-twelfth-century manuscript Laon, BM, 29 neither includes what is the Cassiodorean part of the gloss, ‘per eum’, nor mislabels the Augustinian rest of it. 
                                                71 Even if it is not impossible that a compiler would have done this in one or two instances, we shall see more examples of the same phenomenon below: taken together, these instances make a strong case for the priority of the Gloss. 72 Gross-Diaz, From Lectio Divina, 136. 

--- [p.87] ---

 
 
80 These circumstances lead her to hypothesize that Anselm’s original was a purely Augustinian gloss, which was later influenced by Gilbert’s commentary to add his incorporation of Cassiodorus’s ‘per eum’ (although not managing to preserve Gilbert’s aptness), resulting in GO5 as we have it in the Ordinaria. Gross-Diaz is certainly right in looking at how the different commentaries weave together the patristic material; here, however, we run into the problem of relying on the Rusch edition of the Gloss. As Gross-Diaz herself notes, ‘per eum’ is not found in Laon, BM, 29: in fact, the phrase is not present in any of the other fourteen manuscripts consulted here (although in BAV, Vat. lat. 85 a second hand does add it above the line). Neither do they (with the exception of Paris, BnF, lat. 17213 and BAV, Vat. lat. 85)73 contain any identification of the source of the gloss—that is to say, any mislabelling of it as Cassiodorus; in the two manuscripts where a later hand has added source references (see above), the gloss is correctly identified as Augustine.74 Gross-Diaz is thus right in her assessment that the original Ordinaria-gloss was pure Augustine; it still is! Except for the accusative-forming ‘d’ in ‘quod’, “super hoc quod vivificas omnia” is a verbatim quote of Augustine’s text, and this is what we have in the Gloss-manuscripts.  As evidenced by the addition of ‘per eum’ above the line in BAV, Vat. lat. 85 and in the Rusch edition, Gilbert’s inclusion of Cassiodorus into the original Augustinian gloss did, at some point, enter into the Gloss-tradition, so Gross-Diaz is correct in this conclusion—only, this happened after it had already become the Gloss as we have it in the manuscripts. A comparison to Lombard’s Magna indicates that it was through his successor that Gilbert exerted this influence; Lombard’s version reads:  
                                                73 In neither of the two manuscripts that contain the source reference has this been done by the original scribe, but by a second hand. 74 Herbert, in his edition of the Magna, also gives Augustine as the source. 

--- [p.88] ---

 
 
81 (PL5a) Unde subdit, et super salutare tuum, (PL5b/A3a) id est super hoc quod (PL5c/C3b) per eum (PL5d/A3b) vivificas75 omnia exsultabit vehementer, (PL5e) ut res magna est.76  ((PL5a) Wherefore [the psalmist] adds, and in your salvation, (PL5b/A3a) that is, in the fact that (PL5c/C3b) through him (PL5d/A3b) you give life to everything, he shall rejoice exceedingly, (PL5e/C5) as it is a great thing.)  As we see, Lombard reproduces Cassiodorus’s ‘per eum’, the inspiration for which he would have found in the Media’s ‘per se’. (He may have inserted this independently of looking at the original source, or he may have gone and checked Cassiodorus, as he often seems to do when the Media and the Gloss diverge.)77 As we shall see in Chapter Three, there is, in fact, evidence that Lombardian interpolations had entered into the Gloss-tradition already by the time of Peter the Chanter (that is, by the late twelfth century). This passage does, however, provide us with a nice opportunity to watch a user of the Gloss in action, for Gilbert appears, indeed, to be the one who introduces the Cassiodorus into the Ordinaria-gloss as he modifies it, and Gross-Diaz’s point about his apt use of Cassiodorus is, therefore, still a good one. Her point is further highlighted if we accept that the reading ‘iustificas’ for Augustine’s ‘vivificas’ is Gilbert’s intentional adaptation (see 75n56). As Gross-
                                                75 vivificas] Cambridge, Trinity College, B.5.4, fol. 56v, Admont, SB, 36, fol. 17v; vivificans Migne 76 PETR. LOMB. psalm. 20,2. (PL Database vol. 191, col. 220CD.) 77 Since he does not, however, reintroduce any other part of Cassiodorus’s original statement on salvation, but instead re-works Gilbert’s adapted version by lifting the salvation-part out of this gloss entirely and placing it in another sentence (PL4a–b: “Unde hoc praemium habuit, quod per eum omnia salvata sunt”) the former is perhaps the more likely scenario: we remember that Lombard tends to feel quite free to paraphrase when it comes to Gilbert’s material. In composing this new sentence, Lombard, furthermore, transports the Augustinian ‘omnia’ (A3b) into what is otherwise Cassiodorean material. Two points can be made of this: first, the repetition of ‘omnia’ that is created in this process (PL4b, PL5d) constitutes additional support for the claim that a certain “clunkiness”—in this case, repetition—is a symptom of combining sources; second, the fact that the new sentence does not come out as “pure” Cassiodorus supports the reverse of the “surgical precision”-argument explained above (see 70n45) and below—that is, as Lombard is not looking directly at the original source, he is not able to excise all of the non-Cassiodorean elements of the sentence, indicating that he is getting the material from an adaptation (namely, the Gloss and Gilbert) rather than directly from the patristic original. As we see, the further we move away from the original source, the more disorder increases. 

--- [p.89] ---

 
 
82 Diaz notes, Augustine’s ‘vivificas’ is less pointed than ‘iustificas’.78 We may elaborate on this assessment by observing that, owing to the fact that Augustine uses a neuter plural, ‘omnia’, it is not unequivocally clear from his phrase, “he shall rejoice in that [thing] by which [the Lord] give[s] life to everything,” that he is referring to the salvation of mankind. We thus have a scenario where Gilbert is tweaking the Augustinian gloss of the Ordinaria by incorporating Cassiodorus’s phrase “[he shall rejoice exceedingly,] that is, in the fact that you have saved man through him” (C3a–b); Gilbert melds the two by transforming Cassiodorus’s second person verb ‘salvasti’ into an adjective modifying ‘se’ (adapted from ‘eum’), “per se salvatorem,” thus amping up the salvific message of the Augustinian material. In light of this adaptation, it makes perfect sense that Gilbert should also have changed the vague “you give life to everything” to “you justify everything,” which phrase immediately brings to mind the divine economy. The example thus shows how Gilbert is very much working with the Gloss in combination with the original patristic sources to weave together his own account. If it were, as Gross-Diaz believes, the Media that was exerting influence on the Gloss here, the question would become why its compiler would have changed his rendering back to the original Augustine. It seems more likely that the opposite scenario represents the true state of affairs.  Gross-Diaz makes the same argument—that the Ordinaria-glosses cannot have been excerpted directly from the Fathers, but that Gilbert is the one who must have fused these together—for Gilbert’s combination of GO10 and GO11b, “(GP6b/GO10/A5/C8) quia quicquid dixit factum est, (GP6c/GO11b/C7) scilicet daemones fugati, infirmi sanati, predicatio recepta” (“because whatever he said was done, namely, demons put to flight, the sick cured, his preaching received”). Her argument is based on the understanding that the first part, GP6b, “quia quicquid dixit factum est,” comes from Augustine; she notes that it echoes his quotation of John 14:27,                                                 78 From Lectio Divina, 134. 

--- [p.90] ---

 
 
83 which is, indeed, the next sentence in Gilbert’s account: “(GP7/A5) Dixit enim, Pacem relinquo vobis, et factum est.” (“For he said, My peace I leave with you, and it was done.”)  Gross-Diaz argues that, while Gilbert’s combination of Augustine (GP6b) and Cassiodorus (GP6c) makes perfect sense both stylistically and logically, the Ordinaria’s division of this “Gilbertine whole” into its two constituent parts (GO10 and GO11b) not only makes utter nonsense of the Augustinian snippet (GP6b/G10) by removing it from the context in which it is presented in the Media, but also leads to a redundancy (GO11a) that can only be explained by the Gloss borrowing here from Gilbert.79 The redundancy consists in the fact that the phrase “verbum quoque eius impletum est” (“and his word was also fulfilled”), which Gross-Diaz thus believes is an adapted repetition of the Augustinian “quia quicquid dixit factum est,” is prefixed to the Cassiodorean continuation of the gloss (GO11b). In fact, the two phrases are not from the same source. In addition to the Augustinian ring of GO10 (“quicquid dixit factum est”), the belief that this gloss comes from Augustine is most likely guided by the fact that the Rusch edition identifies GO11 (a–b), “verbum quoque eius … recepta est,” as “Aug[ustine]/Cas[siodorus].” As in the above cases, however, the majority of the manuscripts do not contain any source identifications, and the two that have them as a later addition (Paris, BnF, lat. 17213, and BAV, Vat. lat. 85) both identify the source as Cassiodorus alone, as does that most assiduous checker of references Herbert of Bosham.80 If taken solely from Cassiodorus, the phrase “quicquid dixit factum est” would, instead, be an adaptation of C8, “omnia quae fieri iussit impleta sunt” (“all that which he commanded be done was fulfilled”), dressed in Augustinian wording; the two snippets clearly agree completely in sense.                                                 79 Gross-Diaz, From Lectio Divina, 136–37. 80 Cambridge, Trinity College, B.5.4., fol. 56v. 

--- [p.91] ---

 
 
84 This would, in turn, mean that GO11a, “verbum quoque eius impletum est,” is not an inexplicable “reattachment” of Augustinian material to the Media’s otherwise Cassiodorean gloss, the original combination of which Gross-Diaz believes that the compiler of the Gloss divided into two, thus creating a redundancy of Augustinian material. Instead, GO11a–b is simply a straightforward excerpt of Cassiodorus, slightly paraphrased: the Ordinaria-gloss both agrees in sense and echoes the wording of Cassiodorus’s “omnia quae fieri iussit impleta sunt.” This means that there is, in fact, no fusion of sources here. It is true, however, that the Gloss contains a redundancy, but it is of Cassiodorean material, not Augustinian. The process which created it and its Augustinian form can easily be explained: as before, we imagine that the compiler of the Gloss had both Augustine and Cassiodorus in front of him. In this instance, the two Fathers offer similar, but not identical interpretations: Augustine interprets ‘the will of his lips’ as something very specific, namely the words that Christ speaks in John 14:27, My peace I leave with you, while Cassiodorus is more general, saying “his preaching was also received.” In so doing, Cassiodorus manages to cover Augustine’s sense of “the words of Christ” without limiting these to the John-quotation; the addition of the points that Christ cast out demons and cured the infirm reveals that Cassiodorus wishes to widen Augustine’s interpretation of Christ’s will significantly. Now, in all of the fifteen manuscripts here consulted, GO10, “quia quicquid dixit…” is placed in close proximity to GO9, “Dixit, Pacem meam relinquo vobis …”, that is, the Augustinian gloss on which GO10 is partly modelled;81 in most of them GO10 follows immediately upon GO9. We can therefore imagine a scenario where the compiler, having compared Augustine’s and Cassiodorus’s interpretations, decided he wanted to generalize Augustine’s statement 
                                                81 This gloss is not included in Rusch, presumably for which reason Gross-Diaz does not seem to be aware of its presence in the Gloss. 

--- [p.92] ---

 
 
85 somewhat; he did this by modelling Cassiodorus’s expanded Augustinian interpretation on Augustine’s wording and in the process created something of a redundancy—a redundancy which did not work well in a continuous text, and which Gilbert consequently edited out.82 The redundancy thus functions as a sort of “lectio difficilior”—that is, it is easier to explain why Gilbert would have taken the redundancy out of the Gloss when composing his Media than to explain why the compiler of the Gloss would have put it in when excerpting from the Media, especially considering the rather elaborate process by which this would have had to have been done, as described above. Once again, it seems that the Gloss has priority over the Media, as the standard account holds. The only major difference in terms of clarity in this example is that the admittedly somewhat vague Ordinaria-gloss “Summam proponit, meritum et praemium” (“He advances the main point, the merit and the reward”) is divided into two parts in the Media, with each part being placed so that it is made clear what is the merit and what is the reward, whereas this is less obvious from the Ordinaria-gloss. Once again, however, the rendering of the Gloss could be seen as constituting the lectio difficilior, which would make this, too, an argument in favour of the priority of the Gloss: we know that the medieval masters thought that the Gloss was brief and obscure and in need of elucidation; this is exactly what Gilbert appears to be doing here. What we may note, however, is that the obscurity of the Gloss does not seem to have created any problem for Gilbert; he seems to have understood perfectly how to interpret the gloss “Summam 
                                                82 Lombard, for his part, deals with the situation differently: true to his habit, he omits nothing, but reproduces everything that he finds in the Gloss, redundancy included, and he finds a way to do it quite elegantly, showing that it is, indeed, possible to create coherence out of the disjointedness of the Gloss: “(PL8) Quidquid enim dixit, factum est: (PL9) dixit enim, Pacem meam relinquo vobis, et factum est. (PL10) Verbum quoque eius impletum est, fugando daemones, sanando infirmos, praedicatio eius quoque recepta est.” (“For whatever he said was done: For he said, My peace I leave with you, and it was done. His word was also fulfilled, by putting demons to flight, by curing the sick, [and] his preaching was also received.”) 

--- [p.93] ---

 
 
86 proponit …” This makes one wonder if he may have in some way been privileged with information regarding the interpretation of the Gloss, in line with what Gross-Diaz is suggesting. Gross-Diaz’s arguments for the Media’s superiority in terms of coherence, and thus its priority over the Gloss, at Psalm 138:18, I rose up and am still with you (Exsurrexi, et adhuc sum tecum), are more convincing. Nevertheless, an analysis based on formalist criteria yields similar conclusions as the ones already outlined. The patristic source here is Augustine; as above, something borrowed by the Gloss, Gilbert and LOMBARD looks like THIS; something borrowed only by Gilbert and the Gloss looks like this; something borrowed only by Gilbert and LOMBARD looks like THIS; and something borrowed only by the Gloss and LOMBARD looks like THIS. Augustine writes: Adhuc tecum sum, (A1) id est, NONDUM CUM IPSIS; QUIA NONDUM AGNOSCUNT. (A2a) SIC enim LEGITUR IN EUANGELIO, (A2b) QUIA POST RESURRECTIONEM Domini nostri Iesu Christi, (A2c) sibi apparentem (A2d) NON continuo COGNOUERUNT.83 (A3a) EST ET ALIUS SENSUS: Exsurrexi, et adhuc sum tecum: ut hoc TEMPus significare uoluerit, quO (A3b) adhuc IN OCCULTO EST AD DEXTERAM PATRIS, ANTEQUAM REUELETUR IN CLARITATE QUA UENTURUS EST ad iudicandum de uiuis et mortuis.  (A4a) Et deinde dicit QUID INTEREA (A4b) per TOTum HOC TEMPus, (A4c) dum iam resurrexit et adhuc cum Patre est, PATIATUR hic (A4d) PER COMMIXTIONEM peccatORUM IN corpore suo, quod est ECCLESIA, ET PER SEPARATIONEM HAERETICORUM.84 (I am still with you, (A1) that is, NOT YET WITH THEM; SINCE THEY DO NOT YET RECOGNIZE [him]. (A2a) For WE READ IN THE GOSPEL (A2b) THAT, AFTER THE RESURRECTION of our Lord Jesus Christ, THEY DID NOT immediately (A2d) RECOGNIZE [him] (A2c) when he appeared to them. (A3a) AND THERE IS ANOTHER MEANING: I rose up, and am still with you: as if [the Psalmist] wanted to signify that TIME at which (A3b) HE IS STILL HIDDEN AT THE RIGHT SIDE OF THE FATHER, BEFORE HE IS REVEALED IN THE SPLENDOUR IN WHICH HE WILL COME to judge the living and the dead.  (A4a) And next, he says WHAT IS ENDURED here IN THE MEANTIME, (A4b) throughout THE ENTIRE TIME (A4c) when he has already risen and is still with the Father, (A4d) THROUGH THE INTERMINGLING WITH sinners IN his body, which is THE CHURCH, AND THROUGH THE SPLITTING OFF OF THE HERETICS.)                                                  83 Matt. 28:17. 84 AVG in psalm. 138,25–26. (CCSL 40, 2008.)

88 REVEALED IN THAT SPLENDOUR IN WHICH HE WILL COME. (GO6e/A4a) [The psalmist] ADDS WHAT IS ENDURED IN THE MEANTIME (GO6f/A4d) THROUGH THE INTERMINGLING IN THE CHURCH WITH THE WICKED, AND THROUGH THE SPLITTING OFF OF THE HERETICS, …)   The Media, with both the Augustinian snippet-numbers and the snippet-numbers of the Gloss included, in turn, presents the following account: (GP1a/GO1) Sequitur: exsurrexi de MORTE, (GP1b/GO3) et sum tecum adhuc, SICUT ANTE MORTEM, QUOD PUTAVERUNT SE TULISSE illi qui me interfecerunt; (GP1c/GO4b) hoc enim illis NON VIDEbaTUR DE MORTUO. (GP2a/GO6a/A1) Vel tecum adhuc, NONDUM CUM IPSIS discipulis, QUIA NONDUM AGNOSCUNT. (GP2b/A2a) SICUT enim IN EVANGELIO LEGITUR, (GP2c/GO6b/A2b) QUOD POST RESURRECTIONEM (GP2d/A2c) sibi apparentem DominUM (GP2e/A2d) NON continuo COGNOVERUNT. (GP3a/GO6c/A3a) VEL adhuc, TOTO HOC TEMPORE, (GP3b/GO5) etsi RESURRECTIO SIGNUM SIT deiTATIS, sum tecum, id est PLURIBUS OCCULTUS, UT TU, (GP3c) id est nondum credor Deus esse, sicut nec tu, (GP3d/GO6d/A3b) et omnibus etiam sum IN OCCULTO AD DEXTERAM tuam, quousque REVELETUR CLARITAs iudicaturi.  (GP4a) Si occideris. Pars quarta, ubi (GP4b/GO6e/A4a) dicit QUID INTEREA, (GP4c/A4c) dum est occultus cum Patre, PATITUR (GP4d/GO6f/A4d) IN corpore suo, quod est ECCLESIA, PER COMMIXTIONEM MALORUM ET PER SEPARATIONEM HAERETICORUM.92 ((GP1a/GO1) Then follows: I rose up FROM DEATH, (GP1b/GO3) and am still with you, as before death, which those who killed me thought they had brought; (GP1c/GO4b) for this [i.e. that I rose up from death and am still with you] was NOT EVIDENT TO THEM FROM THE DEAD [man]. (GP2a/GO6a/A1) Or still with you, NOT YET WITH the disciples THEMselves, SINCE THEY DO NOT YET RECOGNIZE [him]. (GP2b/A2a) For AS IT SAYS IN THE GOSPEL, (GP2c/GO6b/A2b) AFTER THE RESURRECTION, (GP2d/A2c) when the Lord appeared to them (GP2e/A2d) THEY DID NOT immediately RECOGNIZE [him]. (GP3a/GO6c/A3a/A4b) OR I am still, DURING THIS ENTIRE TIME, (GP3b/GO5) even if RESURRECTION IS A SIGN OF deITY, with you, that is, HIDDEN FROM MORE PEOPLE, LIKE YOU, (GP3c) that is, I am not yet believed to be God, as you are not, (GO6d/A3b) and I AM also HIDDEN to everyone AT YOUR RIGHT SIDE, until THE SPLENDOUR of the one who will judge IS REVEALED.  (GP4a) If you will kill. Part four, where (GP4b/GO6e/A4a) he says WHAT IS ENDURED IN THE MEANTIME, (GP4d/GO6f/A4d) THROUGH THE INTERMINGLING WITH THE WICKED AND THROUGH THE SPLITTING OFF OF THE HERETICS, IN his body, which is THE CHURCH, (GP4c/A4c) while he is hidden with the Father.)  Gross-Diaz points here to the fact that Gilbert expresses as a single complete idea what the Gloss presents as two alternative readings, namely the sections GP1a–c, “Then follows: I rose up 
                                                92 GILB. PICT. in psalm. 138,18. (Paris, BnF, lat. 439, fol. 179v.) 

--- [p.96] ---

 
 
89 from death, and am still with you, as before death, which those who killed me thought they had brought; for this [i.e. that I rose up from death and am still with you] was not evident to them from the dead [man],” as compared to GO3–GO4b, “I am still with you. As before death, which the Jews thought they had brought. Or still, that is, in death itself, which was not evident from the dead [man].”  Looking thus at the sense of the accounts, the superiority of Gilbert’s rendering seems to consist of the fact that he fuses the gloss stating that it was not evident from the dead man (or body) that Christ was still with God with the gloss stating that someone—unclear who—thought they had brought death to Christ, thus effecting the meaning that it was to those very people who thought they had killed Christ that it was not evident that he was still with God. As the Ordinaria presents the gloss “[still with you] which was not evident from the dead [man]” as an alternative reading to “[still with you] as before death etc.,” it does not make it clear to whom it was not evident that Christ had risen; Gross-Diaz remarks that “this would be a mildly surprising assertion, if it is meant to refer to Christians.”93  In an ex postfacto sense, this is certainly true, indeed making the Ordinaria-gloss seem somewhat puzzling taken on its own. But if we read it from the historical perspective of those followers of Christ who were present at the crucifixion, it is not necessarily so: to them it certainly appeared that Christ was dead—Christ the man was!—and it was not obvious even to them that he would rise up again on the third day; even after the resurrection, they continued to doubt.94 This would thus be a reading of this gloss that works even when it is taken on its own, namely, as referring to the moments and days immediately following Christ’s death on the cross; the clarification “in death” (GO4a, which is not present in the manuscripts, but appears to be a                                                 93 From Lectio Divina, 141. 94 Cf. Matt. 28:17; Mark 16:11, and 16:13; Luke 24:11. 

--- [p.97] ---

 
 
90 Lombardian interpolation)95 indicates that this is how the gloss was, indeed, interpreted. We must also consider that GO4b is added as an alternative vel-explanation to “ante mortem,” and that it thus makes less sense to imagine that someone would have broken Gilbert’s coherent whole into two in this way. Gross-Diaz puzzles also over the Ordinaria’s “enigmatic tag”96 ‘iudaei’ (“the Jews”), arguing that it is more likely that the compiler of the Gloss changed Gilbert’s “those who killed me” to “the Jews” than that Gilbert changed the Gloss’s “the Jews” to “those who killed me”: even though, she says, Gilbert otherwise tends towards the opinion that the Romans were technically responsible for Christ’s death, it would be remarkable for him to have made this substitution in a context where he wasn’t specifically making an argument about this point. Once again, however, the manuscripts tell a different story: they all omit ‘iudaei’; this specification is instead, again, an addition by Lombard (see 90n95). 
                                                95 Lombard weaves the snippets together in a different way, leaving the two Ordinaria-glosses as separate ideas, and even breaking down the snippets into smaller pieces: “Et hoc totum ideo, et, id est quia, (GO1) surrexi a morte et adhuc sum tecum, (A1) nondum cum ipsis per notitiam, quia nondum agnoscunt, (A2b) quia non mox post resurrectionem Christum agnoverunt, (A2a) sicut in evangelio legitur. (A3a) Vel ita: surrexi, et adhuc, id est toto tempore vitae praesentis, tecum sum, id est occultus ad dexteram tuam, (GO2) donec omnibus videar in iudicio. (GO6c/A3a/A4b) Toto enim hoc tempore (GO6d/A3b) Christus in occulto est ad dexteram Patris, antequam reveletur in ea claritate in qua venturus est. Vel ita: surrexi a morte—hoc non mutatur—(GO3) et adhuc, id est post mortem, sum tecum, sicut ante mortem, quod putaverunt se tulisse iudaei. (GO4a) Vel ita: et adhuc, scilicet in ipsa morte, sum tecum: (GO4b) quod non videtur de mortuo.” (“And all this because, and, that is, since, (GO1) I rose up from death and am still with you, (A1) not yet with them through knowledge [of him], since they do not yet recognize [him], (A2b) since soon after the resurrection they did not recognize [him], (A2a) as it says in the gospel. (A3a) Or like this: I rose up, and am still, that is, during the entire time of the present life, with you, that is, hidden at your right side, (GO2) until I am seen by everyone at the judgement. (GO6c/A3a/A4b) For this entire time (GO6d/A3b) Christ is hidden at the right side of the Father, before he is revealed in the splendour in which he will come. Or like this: I rose up from death—this does not change—(GO3) and am still, that is after death, with you, as before death, which the Jews thought they had brought. (GO4a) Or like this: and am still, that is, in death itself, with you, (GO4b) which is not evident from the dead [man].”) PETR. LOMB. psalm. 138,18. (PL Database vol. 191, col. 1220CD.) Although Lombard has taken over some features from Gilbert—such as adding A2a (note the switch of its location) and a direct object in A2b, and specifying “those who killed me” as “the Jews”—his account, as usual, mirrors more or less exactly the structure of the Gloss, presenting first the Augustinian gloss (with his own additions), and then the “magisterial” chunk (see Gross-Diaz’s words below) as one whole, in the same order as the Gloss. 96 From Lectio Divina, 140. 

--- [p.98] ---

 
 
91 This means that the Ordinaria-gloss is, by itself, rather vague indeed, not giving any indication whatsoever as to who it was that thought they had brought death to Christ, demonstrating, once again, the need for the Gloss’s elucidation. (We may, of course, assume that the gloss has been excerpted from some context in which the answer was clear; the reflexive pronoun ‘se’ must have had an antecedent somewhere!) What we thus see here is two different commentators dealing in different ways with the disjointed snippets and relative obscurity of the Gloss:97 Gilbert’s elucidation consists in adding “those who killed me,” completely in line with Gross-Diaz’s point that he does not place the blame solely on the Jews, but Lombard is of a different opinion as to where to place the blame and thus decides to replace Gilbert’s equally vague statement with a more precise tag. Looking at more purely formalist criteria, we also have here that fact—also noted by Gross-Diaz—that the Gloss clearly separates the Augustinian material from what appears to be the non-patristic material gathered together as GO3–GO5—what she terms “mere ‘magisterial’ words.”98 What Gross-Diaz imagines is that Gilbert used for his lectures Anselm’s adaptation of Augustine—which was purely patristic—as he found it in Anselm’s “little gloss,” and that he added to it his own contributions; some of these contributions were then imported back into the Gloss (unclear, however, by whom). As the process is similar to what we see happening with Lombard, who uses both the Gloss and the Media, and whose elaborations, as we have seen, are 
                                                97 See also 85n82 for the argument that it is possible to create coherence out of the snippets of the Gloss. For Psalm 138:18, the argument could also be made that Gilbert’s “whole” is not necessarily smoother: his rendering becomes one long sentence, where ‘hoc’ (“this”)—referring to that which was not evident from the dead man—has to reach all the way back through the rest of the sentence to the lemma, ‘I rose up from death and am still with you’; in the Gloss, each of the three alternative interpretations is easily related back to the lemma, without the reader having to keep track of what ‘hoc’ refers back to—the distance, so to speak, between lemma and interpretation is shorter in the Gloss, as this does not connect the snippets into a long, continuous thought. 98 From Lectio Divina, 141. None of these interpretations is to be found in any of the authoritative sources nor, indeed, in any contemporary Psalms-commentary and may thus, in fact, represent an original, contemporary magisterial contribution to the Gloss. (Migne labels GO5 as Alcuin, but I have not been able to verify this.)  

--- [p.99] ---

 
 
92 sometimes found in Rusch,99 the scenario, by analogy, seems perfectly plausible. A closer examination, however, reveals that the two scenarios are not, in fact, quite analogous, and that it is highly unlikely that the Gloss as we have it is dependent on the Media.  What the Augustinian snippet-numbers included in the twelfth-century accounts tell us is that the Gloss presents the Augustinian material in un-mixed form: A1–A2b–A2d–A3a–A3b–A4a–A4d. Gilbert, on the other hand, drops GO5 (accompanied by a faint echo of GO2: ‘omnibus’) into his rendering of the Augustinian chunk: A1–A2a–A2b–A2c–A2d–A3a–GO5–A3b–A4a–A4c–A4d. The Gloss thus, again, contains the purer form of the patristic material, which suggests the opposite conclusion—that is, it speaks for the priority of the Gloss, without any influence from the Media.100 If we accept Gross-Diaz’s theory, we would have to imagine a scenario where, first, Gilbert elaborates on what is purely Augustinian material by, among other things, inserting into it his magisterial phrase GO5; whoever then introduced the Gilbertine contributions into the Gloss did not reproduce these as he saw them in the Media, but must, once again, have identified and with surgical precision excised the foreign phrase (GO5, and also the trace of GO2) from the Augustinian material, thus “purifying” the patristic excerpt—and this at the same time as declining to fully restore the excerpt to its original form, for the Gloss preserves such adaptations as “toto hoc tempore” and “commixtionem malorum.” (The Lombardian interpolations, in contrast, are incorporated into the Gloss in the same form and location as they are found in the Magna; they are not “purged” out of the chunks into which he inserted them.) As such a task of purifying the patristic material not only seems as if it would be tremendously tedious, but of such                                                 99 No manuscript of the Gloss containing the Lombardian additions has, as yet, been discovered, and it thus remains unclear to scholars what manuscript(s) Rusch used for his edition, but we shall see in Chapter Three that there is evidence that these interpolations had entered the Gloss-tradition already be the late twelfth century. 100 We also see, however, that Gilbert cannot have relied on the Gloss alone for his access to Augustine, as he incorporates also A2a, A2c, and A4c into his account. 

--- [p.100] ---

 
 
93 proportions in terms of effort that it is difficult to imagine why anyone would undertake it,101 it seems highly unlikely that the Ordinaria’s version of these glosses would be dependent on the Media in any way.102 An example from another psalm will serve to illustrate this point further. In its exegesis of Psalm 1:1, Blessed is the man who has not walked away in the counsel of the ungodly, nor stood in the way of sinners, nor sat in the chair of pestilence (Beatus vir qui non abiit in consilio impiorum, et in via peccatorum non stetit, et in cathedra pestilentiae non sedit), the Ordinaria offers the following gloss: (GO1) ¶ Ordo: (GO2) Abiit Adam, cum a Deo recessit; (GO3) stetit, cum delectatus est peccato; (GO4a) sedit, cum in superbia confirmatus (GO4b) redire non potuit, nisi per istum liberatus (GO4c) qui nihil horum habuit.103  ((GO1) ¶ The order: (GO2) Adam walked when he withdrew from God; (GO3) he stood when he delighted in sin; (GO4a) he sat when emboldened in his pride (GO4b) he was not able to turn back unless set free by him (GO4c) who had [done] none of these.)  This is straight out of Augustine; we see a perfect alignment between the snippets, with GO4c being a heavy abridgement of Augustine’s final enumeration of sins in A4c: (A1/GO1) Deinde considerandus est ordo uerborum, abiit, stetit, sedit. (A2/GO2) Abiit enim ille, cum recessit a Deo; (A3/GO3) stetit, cum delectatus est peccato; (A4a/GO4a) sedit, cum in sua superbia confirmatus, (A4b/GO4b) redire non potuit, nisi per eum liberatus (A4c/GO4c) qui neque abiit in consilio impiorum, nec in uia peccatorum stetit, nec in cathedra pestilentiae sedit.104 
                                                101 The only circumstance under which such a project seems feasible is if Gilbert and the compiler of the Gloss are, in fact, one and the same person. We shall return to this idea below. 102 It is, however, true that the “magisterial” glosses seem somewhat disconnected to any context; they certainly seem as if they have been extracted from some more elaborate exposition. It is also true that Gilbert cannot be getting his Augustine only from the Gloss, for he includes additional Augustinian material (A2 and A4c); neither, however, can the Gloss have gotten its Augustine directly from Gilbert, as it preserves the source in more original form, both structurally and in wording. Whatever hypothesis we arrive at, it must thus be able to account for this independence vis à vis the sources that we find in both the Gloss and the Media. 103 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 1,1. (Laon, BM, 29, fol. 1v; Lund, UB, Med. 4, deficit; Paris, BnF, lat. 105, fol. 1v; Paris, BnF, lat. 106, fol. 1v; Paris, BnF, lat. 442, fol. 2v; Paris, BnF, lat. 8846, fol. 6r; Paris, BnF, lat. 14403, fol. 1r; Paris, BnF, 17213, fol. 6r; Reims, BM, 150, fol. 1r; Troyes, BM, 511, fol. 1r; Troyes, BM, 881, fol. 4r; Troyes, BM, 924, fol. 1r; Troyes, BM, 925, fol. 2v; BAV, Vat. lat. 85, fol. 1r; BAV, Vat. lat. 86, fol. 5r.) 104 AVG in psalm. 1,1. (CCSL 38, 1.)
