94 ((A1/GO1) Next, the order of the words, walked, stood, sat, should be considered. (A2/GO2) For he walked when he withdrew from God; (A3/GO3) he stood when he delighted in sin; (A4a/GO4a) he sat when emboldened in his pride (A4b/GO4b) he was not able to turn back unless set free by him (A4c/GO4c) who neither walked in the counsel of the ungodly, nor stood in the way of sinners, nor sat in the chair of pestilence.)  As (part of) another gloss,105 the Ordinaria gives the following passage: (GOI) ¶ Abiit Adam, cum persuasioni diaboli consensit; (GOII) stetit, cum pomum comedit;  (GOIII) sedit, cum se excusavit: Mulier quam dedisti mihi seduxit me.106 107 ((GOI) ¶ Adam walked when he consented to the persuasion of the Devil; (GOII) he stood when he ate the apple; (GOIII) he sat when he made himself the excuse: The woman whom you gave me led me astray.)  The first two interpretations (of abiit and stetit) come from Remigus, who writes: (R1/GOI) [Adam] in consilio impiorum abiit, cum diabolo suadenti assensum praebuit; (R2/GOII) in via peccatorum stetit, cum pomum ligni vetiti gustavit; (R3/A4a) in cathedra pestilentiae sedit, cum ad peccandum per superbiam se obfirmavit.108 ((R1/GOI) [Adam] walked in the counsel of the ungodly when he gave his assent to the Devil persuading [him]; (R2/GOII) he stood in the way of sinners when he tasted the apple of the forbidden tree; (R3/A4a) he sat in the chair of pestilence when he entrenched himself in sin through pride.)  As we see, the Gloss presents a somewhat adapted form of Remigius, with his “assensum praebuit” (“he gave assent”) having been turned into “consensit” (“he consented”), and ‘the Devil’ modified by the participle ‘persuading’ (suadenti) having been turned into the noun ‘persuasion’ with ‘the Devil’ in the genitive; in the second part of the Remigius-passage, the verb                                                 105 This gloss is marginal in all of the fifteen manuscripts here consulted but differs somewhat in what other material/glosses it is combined with; in none of these manuscripts does this passage, however, occur in the same gloss as the preceding, Augustinian passage. 106 Gen. 3:12. 107 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 1,1. (Laon, BM, 29, fol. 1v; Lund, UB, Med. 4, deficit; Paris, BnF, lat. 105, fol. 1v; Paris, BnF, lat. 106, fol. 1v; Paris, BnF, lat. 442, fol. 2v; Paris, BnF, lat. 8846, fol. 6r; Paris, BnF, lat. 14403, fol. 1r; Paris, BnF, 17213, fol. 6r; Reims, BM, 150, fol. 1r; Troyes, BM, 511, fol. 1r; Troyes, BM, 881, fol. 4r; Troyes, BM, 924, fol. 1r; Troyes, BM, 925, fol. 2v; BAV, Vat. lat. 85, fol. 1r; BAV, Vat. lat. 86, fol. 5r.) 108 REMIG. in psalm.I 1,1. (Reims, BM, 132, fol. 1v; St. Gallen, SB, Cod. Sang. 312, p. 3–4.) This passage is not present in the long version. 

--- [p.102] ---

 
 
95 ‘tasted’ (gustavit) has been changed to ‘ate of’ (comedit). For the interpretation of ‘sedit’, Remigius is clearly borrowing from the Augustine-passage quoted above (A4a); the Gloss’s choice to alter this last interpretation in its excerpt from Remigius—presumably because it already had it from Augustine—may thus represent what Gross-Diaz refers to as a magisterial gloss—that is to say, an original addition by a twelfth-century master. Gross-Diaz wants to ascribe her examples of such glosses to Gilbert, but another possibility would, of course, be that this is Anselm’s original exegesis passed on by Gilbert. In any case, as the excerpt as a whole is clearly an adaptation of Remigius by someone, we shall call it the magisterial Remigius-gloss even though we cannot say who, exactly, is responsible for the adaptation. Now we look at how these Ordinaria’s glosses are rendered in Gilbert’s Media: (GP1a/GO2/A2) Adam abiit a Deo, non loco, sed animi cogitatione; consilio impiorum, (GP1b/GOI/R1) id est, suggestioni serpentis et Evae voluntate consentit.109 … (GP2a/GO3/A3) Prosequere: stetit delectione in via peccatorum, (GP2b/GOII/R2) dum pomum comedit; (GP3a/GO4a/A4a) sedit consensu et quasi consuetudine, cum superbia confirmatus (GP3b/GOIII) sese excusando culpam in auctorem retorsit, dicens, Mulier quam dedisti mihi seduxit me.110 ((GP1a/GO2/A2) Adam walked away from God, not in space, but in the thinking of his mind; the counsel of the undgodly, (GP1b/GOI/R1) that is, he consented in his will to the suggestion of the serpent and Evae. … (GP2a/GO3/A3) Continue: he stood in delight in the way of sinners, (GP2b/GOII/R2) when he ate the apple; (GP3a/GO4a/A4a) he sat in consent and as if in habit when, emboldened in his pride (GP3b/GOIII) by excusing himself of the blame to his master, he retorted, saying, The woman whom you gave me led me astray.)  As above, we see that Gilbert has created a perfect weave of the Ordinaria’s two purely patristic/Carolingian glosses, beginning with the first part of the Augustine-gloss and then inserting the first part of the magisterial Remigius-gloss, then the second part of the Augustine-                                                109 Gilbert has substituted Eve and the serpent for the Devil, mapping the example more precisely onto the biblical account, but as the two former characters are the manifestations of the latter, the expressions can be said to be equivalent. 110 GILB. PICT. in psalm. 1,1. (Paris, BnF, lat. 439, fol. 1v.) 

--- [p.103] ---

 
 
96 gloss and the second part of the magisterial Remigius-gloss, and so on. The fact that we repeatedly see this same pattern in the structure of the patristic material is clear evidence that Gloss precedes the Media and that Gilbert must have been using it rather than vice versa: it is simply inconceivable that the compiler of the Gloss would have been able and willing to take all of Gilbert’s patristic weaves and separated them out again into their original, “pure” form. Lombard’s dependence on the Gloss is proven in the same way; the same weaves are present in the Magna:  (PL1a/GP1a) Abiit ergo Adam a Deo, non loco, (PL1b) sed dissimilitudine, (PL1c/GOI/R1) cum persuasioni diaboli et Evae consentiens (PL1d/GO2/A2) a Deo recessit; (PL2a/GOII/R2) stetit, cum pomum vetitum comedens (PL2b/GO3/A3) in peccato delectatus est; (PL3a/GOIII) sedit, cum se stulte excusando et culpam in creatorem referendo (PL3b/GO4a/A4a) in superbia confirmatus est, (PL3c/GOIII) dicens, Mulier quam dedisti mihi seduxit me, (PL3d/GO4b/A4b) non valens redire nisi per eum liberatus (PL3e/GO4c/A4c) qui nihil horum habuit.111  ((PL1a/GO2/A2) Therefore, Adam walked away from God, not in space, (PL1b) but in dissimilitude, (PL1c/GOI/R1) when, consenting to the persuasion of the devil and Eve, (PL1d/GO2/A2) he withdrew from God; (PL2a/GOII/R2) he stood when, eating the forbidden apple, (PL2b/GO3/A3) he delighted in sin; (PL3a/GOIII) he sat when, foolishly making excuses for himself and placing the blame on his creator, (PL3b/GO4a/A4a) he was emboldened in his pride, (PL3c/GOIII) saying, The woman whom you gave me led me astray, (PL3d/GO4b/A4b)[and]  not being able to turn back unless set free by him (PL3e/GO4c/A4c) who had [done] none of these.)  The structure of Lombard’s passage compared to the other two, once more, indicates that a direction of influence from the Gloss, via the Media, to the Magna is the more likely scenario. But these passages contain also another instance of the “surgical precision”-mechanism.  In addition to the two marginal glosses quoted above, the Ordinaria contains the interlinear gloss “(GO5) non abiit/ a Deo in regionem dissimilitudinis”112 (“(GO5) has not walked away/ 
                                                111 PETR. LOMB. psalm. 1,1. (PL Database vol. 191, col. 60D.) 112 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 1,1. (Laon, BM, 29, fol. 1v; Lund, UB, Med. 4, deficit; Paris, BnF, lat. 105, fol. 1v; Paris, BnF, lat. 106, glosam om.; Paris, BnF, lat. 442, fol. 2v; Paris, BnF, lat. 8846, fol. 6r; Paris, BnF, lat. 14403, fol. 

--- [p.104] ---

 
 
97 from God into the area of dissimilitude”). The expression “regio dissimilitudinis” comes from Augustine, but is used by him in rather a different context, namely in his Confessions.113 The gloss is taken up by Lombard in PL1a–b, but also by Gilbert, who adapts it, somewhat further on in his exegesis: “(GP4) Modo revertere ad seriem ita: Vir est beatus. Quis? (GP5a/GO2/A2) Qui non abiit, id est non recessit (GP5b/GO5) a Deo in regionem deserti, …”114 (“(GP4) Now return to the sequence like this: Blessed is the man. Who? (GP5a/GO2/A2) Who has not walked away, that is, has not withdrawn (GP5b/GO5) from God into the area of the abandoned, …”)  In both the Gloss, the Media and the Magna, the expression “regio dissimilitudinis” has, furthermore, been combined with the phrase “a Deo,” which echoes the first Augustinian passage quoted above, “Abiit enim ille, cum recessit a Deo” (A2). (Supporting this claim further is the fact that Gilbert also includes Augustine’s ‘recessit’ in the ‘regio dissimilitudinis’-gloss.) In other words, both the Gloss and the Media include two Augustinian glosses, one of which (GO2/GP1a, “a Deo”) has, in both commentaries, influenced the other (GO5/GP4, “regionem dissimilitudinis/ deserti”). This, again, is clear evidence that they depend directly on one another for, at least part of, their use of the patristic sources.  If we compare this to Lombard, we see how he, in turn, reproduces a combination of Ordinaria-material and Gilbertine material: in the Media, the interpretation of ‘non abiit’ (‘has not walked away’) as “non loco” (“not in space”; GP1a) is separated from the interpretation of it as “in regionem desertum” (“into the deserted area”, GP5b); in Lombard, however, these two interpretations are combined into one sentence: “Abiit ergo Adam a Deo, non loco, sed                                                 deficit; Paris, BnF, 17213, fol. 6r; Reims, BM, 150, fol. 1r; Troyes, BM, 511, fol. 1r; Troyes, BM, 881, fol. 4r; Troyes, BM, 924, fol. 1r; Troyes, BM, 925, fol. 2v; BAV, Vat. lat. 85, fol. 1r; BAV, Vat. lat. 86, fol. 5r.) 113 “Et reuerberasti infirmitatem aspectus mei radians in me uehementer, et contremui amore et horrore: et inueni longe me esse a te in regione dissimilitudinis, tamquam audirem uocem tuam de excelso.” (“And glowing mightily at me, you struck the weakness of my gaze, and I trembled with love and horror; and I found myself to be far from you in the place of dissimilarity, as if I was hearing your voice from on high.”) AVG conf. 7:10. (CCSL 27, 103.) 114 GILB. PICT. in psalm. 1,1. (Paris, BnF, lat. 439, fol. 1v.) 

--- [p.105] ---

 
 
98 dissimilitudine.” (“Therefore, Adam walked away from God, not in space, but in dissimilitude”; PL1a–b.) Lombard then repeats GO2/A2 in the form that is closer to the Gloss and to the patristic original. If we are imagining that the Gloss is an abridgement of the Magna, we would, once more, have to imagine that its compiler was able to identify exactly what was the Gilbertine addition to Lombard’s account, “non loco,” and that he excised it to make the gloss more purely Augustinian. This, again, seems like a highly unlikely scenario. Rather, we observe in this passage the complex process by which the glossators excerpted and adapted their sources: the patristic original is first excerpted and adapted by the compiler of the Gloss, which is turn is adapted by Gilbert, and finally by Lombard, who also uses and adapts Gilbert’s Media.  CONCLUSIONS AND A REVISED STANDARD ACCOUNT The above examples show that the intuition about the relationship between order and disorder invoked by Glunz, Woodward, and Gross-Diaz is, indeed, valid: what their arguments lack is simply a focus on the criterion that we must use a measuring-stick against which the degree of order and disorder can be objectively gauged; we find this measuring-stick and fulfil this criterion when we apply the “surgical precision”-argument to the internal structure of individual passages that have been excerpted from the patristic and Carolingian auctoritates, which provide an “answer key” to the “correct” form of the excerpts.  What we see when this criterion is applied is that it is most often the Gloss which presents the purest form of the patristic and Carolingian excerpts. In those instances when the Media or the Magna presents the original patristic form of an excerpt, it is most often material which is not found in the others, showing that our commentators did not rely solely on the Gloss itself as a source for patristic material. The priority of the Gloss is, however, demonstrated especially 

--- [p.106] ---

 
 
99 clearly when we have a situation where two different authorities have been excerpted and woven together in the continuous commentaries.  From the passages analysed above, it should thus be sufficiently clear that the standard account must hold, and that the Gloss has priority over both the Media and the Magna glosatura;115 this is the scenario which best explains the patterns of agreement which we have seen: it would mean that Lombard, indeed, faithfully reproduces every word of the Gloss while also glossing it and, in many parts, using the Media to do so. Towards the latter work he, however, has a much more relaxed attitude. Gilbert, on the other hand, seems to use the Gloss much more loosely as a general guide. At the same time, many of Gross-Diaz’s points about the superior coherence and contextualization of Gilbert’s Media as opposed to the inarguably obscurely terse and disjointed snippets of the Gloss are good ones—even though we have seen that such arguments must, without exception, be based on a reading of the manuscripts and not on the Rusch edition (nor, indeed, as we shall see in Chapter Three, on any of the printed editions). As mentioned, she believes that the fact that Gilbert is sometimes the one who reproduces the patristic original more faithfully than the Gloss should be explained by a scenario where Gilbert is imagined to be working with, and consequently influencing, a purely patristic gloss compiled by Anselm (even though recourse must then be had to the rather involved idea of the subsequent compiler of the Gloss as we have it sometimes—and only sometimes!—“retrofitting” Gilbert’s adaptations of the Fathers back to their original form).116 However, there is an even more far-reaching suggestion 
                                                115 We have, however, also seen that the Gloss, at some point, became interpolated with material from Lombard’s commentary, and that Gilbert has thus, at least indirectly, influenced some version of the Gloss. 116 See Gross-Diaz, From Lectio Divina, 137. 

--- [p.107] ---

 
 
100 which would also explain the non-unilateral relationship which we see between the Gloss and the Media, and that is that Gilbert is, in fact, the compiler of the Gloss as we have it.  If we imagine that the Gloss as we have it is the result of Gilbert’s collection of authoritative exegesis—perhaps originally crib notes for his lectures—it would make perfect sense that, when he was transforming his notes into a polished continuous commentary, he would sometimes have gone back to look more closely at an original patristic passage and also sometimes have chosen to paraphrase and adapt some of the excerpts which he had originally compiled in the Gloss, when fitting these into his continuous prose-account. Gilbert had been a student of Anselm’s, and the conclusion of the next two chapters—namely, that there appears to be some source behind the Gloss and the Media which seems to have influenced the Psalms-exegesis of the twelfth-century independently of the Gloss, and from which certain adaptations of patristic material as well as some non-patristic material seems to stem—fits well with Gross-Diaz’s suggestion that Gilbert may have brought his own reportatio of Anselm’s lectures with him to Chartres, and with her theory about magisterial versus authoritative patristic exegesis; what she believes to be Gilbert’s contributions may instead stem from the lectures of his master. Furthermore, if Gilbert himself is the “authority” behind the Gloss, this would explain why he would have allowed himself considerable freedom in his use of it as compared to Lombard, who takes such care to preserve the exact form and content of the Gloss, presumably due to his perception of its Anselmian origin and consequent authoritative status. The disjointed snippets of the Gloss read quite well as rough notes for the Media (although presumably originally intended for lecturing); the scenario would explain all of the instances where Gilbert’s continuous account appears to be superior in sense, even while the Gloss preserves the most original and pure form of the patristic excerpts: for example, the imprecise introductory gloss on Psalm 20:2, “He advances the main point, the merit and the reward,” would 

--- [p.108] ---

 
 
101 read as a note serving to remind the lecturer to make this specific point, knowing full well himself what each term refers to; it would also explain the glaring lack of an antecedent in the gloss “as before death, which they thought they themselves [Who?] had brought,” found at Psalm 138:18.117 An analysis of Gross-Diaz’ last example will serve to illustrate this idea further. For our commentaries’ exegesis of Psalm 138:11, And I said: Perhaps the shadows shall trample me: and night shall be my light in my pleasures (Et dixi: Forsitan tenebrae conculcabunt me; et nox illuminatio mea in deliciis meis), Gross-Diaz makes the argument that, even though the two are clearly related (as evidenced by their non-coincidental shared use of the verb ‘opinari’), the Gloss’s abridgement of their shared patristic source—in this case, Cassiodorus— cannot be the source of Gilbert’s rendering. The passages in question read as follows:  Cassiodorus Prima pars uersus istius sub IRONIA pronuntianda est. Frequenter enim aliqua quasi sub ambiguitate proferimus, de quibus DUBITAre non possumus, sicut in alio psalmo iam dictum est: Forsitan uiuos deglutissent nos118 et reliqua. Quomodo enim tenebrae conculcare poterant, quae ius in tanta gloria non habebant? Ipse enim dicit de se: Ego sum LUX huius MUNDI.119 Sed conculcauit ille potius tenebras, qui caecitatem primi hominis ad eius posteros transeuntem, misericordiae suae luce superauit. Quapropter hoc commate quo ait, forsitan tenebrae conculcabunt me, IRRIdentur illi qui de ipso poterant timoris alicuius nebulas suspicari.120 (The first part of this verse should be spoken in IRONY. For we often say things about which we can have no doubt as if ambiguously, as it was already said in another psalm: Perhaps they had swallowed us up alive, and the rest. For how could the shadows, which had no power in such glory, trample [him]? For he says about himself, I am THE LIGHT OF this WORLD. But rather, he who by the light of his mercy conquered the blindness of the first man, which was passing on to his descendants, trampled the shadows. For which reason, those who were 
                                                117 We may also note that something appears to be off about the grammar here, as ‘quod’ makes more sense in this context as a neuter relative pronoun than as the conjunction ‘because’, but as a relative pronoun does not agree with ‘mors’, which is feminine. However, as both the Gloss, the Media and the Magna have ‘quod’ here, it appears to be more than a misinterpretation of the abbreviation. A possible explanation could thus be that the snippet has been extracted from a larger context in which the grammar made sense, and then heavily abridged. 118 Ps. 123:3. 119 John 8:12. 120 CASSIOD. in psalm. 138,11. (CCSL 98, 1246–1247.) 

--- [p.109] ---

 
 
102 capable of suspecting a whiff of any fear about him are DERIDed in the line where he says, Perhaps the shadows will trample me.)  Gilbert P HOC NON DUBITATIVE SED IRONIce DICIT, IRRIdens eos QUI HOC OPINANTUR, QUOD IPSE QUI EST LUX MUNDI CONCULCETUR A TENEBRIS.121 (HE DOES NOT SAY THIS DOUBTFULLY, BUT IRONICALLY, DERIDING THOSE WHO THINK THAT HE WHO IS THE LIGHT OF THE WORLD WILL BE TRAMPLED BY DARKNESS.)  Glossa O Et dixi: Forsitan/ IRONIA: IRRISIO HOC OPINANTium122 QUOD IPSE QUI LUX CONCULCETUR A TENEBRIS123 (And I said: Perhaps/ IRONY: THE DERISION OF THOSE WHO THINK THIS THAT HE WHO IS THE LIGHT WILL BE TRAMPLED BY DARKNESS.)  Lombard Et dixi hoc: Forsitan tenebrae conculcabunt me. Ironia est et irrisio opinantium haec, quod ipse qui est lux mundi conculcetur a tenebris. Non ergo dubitative hoc dicit, sed ad irrisionem illorum qui haec opinantur.124 (And I said this: Perhaps the shadows will trample me. This is irony and the derision of those who think this, that he who is the light of the world will be trampled by darkness. Therefore, he does not say this doubtingly, but in derision of those who think this.)  As Gross-Diaz notes, Gilbert cannot have gotten his Cassiodorus from the Gloss alone, as he, unlike the Gloss, includes Cassiodorus’s reference to John 8:12, I am the light of the world, and touches upon the topic of statements that can be doubted. As above, however, we see that the manuscripts present a shorter phrase than does the Rusch edition, and that the material in question

(Laon, BM, 29, fol. 227r; Lund, UB, Med. 4, fol. 134v; Paris, BnF, lat. 105, fol. 186r (1); Paris, BnF, lat. 106, fol. 186v; Paris, BnF, lat. 442, fol. 169r; Paris, BnF, lat. 8846 deficit; Paris, BnF, lat. 14403, fol. 189v; Paris, BnF, 17213, fol. 199r; Reims, BM, 150, fol. 251v; Troyes, BM, 511, fol. 182v; Troyes, BM, 881, fol. 167r; Troyes, BM, 924, fol. 163r; Troyes, BM, 925, fol. 218v; BAV, Vat. lat. 85, fol. 196v; BAV, Vat. lat. 86, fol. 171v.) 123 quod … tenebris] sic Rusch; om. codices 124 PETR. LOMB. psalm. 138,11. (PL Database vol. 191, col. 1218CD.) 

--- [p.110] ---

 
 
103 is found in Lombard, who is borrowing directly from Gilbert. (Note how he incorporates both the full Ordinaria-gloss and Gilbert’s material, resulting in the repetition of “haec opinant-”.)  Once again, Gross-Diaz’s point that the quotation of John 8:12, which is still recognizable in the Media, has, in the Gloss, been reduced to a generic reference to “light” is a good one,125 only, it is Lombard who has priority here, not Gilbert. At the same time, the Gloss is closer to Cassiodorus in its use of the noun ‘ironia’, where Gilbert has the adverb ‘ironice’; this is often the case and one for which Gross-Diaz has no decisive answer, but if we imagine that what we have in the Gloss are the crib notes on the different patristic sources—either for a lecture or for a continuous commentary—all of the above circumstances are explained. Equally importantly, this hypothesis would explain the appearance of the earliest datable manuscripts around the time that Gilbert had just finished his teaching career. In fact, Stirnemann argues convincingly that the earliest copies of certain books of the Gloss—books that had not already been produced (and presumably compiled) at Laon before 1135, one of these being the Psalms—were produced at Paris, starting around 1140, by artisans using a style that had originated at Chartres.126 This movement in the production of the Gloss from Chartres to Paris, she observes, coincides exactly with the teaching career of Gilbert of Poitiers and his presence in those two cities consecutively; in fact, some of the earliest copies of Gilbert’s Media belong to the very group of books which supplies Stirnemann with the evidence for her argument, namely the ones that were bequeathed by prince Henry to Clairvaux around 1146. She does not believe that this could be a coincidence.127  
                                                125 Gross-Diaz, From Lectio Divina, 145. 126 Stirnemann, “Où ont été fabriqués,” 264–276. See also Patricia Stirnemann, “Gilbert de la Porrée et les livres glosés à Laon, à Chartres et a Paris,” in Monde médiéval et société chartraine: Actes du colloque, Chartres, 8–10 septembre 1994, ed. Jean-Robert Armogathe (Paris: Picard, 1997), 83–96.  127 Stirnemann, “Gilbert et les livres glosés,” 89. 

--- [p.111] ---

 
 
104 Henry’s ownership of both works—that is, the Gloss and the Media on the Psalms—shows us, furthermore, that the latter was not necessarily seen as a replacement for the former, as is generally assumed, and the fact that the two appear on the scene simultaneously challenges this assumption of the standard account. Stirnemann goes on to suggest that Gilbert may even have been personally involved in the design and diffusion of his own work (that is, of the Media).128 It is not a far cry from here to propose that he may have played a similar role in the diffusion of the Gloss—at least on the Psalms—and from here to suggest that his motivation for doing so was the same one: that the Gloss on the Psalms as we have it is also his work. This hypothesis is at the same time compatible with the state of affairs that holds true for the Gloss on the books discussed in the previous chapter: Gross-Diaz could thus still be correct in her suggestion that Anselm’s original “little gloss” served as the basis of Gilbert’s teaching—at least originally—explaining why it was Anselm who received the credit for the work as a whole. This would put the Media in a similar relationship to the Gloss as the Glosae on John; we would only have to imagine that the Gloss was created either simultaneously or, at least, with the Media in mind, as we have seen from the purity of its patristic and Carolingian excerpts that it must precede its continuous counterpart. This would, however, not be difficult to explain, seeing as the Gloss-project was well under way by the late 1130s; Gilbert may thus have started out with the intention of contributing to the project.129 
                                                128 Stirnemann, “Gilbert et les livres glosés,” 89–90. 129 We may also note that Gilbert’s and Lombard’s commentaries are unique in their close relationship to the Gloss, which raises the question of why we have these two particular commentaries: if two rather than just one (i.e. Gilbert’s, which one would have thought would be enough of an elucidation), then why not even more? If Gilbert is, however, the compiler of the Gloss, the co-existence of the Gloss and the Media would be explained in the same way as the co-existence of the Gloss on John and the Glosae, and Lombard’s elaboration of the Gloss using the Media would be a singularity. We may remind ourselves that Lombard assures Herbert that he never intended for his work to be publicly circulated, which indicates that the general view was that the Gloss already had a sufficiently lucid continuous companion, namely the Media. 

--- [p.112] ---

 
 
105 In the absence of further manuscript evidence, however, this hypothesis is difficult to prove; for now, we must be content with noting that it would go a long way towards explaining the patterns of agreement that we see between the Gloss, the Media, and the patristic originals. We turn now instead to a theory which speaks to the suggestion advanced in Chapter One, namely that Anselm may have written a continuous commentary which served as a source for the Gloss. 

--- [p.113] ---

 
106 
Chapter Three A CONTINUOUS PSALMS-COMMENTARY ATTRIBUTED TO ANSELM OF LAON Anselm’s Potential Authorship of Pseudo-Haimo   The suggestion that Anselm is the author of a continuous commentary on the Psalms is not new; it was proposed already in 1936 by André Wilmart.1 The commentary in question is printed by Migne under the name of Haimo of Halberstadt, who was bishop of that city 840–853.2 The text came to the attention of posterity thanks to Erasmus, who was the first to edit it in 1533.3 In his preface, printed in the PL, Erasmus attributes the work to Haimo but does not say on what grounds. As the manuscript he used—which, according to him, came from the abbey of Marbach in Alsace—is no longer extant, we cannot know if this is where he found the attribution, but the presence in his edition of a list of medieval works with attributions to Haimo by Trithemius would indicate that he is Erasmus’s source for this information.4  By the turn of the nineteenth century, scholars had begun to question Trithemius’s attributions to Haimo. Among them was Albert Hauck, who in 1890 noted that there are passages in the Psalms-commentary that cannot possibly be ascribed to the ninth century bishop, but that the text seems rather to reflect views that were prevalent in the circle emanating from the abbey 
                                                1 André Wilmart, “Un commentaire restitué.” 2 (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. (PL Database vol. 116, cols 191–696.) 3 Erasmus’s edition was reproduced at Cologne in 1561; this edition then served as the basis of Migne’s text: “Ex editione data Coloniae anno 1561, curante Erasmo, apud haeredes Joannis Quentel.” PL Database vol. 116, col. 191. 4 See PL Database vol. 116, cols 189B–190C; Wilmart, “Un commentaire restitué,” 325–26. 

--- [p.114] ---

 
 
107 of Hirsau at the end of the tenth century. He suggested, therefore, that the work should be attributed to the monk Haimo of Hirsau.5  The two most telling passages are found in the commentary on Psalms 48:11–12 and 90:6. In his exegesis of Psalm 48:11–12, the commentator mentions a turris Crescentii, “the tower of Crescentius,” which Hauck points out is a reference to the Castel Sant’Angelo in Rome, built originally as a mausoleum for the emperor Hadrian, but used, in the Middle Ages, as a fortress. The nickname turris Crescentii dates to the late tenth century, when the castle was in the control of the Crescenzi, a prominent family of the Italian aristocracy that was leading the resistance against the Ottonian emperors.6 The nickname used by the commentator thus postdates Haimo of Halberstadt by at least a century. (Hence our designation of the commentary as Pseudo-Haimo.)   The second reference reinforces the implication of the first: at Psalm 90:6, Pseudo-Haimo mentions the name Guibertus demens, “Guibert the mad,” which was the clever nickname given to the infamous anti-pope Clement III (Guibert of Ravenna), who was supported by Henry IV against Gregory VII during the so-called Investiture Contest.7 Clement was elected (anti-)pope in 1080, giving us a terminus post quem for the commentary (or, at least, for the comment itself; see Chapter Four).  A final piece of evidence invoked by Hauck against Haimo of Halberstadt’s authorship is the commentary’s juxtaposition, at Psalm 101:8, of “monks and good canons” (monachi et boni canonici), which Hauck reads as a contrasting between regular and secular canons, and takes as 
                                                5 See Albert Hauck, Kirchengeschichte Deutschlands, 2nd ed., vol. 3 (Leipzig: J. C. Hinrichs'sche Buchhandlung, 1906), 1043–1047; for his original suggestion, he cites here the first edition of the same work (Leipzig, 1890), vol. 2, 597–598. I have not, however, been able to consult this edition. 6 See Emmanuel Rodocanachi, Le château Saint-Ange: Traveaux de défense, appartements des papes, sièges, prisoniers – executions, le trésor (Paris: Librairie Hachette & Cie, 1909), 20–21. 7 For the Investiture Contest, see Tellenbach, Church, State and Christian Society. The nickname Guibertus demens plays on the rhyme between the Latin word for ‘crazy’, ‘demens’, and Guibert’s papal name, Clemens. 

--- [p.115] ---

 
 
108 another reason to place the commentary in the context of the Gregorian reform.8 As for its terminus ante quem, Hauck argues (in each case based on a single textual parallel) that Pseudo-Haimo was known to both Bruno of Segni (d. 1123) and Honorius of Autun (d. 1154), and used by them in their respective Psalms-commentaries, the older of which (Bruno’s) was completed between 1100 and 1110. We shall be returning to this question below.  Then, in 1936, Wilmart, discovered a manuscript of Pseudo-Haimo, Vatican City, BAV, Reg. lat. 295, that bears the title Incipiunt glosule magistri Anselli archidiaconi super Psalterium (“The glosses on the Psalter of master Anselm the archdeacon begin”). In the resulting article, Wilmart argues that the very detailed information provided in this title fits perfectly with Anselm of Laon and what we know of him: most often styled magister in the sources, he also became archdeacon at Laon in 1114.9 Wilmart argues further that ‘Ansellus’ is the “true” form of the name, giving several contemporary examples of the spelling.10 Only a well-informed scribe, he posits, would have given his copy such a comprehensive and precise title.11 In light of the reference to “Guibert the mad,” Wilmart argues that the commentary should be placed somewhere between 1095 or -96, when pope Urban II was campaigning widely for his cause throughout France, and 1100, before the collective memory of the schism had faded away completely.12 As for the problem of sorting out the relationship between Pseudo-Haimo and the Gloss—both, in other words, believed by Wilmart to be composed by Anselm—he is content to speculate that the continuous commentary came first and the Gloss second, positing that the two                                                 8 Hauck, Kirchengeschichte, 1044. 9 See Chapter One, 21n25. 10 Wilmart, “Un commentaire restitué,” 341–42. Giraud, too, looks at the evidence for the “correct” form of Anselm’s name and notes that more or less all manuscripts that carry an attribution to Anselm (i.e. those containing his sentences) give ‘Anselmus’ (one gives ‘Ansselmus’), but that all but two of the documents he signed in the capacities of his various church offices have ‘Ansellus’ (see Giraud, Per verba magistri, 36 and 36n2).  11 Wilmart, “Un commentaire restitué,” 338. 12 Wilmart, “Un commentaire restitué,” 335. 

--- [p.116] ---

 
 
109 served different purposes, which would explain the difference in their character. With the recent findings presented in Chapter One regarding the other Gloss-books associated by the tradition with Anselm, such a suggestion does not seem at all implausible.13   OBJECTIONS TO WILMART’S THEORY: LANDGRAF In 1942, Wilmart’s theory was dealt what seemed to be a fatal blow. Artur Landgraf had identified a number of passages where a magister Anselmus or glosatura Anselmi is explicitly quoted or referenced by a later twelfth-century master, and upon cross-referencing these quotations with both the Gloss and Pseudo-Haimo, found that, in each case, the reference can, indeed, be matched with the Gloss, but not, however, with Pseudo-Haimo.14 This led him to conclude that the twelfth century associated only one Psalms-commentary with Anselm,15 and that this commentary is the Gloss and not Pseudo-Haimo.  At first glance, the arguments presented by Landgraf appears solid. The conclusion that it was the Gloss on the Psalms that the twelfth century referred to as Anselm’s work need not, however, surprise us: as we have seen, this work was very clearly associated with Anselm. What is new information since Landgraf’s article, however, is that the nature of Anselm’s involvement in the 
                                                13 Importantly for our purposes, Wilmart notes that the Gloss on the Psalms and Pseudo-Haimo use the same sources (see Wilmart, “Un commentaire restitué,” 340). He concedes, however, that the evidence he puts forward is in no way conclusive. As a final addition to his argument, he points to what he thinks is a significant sign of kinship between Pseudo-Haimo and the Anselmian corpus, namely the fact that Pseudo-Haimo and the Enarrationes on the Gospel of Matthew (see Chapter One, 36n74 and 42n94) both make use of the transitional term ‘continuatio’. (Wilmart, “Un commentaire restitué,” 344.) It therefore seems worth mentioning that Van den Eynde discusses the use of this term in the context of the authorship of the Enarrationes, claiming that it is entirely missing from the theological literature that can be securely dated to before 1130–40. (Van den Eynde, “Autour des Enarrationes,” 69.) According to him, the term appears first in Abelard’s commentary on Romans, as well as in Hugh of St. Victor.   14 Artur Landgraf, “Die Zuweisung eines Psalmkommentars an Anselm von Laon,” Biblica 23 (1942): 170–174. Landgraf’s twelfth-century authors include Peter of Poitiers (d. 1215), Peter the Chanter, and Gilbert of Poitiers. 15 Landgraf expresses it as two Psalms-commentaries, treating the marginal and the interlinear glosses, like Glunz, as two separate works. 

--- [p.117] ---

 
 
110 creation of the Gloss on other biblical books associated with him seems to be of a more indirect nature. This newly established state of affairs thus raises the question of whether Anselm might also, or perhaps even only, have written a continuous commentary on the Psalms—one which then served as a source for the Gloss in a scenario analogous to those demonstrated to hold for Canticles, the Gospel of John, and, potentially, for Matthew. (Or, if Gilbert should turn out to be the compiler of the Gloss, a commentary that may have been one of his sources.)  The possibility of this scenario, in turn, re-opens the question of whether Wilmart’s theory could, in fact, be correct and Pseudo-Haimo be Anselm’s work, for, given this newly established state of affairs, any evidence that the Gloss and Pseudo-Haimo are not the work of the same author no longer precludes Wilmart’s suggestion.16 The first question which thus instead presents itself is whether Pseudo-Haimo could have been a source for the Gloss.  An argument for this scenario was, in fact, recently made by Kraebel, who claims to have found many examples of unattributed glosses in the Ordinaria for which he has found no patristic or Carolingian source, but which can be found in Pseudo-Haimo.17 Based on these findings and the theory that Anselm’s involvement in the creation of the Gloss is of a more indirect nature, Kraebel takes Anselm’s authorship of Pseudo-Haimo as a proven fact, accepting Wilmart’s arguments. As he gives, however, only two examples of such glosses, one from Psalm 9:5 and one from the prologue, and as an examination of Landgraf’s arguments gives a different picture, we shall begin our attempt to settle this question by revisiting Landgraf—more 
                                                16 For this reason, we I shall not be considering the arguments against Anselm’s authorship made by Lottin on the basis of the observation of contradictory statements in the two texts (see Lottin, Psychologie et morale, 5:170–175). Furthermore, as the Gloss is mainly a compilation of patristic and Carolingian biblical authorities, we should not necessarily expect to find in it any coherent “theological programme.” It is possible that the excerpts were selected with such a programme in mind, but not necessary. 17 Kraebel, “The Education of Anselm.” 

--- [p.118] ---

 
 
111 specifically, those arguments of his that involve in-text references to Anselm by later twelfth-century theologians.18 Like Gross-Diaz, Landgraf relies for the analysis of his selected passages on one of the early printed editions of the Gloss—in his case the 1498 Basel edition.19 The renderings found in this edition—as cited by Landgraf—do, indeed, correspond to the quotations of the two Peters. However, when one looks at the manuscripts, the passages in question turn out to be shorter than the renderings of the printed edition, just as we saw in the case of the Rusch edition. Thus, in two of Landgraf’s four examples of an in-text reference to Anselm, a comparison of the text of Pseudo-Haimo with that of the manuscripts of the Gloss shows that the two texts, in fact, correspond quite well; in the third, there is, at least, a possible connection between the two, and, in the last example, the source is not, in fact, the Gloss, but rather Gilbert P. The first example where an agreement can, in fact, be found is Peter of Poitiers’s comment on Psalm 109:1, The Lord said to my Lord: Sit at my right hand, until I make your enemies your footstool (Dixit Dominus Domino meo: Sede a dextris meis, donec ponam inimicos tuos scabellum pedum tuorum). Peter refers here to the three ways in which God is said to speak:  Dixit Dominus domino meo. In principio erat verbum20 refert ad id quod dixerat omnipotenti deitate, Verbum caro factum est21 ad id quod dixerat de incarnatione. Quot modis Deus dicatur dicere, habes supra in alio ‘confitemini’, et potest hic accipi illis tribus modis. Et nota quod exponit hanc particulam Re[migius], quae erat in brevi glosatura magistri Anselmi.22  
                                                18 Two of Landgraf’s citations involve the manuscript Paris, BnF, lat. 12004, which is a copy of Gilbert P. Here, Landgraf claims to have found in the margin two references to Anselm which cite passages that Landgraf has, indeed, found in the Gloss while he finds nothing similar in Pseudo-Haimo. However, no such marginal references are visible in the digitized version of the manuscript; what we do find are references to Augustine, from whom both passages have, indeed, been excerpted. For this reason, I shall not be addressing this argument. 19 See Froehlich, “The Printed Gloss,” xvii–xix. All of the printed editions are, however, ultimately based on Rusch. 20 John 1:1. 21 John 1:14. 22 PETR. PICT. II dist. psalt. 109,1. (Paris, BnF, lat. 14423, fol. 32v.) 

--- [p.119] ---

 
 
112 (The Lord said to my lord. [The phrase] In the beginning was the word refers to what he said in his all-powerful divinity, [and the phrase] The word was made flesh [refers] to what he said about the incarnation. Above, in another [instance of] ‘give praise’, you have in how many ways God is said to speak, and it can be understood here in those three ways. And note that Re[migius] expounds this bit, which was found in master Anselm’s short gloss.)  The cross-referenced Gloss-text cited by Landgraf reads: “Dixit Dominus. Remi[gius]: id               est effectu vel generatione vel eternaliter disposuit.”23 (“The Lord said. Remi[gius]: that is, through the bringing about, or through the begetting, or he arranged eternally.”) The fact that he does not find any corresponding gloss in Pseudo-Haimo leads Landgraf to believe that this cannot be the work referred to by Peter as Anselm’s. While this may itself be true, it nevertheless does not disprove that the Gloss and Pseudo-Haimo could have the same author, or could, at least, have used the same source, for what Remigius, in fact says (according to the long version) is the following: Dixit autem non motu labiorum vel volubilitate linguae, sed dixit, id est disposuit et ordinavit ante omnia saecula glorificandum Christum post laborem passionis.24 (Indeed, he did not say [this] with movement of the lips or rolling of the tongue, but he said, that is, he arranged and ordered before all time that Christ should be glorified after the labour of his Passion.)  What we find in our Gloss-manuscripts is, in fact, the significantly shorter interlinear gloss “re. [or rē.] vel aeternaliter disposuit”25—that is to say, a reference to Remigius with only one interpretation of ‘said’, namely, “he arranged eternally,” which corresponds in essence to what 
                                                23 Cited in Landgraf, “Die Zuweisung,” 171. 24 REMIG. in psalm.II 109,1. (BAV, Urb. lat., fol. 146r.) The short version does not include this exegesis. 25 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 109,1. (Laon, BM, 29, fol. 189v; Lund, UB, Med. 4 deficit; Paris, BnF, lat. 105, fol. 150r; Paris, BnF, lat. 106, fol. 155v; Paris, BnF, lat. 442, fol. 138v; Paris, BnF, lat. 8846 deficit; Paris, BnF, lat. 14403 fol. 147v; Paris, BnF, lat. 17213, fol. 165r; Reims, BM, 150, fol. 209v; Troyes, BM, 511, fol. 150v; Troyes, BM, 881, fol. 138r; Troyes, BM, 924, fol. 135r; Troyes, BM, 925, fol. 183r; BAV, Vat. lat. 85, fol. 162v; BAV, Vat. lat. 86, fol. 146r.) In BAV, Vat. lat. 85, a later hand has added “id est effectu vel potius generatione” above the line (fol. 162v)—clearly an influence from Lombard (see next paragraph). 

--- [p.120] ---

 
 
113 we find in the long version of Remigius, his “before all time” having been recast as “eternally.” What Pseudo-Haimo, in turn, has is: “Dominus Pater dixit, id est ab aeterno disposuit, Domino meo …” (“The Lord the Father said, that is arranged from eternity, to my Lord …”) This corresponds exactly to the Gloss-manuscripts in all but omitting the reference to Remigius.  The truly telling insight obtained by this example is, however, the fact that the Gloss and Pseudo-Haimo share the same adaptation of Remigius’s “ante omnia saecula” as “aeternaliter/ab aeterno,” showing that they cannot simply be relying independently on Remigius here, but that they are directly connected to each other in some way. This implication shall be explored further in the following. What is also interesting here is Peter’s perception of what is Anselm’s work. As it turns out—and in line with what we saw in the previous chapter—the additional material of the Basel edition can be found in Lombard’s Magna (from this chapter onward, boldface shall be used to highlight general agreements between the different commentaries): Dominus Pater dixit. More nostro loquitur propheta cum ait, dixit, ut homo scilicet instruatur; non enim ore protulit, sed dixit re, id est effectu; vel potius re, id est generatione. Vel dixit, id est aeternaliter disposuit, Domino meo …26 (The Lord Father said. The prophet speaks in our way when he says, said, namely, so that man should receive instruction; for [the Lord] did not utter [this] with his mouth, but he said it through an act, that is, through the bringing about, or rather, through an act, that is, through the begetting [of the Son]. Or said, that is, arranged eternally, to my Lord …)  As we see, the items referred to by Peter of Poitiers as three modes of speaking, and enumerated in the Basel edition of the Gloss, are all present in Lombard’s explanation.27 These                                                 26 PETR. LOMB. psalm. 109,1. (PL Database vol. 191, col. 997D.) 27 In Lombard’s rendering, however, ‘re’ is not interpreted as a reference to Remigius, but instead as the ablative of ‘res’—that is, literally, “through the thing.” Lombard’s understanding of ‘re’ as the word ‘res’ is evident from the fact that he repeats the word twice, giving alternative interpretations of it, namely ‘effectu’ (“through the bringing about”) and ‘generatione’ (“through the begetting”). We may note that Lombard is borrowing here from Gilbert P, who writes: “… propter quod dicit: Dominus Pater domino meo Filio dixit hoc: Sede a dextris meis. More nostro 

--- [p.121] ---

 
 
114 additional interpretations have then made their way into the original gloss of the Ordinaria, which becomes, as seen in the Basel edition, “Dixit Dominus. Remi.: id est effectu vel generatione vel eternaliter disposuit”—that is, three modes of speaking with a reference to Remigius, as Peter of Poitiers reports. What this example thus provides us with is additional evidence that the Paris masters of the late twelfth century were working with a version of the Gloss which had become interpolated with Lombardian material, for which Anselm, nevertheless, received credit.28 There is, however, nothing in this gloss that disqualifies the suggestion that the author of Pseudo-Haimo could be Anselm. At the very least, we see that Pseudo-Haimo and the Gloss are connected to each other in some way other than by a simple mutual dependence on the Psalms-authorities. The second instance where a closer examination reveals that Pseudo-Haimo does not, in fact, differ greatly from the Gloss is Landgraf’s fourth example, which comes from Peter the Chanter’s commentary on Paul. The Chanter refers here to Psalm 125:7, But coming they shall come with joyfulness, carrying their sheaves (Venientes autem venient cum exsultatione, portantes manipulos suos): 
                                                ponit propheta ‘dixit’, ut homo scilicet instruatur; non enim ore hoc protulit sed effectu vel dispositione aeterna.” (“… because of this he says: The Lord Father said this to my Lord the Son: Sit at my right hand. The prophet puts ‘said’ in our way, namely, so that man should receive instruction; for [the Lord] did not utter this with his mouth, but through the bringing about or through the eternal arrangement.”) GILB. PICT. in psalm. 109,1. (My rendering is a conflation of Paris, BnF, 439, fol. 142v, and Troyes, BM, 988, fol. 177rv: Troyes 988 omits the second ‘hoc’, which I have chosen to include, but BnF 439 transposes ‘meo filio’, which gives the awkward reading ‘The Lord said to my son the Lord’, which cannot be the intended meaning.) Gilbert gives no reference to Remigius, but includes two modes of speaking, namely “through the bringing about” and “through the eternal arrangement.” The question which thus presents itself is if it is, perhaps, Gilbert who has misunderstood the Gloss’s abbreviated reference to Remigius as the ablative of ‘res’, and replaced this with a synonym, namely ‘effectu’. Such a scenario would speak against the suggestion that Gilbert is the original compiler of the Gloss but would still be compatible with the idea that Gilbert elaborated on and contributed to an early version of the Gloss. 28 The Distinctiones, according to Philip Moore, dates to sometime shortly before 1196. (Philip S. Moore, The Works of Peter of Poitiers: Master in Theology and Chancellor of Paris (1193–1205) [Notre Dame, IN: University of Notre Dame, 1936], p. 91.) I have, as of yet, not found any manuscript of the Gloss which contains these Lombardian interpolations; the spread of such a version has thus yet to be determined. 

--- [p.122] ---

 
 
115 … magister Anselmus sic hunc locum exponit: portantes tunc manipulos, fructum meritorum in spe. Hoc supplet: quam spem in proximo cognoscunt esse implendam in re certi de coronis, ac si dicerent: Repositae sunt nobis coronae, quas iam recipiemus, quando nobis dicetur, Venite, benedicti, etc.)29  (… master Anselm expounds this verse thus: carrying their sheaves, the fruit of their merits in hope. He adds this: which hope they know will be fulfilled in the near future, being certain in fact of their crowns, as if they were saying: Our crowns have been restored to us, which we shall already receive when it is said to us, Come, ye blessed, etc.)30   The Basel edition of the Gloss, in turn, reads, “manipulos: fructum seminis, id est coronam gaudiorum et exultationis”31 (“sheaves: the fruit of their seed, that is, the crown of their joys and exultation”), which Landgraf equates with the Chanter’s reference to Anselm. However, as we have seen before, the version of the manuscripts is shorter, and the additional material of the printed edition can be found in Lombard, who has taken it straight out of Augustine, who, in turn, is also the source of the shorter Ordinaria-gloss: Augustine In illa enim resurrectione mortuorum recipiet quisque manipulos suos, id est fructum seminis, coronam gaudiorum et exsultationis.32 (For in that resurrection of the dead everyone shall receive his sheaves, that is, the fruit of their seed, the crown of their joys and exultation.)
