--- [p.123] ---

 
 
116 Lombard Venient, dico, portantes manipulos suos, fructum seminis, scilicet coronam gaudiorum et exsultationis.34 (They shall come, I say, carrying their sheaves, the fruit of their seed, namely, the crown of their joys and exultation.)  If we look at Pseudo-Haimo, we find that it reproduces at least a part of the same Augustinian snippet which appears in the Gloss: Ideo dico converte, quia tunc, id est facta conversione, metent illi in exsultatione, id est exsultanter recipient mercedem vitae aeternae qui seminant bona opera sua, unde multiplicem fructum exspectent, in lacrimis, id est in maerore et tristitia huius vitae.35 (Therefore, I say turn, because then, that is, when the turn has been completed, they shall reap in joy, that is, joyfully those who sow their good works shall receive their reward of eternal life, wherefore they expect plentiful fruit, in tears, that is, in the sorrow and sadness of this life.)  Since the Gloss incorporates more of the original Augustine here (“fructum seminis”), it seems unlikely that Pseudo-Haimo would have been its source. The opposite relationship is, however, a possibility, as is the scenario that they drew from the same intermediary source. What we may note, however, is that the connection between the Gloss and the Chanter’s purported paraphrase of Anselm’s exegesis is not entirely obvious: the only similarities are, first, the interpretation of ‘sheaves’ as “the fruit,” and second, the mention of crowns, which accords with the version of the Gloss that has been interpolated with Lombard’s addition. The Chanter seems, however, to be attributing more than these two exegetical details to Anselm; his main concern here appears, rather, to be the question of hope. The full context of his reference to Anselm reads as follows: 
                                                34 PETR. LOMB. psalm. 125,7. (PL Database vol. 191, col. 1156C.) 35 (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. 125,7. (Troyes, BM, 904, fol. 139v; PL Database vol. 116, col. 640B.) 

--- [p.124] ---

 
 
117 Omnes quidem resurgent impassibiles et immortales et simul venient. Verum purgandi per ignem venient cum tremore et tamen non spe premii; perfecti vero cum exaltatione sine pena et certi de premio. Unde magister Anselmus sic hunc locum exponit: portantes tunc manipulos, fructum meritorum in spe. Hoc supplet: quam spem in proximo cognoscunt esse implendam in re certi de coronis, …36 (For everyone shall rise up removed from suffering and immortal, and they shall come at the same time. But they shall come trembling to be purged through fire and nevertheless not with hope of reward; but the perfect [shall come] with exultation, without punishment, and certain of reward. Wherefore master Anselm expounds this verse thus: carrying their sheaves, the fruit of their merits in hope. He adds this: which hope they know will be fulfilled in the near future, being certain in fact of their crowns, …)  As we see, the Chanter’s quotation of the Ordinaria-gloss includes a comment about hope (“fructum meritorum in spe”), but this topic is not present in either the version of the Basel edition or in the manuscripts. The closest thing to it that we find in the Ordinaria is a gloss taken from Cassiodorus, which reads, “Venientes. Hoc commune omnium, sed ad gaudium non nisi boni venient”37 (“Coming. This applies to everyone, but only the good shall come to joy”). This gloss does not appear in Pseudo-Haimo, but to say that this is what the Chanter has recast as “in spe” seems, in any case, as something of a stretch. The Chanter thus seems, in fact, to be attributing exegesis to Anselm which is found neither in Pseudo-Haimo nor in the Gloss. Interestingly, however, it happens that Gilbert P makes a point about the relation of the lemma to hope: … portantes manipulos suos, id est seminis fructum. Attende quod in veniendo non portant manipulos, quia in resurrectione fructum seminis sunt recepturi. Quia tamen iam spe salvi facti sumus, dicuntur iam portare et in exultatione venire.38  
                                                36 Cited in Landgraf, “Die Zuweisung,” 172. 37 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 125,7. (Laon, BM, 29, fol. 217v; Lund, UB, Med. 4, fol. 127r; Paris, BnF, lat. 105, fol. 176v; Paris, BnF, lat. 106, fol. 177v; Paris, BnF, lat. 442, fol. 160v; Paris, BnF, lat. 8846 deficit; Paris, BnF, lat. 14403 fol. 181r; Paris, BnF, lat. 17213, fol. 190r; Reims, BM, 150, fol. 240r; Troyes, BM, 511, fol. 174r; Troyes, BM, 881, fol. 158v; Troyes, BM, 924, fol. 155r; Troyes, BM, 925, fol. 209r; BAV, Vat. lat. 85, fol. 187v; BAV, Vat. lat. 86, fol. 164v.) Cf: “Omnibus enim uenire commune est; sed ad gaudium peruenire, beatorum.” (“Coming applies to everyone; but to arrive at joy is for the blessed.”) CASSIOD. in psalm. 125,6. (CCSL 98, 1171.) 38 GILB. PICT. in psalm. 125,7. (Paris, BnF, lat. 439, fol. 170r.) 

--- [p.125] ---

 
 
118 (… carrying their sheaves, that is, the fruit of their seed. Note that in coming they do not carry sheaves, since they will receive the fruit of their seed in the resurrection. But since we nevertheless are already made whole in hope, they are said to already carry [their sheaves] and to come in exultation.)  This may be slim evidence, but we may at least suggest that, if there was an interpretation of this lemma associated with Anselm and describing it as something not yet realized but existing in hope, then it appears to have been transmitted by other routes than the Gloss and Pseudo-Haimo, and that it is found in the work of Anselm’s former student. This could thus potentially be taken as another indication that Gilbert is a key figure in the transmission of Anselm’s exegesis on the Psalms. In Landgraf’s second example, any agreement between the Gloss and Pseudo-Haimo is harder to discern. The reference to Anselm is found in Peter of Poitiers’s commentary on Psalm 68:22, And they gave me gall for my food, and in my thirst they gave me vinegar to drink (Et dederunt in escam meam fel, et in siti mea potaverunt me aceto). Peter notes here that Anselm’s Gloss accords with Augustine:  In potum fieri, [non]39 in escam, scilicet loco potus, non loco escae. In Augustino sic est, scilicet “iam acceperat,” et in glosatura magistri Anselmi, quae brevis est, locum habuit, …40  (It is made into drink, [not] into food, namely, in place of drink, not in place of food. It is this way in Augustine, namely, “he had already received [food],” and [this gloss] had a place in the Gloss of master Anselm, which is brief, …)   The exegetical question at hand is how to match the psalm-verse’s “gall for my food” to the events surrounding Christ’s Passion, when the gospel states that he was given gall to drink (mixed with wine, according to Matthew),41 not to eat, as the psalm would have it. Augustine                                                 39 Landgraf’s emendation. 40 PETR. PICT. II dist. psalt. 68, 22. (Paris, BnF, lat. 14423, fol. 19v.) 41 Matt. 27:34. 

--- [p.126] ---

 
 
119 solves this by saying that the food of the verse mystically refers to the food which Christ had already received at the Passover meal, not to the gall that was offered to him on the cross, but that this gall was, in a sense, given “on top” of that food:42  Et dederunt in escam meam fel, et in siti mea potauerunt me aceto. Factum est quidem ad litteram, et euangelium hoc nobis indicat. … Namque legimus illi quidem oblatum fel, sicut euangelium loquitur; sed in potum, non in escam. Verumtamen sic accipiendum et impletum quod hic fuerat ante praedictum: Dederunt in escam meam fel, et in isto ipso facto, non solum in hoc dicto, mysterium requirere debemus, secreta pulsare, uelum templi conscissum intrare, uidere ibi sacramentum, uel quod ita dictum est, uel quod ita factum est. Dederunt, inquit, in escam meam fel. Non ipsum quod dederunt esca erat; potus enim erat; sed in escam dederunt; quia iam Dominus escam acceperat, et in ipsam iniectum est fel. Acceperat autem ipse escam suauem, quando pascha manducauit cum discipulis suis; ibi sacramentum sui corporis demonstrauit. In hanc escam tam suauem, tam dulcem unitatis Christi, … in hanc escam suauem quis est qui dat fel, nisi contradictores euangelii, tamquam illi persecutores Christi? Minus enim peccauerunt Iudaei crucifigentes in terra ambulantem, quam qui contemnunt in caelo sedentem. Quod ergo fecerunt Iudaei, ut in escam quam iam acceperat darent bibendum amarum illum potum, hoc faciunt qui male uiuendo scandalum inferunt ecclesiae: hoc faciunt haeretici amaricantes; … Dant fel super tam iucundum cibum. Sed quid facit Dominus? Non admittit ad corpus suum. Hoc sacramento ipse Dominus, quando illi obtulerunt fel, gustauit, et noluit bibere. Si non eos pateremur, nec omnino gustaremus; quia uero necesse est eos pati, necesse est gustari. Sed quia in membris Christi tales esse non possunt, gustari possunt, recipi in corpus non possunt.43 (And they gave me gall for my food, and in my thirst they gave me vinegar to drink. This was done literally, and the gospel indicates this to us. … For we read that gall was offered to him, as the gospel says, but [it was given to him] to drink, not to eat. Nevertheless, what had been predicted before should be understood and fulfilled here in this way: They gave me gall for my food: not only in this word, [but] also in this deed itself must we seek the mystery, knock at the secrets, enter through the torn veil of the temple, [and] see there the sacrament, either what is so said, or what is so done. They gave me gall, he says, for my food. The thing itself which they gave him was not food; for it was drink: but [it says] they gave for my food; because the Lord had already received the food, and gall was thrown onto it. For he had already received the delightful food when he ate the Passover meal with his disciples: there he showed the sacrament of his body. For this so delightful food, so sweet of the unity of Christ, … who gives gall as this delightful food, except the contradictors of the gospel, like persecutors of Christ? For the Jews who crucified him who walked the earth sinned less than those who spurn him who sits in heaven. Thus, what the Jews did, that they gave him that bitter drink to drink to the food which he had already received, this they do who by living poorly scandalize the Church:                                                 42 It seems that Augustine is taking advantage of the double sense of ‘in’ here, transferring it from the psalm’s meaning of “gall as food” to “gall onto/into the food.” 43 AVG. in psalm. 68,6. (CCSL 39, 921–922.) 

--- [p.127] ---

 
 
120 this do the embittering heretics do; … They put gall over such lovely food. But what does the Lord do? He does not admit it into his body. By this sacrament, the Lord himself tasted when they offered him gall, but he did not want to drink. If we did not suffer them, we would not taste [them] at all; but since it is necessary to suffer them, it is necessary [that they] be tasted. But since such people cannot be present in the body of Christ, they can be tasted, [but] they cannot be received into his body.)  When we look at the Gloss, we find that it, indeed, presents an abridged version of Augustine’s account: ¶ … Legitur in evangelio fel ei oblatum, sed in potum, non in escam. Mystice ergo dicitur, id est super escam suavem quam cum discipulis acceperat, quae est esca unitatis Christi, iniecerunt fel, quod adhuc facit, et gravius peccat contemptor sedentis in caelo, sicut44 qui crucifixit ambulantem in terra. Dant bibendum amarum potum, qui male vivendo faciunt scandalum Ecclesiae, ut haeretici. Gustat Dominus docens nos gustare, id est pati tales, sed non bibit, quia tales non possunt in corpus eius recipi. Mystice ergo hic praedicitur, sicut factum est mystice in passione.45 (¶ … It is written in the gospel that gall was given to him, but to drink, not to eat. This is therefore said mystically, that is, over the delightful food which he had received with the disciples, which is the food of the unity of Christ, they threw gall, which he does still, and he who spurns the one who sits in the sky sins more gravely than the one who crucified him who walked the earth. Those who, like the heretics, scandalize the Church by living poorly give a bitter drink to drink. The Lord tastes and teaches us to taste, that is, to suffer such people, but he does not drink, because such people cannot be received into his body. This is, therefore, said mystically here, as it was done mystically in the Passion.)  Nothing that looks recognizably like the Augustinian passage is, however, found in Pseudo-Haimo, who expounds the lemma in the following way: Et dederunt in escam meam fel, quoniam non aliam escam vel potum dederunt, nisi acetum cum felle mixtum. Et hoc figura fuit; cum enim ipse sitiret salutem eorum, non habuit refectionem ex eis, sed exhibuerunt ei amaros et diros mores.46 
                                                44 sicut] quam Paris, BnF, lat. 106, Paris, BnF, lat. 442, Paris, BnF, lat. 17213, Reims, BM, 150 45 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 68,22. (Laon, BM, 29, 117v; Lund, UB, Med. 4 deficit; Paris, BnF, lat. 105, fol. 85r; Paris, BnF, lat. 106, fol. 93v; Paris, BnF, lat. 442, fol. 81v; Paris, BnF, lat. 8846, fol. 119r; Paris, BnF, lat. 14403 fol. 92v; Paris, BnF, lat. 17213, fol. 97rv; Reims, BM, 150, fol. 125r; Troyes, BM, 511, fol. 89r; Troyes, BM, 881, fol. 80r–80v; Troyes 924, fol. 81v; Troyes, BM, 925, fol. 110r; BAV, Vat. lat. 85, fol. 95v; BAV, Vat. lat. 86, fol. 88r.) 46 (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. 68,22. (Troyes, BM, 904, fol. 73v; PL Database vol. 116, col. 425CD.) 

--- [p.128] ---

 
 
121 (And they gave me gall for my food, since they did not give any other food or drink except vinegar mixed with gall. And this was a figure of speech; for when he thirsted for their salvation, he did not receive refreshment from them, but they showed him their bitter and dire ways.)  Pseudo-Haimo’s exegesis comes, instead, from Cassiodorus, who says: Ille enim dixit, Sitio,47 quia fidem in ipsis desideratam non poterat inuenire. Isti obtulerunt amarissimos mores suos, qui nulla compunctione conuersi sunt.48  (For he said, I am thirsty, since he had not been able to find in them the desired faith. They, who were not moved by any remorse, offered him their bitterest ways.)  This Cassiodorean gloss is, however, also present in the Gloss: ¶ Et dederunt. Haec est consummatio passionis: cum dixisset, Sitio, fel cum aceto obtulerunt, hoc facto mores suos amarissimos indicantes49 (¶ And they gave. This is the fulfilment of the Passion: when he had said, I am thirsty, they offered him gall with vinegar, showing, in doing this, their bitterest ways.)  Even if it is thus clear that Peter of Poitiers cannot be referring to Pseudo-Haimo as such, but must have had the Gloss (or some version of it) in front of him, this does not preclude the possibility that there exists some form of kinship between these two works: we may note that they do pick some of the very same patristic excerpts. As for the nature of this relationship, however, we may note that Pseudo-Haimo is not likely to have been the Ordinaria’s source for the Cassiodorean excerpt here, as the latter is closer to Cassiodorus’s original, retaining his ‘obtulerunt’ where Pseudo-Haimo has ‘exhibuerunt’. More information is needed in order to determine the exact nature of the relationship between the two commentaries.                                                 47 John 19:28. 48 CASSIOD. in psalm. 68,22. (CCSL 97, 616.) 49 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 68,22. (Laon, BM, 29, 117v; Lund, UB, Med. 4 deficit; Paris, BnF, lat. 105, fol. 84v; Paris, BnF, lat. 106, fol. 93v; Paris, BnF, lat. 442, fol. 81v; Paris, BnF, lat. 8846, fol. 119r; Paris, BnF, lat. 14403 fol. 92v; Paris, BnF, lat. 17213, fol. 97r; Reims, BM, 150, fol. 125r; Troyes, BM, 511, fol. 89r; Troyes, BM, 881, fol. 80r; Troyes 924, fol. 81v; Troyes, BM, 925, fol. 110r; BAV, Vat. lat. 85, fol. 95v; BAV, Vat. lat. 86, fol. 88r.) 

--- [p.129] ---

 
 
122 In the case of Landgraf’s last example of a twelfth-century in-text reference to Anselm, which comes from Peter the Chanter’s commentary on Psalm 24:19, Consider my enemies for they are multiplied, and have hated me with an unjust hatred (Respice inimicos meos, quoniam multiplicati sunt, et odio iniquo oderunt me), we do not, as Landgraf rightly notes, find any exegetical agreement between the Gloss and Pseudo-Haimo. However, in this case we find that the reference to Anselm is, in fact, mistaken, and that the material in question comes, instead from Gilbert P.  What the Chanter reports, according to the manuscript used by Landgraf, namely Paris, Mazarine, lat. 176, is the following:  Deinde pro inimicis orat dicens: respice, id est converte, inimicos meos. Et quia nescimus, quid oremus, sic[ut] oportet,50 et pro quibus sit orandum, potest talis esse sensus, dicit magister Petrus Lombardus: respice inimicos meos, id est fac de eis sicut scis. Sed cum sciat reprobos esse dampnandos, ergo orat, ut dampnentur. Igitur melius legitur hoc a magistro Anselmo sic: respice inimicos meos, converte, et quia nescimus, qui sint reprobi et qui non, indeffinite oro sic: respice inimicos meos, id est vel converte vel fac de eis, sicut scis, de quibus aliqui convertendi sunt.51 (Then he prays for his enemies, saying: consider, that is convert, my enemies. And because we know not what we should pray, as we ought, and for whom one should pray, the meaning, says master Peter Lombard, may be such: Consider my enemies, that is, do about them as you know.52 But since he knows that the reprobate should be damned, he consequently prays that they be damned. Therefore, this is better interpreted by master Anselm in this way: consider my enemies; convert [them], and because we do not know who is a reprobate and who is not, I pray indefinitely thus: consider my enemies, that is, either convert [them], or do about them as you know, about those of whom some should be converted.)  The Chanter is contrasting Lombard’s interpretation of the lemma53 with, as he claims, Anselm’s: his issue with the former seems to be that the interpretation “do about them as you                                                 50 Rom. 8:26. 51 Cited in Landgraf, “Die Zuweisung,” 172. 52 This is a very literal translation: the sense is something more like “act according to your knowledge.” 53 “Deinde pro inimicis orat, dicens: Et etiam respice, id est converte, inimicos meos. Vel quia nescimus quid oremus, sicut oportet, potest esse talis sensus; respice inimicos meos, id est fac de eis sicut scis, …” (“Then he 

--- [p.130] ---

 
 
123 know” seems to become a request to damn certain people, since, like God, the Psalmist himself knows that the wicked are to be damned. Hence, Peter prefers the interpretation which recognizes a certain uncertainty about who is to be damned, saying that the request to “do about them as you know” is made indefinitely. When we look at the Ordinaria, however, we find only the following gloss: respice inimicos meos/ converte vel fac sicut scis54 (consider my enemies/ convert [them] or do as you know)  Although this certainly looks as if it corresponds to what is reported as Anselm’s gloss, the picture changes when we compare this with Gilbert P: Respice. Orat pro inimicis, sed quia nescimus quid oremus, indefinite dicit: Respice inimicos meos, vel converte, vel fac sicut scis, quoniam multiplicati sunt ...55                                                 prays for his enemies, saying: And also consider, that is convert, my enemies. Or, because we know not what we should pray, as we ought, the meaning may be such: Consider my enemies, that is, do about them as you know, …”) PETR. LOMB. psalm. 24,9. (PL Database vol. 191, col. 259CD.) We may note that the phrase “et pro quibus sit orandum” (“and for whom one should pray”) is not present in Lombard’s text, but that it is, however, found in the margin of Herbert of Bosham’s edition of Lombard’s work, which Herbert completed sometime before 1176. (Cambridge, Trinity College, B.5.4, fol. 69r.) This raises an interesting question about the influence of Herbert’s edition on the transmission of Lombards commentary—a question that cannot, however, be answered within the scope of this thesis, except to say that the phrase does not appear in any of the 22 manuscripts of the Magna that I have been able to consult so far. We may also note that, despite reproducing most of Gilbert’s account verbatim, Lombard ignores his clarification of the (potentially) magisterial gloss (see 123n54), and sticks with the Ordinaria’s original.  54 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 24,19. (Laon, BM, 29, fol. 35v; Lund, UB, Med. 4 deficit; Paris, BnF, lat. 105, fol. 27v; Paris, BnF, lat. 106, fol. 30r; Paris, BnF, lat. 442, fol. 27v; Paris, BnF, lat. 8846, fol. 43r; Paris, BnF, lat. 14403 fol. 31v; Paris, BnF, lat. 17213, fol. 34v; Reims, BM, 150, fol. 42v; Troyes, BM, 511, fol. 29r; Troyes, BM, 881, fol. 31v; Troyes, BM; 924, fol. 27r; Troyes, BM, 925, fol. 40r; BAV, Vat. lat. 85, fol. 33r; BAV, Vat. lat. 86, fol. 32r [id est dampna eos sup. lin. add.].) In this instance, the Basel edition cited by Landgraf agrees with the manuscripts, except in that it adds patristic source references to each of the two parts of the gloss: “Cassiodorus: Converte, vel Augustinus: fac sicut scis.” Cited in Landgraf, “Die Zuweisung,” 172. The first part of the disjunction is, indeed, from Cassiodorus: “Respice inimicos meos, pro ipsis orat, ut redeant, quia quos ille respicit, sine dilatione conuertit, sicut in euangelio Petrum Dominus respexit ut fleret [cf. Luke 22:61–62].” (“Consider my enemies. He prays for them, so that they should return, because he converts without delay those whom he considers [lit. “upon whom he looks back”], like in the gospel [where] he looked back upon Peter so that he wept.”) CASSIOD. in psalm. 24,19. (CCSL 97, 227–228.) The Basel edition gives Augustine as the source of the second part of the gloss, (as does the manuscript Admont, SB, 36 of Lombard’s Magna), but it is not from his Psalms-commentary. The Gloss-manuscripts do not give any references for this snippet. Pseudo-Haimo is not the source of the Gloss here, for it contains neither of these snippets in any form. The gloss may thus be magisterial. 55 GILB. PICT. in psalm. 24,19. (Paris, BnF, lat. 439, fol. 30rv.) 

--- [p.131] ---

 
 
124 (Consider. He prays for his enemies, but since we know not what we should pray, he says it indefinitely: Consider my enemies, either convert [them] or do as you know, for they are multiplied ...)  As we see, Gilbert is the one who specifies that the request is made indefinitely. As it happens, the manuscript Paris, BnF, lat. 12011 presents a completely different version of the Chanter’s text, correctly identifying the source of the above gloss as Gilbert: Respice inimicos meos, id est converte eos. Vel: Quia nescimus quid oremus, sicut oportet, fac sicut scis. Sed secundum hoc videtur quod non est orandum pro inimicis. Unde expressius dicit glosa magistri Gilleberti sic: Respice inimicos meos, id est converte eos. Sed quia nescimus quid oremus, sicut oportet, ideo dixit inimicos indefinite, et non dixit “omnes.” Si enim sciremus qui essent reprobi, numquam oraremus pro eis.56 (Consider my enemies, that is, convert them. Or: Because we know not what we should pray, as we ought, do as you know. But according to this, it seems as if one should not pray for one’s enemies. Wherefore master Gilbert’s gloss says it more clearly thus: Consider my enemies, that is, convert them. But because we know not what we should pray, as we ought, therefore, [the Psalmist] says it indefinitely, and does not say “all.” For if we knew who was a reprobate, we would never pray for them.)  Without any critical edition in existence, we are in no position to determine which reading is original to the Chanter. A possibility is, of course, that the two texts correspond to two different reportationes of the Chanter’s lectures,57 or that the Chanter revised his original upon the discovery of the true source of the modified interpretation. (‘Anselm’ would certainly be the lectio difficilior—the mistake easily explainable by virtue of the gloss’s similarity to the Ordinaria.) Either way, we may conclude that Anselm was sometimes credited with what was, in fact, Gilbert’s work, and that this reference to Anselm does nothing to exclude Pseudo-Haimo from potentially being Anselm’s work. 
                                                56 PETR. CANTOR psalt. 24,9. (Paris, BnF, lat. 12011, fol. 42v.) 57 We may note that the two manuscripts of the Chanter’s commentary conceive of the exegetical issue in slightly different ways: whereas the Mazarine manuscript frames the problem with the interpretation “do as you know” as its seeming to be request that God damn certain people, the text of the BnF manuscript objects that the interpretation makes it seem as if one should not pray for one’s enemies, which contradicts the main interpretation of the lemma.  

--- [p.132] ---

 
 
125 In sum, while Landgraf’s examples thus do, indeed, show that it was clearly the Gloss on the Psalms rather than Pseudo-Haimo that the later twelfth century viewed as the work of Anselm of Laon, a closer examination of these passages—particularly of the Gloss as presented in the manuscripts rather than the printed edition—also shows that the two have more in common than Landgraf would have us believe: not only is there a certain amount of overlap in their use of material, there is also some evidence that their respective use of the authoritative sources is not independent of each other. As the Gloss displays a closer resemblance to the patristic originals, it does not seem that Pseudo-Haimo could have been the Gloss’s source for this material. This does not, however, preclude the possibility that the two rely on a shared source or that such a source could be associated in some way with Anselm. In addition to these results, Landgraf’s passages provide evidence for some of the suggestions that have been put forward in the previous chapters: they show that the Gloss had already by the late twelfth century been interpolated with material from Lombard’s Magna glosatura, and, most interestingly, we find more indications that the work of Gilbert of Poitiers seems to be particularly tied to the transmission of Psalms-exegesis associated with Anselm, the exact nature of whose contribution has not yet been securely established.  Most importantly, however, none of the above conclusions mean that Pseudo-Haimo could not be linked in some more or less direct way to Anselm and his original Psalms-exegesis, especially in light of our growing suspicion that Anselm may not have been directly involved in the compilation of the Gloss as we have it, but that Gilbert, on the other hand, may have. As the question of the nature of Anselm’s involvement in the Gloss remains unanswered, a fuller examination of the various agreements between our continuous commentaries is needed. We turn, therefore, to look at Pseudo-Haimo’s relationship to some other twelfth-century Psalms-commentaries and some of the suggestions made for these.  

--- [p.133] ---

 
 
126 OBJECTIONS TO WILMART’S THEORY: VAN DEN EYNDE Wilmart’s suggestion was challenged again in 1954, this time by Damien Van den Eynde, whose aim was to solve the “rather intricate problem concerning the literary connexions and the chronological succession”58 of the group of supposedly late eleventh-/early twelfth-century Psalms-commentaries which contain in their exegesis of Psalm 1:1 the Augustinian expression ‘regio dissimilitudinis’ (“the area of dissimilitude”). As this expression is found not in Augustine’s Psalms-commentary, but rather in his Confessions,59 Van den Eynde, very reasonably, believes that its appearance in these twelfth-century commentaries’ exegesis of Psalm 1:1 is not a fortuitous coincidence, but that it places them in a relationship of some form of dependence upon each other. The commentaries that include the expression are the Gloss, Gilbert P, Lombard’s Magna, Gilbert U, Pseudo-Bede, the commentary spuriously printed under the name of Bruno of Würtzburg in volume 142 of the PL,60 and, finally, Pseudo-Haimo.61  Van den Eynde’s principal reason for denying Anselm the authorship of Pseudo-Haimo is his belief that the commentary dates to sometime around 1140, over two decades after Anselm’s death. One of his main arguments for the chronology he wants to establish is that some of the works in the group—among them, Pseudo-Haimo—borrow material from the Psalms-commentary of Letbert of Lille, who began his ecclesiastical career as a canon at Lille and was abbot of the Augustinian house of St. Ruf from 1100 until his death in 1110. His commentary is 
                                                58 Van den Eynde, “Literary Note,” 122. 59 AVG conf. 7:10. (CCSL 27, 103.) (See Chapter Two, 97n113.) 60 (PS.) BRUNO HERB. psalm. (PL Database vol. 142, cols 49–530.)  61 Gilbert P and Pseudo-Haimo differ somewhat in their renderings of the expression, but are clearly still related to the group: Gilbert has “in regionem deserti.” GILB. PICT. in psalm. 1,1. (Paris, BnF, lat. 439, fol. 1v.) Pseudo-Haimo has “per dissimilitudinem” (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. 1,1. (Troyes, BM, 904, fol. 1v; PL Database vol. 116, col. 197B.) 

--- [p.134] ---

 
 
127 printed by Migne under the name of the fourth-century theologian Rufinus of Aquileia but was restored to Letbert, once and for all, by Wilmart.62  In the majority of the twenty-four manuscripts listed by Wilmart, Letbert’s commentary is prefaced by a letter written in 1125 by Walter, bishop of Maguelonne, presenting the work to the canons at Lille.63 Walter describes here how he is responding to the request of one of the canons, who during a previous visit of Walter’s to Lille had asked him eagerly about the commentary which he had heard that his former co-canon had compiled. The prevalence of the prefatory letter in the surviving manuscripts leads Wilmart to believe that Walter’s copy is the point of departure for the entire tradition of Letbert’s commentary.64  Following Wilmart’s theory, Van den Eynde states that anyone who has borrowed from Letbert’s commentary must have done so after 1125. This, however, is only an assumption. As Wilmart notes, not all manuscripts contain the letter, and we may also point out that knowledge of Letbert’s commentary clearly had reached Lille well before 1125.65 The only chronological reference point that is certain is thus the commentary’s terminus ante quem of 1110. Both Wilmart and Van den Eynde speak of the success enjoyed by Letbert’s commentary, as evidenced not only by the number of extant manuscripts, but also by its apparent influence on other Psalms-commentators. In arguing for his chronology, however, Van den Eynde makes an odd point: while granting that Letbert’s commentary was published after the Gloss—based, of course, on the assumption that the latter is the work of Anselm, who died in 1117—he speaks of                                                 62 André Wilmart, “Le commentaire sur les Psaumes imprimé sous le nom de Rufin,” Revue bénédictine 31 (1914−1919): 258−276. Seventeen of the twenty-four manuscripts listed by Wilmart are incomplete, most of them preserving only the first half of the work. 63 See Wilmart, “Le commentaire imprimé,” 267–274. 64 Wilmart, “Le commentaire imprimé,” 261–262, and 261n1. Contrary to previous suggestions, Wilmart does not believe that Letbert began writing his commentary at Lille, but during his time at St. Ruf (see “Le commentaire imprimé,” 263). 65 As Walter explains why he has not been able to fulfil the canon’s request sooner, we may infer that some time must have passed between his original visit and the completion of the edition.  

--- [p.135] ---

 
 
128 “a moment [meaning 1130–1140] when Letbert’s work, though still outshining all the others, had already found a dangerous competitor in Anselm of Laon,”66 even though the latter work had, presumably, been circulating for over a decade. But if the time between 1125 and 1140 was, indeed, one when Letbert’s was the commentary exerting the most influence on other Psalms-commentators and had not yet found any competition from the Gloss, what we would have here is, rather, another argument against Anselm’s authorship of the latter work. In any case, Van den Eynde concludes, based on this timeline, that Pseudo-Haimo contains too much of Letbert and too little of the Gloss to date to any later than the 1150s.67 At the same time, he does not want to push Pseudo-Haimo back too far, because of its mention of Guibertus demens; 40-plus years after the anti-pope’s death appears to be his limit. Due to a certain resemblance of one of its passages to Lombard’s Magna, he settles for some time between 1130 and 1150, but probably shortly after 1140.68 This is, of course, possible, but even if this dating of Pseudo-Haimo should turn out to be correct, thus precluding Anselm’s authorship of this particular commentary, this does not mean that the commentary could not still be tapping into an Anselmian stream of exegesis which is also related to, but separate from, the Gloss.  Relevant to this idea is Van den Eynde’s observation that Pseudo-Haimo shares an identical passage with Pseudo-Bede, namely the part of their respective prologues that explains what prophecy is: while their accounts are modelled on Cassiodorus, they display shared adaptations which show that they are not getting their material directly from the original, but that their renderings are related to each other in some way; agreements with Cassiodorus are in bold and agreements between Pseudo-Haimo and Pseudo-Bede are underlined:  
                                                66 Van den Eynde, “Literary Note,” 132. 67 Van den Eynde, “Literary Note,” 138. 68 Van den Eynde, “Literary Note,” 139.

129 Cassiodorus (C1a) Prophetia est aspiratio diuina, quae euentus rerum, (C1b) aut per facta (C1c) aut per dicta quorumdam (C1d) immobili ueritate pronuntiat. (C2) De qua bene quidam dixit: Prophetia est suauis dictio caelestis doctrinae fauos et dulcia diuini eloquii mella componens. … (C4) Multis autem modis gratiae istius munera praestabantur. (C5) Sed, ut de plurimis pauca complectar, (C6a) acta est per operationes hominum, (C6b) ut fuit arca Noe (C6c) et sacrificium Abrahae (C6d) et transitus maris Rubri; (C6e) per natiuitates quoque geminorum Esau et Iacob, qui futurarum rerum sacramenta gestabant; (C7a) per angelos, (C7b) sicut locuti sunt Abrahae (C7c) uel Loth, (C7d) Zachariae (C7e) et Mariae; (C8a) per uisiones, (C8b) sicut Isaiae (C8c) et Ezechielo, (C8d) et ceteris sanctis; (C9a) per somnia, (C9b) sicut Salomoni (C9c) et Danielo; (C9d) per nubem et uocem de caelo, sicut Moysi.69 (Prophecy is the divine breath which proclaims with immovable truth the unfolding of events either through the deeds or the words of certain people. Someone appropriately said about it: Prophecy is the sweet utterance which brings together the honey-combs of heavenly teaching and the sweet honey of divine speech. … The gifts of that grace are offered in many ways. But to include a few of the many, it is done through the actions of men, as Noah’s ark was, and Abraham’s sacrifice, and the crossing of the Red Sea; also through the births of the twins Esau and Jacob, who carried the sacraments of the things to come; through the angels, as they spoke to Abraham, or to Loth, Zacharias, and Mary; through visions, as [it happened] to Isaiah and Ezechiel, and the other holy men; through dreams, as [it happened] to Salomon and Daniel; through a cloud and voice from the sky, as [it happened] to Moses.)  Ps.-Haimo (PH11) Diffinitur autem prophetia sic: (PH12a/C1a) Prophetia est divina inspiratio quae rerum eventus, (PH12b) aut per visiones, (PH12c/C1b) aut per facta, (PH12d/C1c) aut per quorumdam dicta (PH12e/C1d) immutabili veritate demonstrat. (PH13a/C8a) Per visiones, (PH13b/C8c) ut Ezechieli (PH13c) et beato Iohanni in exstasi positis. (PH14a/C6a) Per facta, (PH14b/C6c) ut Abrahae, per immolationem arietis; (PH14c/C6e) et Iacob, (PH14d) qui transpositis manibus Manassen et Ephraim filios Ioseph benedixit. (PH15a/C9d) Per dicta, ut in Moyse de Christo legitur: (PH15b) Suscitabo de fratribus vestris prophetam, tanquam me ipsum audite;70 (PH15c) vel in Psalterio.71 (For prophecy is defined thus: Prophecy is the divine inspiration which shows with immutable truth the unfolding of events either through visions, or through deeds, or through certain people’s words. Through visions, as [it happened] to Ezechiel and blessed John, who were placed in ecstasy. Through deeds, as [it happened] to Abraham, through the sacrifice of the ram, 
                                                69 CASSIOD. in psalm. Praefatio I. (CCSL vol. 97, 7–25.)  70 Cf. Deut. 18:18. 71 (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. 1, acces. (Troyes, BM, 904, fol. 1r; PL Database vol. 116, col. 195BC.)

130 and Jacob, who, after having crossed his hands, blessed Ephraim and Manasses, the sons of Joseph. Through words, as we read about Christ in Moses: I will raise up a prophet from your brethren, hear him as if [he were] myself; or in the Psalter.)  Ps.-Bede (PB1) Definitur autem sic prophetia: (PB2a/C1a) prophetia est divina inspiratio quae rerum eventus (PB2b) aut per visiones (PB2c/C1b) aut per facta, (PB2d/C1c) aut per quorumdam dicta (PB2e/C1d) immutabili veritate demonstrat. (PB3a/C8a) Per visiones, (PB3b/C8c) ut Ezechieli, (PB3c/C9c) Danieli, (PB3d) et beato Iohanni in exstasi positis; (PB3e) divina inspiratio per quasdam imagines futura praemonstravit. (PB4a/C6a) Per facta, (PB4b/C6c) ut in immolatione Abraham, (PB4c) qui monitus a Deo proprium immolare filium, nec dubitans … (PB5a/C6e) Potest quoque prophetia per facta in Jacob accipi, (PB5b) qui transpositis manibus Manassen et Ephraim filios Joseph benedixit, dexteram superponendo Ephraim minori, sinistram vero superponendo … (PB6a/C9d) Prophetia autem per dicta est, ut quod in Moyse de Christo legitur: (PB6b) Suscitabo de fratribus vestris prophetam, tanquam me ipsum audite; et ut Psalterium, …72 (For prophecy is defined thus: Prophecy is the divine inspiration which shows with immutable truth the unfolding of events either through visions, or through deeds, or through certain people’s words. Through visions, as [it happened] to Ezechiel, Daniel, and blessed John, who were placed in ecstasy; divine inspiration foretold the future through certain images. Through deeds, as Abraham in the sacrifice, who having been exhorted by God to sacrifice his own son, and not doubting … Prophecy can also be understood through deeds in Jacob, who, after having crossed his hands, blessed Ephraim and Manasses, the sons of Joseph, by placing his right hand on Ephraim, the younger, and placing his left … And prophecy is through words, as what we read about Christ in Moses: I will raise up a prophet from your brethren, hear him as if [he were] myself; and as the Psalter.)  We see here that the two commentaries display more or less the exact same adaptation of Cassiodorus. Their differences include the fact that Pseudo-Bede adds some extensive explanation of the biblical examples (not included in full above), which leads Van den Eynde to believe that he must be using Pseudo-Haimo rather than vice versa.73 There are, however, a few small details which indicate that they may, instead, both be getting this adaptation from a shared 
                                                72 PS. BEDA psalm., Praef. (PL Database vol. 93, col. 479CD.) 73 Van den Eynde, “Literary Note,” 142. 

--- [p.138] ---

 
 
131 source: for one, we may note that they both add “through visions” to Cassiodorus’s initial enumeration of the ways in which prophecy happens (PH12b/PB2b). In Pseudo-Bede, there is no obvious reason for this addition; in Pseudo-Haimo, however, the passage is preceded by an alternative explanation of prophecy, also containing several of the Cassiodorean elements, but which defines it overall as a vision:  (PH1a) Prophetia idem est quod visio: (PH1b) unde in libro Regum prophetam Samuelem Saul videntem appellat dicens: Ubi est domus videntis?74 (PH2) Non tamen quaelibet visio prophetia dicitur, sed ea tantum quae a solo Spiritu Sancto ministratur; (PH3a) ea fit de praeterito, (PH3b) ut Moyses: In principio creavit Deus caelum et terram;75 (PH4a) vel de praesenti, (PH4b) ut si quis per spiritum ea in76 praesentia videat quae per naturam videre non posset, (PH4c) ut spiritus Ezechielis transferebatur in Ierusalem, ut videret ea quae ibi fiebant; (PH5a) de futuro, (PH5b) ut haec eadem prophetia.  (PH6a/C9a) Inspiratur autem prophetia vel per somnium, (PH6b) ut Ioseph; (PH7a/C7a) vel per angelum, (PH7b/C7d) ut Zachariae; (PH8a/C10b) vel per internam inspirationem,77 (PH8b) ut haec eadem, sciliect psalterium; (PH9a) vel per figuras, (PH9b/C6b) ut Noe per archam quam rexit in fluctu diluvii figuravit Ecclesiam a Christo per mundi pericula regendam.78 (Prophecy is the same thing as a vision: whence, in the Book of Kings, Saul calls the prophet Samuel a seer, saying: Where is the house of the seer? However, not just any vision is a prophecy, but only that which is delivered by the Holy Spirit alone; this is about the past, as [in the case of] Moses: In the beginning God created heaven and earth; or about the present, as when someone sees through the spirit those things in the present which he cannot see through nature, like [when] Ezechiel’s spirit was transported to Jerusalem, so that he saw those things which transpired there; [or] about the future, like this very prophecy. And prophecy is instilled either through a dream, as [in the case of] Joseph; or through an angel, as [in the case of] Zacharias; or through internal inspiration, like this very [prophecy], that is, the Psalter; or through signs, as Noah guiding his ark on the wave of the flood signified that the Church would be directed through the perils of this world by Christ.)  
                                                74 1 Kings 9:18. 75 Gen. 1:1. 76 ea in] Dei Troyes, BM, 904 77 inspirationem] conspirationem Troyes, BM, 904 78 (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. 1, acces. (Troyes, BM, 904, fol. 1r; PL Database vol. 116, col. 195BC.) 

--- [p.139] ---

 
 
132 In other words, we find in Pseudo-Haimo an explanation for why “through visions” has been added to the Cassiodorean list of modes. This indicates that Pseudo-Bede is, indeed, not Pseudo-Haimo’s source for the Cassiodorean adaptation. At the same time, however, we find one Cassiodorean element in Pseudo-Bede which is not present in Pseudo-Haimo, namely the mention of Daniel as an example of prophecy through visions (PB3c/C9c; although, in Cassiodorus, Daniel serves as an example of prophecy through dreams, which is not included by either Pseudo-Haimo or Pseudo-Bede). This thus raises the question whether there may have been some other intermediary source which included all of these elements, from which our two commentators may have borrowed their respective accounts. The objections to Van den Eynde’s arguments raised by Valerie Flint will give us an opportunity to examine this possibility further.  OBJECTIONS TO THE OBJECTIONS TO WILMART’S THEORY: FLINT In an article of 1971, Flint raised a number of objections to Van den Eynde’s arguments against Wilmart’s theory. As our current objective is not, however, to prove that Pseudo-Haimo is itself Anselm’s work, those arguments that concern Van den Eynde’s dating of this commentary need not concern us here; we may simply note that they are all based on his perception of what constitutes “doctrinal development” and can easily be refuted, as Flint also succeeds in doing.79                                                 79 Van den Eynde makes two arguments, both based on our commentators’ renderings of Cassiodorean definitions: the first is Cassiodorus’s definition of prophecy, “Prophecy is the divine breath which proclaims with immutable truth the unfolding of events either through the deeds or the words of certain people” (see above). Van den Eynde thinks this definition problematic, as “the restriction aut per facta aut per dicta seems to exclude from [it] the highest kind of prophecy, namely, the coelestis inspiratio proper to the Psalter.” (Van den Eynde, “Literary Note,” 136.) The two ways in which he thinks that our twelfth-century commentators improve upon Cassiodorus is by either 1) cancelling the words “aut per facta aut per dicta” from the definition proper, thus making it broader (Gilbert P), or 2), specifying, while retaining the original definition, that the Psalms count as dicta (Pseudo-Haimo and Pseudo-Bede). Of these two solutions, he thinks that Pseudo-Haimo’s is the later one. However, it is not true that Gilbert cancels out the specification “aut per facta aut per dicta,” he simply breaks it up and moves it: “(GP2a/C1a) Prophetia est inspiratio divina quae rerum eventus (GP2b/C1d) immobili veritate pronuntiat. … (GP3a/C1c) Fit autem per dicta, (GP3b/C7a) ut angelis (GP3c/C7b) loquentibus Abrahae, (GP3d/C7c) Loth, (GP3e/C7d) Zachariae, (GP3f/C7e) Mariae, (GP4a/C1b) et per facta, (GP4b/C6b) ut per arcam Noae, (GP4c/C6c) et per 

--- [p.140] ---

 
 
133 More relevant to our purposes, is, however, that Flint also makes the case that both Pseudo-Haimo and Pseudo-Bede were used by Honorius of Autun, whose Psalms-commentary has been dated to sometime between 1115 and 1120 (or at least to before 1126).80  After examining a few shared phrases and sections from their respective prologues and expositions of Psalm 1, Flint concludes that, although “Honorius and the Pseudo-Haimo … both seem to owe something to the Gloss, … their resemblance to each other is the more striking                                                 sacrificium Abrahae, (GP4d/C6e) per nativitates Esau et Iacob.” (“Prophecy is the divine inspiration which proclaims with immutable truth the unfolding of events. It happens through words, as with the angels who spoke to Abraham, to Loth, to Zacharias, [and] to Mary, and through deeds, as through Noah’s ark, and through Abraham’s sacrifice, through the births of Esau and Jacob.”) GILB. PICT. in psalm., praef. (Paris, BnF, lat. 439, fol. 1r.) Just as Cassiodorus himself adds “inspiration through the spirit alone”—that is, the type of prophecy constituted by the Psalms—after having explained the “regular” types of prophecy, so Gilbert, too, adds this at the end: “(GP7a) Fit etiam inspiratio cessantibus dictis et factis, (GP7b) solo sancto Spiritu interius illustrante, (GP7c) ut David, …” (“Inspiration also happens when words and deeds cease, with only the Holy Spirit illuminating from within, like David, …”) Ibid. Either way, there is nothing in the two commentators’ handling of Cassiodorus’s definition that proves that one is later than the other. Van den Eynde’s second claim of doctrinal development takes its departure in Cassiodorus’s definition at Psalm 1:2 of ‘lex Domini’ (“the law of the Lord”), which reads, “Lex ergo Domini est in declinandis peccatis sancta praeceptio. … Lex enim dicitur ex eo quod animos nostros liget.” (“The law of the Lord is thus the sacred mandate to turn away sin. … It is called law due to the fact that it binds our souls.”) CASSIOD. in psalm. 1,2. (CCSL 97, 32.) The first commentator to pick up these phrases is, Van den Eynde believes, Gilbert P, who writes, “Lex est in declinandis peccatis sancta praeceptio; lex dicta quod liget.” GILB. PICT. in psalm. 1,2. (Paris, BnF, lat. 439, fols 1v–2r.) Pseudo-Haimo also contains the definition, but makes a small addition to Cassiodorus’s phrase, writing, “Lex enim Dei est in devitandis peccatis bonisque perpetrandis sancta praeceptio.” (“For God’s law is the sacred mandate to avoid sin and do good.”) (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. 1,2. (Troyes, BM, 904, fol. 2r; PL Database vol. 116, col. 198C.) According to Lottin, this definition became in the second half of the twelfth century one of the classical definitions of ‘law’. (Lottin, Psychologie et morale, 2:13n5. The definition Lottin cites reads, “Lex est in declinandis vitiis et sectandis virtutibus sancta praescriptio.” He notes that he has not been able to identify the author of this definition.) Van den Eynde thus concludes that Pseudo-Haimo, once again, comes at the end of an evolution. (Van den Eynde, “Literary Note,” 138.) The problem, however, with using an argument of the type “person x said z before person y” to establish the chronology between person x and person y is that it assumes that you know that person x is anterior to person y, which is, of course, circular. It is therefore relevant to note here Flint’s observation that the notion that avoiding sin is not enough for being in the law of the Lord, but that one must also do good, is found also in Honorius’s Psalms-commentary, which is dated to sometime between 1115 and 1120 (see below): “Lex Domini est mala devitare et bona facere.” (“The law of the Lord is to avoid evil and do good.”) HONOR. AUG. psalm. 1,2. (PL Database vol. 172, col. 267B.) This idea was not, in fact, new in the twelfth century: the seed of it is present already in Cassiodorus, who further on in his exposition on Psalm 1:2 writes, “quia sancto proposito non sufficiebat tantum bona velle, nisi etiam desiderium suum continuo pioque labore satiaret” (“since only wanting good is not enough for the sacred injunction unless [the person] also fulfils his desire through continuous and pious work”) CASSIOD. in psalm. 1,2. (CCSL 97, 32.) And the short version of Remigius makes the connection between doing good and being in the law of the Lord even stronger: “Discenda est ergo lex Dei meditando, et bonis operibus exequendo” (“The law of God, therefore, should be learned through reflection and through following [it] by good works”) REMIG. in psalm.I 1, 2. (Reims, BM, 132, fol. 1v; St. Gallen, SB, Cod. Sang. 312, p. 4.) It is thus clear that the arguments presented by Van den Eynde for claiming the relative lateness of Pseudo-Haimo based on doctrinal development are not well-founded. 80 See Hermann Menhardt, “Der Nachlass des Honorius Augustodunensis,” Zeitschrift für deutsches Altertum und deutsche Literatur 89 (1958): 23–69 (at p. 68). Menhardt gives the commentary a terminus ante quem of at least 1126. (Menhardt, “Der Nachlass,” 65.) 

--- [p.141] ---

 
 
134 one,”81 and that “Honorius’s [commentary] seems to have been drawn from the same assemblage of sources Anselm put together for the Gloss on Psalms,” by which she means Pseudo-Haimo. She thus believes that the patterns of agreement that exist between the three commentaries are best explained by Wilmart’s theory and that Pseudo-Haimo is, indeed, the work of Anselm.  Flint’s arguments for Honorius’s use of Pseudo-Haimo and Pseudo-Bede give us an opportunity to examine more closely the relationships between these commentaries, and to show that something is at work here which is both related to the Gloss but, at the same time, independent of it. A look at her first piece of evidence, the interlinear gloss “in prosperis et adversis” (“in prosperity and adversity”) explaining the lemma ‘die ac nocte’ (‘day and night’) of Psalm 1:2, immediately shows us that the situation is not as straightforward as scholars have tried to argue. The full interlinear Ordinaria-gloss reads “die ac nocte/ prosperis et adversis, vel assidue” (“day and night/ in prosperity and adversity, or constantly”). This is an adaptation of Augustine’s “… die ac nocte: aut sine intermissione intellegendum est; aut die in laetitia, nocte in tribulationibus.”82 (“… day and night: this is to be understood either as “without intermission,” or “by day, in joy, by night, in tribulation.”) Flint takes the presence of the second Augustinian snippet, “in prosperis et adversis”, in Pseudo-Haimo, Honorius, and the Gloss as a sign of direct source influence, and her observation that Pseudo-Haimo and Honorius are in closer agreement with each other than with the Ordinaria is, indeed, correct. The same gloss, is, however, present in all of the other commentaries in our twelfth-century group—that is to say, in Pseudo-Bede, Remi M, Remi A, Bruno, Gilbert P, and Gilbert U—as well as in Letbert: 
                                                81 Flint, “Some Notes,” 84–85. 82 AVG. in psalm. 1,2. (CCSL 38, 1.)

PICT. in psalm. 1,2. (Paris, BnF, lat. 439, fol. 1v.) 92 GILB. UNIV. sup. psalt. 1,2. (Laon, BM, 17, fol. 2v.) 

--- [p.143] ---

 
 
136 Letbert die ac nocte, id est, assidue meditatur: vel die ac nocte, id est, in prosperis et adversis.93  As we see here, the gloss contains both parts of Augustine’s original; ‘omni tempore’ appears to be an influence from Cassiodorus’s adaptation of Augustine’s “sine intermissione,” namely “Die ac nocte. Haec complexio continuum tempus ostendit.”94 (“Day and night. This combination expresses continuous time.”) We may note, therefore, that only Pseudo-Haimo retains the Cassiodorean rendering, ‘continue’, and in so doing, sets himself the farthest apart from the rest—and this together with Bruno, who omits the prosperity/adversity-interpretation, and, in fact, Honorius, who omits the comment about time. This state of affairs would seem to indicate that neither of these commentaries could itself be a common source of the others.  We see also that there is a clear division between those commentaries that cleave somewhat closer to the Cassiodorean adaptation with ‘omni tempore’, namely Pseudo-Bede, Remi M, Remi A, and Bruno, and those that display yet another adaptation, ‘assidue’, namely the Gloss, Gilbert P, Gilbert U, and Letbert. Despite these differences in detail, the similarity of our commentaries’ adaptation of Augustine strongly suggests that they did not arrive at it independently of each other. A possibility is that they are all dependent to some degree on the long version of Remigius, which reads: Die ac nocte, id est omni tempore. Extra enim diem et noctem nullum tempus est. Vel, in adversis et prosperis.95  (Day or night, that is, all the time. For outside of the day and night, there is no time. Or, in adverse [times] and in prosperous.)   
                                                93 LETBER. INS. in psalm. 1,2. (PL Database vol. 21, col. 646C.) 94 CASSIOD. in psalm. 1,2. (CCSL 97, 33.) 95 REMIG. in psalm.II 1,2. (BAV, Urb. lat. 85, fol. 1v.) 

--- [p.144] ---

 
 
137 At the same time, we may note that nothing of this version’s comment about no time existing outside the night and the day has made it into any of our commentaries, which suggests that our commentaries may not be relying independently on the long version of Remigius itself, but on a briefer source which they all have in common. This source cannot, however, be the short version of Remigius, which does not include the Cassiodorean adaptation of Augustine, but simply reads: Vel die ac nocte, id est prosperitate et adversitate, quia dies aliquando prosperitatem, nox vero adversitatem significat.96  (Or day and night, that is, in prosperity and adversity, since the day sometimes signifies prosperity, and the night adversity.)  Although the exact relationship of the two versions of Remigius has yet to be determined, we may note that Kraebel has found one manuscript in which the long version is mixed with the short.97 Believing the short version to be the authentic one, he suggests that the long version is the result of various accretions to the original text. Thus, although we are not in a position to say whether the combination of Augustine and Cassiodorus which we see in our commentaries depends specifically on Remigius himself, we can say that all our commentaries depend in some way on the original combination of the Augustinian and Cassiodorean glosses, and that this combination ought to predate 1110, the terminus ante quem of Letbert’s commentary.  That Letbert himself is not the originator of the original combination is suggested by the fact that Pseudo-Bede’s and Pseudo-Haimo’s group, including the long version of Remigius, shows a closer proximity to the original Cassiodorean adaptation. The question is thus who Letbert’s                                                 96 REMIG. in psalm.I 1,2. (Reims, BM, 132, fol. 1r; St. Gallen, SB, Cod. Sang. 312, p. 4.) Pseudo-Bede, it is worth noting, includes elements from both the long version—‘omne tempus’—and the short version, namely the specification that the day signifies prosperity and the night adversity: “per diem prosperitatem, per noctem vero adversitatem.” 97 This is the manuscript Paris, BnF, lat. 2460 (eee Andrew Kraebel, “The Psalms-commentary of Remigius of Auxerre: its Manuscript Tradition and its Influence,” paper delivered at the 44th International Congress on Medieval Studies, Western Michigan University, Kalamazoo, MI, May 2009). 

--- [p.145] ---

 
 
138 source for the Augustinian-Cassiodorean combination-gloss is. If we want to say it is the Gloss, and that this work should be ascribed to Anselm, we have to imagine that the Gloss was finished well before Letbert’s demise in 1110.98 The lack of extant Psalms-Gloss manuscripts dating to before the mid-1140’s, however, makes this scenario seem unlikely. What this gloss thus suggests is that all of our commentaries rely in some way on a shared intermediary source, related in some way but not identical to the long version of Remigius, and that Pseudo-Haimo may be closer to that source than the others. Continuing with Flint’s examples, we find further indications that it is a shared non-patristic intermediary source that lies behind the similarities we see in our twelfth-century Psalms-commentaries: we thus note that the same distinction which she points out that Pseudo-Haimo, Honorius, and the Gloss make between the impii (“the wicked”) and the peccatores (“the sinners”) at Psalm 1:5 is also made by Gilbert P, Gilbert U, Remi A, Remi M, Bruno, and Pseudo-Bede. As is so often the case, the exegesis in question has its roots in the Fathers, and also this time in Augustine, who offers two readings of the lemma: one in which the wicked and the sinners are understood to refer to the same group of people, and one in which they represent two different groups. The second interpretation, where they are understood as two different groups, reads: Aut certe si aliud sunt impii, aliud peccatores, ut, quamquam omnis impius peccator sit, non tamen omnis peccator sit impius. Impii non resurgunt in iudicio. Id est, resurgent quidem, sed non ut iudicentur, quia iam poenis certissimis destinati sunt; peccatores autem non resurgunt in consilio iustorum, id est ut iudicent, sed forte ut iudicentur, ut de his dictum sit: Vniuscuiusque opus quale sit, ignis probabit.99 100 
                                                98 For Gilbert U, we know that he borrows from Letbert and thus must postdate his commentary, and for Gilbert P, we have seen that he must have used the Gloss, even if he may also have had a hand in its creation. 99 1 Cor. 3:13. 100 AVG. in psalm. 1,5. (CCSL 38, 3.) 

--- [p.146] ---

 
 
139 (Or, indeed, if the wicked are one thing and the sinners another, so that even though every wicked person is a sinner, not every sinner is, nevertheless, a wicked person, the wicked do not rise again in judgement, that is, they shall indeed rise up, but not to be judged, since they are already destined for certain punishment; but the sinners shall not rise in the council of the just, that is, to judge, but perhaps to be judged, as it is said of them: The fire shall try every man’s work, of what sort it is.)  Augustine does not specify exactly who the wicked or the sinners are, but Cassiodorus, who follows Augustine’s distinction, breaks it down and elaborates on the theory of who is to be judged:  Resurgit enim iustus, ut iudicet; peccator, ut iudicetur; impius, ut sine iudicio puniatur. Neque peccatores in consilio iustorum. Peccatores sunt professione quidem Christiani, sed minoribus peccatis obnoxii, quibus dominica datur oratio, ut se exuant a delictis; impii autem sunt qui suum nescientes auctorem diuersis sceleribus polluuntur, ut blasphemi, impaenitentes, cultores idolorum, et principalibus uitiis obligati.101 (For the just man rises again to judge; the sinner to be judged; the wicked to be punished without judgement. Nor sinners in the council of the just. Sinners are indeed Christians by their declaration, but are liable to smaller sins; it is preached to them, so that they free themselves from transgressions; but the wicked are those who, not knowing their maker, are defiled by various misdeeds, like the blasphemers, the impenitent, the worshippers of idols, and those fettered by mortal sins.)  Sinners are thus Christians who commit minor sins but can be persuaded to change their ways, while the wicked are people who do not believe in the Christian God at all, and who thus end up committing graver sins like blasphemy, impenitence, and idol-worship. When we get to Remigius, we find that Augustine’s distinction has developed into a comprehensive theory of “four orders in the resurrection”: Remigius explains that two of the orders are of those who will be saved, and two are of those who will be damned; each group consists of one subgroup which will be judged and one which will not be judged, so that the wicked and the perfect saints, the apostles, and the martyrs etc. will not be judged—the wicked because they are already judged, and the rest because they are already saved; the group that will                                                 101 CASSIOD. in psalm. 1,5. (CCSL 97, 37.)  

--- [p.147] ---

 
 
140 be judged is the sinners, one subgroup of which consists of those who will be judged to salvation, and the other of those who will be judged to damnation. Remigius has also adapted the make-up of the two groups of the lemma somewhat, so that those sinners who will be damned are construed specifically as the Jews, the false Christians, and the heretics; the wicked are still unbelievers, but labelled specifically as pagans: Impii proprie dicuntur pagani irreligiosi. … Ideo non resurgunt impii in iudicio. Ad quod dicendum quia quattuor ordines erunt in resurrectione; duo electorum et duo reproborum. Primus ordo electorum non iudicabitur, sed iudicabit. Qui erit apostolorum, quibus dictum est, Sedebitis super sedes duodecim iudicantes duodecim tribus Israel.102 Alius ordo electorum erit qui non iudicabit sed iudicabitur et salvabitur, cui dicetur, Venite, benedicti Patris mei,103 etc. Similiter duo reproborum, quorum unus non iudicabit sed iudicabitur, et tamen damnabitur, qui erit ex iudaeis falsisque christianis et haereticis, quibus dicetur, Ite in ignem aeternum,104 etc. Secundus reproborum non iudicabit nec iudicabitur, quia iam iudicatus est, de quo dictum est, Qui non credit, iam iudicatus est.105 De quibus nunc dicitur, Ideo non resurgunt impii in iudicio, id est pagani, ut iudicentur, quia iam iudicati sunt.106 (The wicked are properly called irreligious pagans. … Therefore the wicked do not rise again in judgement. To this it should be said that there will be four orders in the resurrection; two of the elect and two of the damned. The first order of the elect will not be judged, but shall judge. This shall be [the order] of the apostles, of whom it is said, You shall sit on twelve seats judging the twelve tribes of Israel. The other order of the elect shall be the one that will not judge but be judged and saved, to whom it shall be said, Come, ye blessed of my Father, etc. Similarly, [there shall be] two [orders] of the damned, of which one shall not judge but be judged and nevertheless be damned, which will be [made up] of the Jews and the false Christians and the heretics, to whom it shall be said, Go into everlasting fire, etc. The second [order] of the damned shall not judge nor be judged, since it is already judged, of which it is said, He that does not believe, is already judged. About whom it is now said, Therefore the wicked shall not rise again in judgement, that is, the pagans, to be judged, since they are already judged.)  
                                                102 Matt. 19:28. 103 Matt. 25:34. 104 Matt. 25:41. 105 John 3:18. 106 REMIG. in psalm.I 1,5. (Reims, BM, 132, fol. 2r; St. Gallen, SB, Cod. Sang. 312, p. 4.)

141 Every commentary in our group, as well as Honorius, owes some part of their exegesis to Remigius’s adaptation of Augustine: all of them construe the wicked as some form of unbelievers—whether they be called infidels, apostates, idolaters, pagans, or Gentiles—and all except Pseudo-Haimo reproduce Remigius’s characterization of the sinners as false Christians; the majority also reproduces a version of his “four orders” theory:107  Ps.-Haimo … impii ideo non resurgunt, … et quia omnes108 resurgunt, tam boni quam mali, determinat sic: in iudicio, id est, non ita resurgent ut iudicentur vel iudicent. Impii enim, id est infideles, ut iudaei vel gentiles, et etiam illi qui ab hac vita divelluntur, expulsi ab Ecclesia, non in secunda resurrectione iudicabuntur, quia hic iam iudicati sunt, ut apostolus ait: Qui sine lege peccaverunt, sine lege peribunt. Illi vero qui in Ecclesia existentes a praesenti vita recedunt, de quorum operibus an bona an mala sint, incertum est, iudicabuntur, quidam ad vitam, quidam ad mortem. Illi vero de quorum sanctitate in Ecclesia certum est, non iudicabuntur, … sed109 iudicabunt; … Impii quidem non resurgent, neque peccatores, id est Christiani occulte peccantes, resurgent, ita quod sint in consilio iustorum, id est in iudicio cum iustis, …110 (… therefore the wicked do not rise again, … and since everyone rises again, good as well as bad, he specifies it thus: in judgement, that is, they shall not rise in such a way as to be judged or to judge. For the wicked, that is, the infidels, like the Jews or the Gentiles, and also those who are torn apart by this life, shall not be judged in the second resurrection, having been expelled from the Church, since they have already been judged here, as the apostle says: Those who have sinned without the law, shall perish without the law. But those who, while [they are] in the Church, withdraw from this life, [and] of whose works it is uncertain whether they are good or bad, shall be judged, some to life, some to death. But those whose sanctity in the Church is certain shall not be judged, but they shall judge; … The wicked shall, indeed,

Peccatores autem non resurgunt in consilio iustorum: subaudis, ut iudicent, sed ut iudicentur.” (“The wicked do not rise again in judgement: you understand, “in order to be judged,” since they are already destined for very certain punishment. But the sinners do not rise in the council of the just: you understand, in order to judge, but to be judged.”) LETBER. INS. in psalm. 1,5. 143 does not believe is already judged. The wicked shall, indeed, not be judged, but the sinners shall be judged, and the fire shall try every man’s work, of what sort it is.)  Gilbert U Ubi dicit, impii, generaliter intelligit sive illos, qui habuerunt notitiam Dei et postea apostataverunt, sive idolatras, qui nullam Dei habent notitiam … Ideo impii, id est increduli carentes notitia Dei, non resurgunt, id est non resurgent, in iudicio, id est ut iudicentur … Neque peccatores, id est falsi christiani ... Notat quod resurgendorum alii iudicandi, alii non iudicandi; iudicandi alii ad vitam, alii ad perditionem. Non iudicandi alii sancti perfecti iudicantes falsos christianos, id est haereticos in comparatione sui, alii non iudicandi, sicut mali, qui iam iudicati sunt, testante apostolo, qui dicit, Quia sine lege peccaverunt, sine lege peribunt.114 115 (When he says, wicked, he generally means either those who have had knowledge of God and then become apostates, or the idolaters, who have no knowledge of God … Therefore the wicked, that is, the unbelievers who lack knowledge of God, do not rise again, that is, shall not rise, in judgement, that is, to be judged … Nor sinners, that is, the false Christians … He notes that some of those who will rise are to be judged, some not to be judged; some are to be judged to life, others to damnation. Some [of those] not to be judged [are] the perfect saints who judge the false Christians, that is, the heretics compared to themselves. Some are not to be judged, such as the wicked, who are already judged, as attested by the apostle, who says, Since they have sinned without the law, they shall perish without the law.) Non iudicandi alii sancti perfecti iudicantes falsos christianos et iudaeos in comparatione sui, alii gentiles, qui iam iudicati sunt, testante apostolo: Qui sine lege peccaverunt, sine lege peribunt.116 (In another way: not so the wicked, that is whichever unbeliever, not so etc. Therefore [the wicked] do not rise again, since the unbelievers were not thus, but like sand, they shall not rise again to be judged; for they are already judged. … nor sinners in the council of the just, that is, the false Christians shall not rise again to be with the just … Note that some of those who will rise are to be judged, some not to be judged; Some are to be judged to life, others to

144 death. Some [of those] not to be judged [are] the perfect saints who judge the false Christians and the Jews compared to themselves, some [are] Gentiles, who are already judged, as attested by the apostle: Those who have sinned without the law, shall perish without the law.)  Bruno Impii namque Deum ignorantes proprie dicuntur. … Et ideo … impii non resurgunt, id est non resurgent, in iudicio, id est ad hoc ut iudicentur, testante apostolo: Qui sine lege peccaverunt, sine lege peribunt. Sciendum vero est, quod in iudicii die alii resurgent in iudicium, alii non in iudicium, ut gentiles. In iudicium autem alii ut iudicent, sicut perfecti, alii, ut iudicentur. Qui iudicabuntur, alii salvabuntur, ut boni, alii damnabantur, ut falsi christiani, haeretici et iudaei. … impii non resurgent in iudicio, id est ut tunc tandem certum sit eos esse damnandos, iam enim certum est; … Impii non resurgent in iudicio, neque peccatores, id est a Deo apostatantes, in concilio iustorum, …117 (For those who are ignorant of God are appropriately called wicked. … And therefore … the wicked do not rise, that is, shall not rise, in judgement, that is, to be judged, as attested by the apostle: Those who have sinned without the law shall perish without the law. But it should be known that, on the day of judgement, some will rise to judgement, and some not to judgement, like the Gentiles. And some to judgement so that they shall judge, like the perfect, others to be judged. Some of those who shall be judged shall be saved, like the good, others shall be damned, like the false Christians, the heretics, and the Jews. … the wicked shall not rise again in judgement, that is, so that only then it becomes clear that they are to be damned, for this is already certain; … the wicked shall not rise again in judgement, nor sinners, that is, the apostates from God, in the council of the just, …)   Ps.-Bede … ideo neque vere impii, id est idololatrae, resurgent ut sint in iudicio, quia non venient ad iudicium. De his enim dictum est, Impius iam iudicatus est, et, Qui sine lege peccaverunt, sine lege peribunt. Neque peccatores, id est scriptae legis praevaricatores, id est falsi christiani, … Alii enim ita resurgent ut veniant ad iudicium, alii ut non veniant ad iudicium, quia iam iudicati sunt. Venientium alii iudicabunt, alii iudicabuntur. Eorum qui iudicabuntur, alii salvabuntur, alii non.118 (… therefore, truly, the wicked, that is the idolaters, shall not rise again in judgement, since they shall not come to judgement. For it is said of them, The wicked man is already judged, and, Those who have sinned without the law shall perish without the law. Nor sinners, that is, the transgressors of the written law, that is, the false Christians, … For others shall rise again to come to judgement, others to not come to judgement, since they are already judged.
