--- [p.152] ---

 
 
145 Of those who come, some shall judge, others shall be judged. Of those who shall be judged, some shall be saved, others not.)  Remi M Ideo non resurgunt impii, id est, idololatrae, in iudicio, id est, ut iudicent vel iudicentur, quia iam damnati sunt. Resurgentium alii resurgunt ad iudicium, alii non ad iudicium resurgunt. Qui resurgunt ad iudicium, aut iudicabuntur ad salutem iam purgati in purgatoriis, aut ad damnationem, ut illi quorum perditio non est in hac vita manifesta. Resurgentium non in iudicium, alii non iudicabuntur, quia iudicabunt alios, ut sancti apostoli et alii perfecti in Ecclesia, alii non iudicabuntur, quia iam evidenter damnati sunt, non solum apud Deum, sed apud Ecclesiam, ut gentiles, iudaei, et haeretici, … neque peccatores—scilicet resurgunt— praevaricatores legis, iudaei et falsi christiani ad119 hoc ut sint in consilio iustorum, quia hic noluerunt esse in consilio iustorum.120 (Therefore the wicked, that is, the idolaters, do not rise again in judgement, that is, to judge or be judged, since they are already condemned. Some of those that shall rise again shall rise to judgement, others shall not rise to judgement. Those who rise to judgement shall either be judged to salvation, having already been purged in purgatory, or to damnation, like those whose perdition is not yet apparent in this life. Of those who shall not rise to judgement, some shall not be judged, because they are clearly already condemned, not only by God, but by the Church, such as the Gentiles, the Jews, and the heretics, … nor sinners, [that is,] the transgressors of the law, the Jews, and the false Christians—that is, [do not] rise again—in the council of the just, since they did not want to be in the council of the just here.)  Honorius Vel impii sunt pagani et iudaei, peccatores haeretici et falsi christiani. … Sed impii in iudicio non resurgent, id est ut iustos ibi iudicent, sicut hic fecerunt, sed ut a iustis iudicentur, et pro factis suis iuste damnentur. Neque peccatores ibi resurgent in consilio, id est in congregatione iustorum, quia hic noluerunt resurgere ad iustitiam in consilio iustorum, et ideo non erunt ibi participes beatitudinis eorum.121 (Or the wicked are the pagans and the Jews, the sinners the heretics and the false Christians. … But the wicked shall not rise again in judgement, that is, to judge the just there, as they have done here, but to be judged by the just, and to be justly condemned for their actions. Nor shall the sinners rise again there, in the council, that is, in the gathering of the just, since they did not want to rise again to justice in the council of the just here, and therefore they shall not take part in their beatitude there.) 
                                                119 ad] ab Migne 120 (PS.) REMIG. psalm. 1,5. (PL Database vol. 131, cols 153D–154A.) 121 HONOR. AUG. psalm. 1,5. (PL Database vol. 172, col. 281AB.) 

--- [p.153] ---

 
 
146 A sign that the commentaries of our group of eight ultimately stem from the same adaptation of Remigius is the fact that, although they all depend in some way on his adaptation of Augustine, none of them frame the theory in his terms of the four orders in the resurrection (“duo ordines electorum” and “duo ordines reproborum”); another is that they reproduce the same selection of Remigius’s biblical quotations, namely only John 3:18, iam iudicatus est ([he] is already judged), which grounds biblically the eternal outcome of the wicked; all except Bruno, furthermore, display the same adaptation of this quote, namely “iam iudicati sunt.”122 None of them, however, reproduces any of Remigius’s quotations of Matthew, which he uses to biblically ground the other three orders. (Several [but not all] of them also quote Romans 2:12, Since they have sinned without the law, etc.)  Honorius still displays an affinity to the group, but, as in the preceding example, his connection is loose in comparison to the core group: beyond labelling the wicked and the sinners in similar terms as the others, the only sign of his connection to the group is his comment, shared with Remi M, that the sinners “did not want” (noluerunt) to be in the council of the just in this life (“hic,” “here”). In fact, Honorius is, unlike the others, of the opinion that both the wicked and the sinners will be judged at the resurrection, and he breaks further with the majority of the others (and with Remigius) by placing the Jews among the wicked. Pseudo-Haimo, however, also places the Jews in this group, and, in so doing, once again breaks away from the rest, as he is the only one who says that the Jews will not be judged at the resurrection.123 As Pseudo-
                                                122 Remi M replaces ‘iudicati’ with ‘damnati’, but it is the same construction. Bruno rephrases it as “…eos esse damnandos; iam enim certum est.” (“… that they are to be damned; for this is already certain.”) 123 If we look at Remi M, he, in fact, places the Jews in both groups, which seems likely to be an interpolation or a transcription error: his terminological agreement with Pseudo-Bede (“praevaricatores legis”) suggests that he, too, meant to place the Jews among the sinners, for unless the Jews and the heretics have been placed among the wicked by mistake, he would be the only one who places the former among those who are already judged. It thus seems more likely that Honorius and Pseudo-Haimo, instead, stand out together as those who do not think that the Jews have been judged already before the day of judgement. 

--- [p.154] ---

 
 
147 Haimo is also the only one who does not use the term ‘falsi christiani’ to designate the sinners, we have here an indication in line with what we found in Landgraf’s examples—that is, that this commentary, although clearly belonging to the same tradition, is not itself the direct source of it. We observe also a division between the Gloss and Gilbert on the one hand, and the rest of the group (again, excluding Honorius) on the other, as the former two, following Augustine, comment only on the judgement-status of two of the four orders in the resurrection, namely those who will not be judged because they are already judged and those who will be judged, with no distinction between being judged to salvation or to damnation. They are still, however, using Remigian labels, and they also quote John 3:18, which is to say that, despite diverging somewhat, they clearly belong to the same tradition as the others in terms of this adaptation. Once again, however, neither of them could themselves be the source of it, as the others are connected through their full account of Remigius’s four orders-theory. This also means that, with the exception of Gilbert U, who in the example of the interpretation of the lemma ‘die ac nocte’ sided with the Gloss and Gilbert P, we observe here the same division into two branches in the tradition, with the Gloss and Gilbert P on one side against the rest. In summary, this example gives us further evidence that Pseudo-Haimo and the Gloss are, indeed, closely related, but that they belong together rather as parts of a larger tradition—a tradition which was also known to Honorius. None of them, however, appears itself to be the source of the shared tradition, nor, for the same reason, does Gilbert P, which is to say that there seems to be something at work behind the extant texts which is not at the moment visible to us. Flint argues also that Pseudo-Bede, too, may have been a source for Honorius. She invokes for this claim their mutual incorporation of a certain theory of “the three deaths of the soul.”124                                                 124 In addition to this, Flint claims that Honorius, in two particular sections of his prologue, “adopts conclusions which appear [in Pseudo-Bede], and not in Pseudo-Haimo or the Gloss.” (Flint, “Some Notes,” 86.) It is, however, unclear exactly which conclusions she is referring to: regarding the order of the Psalms, the two do share the specific 

--- [p.155] ---

 
 
148 As above, however, the conclusion is too hasty, and a closer examination of this example yields results similar to those we have found so far, namely that the relationship between Honorius and the other commentaries is not so directly binary, and that our group is connected by something other than a direct dependence on the patristic originals or on the Gloss or Pseudo-Haimo as such. For this argument, the exegesis in question has its roots in our commentaries’ application to Psalm 1:1 of a passage from Augustine’s Sermon 98 on Luke. In this sermon, Augustine outlines his conception of the three kinds of sinners (tria genera peccatorum), and links these to the three people whom Christ raised from the dead.125 The first type of sinner is the one who consents to sin (“consensit”), represented by the synagogue-master Jairus’s daughter, who was still inside 
                                                comment that Ezra collected the Psalms in one volume (Honorius: “Esdras propheta … psalmos in unum volumen redegit.” [“The prophet Ezra collected the psalms into one volume.”] HONOR. AUG. psalm., praef. [PL Database vol. 172, col. 271C.]; Pseudo-Bede: “Esdra … in volumen unum collegit et retulit.” [“Ezra collected and restored [the psalms] into one volume.”] PS. BEDA psalm., praef. [PL Database vol. 93, col. 478B.]), but in light of the fact that this is also mentioned by Gilbert P (and attributed there to Hilary), who, along with Pseudo-Bede, Honorius, Pseudo-Haimo, and Gilbert U, also refers to Ezra’s re-ordering of the Psalms, this evidence for a particular connection between Pseudo-Bede and Honorius appears slim. As for the number of the Psalms, Pseudo-Bede and Honorius share nothing specific except for the statement that they are 150 in number, which, again, is mentioned by more or less every other Psalms-commentator. It is unclear what other conclusions Flint could be referring to here. 125 “Ista tria genera mortuorum, sunt tria genera peccatorum, quos hodieque suscitat Christus. Illa enim mortua filia Archisynagogi intus erat in domo, nondum erat de secretis parietibus elata in publicum. Ibi intus suscitata est, viva parentibus reddita. Iste autem iam quidem non in domo, sed tamen nondum in sepulcro, parietibus elatus erat, terrae mandatus non erat. Qui suscitavit mortuam nondum elatam, suscitavit mortuum iam elatum, nondum sepultum. Tertium restabat, ut et sepultum suscitaret: et hoc fecit in Lazaro. Sunt ergo qui peccatum intus in corde habent, in facto nondum habent. … Nondum accessit corpore, consensit in corde: mortuum intus habet, nondum extulit. … Surgit mortuus in domo, reviviscit cor in cogitationis secreto. Facta est ista resurrectio animae mortuae intus intra latebras conscientiae, tanquam intra domesticos parietes. Alii post consensum eunt in factum, tanquam efferentes mortuum, ut quod latebat in secreto, appareat in publico. … Qui autem faciendo quod malum est, etiam mala consuetudine se implicant, ut ipsa consuetudo mali non eos sinat videre quia malum est, fiunt defensores malorum factorum suorum.” (“These three kinds of deceased people are the three kinds of sinners, whom Christ also today awakens. For the dead daughter of the synagogue-master was inside in her house, [and] had not yet been brought out in public from the private walls. There inside she was woken up, [and] returned to her parents alive. [The young son of the widow] however, was not in his house anymore, but, although not yet in the grave, had been brought outside its walls, [but] had not been given over to the earth. He who raised the dead girl who had not yet been brought out, raised the dead boy who had already been brought out. A third remained, that he should also raise one who had been buried: and this he did in Lazarus. There are therefore those who have sin inside in their heart, [but] do not yet have it in deed. … [That one] has not yet approached [sin] in body, [but] has consented in his heart: he has the dead one inside, [but] has not yet brought him out. … The dead one rises in his house, the heart is revived in the privacy of thought. This resurrection of the dead soul is done inside, within the hiding-places of the conscience, as if within one’s domestic walls. Others, after consenting, proceed to deed, as if bringing the dead one outside, so that what was hidden in private appears in public. … But those who, by doing what is evil, also involve themselves in wicked habit, so that the very habit of wickedness does not allow them to see what is wicked, become defenders of their own wicked deeds.”) Augustine, Sermo 98, PL Database vol. 38, cols 593–594. 

--- [p.156] ---

 
 
149 her home when she was resurrected (cf. Mark 5:22–24 and 38–42). The second type is the sinner who proceeds from consent to deed (factum), represented by the son of the widow at Naim, whose body had already been brought out in public but not yet been buried (cf. Luke 7:11–15). The third kind is the person who has made a habit (consuetudo) of sin, represented by Lazarus, who, at the time of his resurrection, had already been buried (cf. John 11:1–44). As the three sinners embody three different degrees of entrenchment in sin, we may express this as a theory of the three stages of sin rather than of its types. While Augustine does not include anything similar to his three stages-theory in his exegesis of Psalm 1:1, Jerome relates the three actions of the verse—namely, walking away (abiit), standing (stetit), and sitting (sedit)—to something similar but not identical to Augustine’s three stages, namely to thinking (cogitare), doing, (facere), and teaching (docere), so that walking away is equated with thinking, standing with doing, and sitting with teaching: Aliter: beatus qui non cogitauit, non fecit, non docuit mala.126 (In another way: blessed [is the man] who has not thought, not done, nor taught evil.)  Beatus uir qui non abiit in consilio impiorum, qui non cogitauit malum: et in uia peccatorum non stetit, qui non fecit malum: et in cathedra pestilentiae non sedit, hoc est, qui nec alios docuit.127 (Blessed is the man who has not walked away in the counsel of the ungodly, who has not thought evil: and has not stood in the way of sinners, who has not done evil: and who has not sat in the chair of pestilence, and who has not taught others evil.)  
                                                126 HIER. in psalm. 1. (CCSL 72, 179.)  127 HIER. tract. in psalm. p. 4, 39–42. 

--- [p.157] ---

 
 
150 Except for the fact that Jerome does not mention consent, the first two actions agree with the account of Augustine’s sermon; that Jerome conceives of the third action as teaching is clearly due to the fact that the psalm specifically mentions sitting in a cathedra, a “teacher’s chair.”128 Cassiodorus follows Jerome in his exegesis of the three actions of Psalm 1, with thought (cogitatio), deed, (factum/actus), and word/teaching/preaching (dictum/doctrina/praedicatio) being correlated to these, but also seems to be aware of the existence of Augustine’s theory    of the three kinds of sinners, as he mentions specifically that sins, errores, happen in three different ways: Tribus itaque modis prouenire humanos constat errores: cogitatione, facto et dicto, quos hic per ordinem abnegat dicens: Non abiit in consilio impiorum. Primum detestabiles denegat cogitationes, quas sibi familiariter uindicat genus humanum, quas Dominus Christus omnino non habuit. … Deinde actus uitiosae conuersationis excludit dicendo: Et in uia peccatorum non stetit; … Tertio loco subiunxit: Et in cathedra pestilentiae non sedit. Ad doctrinas hoc uidetur abominabiles pertinere, quae pestilentis dogmatis uenena disseminant. Haec merito negantur in Christo, qui totius mundi uulnera medicabili praedicatione saluauit.129 (It is known that people err in three different ways, by thought, deed, and word, which [the Psalmist] denies here in order, saying: [… who] has not walked in the counsel of the ungodly. First, he rejects the detestable thoughts that the human race appropriately claims for itself, which the Lord Christ did not have in any way. … Then he excludes the deeds of vicious intercourse by saying: nor stood in the way of sinners, … In third place, he adds: … nor sat in the chair of pestilence. This appears to pertain to abominable teachings, which spread the poisons of pernicious dogma. These are rightfully denied in Christ, who healed the wounds of the whole world with his healing preaching.)  Neither Jerome nor Cassiodorus thus includes Augustine’s ‘habit’ in their exegesis of Psalm 1:1, nor do they include Christ’s three acts of resurrection.  Remigius, in turn, follows Jerome’s and Cassiodorus’s interpretation of the three actions of Psalm 1:1 in terms similar to Augustine’s theory of the three stages of sin, cleaving closer,                                                 128 In his Psalms-commentary, Augustine is on the same track, interpreting ‘cathedra’ as ‘perniciosa doctrina’, “pernicious teaching.” AVG. in psalm. 1,1. (CCSL 38, 1.) Jerome only changes the noun to a verb. 129 CASSIOD. in psalm. 1,1. (CCSL 97, 30–31.) 

--- [p.158] ---

 
 
151 however, to the original Augustine in the last stage;130 importantly for our purposes Remigius also leaves out the three resurrections: Tria sunt genera quibus omne peccatum peragitur, cogitatione, operatione, et studio vel perseverantia. Ab his tribus alienus dicitur beatus esse; nam cum dicit, Beatus vir qui non abiit etc., ostendit eum non habere voluntatem peccandi. Cum vero ait, non stetit, manifestat operationem, id est mala non egit; nec dicit, “non peccavit,” sed, non stetit in via peccatorum, id est non perseveravit obstinate in malo. Via autem peccatorum diabolus est, sicut via iustorum Deus, vel etiam via ipsa cogitatio ipsa mala intelligitur. … Et in cathedra pestilentiae non sedit, id est malam et pestilentem doctrinam non docuit. … His tribus modis peccavit Adam: in consilio impiorum abiit, cum diabolo suadenti assensum praebuit; in via peccatorum stetit, cum pomum ligni vetiti gustavit; in cathedra pestilentiae sedit, cum ad peccandum per superbiam se obfirmavit.131 (There are three types through which every sin is committed, through thought, deed, and zeal or persevering. The blessed man is said to be foreign to these three; for when [the psalmist] says, Blessed is the man who has not walked etc., he shows that this man did not have the will to sin. And when he says, has not stood, he clearly indicates deed, that is, he has not done evil; he does not say, “he has not sinned,” but, has not stood in the way of sinners, that is, he has not obstinately persevered in evil. For the way of sinners is the Devil, as God is the way of the just, or the way itself is also understood as the thought itself. … And has not sat in the chair of pestilence, that is, has not taught an evil and pernicious teaching. … Adam sinned in these three ways: he walked in the counsel of sinners when he gave assent to the Devil trying to persuade him; he stood in the way of sinners when he tasted the fruit of the forbidden tree; he sat in the chair of pestilence when he fortified himself to sin through pride.)  Both Pseudo-Bede and Honorius expound the three actions in Psalm 1:1 in terms similar to the Psalms-authorities, but with some significant shared modifications: most importantly, both of                                                 130 The long version of Remigius follows Augustine’s original ‘consuetudo’, while the short version casts this as “studio vel perseverantia”: “Tribus modis omne peccatum agitur: cogitatione, opere, consuetudine vel exemplo. His tribus qui immunis fuerit vere dici beatus poterit. Ecce enim beatum a peccato cogitationis eximit dicens, qui non abiit in consilio impiorum, id est malum non cogitavit, et in via peccatorum non stetit, id est, si malum excogitavit, opere tamen non perfecit, et in cathedra pestilentiae non sedit, id est, etsi fragilitate carnis praepeditus facinus aliquod amisit, non tamen in eo perseverare disposuit, nec alios ad id agendum exemplo vel doctrina diu in eis manendo instruxit.” (“Every sin is committed in three ways: by thought, by deed, by habit or example. The one who is free from these three shall truly be able to be called blessed. For behold, [the Psalmist] revmoves the blessed man from the sin of thought, saying, who has not walked away in the counsel of the ungodly, that is, has not thought [of] evil, nor stood in the way of sinners, that is, if he has thought of evil, he has nevertheless not performed [it], nor sat in the chair of pestilence, that is, although he has let slip any bad deed, fettered by the frailty of the flesh, he has nevertheless not arranged to persevere in it, nor instructed others by example or teaching to committ [any bad deed] while remaining in these [bad habits] for a long time.”) REMIG. in psalm.II 1,1. (BAV, Urb. lat. fol. 1v. 131 REMIG. in psalm.I 1,1. (Reims, BM, 132, fols 1r–1v; St. Gallen, SB, Cod. Sang. 312, p. 3–4.) 

--- [p.159] ---

 
 
152 them include the part of Augustine’s sermon that Cassiodorus and Remigius leave out—that is, the linking of the three stages of sin to the three dead who were resurrected by Christ (see passages below). What is more, Pseudo-Bede and Honorius both reframe the three stages of sin as “the three deaths of the soul” (tres mortes animae).  This term most likely also stems from Augustine’s sermon, where we find the phrase “Facta est ista resurrectio animae mortuae intus intra latebras conscientiae”132 (“This resurrection of the dead soul happens on the inside, within the hiding-places of the conscience”). Augustine does not, however, use the concept of the dead soul as the overarching metaphor for understanding the three stages of sin, nor does any one of the other patristic Psalms-authorities; the fact that Pseudo-Bede and Honorius tie the actions of Psalm 1:1 to Augustine’s theory of the three stages of sin, including the exemplifications of the three resurrections, and also use the three deaths of the soul as the overarching metaphor for understanding these sins is a strong indication of a particular, non-patristic connection between the two commentaries.  Flint takes the presence of “the three deaths of the soul” in Pseudo-Bede and Honorius as evidence of direct influence, pointing to the similarity in their terminology (although, without observing its patristic origins). However, when we compare these two commentaries to the rest of our twelfth-century group, we discover that the framework of the three deaths of the soul is found also in Pseudo-Haimo, Gilbert U, Remi A, and Remi M: all of these commentaries use it to understand the three stages of sin symbolized by the three people resurrected by Christ, and link the stages to the three actions in Psalm 1:1:133 
                                                132 See 148n125. 133 Bruno mentions the concept of death in his accessus, but does not explicitly apply it to the actions in his exegesis of the lemma, as do these commentaries: “Intentio enim huius psalmi est a Christo tres mortes illas quibus ad mortem primus homo devenit removere.” (“For the purpose of this psalm is to remove from Christ those three deaths by which the first man came to death.”) (PS.) BRUNO CARTHUS. psalm., prol. (PL Database vol. 152, col. 639C.) We thus understand that Bruno cannot have transmitted the theory to any of the others, but must be

(PH32a) Per filiam archisynagogi quam in domo resuscitavit, (PH32b) peccandi voluntas, (PH32c) id est, prima mors animae, designatur. (PH32d) Qui enim tantum voluntate peccat, adhuc in domo est: nondum enim ad operationem processit. (PH33a) Per eum enim mortuum, quem extra portam resuscitavit, (PH33b) alia mors animae, (PH33c) id est, actus peccati, designatur, (PH34) quia perficiendo peccata extra portas sensuum eximus, non remanentes in sola cogitatione. (PH35a) Per mortuum iam fetentem et auras corrumpentem, (PH35b) peccati doctrina signatur, (PH36) cuius fetore et alii corrumpuntur.135 (For every sin is either in the will—whether of thought, or consent—which will is designated through ‘to walk away’; or in deed, which is understood through ‘to stand’; or in teaching, which is understood through ‘to sit in the chair of pestilence’. The will to sin, that is, the first death, is represented by the daughter of the synagogue-master, whom he resurrected in her home. For he who sins only in will is still in his home; he has not yet proceeded to action. The other death of the soul, that is, the act of sin, is represented through the deceased one whom he resurrected outside the gate, since by committing sin we step outside the gates of the senses, not remaining in thought alone. The teaching of sin is symbolized through the deceased one who was already stinking and corrupting the airs, by whose stench others are also corrupted. 154 legimus a mortuis Dominum Christum suscitasse: (GU19a) unum in domo, filiam archisynagoge, (GU19b) qui significat mortem illam quae est interius (GU19c) secundum consensum. (GU20a) Ille vero quem suscitavit extra portam Naim (GU20b) significat mortem illam quae est exterius (GU20c) secundum operationem, (GU20d) quae est gravior morte illa quae est interius. (GU21a) Lazarus vero, quem suscitavit iam fetidum, (GU21b) significat mortem animae quae iam peccatum habet in consuetudine (GU21c) et omnibus fetet per infamiam.137 (He has not walked, I say, placed in the counsel of the ungodly, that is, of the first parents, an apostate from God through only consent and delight. … [He] has not walked, I say, and stood according to his deed in the way of sinners … [He] has not stood, I say, nor sat in the chair of pestilence, that is, he has not taught others to sin by evil example and habit. For this triple death of the soul signifies those three deceased ones whom we read that the Lord Christ awoke from the dead: one in their home, [namely] the synagogue-master’s daughter, who signifies that death which is internal according to consent. And he whom [the Lord] awoke outside the gate of Naim signifies that death which is external according to action, which is more serious than the one that is internal. And Lazarus, whom [the Lord] awoke when he was already stinking, signifies the death which already has made a habit of sin and stinks to everyone through infamy.) (RA22a) Alter defunctus, id est viduae mulieris filius, ante fores civitatis Naim revixit, (RA22b) per quem secunda mors valet figurari, (RA21c) quasi enim ante fores civitatis aliquis mortuus effertur, (RA22d) quando post animi consensum ad actum usque perducitur. 155 one, namely, the daughter of the synagogue-master, was resurrected in her home, through whom the first death can be denoted, as if someone dies in their home when their soul consents to perverse suggestion. The other deceased, that is, the son of the widowed woman, awoke again before the gates of Naim, through whom the second death can be represented, as if a deceased person is brought out before the city gates when he is led, after his soul’s consent, all the way to action. The third, that is, Lazarus, through Christ returned to life not only being dead, but also stinking. He designates well those who not only consent and act, but through the practice of perverse habit, as if sitting in the chair of pestilence, teaches others to sin.)  Remi M (RM1a) … et ideo infelix, quia abiit a lege Domini Dei sui (RM1b) per delectationem vetitorum; (RM2a) stetit (RM2b) per consensum; (RM3a) sedit (RM3b) per operationem (RM3c) et consuetudinem … (RM28) Has tres mortes animae significant tres mortui illi quos suscitavit Christus.
