(RM32c) Peccatum, cum usque ad consensum perducit, non longe est ab operatione. … (RM35a) Lazarus iam fetidus, quem tamen Dominus resuscitavit de monumento, (RM35b) significat animam illam quae usque ad operationem peccatum perducit, (RM35c) et ex longa consuetudine (RM35d) et nimio vitiorum fetore iam in desperationem suae restaurationis cecidit.139 (… and therefore he was wretched, because he walked away from the law of his Lord God through delight in the forbidden; he stood through consent; he sat through deed and habit … Those three deceased ones whom Christ awoke signify these three deaths of the soul. The daughter of the synagogue-master, whom he revived in her home, signifies the soul which is dead inside due to delight in sin. … The son of the widowed woman, whom the Lord awoke at the gate, signifies the soul dead from consent to sin. Sin, when it leads all the way to consent, is not far from action. … Lazarus already stinking, whom the Lord nevertheless revived from his tomb, signifies that soul which has led sin all the way to action, and out of long habit and too much stench of vices has already fallen into despair of its restoration.)  Ps.-Bede (PB2a) Omnis mors animae fit tribus modis, (PB2b) scilicet aut delectatione tantum, (PB2c) aut delectatione (PB2d) et consensu, (PB2e) et peccati perpetratione. (PB3a) Delectatio voluntas peccandi quae fit in anima ex titillatione et fomite et aptitudine peccandi quae in nobis est, et ex suggestione, id est incitatione incentoris diaboli superveniente: (PB3b) et hoc est prima mors animae. (PB4) Secunda vero est consensus, quando nos ad peragendum quantum possumus extrinsecus nos applicamus. (PB5a) Tertia autem est ipsius peccati actio,                                                 139 (PS.) REMIG. psalm. 1,1. (PL Database vol. 131, cols 149B–151C.) 

--- [p.163] ---

 
 
156 (PB5b) quae dividitur in duo, scilicet in actionem simplicem, et in frequentem, … (PB6a) Dicuntur autem haec tria mors animae, (PB7) Designantur vero hae tres mortes animae per tres mortuos a Domino resuscitatos. (PB8a) Per puellam enim in domo, archisynagogi Iairi filiam, … (PB8c) delectatio designatur, … (PB9a) Per adolescentem vero lascivum atque e140 domo matris antequam suscitaretur elatum, (PB9b) intelligitur consensus, (PB9c) qui in exteriori applicatione positus, exemplum dat cernentibus. (PB11a) Per Lazarum vero quatriduanum in sepulcro et fetidum (PB11b) intelligitur et actus simplex (PB11c) et consuetudo peccandi.  … (PB16b) non abibit a Deo suo voluntate etiam peccati aberrando, nec stabit consentiendo, nec sedebit alios suo exemplo peccare docendo.141 (Every death of the soul happens in three ways, namely, either through delight alone, or through delight and consent, or through the perpetration of sin. Delight [is] the will to sin which occurs in the soul out of enticement and kindling and the aptitude for sin which is in us and [occurs] out of suggestion, that is, by being overcome by the incitement of the instigating Devil: and this is the first death of the soul. The second [death] is consent, when we apply ourselves externally as much as we can to commit [the sin]. The third [death] is the action of the sin itself, which is divided into two, namely, into single and frequent action, … And these three are called the death of the soul. These three deaths are signified by the three deceased ones who were resurrected by the Lord. Delight is signified by the girl in the house, the daughter of the synagogue-master Jairus, … The youth who had been licentious and carried out from his mother’s house before he was resurrected signifies consent, which when put into external application, gives an example to those who perceive it. Lazarus, who had been stinking in his grave for four days, signifies both single action and the habit of sinning. … [Christ] will not walk away from God by erring through his will to sin, nor stand by consenting, nor sit by teaching others to sin through his example.)  Honorius Hinc tres mortes exprimuntur, quibus omnes posteri Adae involvuntur: quae ex consensu, ex actu, ex consuetudine oriuntur.142 … Tres lapsus animae hic notantur, cogitatio, locutio, actio. Beatus vir non abiit in concilio impiorum, dum cogitationi a daemonibus immissae per voluntatem non consentit; in via peccatorum non graditur, dum malum non loquitur; in cathedra pestilentiae non sedet, dum malum operando, non docet. Haec per tres mortuos figurantur qui a Domino resuscitantur: per puellam in domo resuscitatam,143 per 
                                                140 e] in Migne 141 PS. BEDA psalm. 1,1. (PL Database vol. 93, cols 483D–484D.) 142 HONOR. AUG. psalm. 1,1. (PL Database vol. 172, col. 275B.) 143 Cf. Matt. 9:25, Mark 5:41–42. 

--- [p.164] ---

 
 
157 adolescentem extra portam resuscitatum,144 per Lazarum de monumento excitatum.145 Puella in domo mortua est anima in cogitatione per delectationem mortua; adolescens extra portam elatus est animus in consensu operis defunctus; senex in sepulcro fetidus est homo in prava consuetudine tumulatus.146 (Hence are expressed the three deaths by which all descendants of Adam are enfolded: they arise out of consent, out of action, [and] out of habit. … Here the three falls of the soul are signified, thought, speech, action. The blessed man has not walked in the counsel of the ungodly, whilst he did not consent to the thought planted in him by demons through the will; he does not walk in the way of sinners, whilst he does not speak evil; he does not sit in the chair of pestilence, whilst he does not teach by doing evil. These [actions] are represented by the three deceased ones who were resurrected by the Lord: through the girl resurrected in her home, through the youth resurrected outside the gate, [and] through Lazarus woken up from the grave. The girl who had died in her home is the soul that is dead in thought through delight; the youth who had been brought outside the gate is the soul that is deceased in consent to the action; the old man stinking in his grave is the man who is buried in perverse habit.)   As the incorporation of this rendering of the three deaths of the soul by several commentaries in our group is, once again, hardly a coincidence, it complicates the idea of a particular connection between Pseudo-Bede and Honorius but reinforces the idea of a shared, non-patristic intermediary source of our group, and one, furthermore, that is different from the Gloss, as this commentary does not reproduce the framework of the three deaths of the soul.147 If we look closer at the relationship between Honorius and Pseudo-Bede and compare their renderings of Augustine’s three stages-theory to the rest, we find, furthermore, that it seems highly unlikely that Pseudo-Bede would have been the source of this combination of patristic originals for either Honorius or, indeed, any of the other commentaries.  
                                                144 Cf. Luke 7:11–15. 145 Cf. John 11:39–44. 146 HONOR. AUG. psalm. 1,1. (PL Database vol. 172, col. 280AB.) Honorius also mentions consuetudo when speaking of Lazarus. 147 Neither do Gilbert P or Bruno; they do, however, include the Cassiodorean application of the Augustinian theory of the three stages of sin to Psalm 1:1, and their connection to the tradition can be shown in other ways. 

--- [p.165] ---

 
 
158 Beginning with Honorius, we see that he, in fact, includes two versions of the theory:148 the first is straightforwardly Augustinian, with ‘consensus’, ‘actus’, and ‘consuetudo’; the second is Jeromian/Cassiodorean in terminology, with ‘cogitatio’, ‘locutio’ (“speech”), and ‘actio’ (“action”), and, even though Honorius has switched the order of the last two stages, he connects action to teaching in his exegesis of ‘sedit’: “he does not sit in the chair of pestilence, whilst he does not teach by doing evil.” This duplicity of renderings seems, furthermore, to have created some problems in correlating the three stages to the examples of the resurrections, for, in Honorius’s version, the girl becomes the representative of thought due to delight (cogitatio per delectationem) rather than consent, and the young man of consent to action (consensus operis), which is not the same as action. (Lazarus is, however, still the example of habit.)  In the shifting of these concepts, Honorius does, indeed, agree with Pseudo-Bede, who conceives of the girl as an example of delight and the young man as consent. Pseudo-Bede also, as in Honorius’s second, Jeromian/Cassiodorean rendering, conceives of the third stage as “the perpetration/action of sin” (perpetratio/actio peccandi). Unlike Honorius, however, Pseudo-Bede appears at the same time to be making an attempt to include habit in his third stage, as he divides action into simple and frequent (PB5b); indeed, when he gets to the example of Lazarus, Pseudo-Bede switches to the Augustinian term.  Even though Honorius’s account thus shares some peculiarities with Pseudo-Bede’s, it becomes clear, if we take the whole of Pseudo-Bede’s rendering into consideration, that the latter cannot have been Honorius’s source for either of his renderings of the three deaths of the soul, as he conceives of the first stage simply as delight, thus departing from all of the patristic/Carolingian versions, and pushes consent to the second stage (that is, even in his presentation of the theory, not just in its biblical exemplification), where it is coupled with                                                 148 In the second version, he reframes the theory as “the three falls of the soul,” rather than “deaths.” 

--- [p.166] ---

 
 
159 another instance of ‘delight’. Honourius’s two renderings are thus closer to the patristic originals than is Pseudo-Bede’s, which shows that the latter must be further away from any shared intermediary source and can thus not have been Honorius’s source for incorporating the three deaths of the soul at Psalm 1:1.149 If we turn instead to Pseudo-Haimo, we find that his account of the three deaths of the soul has been harmonized to fit the Jeromian/Cassiodorean exposition of the three stages: the first stage has been rendered here as ‘voluntas’, “will” (PH23b), represented by the girl (PH32a–b),150 the second stage as ‘actus’ (PH25a), represented by the young man at Naim (PH33a–c), and the third as the Jeromian/Cassiodorean ‘doctrina’ (PH26a), represented by Lazarus (Ph35a–b). In order to fit this example to his Jeromian/Cassiodorean framework, Pseudo-Haimo thus has to depart from the others and connect the stench of Lazarus’s dead body to the teaching of sin— which, he states, corrupts other people by spreading through the air—rather than, as do the others, to the effect of having habitually practised sin. As such, Pseudo-Haimo’s explanation stands out among the others and cannot have been their shared source. This passage contains, furthermore, another feature, shared by all except the Gloss, Remi A and Pseudo-Haimo (in other words, including here also Gilbert P), which unites our group beyond coincidence and beyond a dependence on the Gloss, namely the conspicuous fact that they all include ‘delectatio’, “delight,” somewhere in their respective accounts.151 Neither 
                                                149 Although Remi M shares some features with Pseudo-Bede and Honorius—such as rendering the first stage as delight and the second as consent (though not in combination with delight) and combining action and habit in the third stage, like Pseudo-Bede, and combining consent and action when giving the example of the young man at Naim, as Honorius—the fact that he agrees in these instances with either Pseudo-Bede or Honorius shows that he could neither have been the source of either of the other two, nor be dependent solely on either of them; their relationships can, in other words, not be so straightforwardly binary.  150 The will is then sub-divided using the two Augustinian terms ‘cogitatio’ (PH23c) and ‘consensus’ (PH23d). 151 Bruno’s account reads: “(BC1a) Adam (BC1b) per delectationem (BC1c) et consensum, (BC1d) et operationem (BC1e) et consuetudinem peccandi moriens, (BC1f) secum genus humanum omne traxit, (BC1g) et sic merito infelix fuit.” (“Adam, dying through delight and consent and deed and the habit of sinning, dragged the 

--- [p.167] ---

 
 
160 Augustine, nor Jerome, Cassiodorus, or Remigius mention delight in this context. A clue to where the idea comes from can, however, be found in Gilbert P’s account.  While Gilbert P’s second and third stages correspond to Augustine’s, he presents, for the first stage, a theory where the three mental states of thought (cogitatio), delight (delectatio), and consent (consensus) together make up the will (voluntas), which he identifies as the first kind of sin, thus solving the problem of the seeming excess of concepts: (GP10a) Temptatio fit tribus modis: (GP10b) cogitatione, (GP10c) delectatione, (GP10d) consensu. (GP11a) Haec tria voluntas una complectitur, (GP11b) quae est primum genus peccandi; (GP12) secundum opus; (GP13) tertium consuetudo.152 (Temptation happens in three ways: through thought, delight, [and] consent. One will, which is the first kind of sinning, encompasses these three; the second [kind is] deed; the third [is] habit.)   This theory appears to have its roots in Gregory the Great, who explicates a very similar idea in several places in his works; for example, he writes in his homily on Matthew, “Sed sciendum nobis est quia tribus modis temptatio agitur, suggestione, delectatione, consensu”153 (“But we should know that temptation happens in three ways, [namely] through suggestion, delight, [and] consent”). The theory is also found in a couple of places in the Moralia, and here, Gregory exemplifies it with the same biblical story which is used by every commentator thus far mentioned in their exegesis of Psalm 1:1, namely that of Adam, Eve, and the serpent.154 The                                                 whole human race with him, and was thus justifiably wretched.”) (PS.) BRUNO CARTHUS. psalm. 1,1. (PL Database, vol. 152, col. 639D.) Gilbert’s is given below. 152 GILB. PICT. in psalm. 1,1. (Paris, BnF, lat. 439, fol. 1v.) 153 GREG. M. in euang., lib. 1, hom. 16, §1:20–21. (CCSL 141, 110.) 154 “Quattuor quippe modis peccatum perpetratur in corde, quattuor consummatur in opere. In corde namque suggestione, delectatione, consensu et defensionis audacia perpetratur. Fit enim suggestio per aduersarium, delectatio per carnem, consensus per spiritum, defensionis audacia per elationem. … Nam serpens suasit, Eua delectata est, Adam consensit; …”  (“For sin is committed in the heart in four ways, [and] fulfilled in action in four. For it is committed in the heart through suggestion, delight, consent, and the audacity of justification. For suggestion arises through an adversary, delight through the flesh, consent through the soul, [and] the audacity of justification through arrogance. For the serpent persuaded, Eve was delighted, Adam consented; …”) GREG. M. moral., lib. 4, §27:49:9–13. (CCSL 143, 193.) And, “Quia enim peccatum tribus modis admittitur, cum uidelicet 

--- [p.168] ---

 
 
161 incorporation of ‘delectatio’ into the threefold Augustinian schema thus seems to be due to an influence from Gregory; we may, therefore, also note that Remi A and Pseudo-Bede both mention suggestio, and that Pseudo-Bede also equates delight with the will to sin. As it, again, seems unlikely that our commentators should have picked these additions up independently, we may take the presence of these foreign elements in Augustine’s three-stages theory as another indication that our commentaries rely on some common source for their exegesis of Psalm 1:1—and, once more, it must be a source other than the Gloss, as this commentary does not add either delectatio, suggestio, or voluntas to the mix.  For this reason, it is also interesting to note that the addition of these elements to the otherwise threefold Augustinian/Cassiodorean schema seems to have been the cause of some confusion. As we have seen, our commentaries all do something slightly different with them, indicating that none of them knew exactly how to fit these concepts together: Gilbert U, Bruno, and Remi A place delight/suggestion in the first stage, on equal terms with consent;155 Remi M, like Pseudo-Bede, instead makes delight alone the first stage, and thus pushes consent to the second stage and combines act with habit as the third, affecting the biblical examples accordingly. Pseudo-Bede, as we have seen, shares the first feature with Remi M but includes delight twice: once by itself, as stage one, and then together with consent in the second stage. This state of affairs thus invites the speculation that this confusion goes back to the root of this tradition, where the concepts of ‘delight’ and ‘suggestion’ were first introduced into the schema—a “problem in the archetype” 
                                                serpentis suggestione, carnis delectatione, spiritus consensione perpetratur, …” (“For sin is committed in three ways, namely, when it is perpetrated through the suggestion of the serpent, the delight of the flesh, the consent of the soul, …”) GREG. M. moral., lib. 32, §19:33:19–21. (CCSL 143, 1654.) 155 For Bruno, see 159n151. 

--- [p.169] ---

 
 
162 of sorts,156 and one which could easily be explained, furthermore, if the “archetype” was a lecture rather than a text, or, alternatively, different reportationes of the same lecture course. Should this suggestion be deemed plausible, we may also note that Gilbert P seems to be the only one who knows what to do with the addition. This could either be because he was a very clever man and managed to devise this solution on his own, or, perhaps more likely, because he was familiar with the Gregorian theory—and more likely than the scenario of Gilbert stumbling upon this mess of concepts in one of his contemporary sources and, just happening to be familiar with the theory, restoring them to their original Gregorian form, is that he knew it because he had a close connection to the root of the tradition, where these theories were originally applied to Psalm 1:1. Once again, it thus appears that Gilbert of Poitiers stands at the centre of this intricate web of twelfth-century psalms exegesis. Remi A, Gilbert U, and Bruno also do well in combining suggestion/delight with consent in the first stage, and may thus be closer to the root of the tradition than Pseudo-Bede and Remi M (and also Honorius). Pseudo-Haimo, for his part, stands off to the side by not including delectatio in his outline; he is, however, the only one who reproduces anything similar to Gilbert’s Gregorian theory of the will. The explanation that this commentary is associated with Anselm due to its occupying a place close to a commentary which, in turn, occupies a place close to the root of this tradition—namely, Gilbert P—is thus tempting. The last of Flint’s examples yields similar conclusions about the relationships of our commentaries to each other. It concerns the interpretation, found in Pseudo-Haimo’s and 
                                                156 The discrepancies between the manuscript and Migne’s edition in the case of Pseudo-Haimo’s account (see 153n134) further illustrate that there seems to have been some confusion as to how, exactly, to construe the theory and accommodate all the concepts. I have chosen to reproduce the reading of the manuscript precisely because it makes sense of the sentence in terms of the theory of the will: thus, the conjunction ‘vel’ (“or”) is what separates the three stages, will (voluntas), deed, (actus), and teaching (doctrina), while ‘sive … sive’ (“whether … or”) is used to make a subdivision in the will with two modifiers in the genitive—that is, voluntas cogitationis (“the will consisting of thought”) and voluntas consensus (“the will consisting of consent”). 

--- [p.170] ---

 
 
163 Honorius’s prologues, of the ten chords of the psaltery as representing the ten commandments, which stems from Augustine’s commentary on Psalm 91:4157 and is transmitted by Cassiodorus at Psalm 32.158 Once again, however, Pseudo-Haimo and Honorius are not the only ones to include it: it appears also in the prologues of Gilbert P, Remi A, Remi M, and Pseudo-Bede.  Our commentaries discuss here the shape and sound of the psaltery and their spiritual significance: Pseudo-Haimo elaborates on an idea found in Augustine’s commentary on Psalm 32159 and brought, in general terms, into the main prologue by Cassiodorus.160 At Psalm 32, Augustine contrasts the shape of the psaltery, which has its sound-box at the top of the instrument, with the shape of the zither, which has its sound-box at the bottom; he relates the latter to the earthly life, and the former, by implication, to the celestial.161 In Pseudo-Haimo, the 
                                                157 “In decachordo, psalterio, cum cantico in cithara. Decachordum psalterium non modo audistis. Decachordum psalterium significat decem praecepta legis.” (“Upon an instrument of ten strings, upon the psaltery: with a canticle upon the harp. You do not only listen to the psaltery of ten strings. The psaltery of ten strings signifies the ten commandments of the law.”) AVG. in psalm. 91,5. (CCSL 39, 1282.) 158 “Psalterium uero decachordum esse diximus, quod ordine conuerso aluum citharae in superioribus habet, unde ad inferiorem partem canora fila descendunt. Cui praecepta decalogi conuenienter aptantur, quia secundum formam instrumenti huius de supernis uenientia Domini iussa suscipimus.” (“Indeed, we say that the psaltery is ten-stringed, which has the hollow of the zither at the top, in the reverse order, whence the sounding strings descend to the lower part. To this the precepts of the Decalogue are easily fitted, since we receive the Lord’s commandments coming from above according to the form of this instrument.”) CASSIOD. in psalm. 32,2. (CCSL 97, 284.) 159 “Mementote citharam ex inferiore parte habere quo sonat, psalterium ex superiore. Ex inferiore uita, id est terrena, habemus prosperitatem et aduersitatem, …” (“Remember that the zither sounds from its lower part, [but] the psaltery from the upper. From the lower life, that is, the earthly [life], we have prosperity and adversity, …”) AVG. in psalm. 32,5 serm. II. (CCSL 38, 250.) 160 “Psalterium est, ut Hieronymus ait, in modum Δ deltae litterae formati ligni sonora concauitas, obesum uentrem in superioribus habens, ubi chordarum fila religata disciplinabiliter plectro percussa, suauissimam dicuntur reddere cantilenam. Hinc citharae positio uidetur esse contraria, dum quod ista in imo continet, illud conuersa uice gestat in capite. Hoc autem genus organi canorum atque singulare aptatur corpori Domini Saluatoris: quoniam sicut istud de altioribus sonat, sic et illud gloriosae institutionis superna concelebrat; …” (“The psaltery is, as Jerome says, a resounding box of wood, shaped in the way of a delta, which has a large belly at the top where the attached strings of the chords, when struck properly with a plectrum, are said to give a most delightful song. From this the positioning of the zither appears to be opposite, since what this [instrument] has at the bottom, [the psaltery] carries at the top in the reverse way. And this sonorous and unique type of organ is fitted to the body of the Lord our Saviour; since as [this instrument] sounds from above, thus [that body] also celebrates the celestial things of that glorious institution; …”) CASSIOD. in psalm. Praefatio IV. (CCSL 97, 11–12.) 161 See 163n159. 

--- [p.171] ---

 
 
164 zither becomes, more specifically, a symbol of the mortification of the flesh, and the psaltery of the vivification of the spirit:  Decem vero chordae illius psalterii decem legis praecepta significant. Quae omnia praecepta bene implet qui ea162 quae in Psalterio leguntur exsequitur. Sicut enim163 psalterium, quia desuper sonat, vivificationem spiritus significat, ita cithara, quia de subtus sonat, carnis mortificationem designat. Ideo autem164 Psalterium vivificationem spiritus significat, quia semper monet nos facere ea, in quibus spiritus vivificatur.165 (The ten strings of the psaltery signify the ten commandments of the Law, all of which he who follows what is written in the Psalter fulfils well. For as the psaltery signifies the vivification of the spirit since it resounds from above, thus the zither represents the mortification of the flesh since it resounds from below. Therefore, the Psalter signifies the vivification of the spirit, since it always urges us to do those things in which the spirit is vivified.)  Honorius, indeed, expresses a similar idea, but builds his simile on the psaltery alone, leaving the zither out of the picture. He likens the psaltery as a whole to the human form,166 explaining that its upper part signifies the soul and its lower part the body; the chastising of the body is then linked to the striking and plucking of the psaltery’s strings, located in its lower part. This creates sound in the upper part, which signifies the strengthening of the soul: Exprimit etiam psalterium formam hominis, qui constat ex superiori et inferiori, corpore videlicet et anima: qui debet inferius percutere, id est corpus suum ieiuniis et orationibus affligere, ut sic superius possit dulce melos Deo reddere; iuxta illud: Carnem macerant, spiritum roborant. Quod fit in triangulo Psalterio, hoc est, in confessione Trinitatis: et in decem chordis, id est in decem praeceptis legis. Chorda exsiccatur, torquetur, 
                                                162 ea] om. Troyes, BM, 904 163 enim] autem Troyes, BM, 904 164 autem] om. Troyes, BM, 904 165 (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. 1, acces. (Troyes, BM, 904, fol. 1r; PL Database vol. 116, cols 195D–196A.) 166 Jerome, in his commentary on Psalm 91, likens the zither to the totality of the human form, that is, body, mind and soul together, saying that these are all made up of strings: “… cithara nostra, et corpus et anima et spiritus: omnibus chordis conposita sunt.” (“… our zither, and body and spirit: they consist of all strings.”) HIER. tract. in psalm. p. 135, 72–73. Although similar in concept, his exegesis does not seem to be the direct influence of our twelfth-century commentators. 

--- [p.172] ---

 
 
165 extenditur: ita debet homo a carnalibus concupiscentiis exsiccari, virtutibus torqueri, charitate extendi, quia nulla operatio valet sine charitate; ut illud: Si distribuero omnes facultates meas,167 etc.168 (The psaltery also represents the form of a human, who consists of an upper and a lower [part], namely, of a body and a soul: he should strike lower, that is, chastise his body through fasting and prayer, so that he is able to give a pleasant melody to God from above, according to this: They weaken the flesh, they strengthen the spirit. This happens in the triangular psaltery, that is, in confessing the Trinity, and in the ten strings, that is, the ten commandments of the law. The string is dried out, twisted, [and] extended: in this way man should be dried out of his carnal lusts, twisted by virtues, [and] extended in charity, because no work is effective without charity; as this [passage states]: If I should distribute all my goods, etc.)  Honorius’s exegesis is, however, more closely mirrored in Remi M, who, like Honorius, uses only the image of the psaltery, letting the lower part of the instrument represent the flesh and the upper part the spirit: Psalterium, ut diximus, musicum instrumentum est triangulatum, habens decem chordas super se extensas; quod inferius percussum dulcem sonum desuper reddit. Ita et nos si psalmodiam, id est dulce melos, Deo canere volumus non solum voce sed et corde, inferiorem nostri partem percutere debemus, id est carnem affligere. Quanto enim carnem affligimus, tanto magis superior pars nostra, id est spiritus, Deo placet. Quod organum digne Deo modulari non valemus, si fides sanctae Trinitatis desit, quia sine fide impossibile est placere Deo.169 In decem chordis, decem verba legis accipimus: quae observando, et carnem macerando, et in fide sanctae Trinitatis perseverando, iucundum psalterii organum desuper in Spiritu Sancto resonamus.170 
                                                167 1 Cor. 13:3. 168 HONOR. AUG. psalm. praef. (PL Database vol. 172, cols 271D–272A.) 169 Heb. 11:6. 170 (PS.) REMIG. psalm. praef. (PL Database vol. 131, cols 147C–148A.) Interestingly, Remi A also brings up the idea of the mortification of the flesh in the context of describing the instrument, but relates it to its being covered by the skins of dead animals: “Erat autem praedictum instrumentum in modo [modum cod.] deltae literae compositum, interius concavum, deforis pellibus mortuorum animalium coopertum, habens decem chordas desuper sonantes, quae singula mysticant nobis aliquod: concavitas interior terrenis facultatibus designat nos fore evacuandos, sicut psalmista ait: Dispersit, dedit pauperibus, etc. [Ps. 111:9]; mortuorum induviae nos ipsos cum vitiis et concupiscentiis debere mortificare, iuxta apostolum: Mortificate membra vestra, etc. [Col. 3:5]. Dechachordi positio decalogi legis est observatio.” (“The aforementioned instrument was made in the shape of the letter delta, was hollow inside, and covered on the outside with the skins of dead animals, with ten strings sounding from above. Each of these things have a mystical meaning to us: the inner hollow means that we will be emptied of our worldly goods, as the psalmist says: He has distributed, he has given to the poor, etc.; the cover of dead [animals] means that we must mortify ourselves with vices and lustings, according to the apostle: Mortify your members, etc. The placement of the ten strings signifies the observance of the ten commandments.”) PS. REMIG. I sup. psalt., praef. (Admont, SB 99, fol. 1r.) Neither Gilbert P nor Lombard, however, relate the shape of the psaltery to the flesh and the spirit, but follow Augustine, connecting it only loosely to the relationship between earthly and heavenly: Gilbert P writes, “Est autem decachordum et resonat a superiori, in quo significantur decem mandata et ab alto data, et in 

--- [p.173] ---

 
 
166 (The psaltery, as we have said, is a triangular musical instrument that has ten strings extended over it, which, when struck at the lower [part], gives a sweet sound from above. Thus, if we wish to sing psalmody, that is, a pleasant melody, for God not only with our voice but also with our heart, we too should strike the lower part of ourselves, that is, chastise our flesh. For as much as we chastise our flesh, the more our upper part, that is our spirit, pleases God. If faith in the holy Trinity is lacking, we do not have the strength to play this organ worthily for God, since without faith it is impossible to please God. By the ten strings we understand the ten words of the law: by observing these, and by weakening our flesh, and by persevering in our faith in the holy Trinity, we sound the sweet instrument of the psaltery from above in the Holy Spirit.)  Honorius and Remi M also share the phrases “dulce melos Deo reddere/canere” (“to give/sound a sweet melody to God”), “debet/debemus inferius/inferiorem percutere” (“he/we ought to strike the lower [part]”), “carnem macerant/macerando” (“by/ weakening our flesh”), and the words ‘triangulo/triangulum’ (“triangular”) and ‘affligere’ (“chastise”); they also both mention the Trinity. (Remi M, furthermore, includes Honorius’s exact phrase “carnem macerant, spiritum roborant” [“they weaken the flesh, [but] strengthen the spirit”], even though he does this in his exegesis of Psalm 67:26, where he also speaks about the drying out of carnal desires and being extended in virtue, only, with the latter concept rendered slightly differently from what we find in Honorius’s prologue.)171 Pseudo-Haimo’s elaboration on Augustine, on the other hand, is closer to that of Pseudo-Bede, who also uses the analogy of the zither for the human body and also contrasts the mortification of the flesh with the vivification of the spirit: 
                                                eorum quasi sono propter sola superna delectandum.” (“[The psaltery] is ten-stringed and resounds from its upper [part], in which the ten commandments, which were also given from above, are signified, and in their sound, so to speak, one ought to delight due to the heavenly alone.”) GILB. PICT. in psalm., praef. (BnF, lat. 439, fol. 1r.) And Lombard has, “Et sicut illud instrumentum reddit sonum a superiori per manuum tactum, sic liber iste docet bene operari, non pro terrenis, sed pro caelestibus, quae sursum sunt.” (“And as that instrument gives sound from its upper [part] through the touch of the hands, so does this book teach [us] to act well, not for the sake of the worldly, but for the sake of the heavenly, which is above.”) PETR. LOMB. psalm., praef. (PL Database vol. 191, col. 55B.) 171 “Tympanum fit ex corio sicco in ligno extenso: unde tympanizantes sunt illi, qui a carnalibus desideriis sunt exsiccati, et in virtutibus extensi; iuxta illud: Carnem macerant, spiritum roborant.” (“A timbrel in made of dry hide stretched out on a wooden frame; wherefore timbrel players are those who are dried out from their carnal desires, and stretched out in virtues, according to this: They weaken the flesh, they strengthen the spirit.”) (PS.) REMIG. psalm. 67,26. (PL Database vol. 131, col. 487A.) 

--- [p.174] ---

 
 
167 Solet autem in divina pagina pro mortificatione carnis accipi cithara, quae ab inferioribus sonat. Pro vivificatione vero spiritus psalterium, quod a superioribus sonat, quia nos si volumus vivificari in spiritu, oportet ut primum ab inferioribus dulcem Deo reddamus sonum, carnem nostram subtractis deliciis mortificando, et quae nobis subtrahimus, pauperibus dando: deinde a superioribus animum, scilicet mortificatis vitiis, virtutibus exornando, et in contemplatione ponendo.172 (The zither, which resounds from the lower [part], is usually interpreted in Scripture as the mortification of the flesh. But the psaltery, which resounds from the upper [part], [is interpreted] as the vivification of the spirit, because, if we wish to be vivified in spirit, it is appropriate that we should give sweet sound to God first from below, by mortifying our flesh through the withdrawal of luxuries, and by giving to the poor those luxuries which we have withdrawn from ourselves: [and] then [give] our soul from above, namely, by mortified vices, by decorating [it] with virtues, and by placing [it] in contemplation.)  At the same time, Honorius, Remi M, and Pseudo-Bede all share one feature that is not present in Pseudo-Haimo, namely some version of the phrase “dulce melos Deo reddere/canere”—a circumstance that is hardly coincidental and shows that these three must be related in some other way than simply depending on Pseudo-Haimo: their relationship is clearly one that is independent of this commentary.  The exact relationship between the commentaries is, however, not so easy to determine: Honorius and Remi M, for example, both use the word ‘melos’ (“melody”) while Pseudo-Bede uses ‘sonus’ (“sound”), but Pseudo-Bede is the one who agrees with Honorius in using ‘reddere’ (“give”) for Remi M’s ‘canere’ (“sing”). Pseudo-Bede and Honorius also both include references to alms-giving and to the importance of eliminating carnal pleasures when mortifying the flesh. 
                                                172 PS. BEDA psalm., praef. II. (PL Database vol. 93, col. 482C.) Their account seems to have come straight from Augustine by way of a common source: this is suggested by the fact that Pseudo-Bede also echoes Augustine’s division of the ten commandments into three relating to God / the Trinity and four relating to man / man’s existence, which reads: “Praecepta enim legis decem sunt; in decem praeceptis legis habes psalterium. Perfecta res est. Habes ibi dilectionem Dei in tribus, et dilectionem proximi in septem.” (“For the commandments of the law are ten; in the ten commandments, you have the psaltery. It is a perfect thing. In three of them you have the love of God, and in seven of them the love of your neighbor.”) AVG. in psalm. 32,6. (CCSL 38, 251.) Cf. Pseudo-Bede: “Significant autem tria praecepta de Deo sanctam Trinitatem, septem vero nostram quae in mundo, qui septenario numero est, conversationem.” (“For three of the commandments from God signify the holy Trinity, and three of them our abiding on earth, which is of a sevenfold number.”) PS. BEDA psalm., praef. II. (PL Database vol. 93, col. 482A.) It seems unlikely that Pseudo-Bede would independently have looked up Augustine on Psalm 32 in order to find and add this section to the original loan. 

--- [p.175] ---

 
 
168 If it were for these agreements alone, we could imagine that Remi M was entirely dependent on Honorius here, and that Honorius, in turn, depended on Pseudo-Bede, having made the requisite changes to his source’s account. However, Honorius is the only one who uses the words ‘corpus’ and ‘anima’ (“body” and “soul”) where the others—including Pseudo-Haimo—use ‘carnis’ and ‘spiritus’ (“flesh” and “spirit”), which indicates that Remi M must have a connection to the others which is independent of Honorius.  What this example thus shows is, once again, that the texts are clearly closely related to each other, and this by means of something more than simple coincidence. At the same time, it also demonstrates that none of the commentaries discussed is itself the direct source of the others. From a closer examination of Flint’s examples, it would, in other words, appear as if these texts—just as Ballentyne observes for the commentaries on Matthew—may not be able to be fitted straightforwardly into any chronological hierarchy. They also show that our commentaries seem to be linked to each other by means of some source which is not the Gloss, and which is not the patristic and Carolingian originals directly. Finally, the fact that Honorius seems to draw on this same intermediary material means that, whatever it was, this source was available already in the 1120s, when Honorius wrote his Psalms-commentary.  OBJECTIONS TO EVERYONE’S THEORIES: GIRAUD The most recent contribution to the discussion of the authorship of Pseudo-Haimo was made by Cédric Giraud, who has two main criticisms of the previous scholarship: he notes, first, that most of it has been based on internal criticism of only parts of the text and that no one has made a comprehensive comparison of the commentary to its potential relatives, and, second, that scholars have operated on the assumption that the relationship between Pseudo-Haimo and any 

--- [p.176] ---

 
 
169 of its potential relatives is direct—that is, that one commentary must be the immediate source of the other.173 Invoking the well-known fact that the Psalter was one of the books most commented upon during the Middle Ages, and adding the observation that being a cathedral school master more or less inevitably entailed the authoring of biblical commentaries, Giraud makes the very pertinent point that a large number of Psalms-commentaries have undoubtedly been lost to us, and that our reconstruction of literary history will therefore be inadequate if we attempt to make our relational map too tight.174 This criticism is in the same vein as Ballentyne’s points about the Gloss on Matthew discussed in Chapter One: both want to allow for the possibility of now lost texts that are the unseen links between the works that have come down to us. Several signs of this type of scenario have been pointed to in the analyses above.  On the topic of internal criticism in general, Giraud furthermore comments that this should not be based on subjective notions of what constitutes “development,” “coherence,” or “contradiction.” As an illustration of this notion, he revisits a passage previously analysed by Lottin and deemed by him to be incompatible with the corresponding sententia of Anselm;175 Giraud argues here that Pseudo-Haimo’s account is not, in fact, that incompatible with the sententia in question.176 What is more, the exact same account as Pseudo-Haimo’s is present in the Gloss, which, based on Lottin’s argument, would force us to reject Anselm’s authorship of this work as well. (Which, on the other hand, I have suggested here that we may well want to do.) Giraud summarizes his methodological position thus: 
                                                173 Cédric Giraud, “Anselme de Laon,” Histoire littéraire de la France, vol 43:2, 239–291 (Paris: L’Académie des inscriptions et belles-lettres, 2014). 174 Giraud, “Anselme de Laon,” 261. 175 See 110n16. The exegesis relates to Psalm 5:7 and treats the topic of the different types of lying. I have, however, not included Lottin’s analysis, as I am not trying to prove that the two works have the same author. 176 Giraud, “Anselme de Laon,” 258–260.  

--- [p.177] ---

 
 
170 La meilleure méthode est encore de remettre l’ovrage dans le contexte des autres commentaires similaires et connus de l’époque, non pour le placer à tout force dans un arbre généalogique, mais pour comprendre à quel rameau d’une arborescence luxuriante il se rattache le moins mal.177  The fruitfulness of this method has already been implied by the above analyses, for we have seen several indications that Pseudo-Haimo appears, indeed, to be part of a larger family tree—a tree which also contains the Gloss, Gilbert P, Pseudo-Bede, and the others.  Spending only a few comments on the content of Pseudo-Haimo,178 Giraud turns his attention instead to the manuscripts and external criticism. He establishes that, of the ten manuscripts confirmed to contain some version of the text, only BAV, Reg. lat. 295 bears the attribution to Anselm. In addition to this manuscript, only six other witnesses contain the (more or less) complete commentary. Five contain only brief extracts attached to, or spliced into, other commentaries.179  The near-complete lack of medieval attributions or associations of this text with Anselm in combination with a weak and spotty circulation of the commentary leads Giraud to doubt highly the authenticity of attribution of BAV, Reg. lat. 295. However, as a point of great interest, he mentions a Czech study of the Olomouc manuscript Státní archiv, 98, which shows that the commentary can be linked to the city’s twelfth-century bishop Henri Zdík (1126–1150), who was a champion of religious and intellectual reform, and that the text was the first one to be copied in 
                                                177 Giraud, “Anselme de Laon,” 261. 178 He notes that Pseudo-Haimo shares some early scholastic-sounding terminology with other early twelfth-century biblical commentaries, especially due to its use of the accessus, but in his opinion, these features have the air of a superficial overlay. He also notes, following Hauck, that the commentator twice makes reference to monachi and boni canonici, implying the view that not all canons are good—a question which does not seem to arise in the context of monks. This, to him, is another indication of an origin in a milieu shaped by clerical reform (see Giraud, “Anselme de Laon,” 262–263). 179 The complete commentaries are: Nîmes, BM, 32; Oxford, Bodleian Library, 737, considered to be interpolated; Paris, BnF, lat. 449, missing the beginning and end; Troyes, BM, 904, missing a small section at the end; Vatican City, BAV, lat. 5997; Zürich, Zentralbibliothek, Rh 26. The incomplete are: Einsiedeln, SB, 175, containing Pseudo-Bede with an extract of Pseudo-Haimo in a version differing from that of the PL; Engelberg, SB, 6, containing only the beginning of the text as a prologue to the Psalter; Olomouc, Státní archiv, 98, see discussion below; Paris, BnF, lat. 298A, contains only part of the prologue; Paris, BnF, lat. 12000, containing only a small part of the prologue, the rest is Ivo of Chartres (see Giraud, “Anselme de Laon,” 263–265). 

--- [p.178] ---

 
 
171 his episcopal scriptorium in the 1130s.180 Van den Eynde’s dating of the commentary to after 1140 seems thus to be contradicted by external evidence as well, and we may conclude that the last word on the authorship of this commentary has yet to be said.  CONCLUSIONS AND NEXT STEPS As we have seen, a great many claims have been made about the authorship of Pseudo-Haimo. By revisiting these claims while at the same time broadening the scope of our examination, and by looking at several of the manuscripts rather than the printed editions, we have discovered that few of these claims hold. Thanks to the research presented in Chapter One, however, we now know that the one claim that does seem to hold—namely, that Pseudo-Haimo cannot have come from the same hand as the Gloss—is no longer necessarily relevant to the question of Anselm’s authorship. This does not, however, mean that I am claiming Anselm’s authorship of Pseudo-Haimo as such; what the research presented in Chapter One suggests is that some form of Anselm’s teaching, perhaps as preserved in the form of a reportatio, could explain his association with the Gloss on the Psalms. If such a source exists, or if it existed in the form of spoken lectures, this could potentially explain the similarities that we observe in our group of commentaries, among them Pseudo-Haimo. What we have looked for are thus signs of the existence of such a source. Our examination of Landgraf’s arguments showed us that the Gloss and Pseudo-Haimo are, in fact, more similar than he would have us believe. However, it also showed that Pseudo-Haimo does not appear to have been a direct source of the Gloss. We also found here the first indications of a possible shared intermediary source of the Gloss and Pseudo-Haimo. 
                                                180 Giraud, “Anselme de Laon,” 265. If this study is correct, we must, furthermore, allow for some time between the writing of the commentary and its reaching bishop Henri’s scriptorium. 

--- [p.179] ---

 
 
172 The results of our analyses of Landgraf’s passages were reinforced by our examination of Van den Eynde’s and Flint’s arguments, pointing to additional indications that our group of commentaries is connected by way of some intermediary adaptation of the patristic and Carolingian Psalms-authorities—one which is not, however, identical to the Gloss, since the Gloss omits certain material which is shared by the others. Thanks to Flint’s observations, we also learned that, even though we cannot tell which particular commentary he used, the core of material shared by these commentaries was also available to Honorius already in the 1120s.  We have also seen that the work of Gilbert of Poitiers seems to be directly involved in what was in the twelfth century perceived to be Anselm’s work; even his lectures could be a potential candidate for our missing, shared intermediary source. Given these results, Giraud’s suggestion seems to be the most reasonable way forward. In the following, we shall thus explore further the path suggested by these investigations—that is, the idea that our group of commentaries belong together in a specific family tree which has its roots in some form of shared, non-patristic, potentially magisterial source. It will be argued that this is, indeed, the case. In light of what we know about the medieval association of the Gloss on the Psalms with Anselm, the oral transmission of early twelfth-century magisterial teaching, and the way in which other books of the Gloss came into being, and given that we, thanks to Letbert, Honorius, and the Moravian bishop Henri, have seen that the roots of this shared tradition go back, at least, to the 1120s, and possibly even further, it is still perfectly possible that Anselm could be hiding somewhere behind the written texts that became the ultimate and lasting products of this shared tradition of twelfth-century Psalms-exegesis. In the next chapter, we shall examine further the possibility that our commentaries are connected to each other by means of some shared, intermediary adaptation of the patristic and Carolingian Psalms-authorities. 

--- [p.180] ---

 
173 
Chapter Four A FAMILY TREE OF TWELFTH-CENTURY PSALMS-EXEGESIS A Response to Everyone’s Objections    In this chapter, we shall pursue the idea introduced in the previous chapter, namely that our eight commentaries belong together in a specific family tree stemming from a shared, non-patristic source (or, perhaps, even two) which is responsible for transmitting the shared material to our group; I shall argue that this hypothesis best explains the pattern of agreements that we observe between them. As we have already seen, this scenario is suggested by our commentaries’ use and renderings of Remigius’s theory of the four orders in the resurrection and the Augustinian theory of the three stages of sin conceived in terms of the three deaths of the soul. In the process of making this case, I shall also be touching upon which commentaries are more closely connected to each other within the family tree. Here, we shall begin with a closer look at of one of the passages that provided Hauck and Wilmart with the basis for their dating of Pseudo-Haimo to the turn of the eleventh century, namely the exegesis of Psalm 90:5–6, … you shall not be afraid of the terror of the night, of the arrow that flies in the day, of the business that walks about in the dark: of invasion and the noonday devil (… non timebis a timore nocturno, a sagitta volante in die, a negotio perambulante in tenebris, ab incursu, et daemonio meridiano), as found in Pseudo-Haimo, the Gloss, Gilbert P, Remi M, and Pseudo-Bede. As the different commentaries connect, in each instance, in slightly different ways to the larger tree, Bruno, Gilbert U, and Remi A will be added to the mix below. It should also be noted that it will never be claimed that any of the following agreements between the commentaries singlehandedly proves the argument; with no critical 

--- [p.181] ---

 
 
174 editions in existence, any given agreement (or disagreement) could be particular to the printed text or manuscript(s) consulted. It is only when taking the entire body of agreements (and disagreements) into account that the argument becomes convincing. With some individual variations on, or additions to, the main theme, Pseudo-Haimo, the Gloss, Gilbert P, Remi M, and Pseudo-Bede contain for Psalm 90:5–6 a common core of exegesis which stems from Augustine. Augustine interprets each of the four objects (or pair of objects) of verse 5 and 6—one, the terror, two, the arrow, three, the business, and four, the invasion and the noonday devil—as representing one of two types of temptation, namely either light (levis) or grave (gravis). Each of these two types, he explains, arises in people under either of two opposing cognitive states, namely either with them being aware of it (scientes, “knowing”) or with them being unaware of it (ignorantes, “unknowing”).1 These two states are then associated with the day and the night respectively: hence, the terror of the night constitutes light temptation that occurs in the person who is unaware of it, the arrow that flies in the day represents light temptation that occurs in the one who is aware, the business that walks about in the dark is grave temptation that occurs in the one who is unaware, and, finally, the invasion and the noonday devil is grave temptation that occurs in the one who is aware.  This fourfold schema constitutes the shared exegetical core of our current subsection.2 In order to show that our commentators are unlikely to have arrived at this interpretative model by                                                 1 Further on in Augustine’s account, the object of the knowing/unknowing is designated as a person’s understanding of what it means to be a Christian, but in this first explication, he seems to mean that the temptation is the object of the knowing/unknowing. 2 For this psalm-verse, the subgroup consisting of Bruno and Remi A instead follow Cassiodorus, with Remi A appearing to be a heavily abridged version of Bruno. (Gilbert U is extant only up to Psalm 78:13, but is likely, in this case as well, to have attached himself to his closest relatives, who differ significantly from the rest of the group here.) Cassiodorus writes, “Timor itaque nocturnus est haereticorum tenebrosa suasio.” (“The terror of the night is thus the shady inducement of the heretics.”) CASSIOD. in psalm. 90,6. (CCSL 98, 831.) Bruno thus associates each of the four objects of the lemma with activities of the heretics: the first three objects (the terror, the arrow, and the business) represent their deception, fraus (BC2b, B5b, B7b), and the fourth (the invasion) is of their doing (BC12a). Remi A speaks more generally of interpretations (sententiae), but he does so exactly in those places where Bruno mentions the “deception of the heretics,” fraus haereticorum (BC2b, RA1c; B5b, RA2b; B7b, RA3b), and, at the very end of his exegesis, Remi A does, in fact, attribute these otherwise anonymous sententiae explicitly to the 

--- [p.182] ---

 
 
175 using Augustine independently, but that they are, instead, very likely using a shared intermediary adaptation, Augustine’s passage is included below in its entirety: the purpose of this is to allow us to see right away that, out of this lengthy patristic account, our commentaries reproduce more or less exactly the same selection of snippets (albeit with a few individual variations). This is shown by lowlighting the parts that are not reproduced by anyone of them.  In order to also show to what extent the loans overlap, each commentary’s loans (that is, phrases, words or forms of the same word) has been given a distinct format: words occurring in Pseudo-Haimo are indicated by increased spacing, words occurring in Gilbert P are underlined, words occurring in Remi M are in SMALL CAPS, words occurring in Pseudo-Bede are in Arial font, and, finally, words present in the Gloss are in bold. Once again, the main purpose of this formatting is not for the reader to keep track of which commentary borrows which words, but to provide a simple illustration of how the same snippets are borrowed by more than one commentary. 
                                                heretics: “(RA6a) Dixi quia non timebis a timore nocturno, id est a sententiis haereticorum” (“I said [this] because you shall not be afraid of the terror of the night, that is, of the interpretations of the heretics”), a connection which Bruno also explicitly makes: “(BC9) Dicitur ergo haereticorum fraudulenta sententia negotium” (“The deceitful interpretation of the heretics is therefore called business”) and “(BC16) eadem res, scilicet haereticorum fraudulenta sententia” (“… the same thing, namely, the deceitful interpretation of the heretics”). Other signs of an affinity between these two commentaries can be seen in 1) their shared association of the day with [the light of] wisdom: “(BC5a) Et non timebis a sagitta volante in die, (BC5b) id est ab eadem fraude haereticorum, … (BC5d) et quae volat in die, id est sublimabitur in sapientia humana, quae videtur dies, id est clara et splendida, admodum diei,” (“And you shall not be afraid of the arrow that flies in the day, that is, of the same deceit of the heretics … and which flies in the day, that is, will be raised in human wisdom, which seems like the day, that is, clear and shining, to the full extent of the day”), and: “(RA2a) Et non timebis a sagitta volante in die, (RA2b) id est ab illa sententia sagittante simplices per hoc, (RA2c) quia videtur eminere in luce sapientiae” (“And you shall not be afraid of the arrow that flies in the day, that is, of the interpretation which pierces the simple through the fact that is seems to tower in the light of wisdom”); 2) their interpretation of the demonic invasion as being one of words, or eloquence: “(BC12a) Et non timebis ab incursu ipsorum haereticorum, (BC12b) id est a nimio impetu facundiae ipsorum haereticorum…” (“And you shall not be afraid of the invasion of the heretics themselves, that is, of the excessive onslaught of the eloquence of the heretics themselves”), and: “(RA4a) Non timebis ab incursu, (RA4a) id est eadem sententia propter nimiam affluentiam verborum” (“You shall not be afraid of the invasion, that is, of the same interpretation because of the excessive affluence of words”); and 3) their quotation of 2 Corinthians 11:14, for Satan himself transforms himself into an angel of light: “(BC15) Unde Apostolus: Satanas transfigurat se in angelum lucis” (“Wherefore the Apostle [says]: Satan transforms himself into an angel of light”), and, “(RA5c) [daemonis] transfigurantis se in angelum lucis” (“[of the demon] who transforms himself into an angel of light”). 

--- [p.183] ---

 
 
176 Where an idea conveyed by Augustine is present in one or more commentaries, but only very loosely paraphrased, the typeface has been left plain. Cognates and synonyms have been marked where deemed relevant to the argument. Snippets which our commentaries present as larger, distinct chunks have been numbered with (A1), (A2), etc., both at the beginning and end of the chunks, with the individual snippets of these larger chunks being distinguished by ‘a’, ‘b’, ‘c’, etc. Loans that occur only in one commentary—and not as part of a larger, shared chunk—have been marked with that commentary’s initials: Non timebis a timore nocturno, a sagitta uolante per diem, a negotio perambulante in tenebris, a ruina et daemonio meridiano. (A1a) Duobus quae supra dixit, redduntur duo quae infra dixit. (A1a) Non timebis (A1b) a timore nocturno, a sagitta uolante per diem; et propter timorem nocturnum, a negotio perambulante in tenebris; et propter sagittam uolantem per diem, a ruina et daemonio meridiano (A1b). Quid est timendum in nocte, et quid in die? Cum (A2) QUIsque IGNORANS peccat, tamquam in NOCTE peccat: cum autem SCIENS peccat, tamquam in DIE peccat (A2). (A3) Duo ergo illa leuiora; ipsa sunt grauiora (A3), quae repetita sunt. Intendite, ut diligenter hoc, si Dominus annuerit, exponatur uobis: obscurum est enim, et erit magnus fructus si intellexeritis. (A4) TEMPTATIONEM QUAE FIT IN IGNORANTIBUS LEUIS, TIMOREM NOCTURNUM appellauit; et TEMPTATIONEM QUAE FIT IN SCIENTIBUS LEUIS, SAGITTAm	VOLANTEm	per DIEm appellauit (A4). Quae sunt leues temptationes? Quae non sic instant, (A5) non sic urgent, ut cogant, sed possunt cito declinata transire (A5). Eadem rursus fac grauia. (A6a) Si persecutor instat, et uehementer terret ignorantes (A6a), (A6b) id est nondum firmos in fide, NEC SCIENTES quod ad hoc sunt CHRISTIANi, ut futuram uitam SPERent; cum coeperint terreri de malis TEMPORALibus, putant quod deseruit illos Christus (A6b), et sine causa sunt christiani; non norunt enim, ut dixi, quia (A6c) ad hoc sunt christiani, ut praesentia superent, et futura sperent (A6c): (A6d) inuenit illos negotium perambulans in tenebris, et capit eos (A6d). (A7a) Sunt autem quidam qui nouerunt SE AD futuram spem UOCATOS (A7a); (A7b) quia quod nobis promisit Deus, non est de ista terra, non est de ista uita (A7b); quia istae omnes temptationes tolerandae sunt, ut illud accipiamus, illud acquiramus quod nobis promisit Deus in (A7c) AETERNum; norunt ista: sed quando coeperit PERSECUTOR INSTAre UEHEMENTIUS, agere minis, poenis, tormentis, aliquando CEDUNT, et SCIENTes tamquam in die cadunt.   (A7c) Quare autem in meridie? Quia multum (A8a) FERUET PERSECUTIO: maiores aestus dixit meridiem 
(A8a). Attendat hoc Caritas uestra de Scripturis me probantem. Quando de seminante dicebat Dominus, quia exiit seminans seminare, et aliud cecidit in uiam, aliud in petrosa, aliud inter spinas; dignatus est ipse exponere similitudinem, et cum uenisset ad petrosa, hoc ait: Hi sunt qui audiunt uerbum, et ad horam gaudent ad uerbum; et in tribulatione quae fit propter uerbum, continuo scandalizantur. Quid enim dixerat de his quae exierant in 

--- [p.184] ---

 
 
177 petrosis? Exorto sole, inquit, aruerunt, quia non habebant (A8b) altam radicem.3 Hi sunt ergo qui ad horam gaudent ad uerbum, et cum persecutio facta fuerit propter uerbum, arescunt. Quare arescunt? Quia non habebant firmam (A8b) radicem. Quae est radix? Caritas: hoc enim dicit Apostolus: Ut in caritate radicati et fundati.4 Quomodo enim radix omnium malorum cupiditas, sic radix omnium bonorum caritas est. Nostis hoc, et saepe dictum est: sed quare hoc uolui commemorare? Ut intellegatis Psalmum, quia (A8c) daemonium meridianum propter aestum VEHEMENTIS PERSECUTIONIS positum est (A8c). Sic enim Dominus dicit: Ortus est sol, et aruit herba, quia radicem non habebat.5 Et exponens nobis quid est a sole herbam arescere, dixit, quia persecutione facta non manent illi, quia altam radicem non habebant. Recte hic intellegimus daemonium meridianum persecutionem uehementem. Qualis fuit illa persecutio aliquando, fratres, commemorem, unde Dominus liberauit Ecclesiam suam, dignetur attendere Caritas uestra. Primo, quod (PB) imperatores et reges saeculi putauerunt se persequendo tollere posse de terra nomen Christi et nomen christianorum, iusserunt ut quisquis se confiteretur Christianum, feriretur (PB). Quicumque noluit feriri, negauit se christianum, sciens quid mali faceret; peruenit ad illum sagitta uolans per diem. Quicumque autem non curauit praesentem uitam, sed certus sperauit futuram, declinauit sagittam uolantem per diem, confessus est se christianum: percussus carne, liberatus est spiritu; exspectare coepit positus apud Deum in quiete, etiam redemptionem corporis sui in resurrectione mortuorum: euasit a temptatione illa, a sagitta uolante per diem. Ergo: Quicumque se confessus fuerit christianum, feriatur; quomodo sagitta uolans per diem fuit. Nondum erat daemonium meridianum, flagrans uehementi persecutione, et faciens magnos aestus etiam fortibus. Audite enim quid secutum sit: cum uidissent inimici quod multi festinarent ad martyrium, et tanto plures crederent in Christum, quanto plures patiebantur, dixerunt apud se: Nos occisuri sumus genus humanum, tot millia quae credunt in hoc nomine, si occiderimus omnes, prope nullus in terra remanebit. Coepit (A8d) feruere sol (A8d), coepit feruere aestus. Audite enim quid iusserint: quomodo antea iusserant: Quicumque confessus se fuerit christianum, feriatur; iusserunt postea: (PB) Quicumque confessus se fuerit christianum, torqueatur, et tamdiu torqueatur, donec neget se esse Christianum (PB). Comparate sagittam uolantem per diem, et daemonium meridianum. Sagitta uolans per diem quid erat? Qui se confessus fuerit christianum, feriatur. Quis fidelis eam mortis celeritate non declinaret? Illud autem: Si se confitetur christianum, non occidatur; sed torqueatur, donec neget; si se negauerit, dimittatur: daemonium meridianum erat. Multi ergo non negantes, in tormentis deficiebant; tamdiu enim torquebantur, donec negarent. Perseuerantibus autem in non negando Christum, quid facturus erat (A9) GLADIUS (A9), uno ictu occidendo corpus, animam ad Deum mittendo? Hoc faciebant et diuturna tormenta: sed quis tandem inueniretur qui duraret adversus tantos et tam longos cruciatus? Multi defecerunt: et, credo, illi defecerunt qui (A10) de se praesumpserunt, qui non habitabant in adiutorio Altissimi (A10), et in protectione Dei caeli; qui non dixerunt Domino: Susceptor meus es;6 qui non sub umbra alarum eius sperauerunt, sed uiribus suis multum 
                                                3 Matt. 13:3–23. 4 Eph. 3:17. 5 Mark 4:6. 6 Ps. 3:4. 

--- [p.185] ---

 
 
178 dederunt. Deiecti sunt a Deo, ut ostenderet illis quia ipse protegit, ipse temperat temptationes, ipse tantum uenire permittit, quantum potest ferre cui uenit.7  If we look at the content of those snippets which appear in our commentaries, we find that Augustine begins his exposition—after introducing the lemma—by explaining in A1a that the two latter objects that the psalmist mentions are correlated with (literally, “returned to”) the two former (“Duobus quae supra dixit, redduntur duo quae infra dixit”); he then clarifies this statement in A1b by pairing the fear with the business, and the arrow with the invasion (or, in his version of the Bible, “the downfall,” ruina) and the noonday devil. In A2, he begins to expound the lemma, explaining that sinning unknowingly is as if sinning in the night, and sinning knowingly is as if sinning in the day. In A3, Augustine introduces the concepts of lighter and graver—not yet having told us that he is speaking about temptation—saying that the two former objects (that is, the fear and the arrow) are lighter, and the two latter (that is, the business, and the invasion and the noonday devil) are graver. In A4, he then returns to the first part of the lemma, reading its objects as “temptation” and applying his schema to it: he explains that the psalmist calls light temptation that occurs in the unknowing the terror of the night and light temptation that occurs in the knowing the arrow that flies in the day.  In A5, Augustine describes what these lighter temptations are like: they are not so pressing that they compel a person but can quickly pass by the things they have skirted. In A6 and A7, we then get an account of what kind of people succumb to the graver temptations. In A8, he explains the significance of the label ‘noonday’ and how the scorching midday signifies the intensity of the onslaught of the temptation. (Here, only Pseudo-Haimo has a different reading, namely that the noonday signifies that the temptation is clear, aperta). After this follows a long section where only a few individual phrases are picked up by one or two commentators, and, finally, at A10, we                                                 7 AVG. in psalm. 90,7 serm. I. (CCSL 39, 1259–1261.) Translations of the relevant snippets will be given below. 

--- [p.186] ---

 
 
179 have a snippet found in Gilbert P, Pseudo-Bede, and the Gloss, referring back to the first verse of the psalm, Qui habitat in adiutorio Altissimi (He that dwells in the aid of the most High).  The considerable overlap between our commentaries as to which parts of this lengthy patristic passage they borrow strongly suggests that their resemblance is due to more than simply an independent use of Augustine. A second feature gives further support to this suggestion: Augustine’s application of his schema to the first two objects of the lemma is structured, brief, and, more importantly, explicit. For the application of the schema to the last two objects, however, we only receive an instruction to “repeat and make the same ones grave.” (“Eadem rursus fac grauia.”) In A6 and A7, Augustine focuses instead on the characteristics of grave temptation itself and the people who are plagued by it, without having spelled out the last two correlations between lemma and interpretation. All five commentaries of our subsection, however, apply the schema explicitly to each of the four objects: Ps.-Haimo (PH1a) Et quia circumdabit te, ideo non timebis a timore nocturno, … (PH1c) scilicet a levi et occulta temptatione. (PH2a) Et etiam non timebis a sagitta volante in die, (PH2b) id est a levi temptatione et manifesta, (PH3a) et etiam non timebis a negotio perambulante in tenebris, (PH3b) id est ab aliqua gravi temptatione et occulta, … (PH6a) Et etiam non timebis ab incursu et daemonio meridiano, (PH6b) id est ab incursu daemonii meridiani, (PH6c) scilicet a gravissima et manifesta temptatione,8 …9 (And because [his truth] shall compass you, therefore you shall not be afraid of the terror of the night, … namely, of light and hidden temptation. Nor shall you be afraid of the arrow that flies in the day, that is, of light and evident [temptation], nor shall you be afraid of the business that walks about in the dark, that is, of grave and hidden temptation, … Nor shall you be afraid of the invasion and the noonday devil, that is, the invasion of the noonday devil, namely, of very grave and evident temptation, …)  Glossa O a timore nocturno/ a levi temptatione quae fit ignorantibus 
                                                8 scilicet … temptatione] id est non timebimus a gravissima persecutione manifesta Migne 9 (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. 90,5–6. (Troyes, BM, 904, fol. 101rv; PL Database vol. 116, cols 509D–510A.)

180 (of the terror of the night/ of light temptation which happens to the unknowing)  a sagitta volante in die/ a levi quae scientibus (of the arrow that flies in the day/ of light [temptation] which [happens to] the knowing)  a negotio perambulante in tenebris/ gravi occulta (of the business that walks about in the dark/ [of] grave hidden)  ab incursu et daemonio meridiano/ gravi aperta10  (of the invasion and the noonday devil/ [of] grave open)  Gilbert P (GP5a) Et est: Circumdabit, et ita non timebis a timore nocturno, (GP5b) id est a levi temptatione quae fit ignoranter peccantibus. (GP6a) Nec a sagitta volante in die, (GP6b) id est a levi quae fit scienter peccantibus. … (GP8a) Non timebis a negotio perambulante in tenebris, (GP8b) id est a gravi temptatione quae fit nescienter peccantibus … (GP11a) Nec etiam timebis ab incursu et daemonio, (GP11b) id est ab incurrente daemonio meridiani, (GP11c) id est a gravi temptatione quae fit scienter peccantibus, …11 (And it says: Shall compass, and thus you shall not be afraid of the terror of the night, that is, of light temptation which happens to those who sin unknowingly. Nor of the arrow that flies in the day, that is, of light [temptation] which happens to those who sin knowingly. You shall not be afraid of the business that walks about in the dark, that is, of grave temptation which happens to those who sin unknowingly … Nor shall you even be afraid of the invasion and the devil, that is, of the invading devil of the noonday, that is, of grave temptation which happens to those who sin knowingly, …)  Remi M (RM6a) Non timebis interius a timore nocturno, (RM6b) id est a persecutione levi quae fit ignorantibus, … (RM8) Non timebis etiam a sagitta volante in die. (RM9a) Per sagittam notatur persecutio levis, … (RM9c) Quod dicit, volante in die, notat eam fieri in illis qui sunt quasi dies, scientes rationem reddere fidei suae; (RM10a) quod dicit, “non timebunt tui a sagitta volante in die,” (RM10b) id est a persecutione levi quae fit scientibus. (RM11a) Non timebis etiam in tuis a negotio perambulante in tenebris, (RM11b) id est a persecutione                                                 10 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 90,5–6.
