181 vehementi, … (RM12a) quae perambulat (RM12b) in tenebris; (RM12c) nescientes (RM12d) temporalia esse contemnenda et aeterna tantum speranda. (RM13a) Non timebis etiam ab incursu et daemonio meridiano, (RM13b) id est ab instantia vehementis persecutionis quae fit scientibus se ad aeterna vocatos; (RM13c) cedunt tamen nimiis molestiis.12  (You shall not be afraid inside of the terror of the night, that is, of light persecution which happens to the unknowing, … You shall not be afraid of the arrow that flies in the day. Through ‘arrow’ light persecution is designated, … That he says, that flies in the day, designates that this happens in those who are like the day, knowing [how] to explain the reason for their faith; that he says, “your people shall not be afraid of the arrow that flies in the day,” that is, of light persecution which happens to the knowing. And you shall not be afraid among your people of the business that walks about in the dark, that is, of the violent persecution … which walks about in the dark; [those] not knowing that temporal things should be spurned and only the eternal hoped for. And you shall not be afraid of the invasion and the noonday devil, that is, of the relentlessness of violent persecution which happens to those who know that they are called to the eternal; nevertheless, they yield to too much distress.)  Ps.-Bede (PB5a) Per timorem itaque nocturnum (PB5b) levis temptatio intelligitur quae fit in ignorantia, … (PB7) Per sagittam volantem in die levis temptatio quae in scientibus fit designatur. … (PB10b) quia illa gravis temptatio quae tam acriter urget ignorantes (PB11a) dicitur bene negotium, … (PB13a) Gravis autem tribulatio quae ingruit scientibus dicitur incursus, …13 (Thus, by ‘terror of the night’ is understood light temptation which happens in ignorance, … Light temptation which happens in the knowing is signified by ‘the arrow that flies in the day’. … because that grave temptation which harries the unknowing so fiercely is aptly called business, … But the grave distress which assaults the knowing is called invasion, …)  These circumstances together make it highly unlikely that our commentaries would have used Augustine independently to arrive at their shared conception of his theory; the more likely scenario is that they all depend, to some degree, on the same intermediary source.  One final detail in the patterns of agreement in our commentaries’ rendering of this Augustinian exegesis must, however, be noted here, and that is their terminology, for the fact is                                                 12 (PS.) REMIG. psalm. 90,5–6. (PL Database vol. 131, col. 627CD.) 13 PS. BEDA psalm. 90,5–6. (PL Database vol. 93, cols 972D–973A.) 

--- [p.189] ---

 
 
182 that only one commentary employs Augustine’s original terms all the way through, namely Gilbert P. Pseudo-Bede is close behind but has substituted the last instance of ‘temptation’ with ‘tribulation’. Remi M, for his part, has replaced ‘temptation’ with ‘persecution’ (a term which also occurs further on in Augustine’s account) throughout. Remi M’s consistent use of this term in his version of the fourfold schema makes him unlikely to be the originator of the comprehensive application of it to the lemma; the same argument applies to Pseudo-Bede. When we turn to Pseudo-Haimo, we find that there is even less original Augustine in his account, for he characterizes the temptations not as happening to the unknowing and the knowing, but as being hidden (occulta) or evident (manifesta). These characterizations appear to have their origin in Remigius, who also clearly depends on Augustine, but who neither explains the theory as carefully as his source nor applies the schema nearly as systematically as our commentaries: apart from these two terms and a couple of other details (also highlighted in bold),14 his exegesis of the verse does not seem to have had any significant influence on our group: Non timebis, o vir iuste, a timore nocturno, id est ab occulta temptatione. A sagitta volante in die. Sagitta, negotium, incursus et daemonium multiformes antiqui hostis designant temptationes, a quibus omnibus virum iustum qui in protectione Dei commoratur invulneratum et inlaesum protectione Dominici clipei promittit futurum. A sagitta, inquit, volante: sagitta volans in die significat temptationem elationis aut cuiuscumque subreptionis in prosperitate, quae volando, id est subtiliter, percutit et sauciat. Iuxta litteram sagitta appellatur quoddam genus serpentis, quod in Lucano iaculum vocatur. Id genus serpentis quicquid obvium habet velut sagitta citissime perforat. A negotio perambulante in tenebris. Negotium perambulans significat assiduitatem peccandi. Sicut enim negotiatio assidue exercetur, ita usus peccandi hominem etiam nescientem ad peccatum 
                                                14 The two other details of Remigius’s account which have made their way into our group are, first, the idea of walking about as signifying persistence: Remigius has “perambulans significat assiduitatem peccandi” (“The business that walks about signifies the frequent repetition of sinning”), which can be compared to Pseudo-Haimo’s “(PH5a) Quod dicit “perambulante” habemus perseuerantiam in illis actibus” (“That he says ‘that walks about’ we understand as persistence in these deeds”) and Pseudo-Bede’s “(PB12a) et est perambulans, (PB12b) id est permanens” (“and it walks about, that is, remains”). The second is the idea that a flying arrow signifies something light because it wounds only lightly. This detail will be discussed further below. 

--- [p.190] ---

 
 
183 illicit. Unde et bene dicit, perambulante in tenebris, pro eo quod etiam nesciendo delinquimus. Ab incursu. Incursus significat impetus temptationum; daemonium vero meridianum <id est> manifestam temptationem.15 (You shall not be afraid, oh just man, of the terror of the night, that is of hidden temptation. Of the arrow that flies in the day. The arrow, the business, [and] the invasion and the devil signify the multiform temptations of the ancient enemy, from all of which [the Psalmist] promises that the just man who abides in the protection of God will be safe and sound under the protection of the Lord’s shield. Of the arrow, he says, that flies: the arrow that flies in the day signifies the temptation of pride or of any loss in prosperity, which [arrow] by flying, that is, subtly, strikes and wounds. According to the letter, a certain kind of snake, which in Lucan is called dart, is called arrow. Whatever this kind of snake has before [it] it pierces very quickly, like an arrow. Of the business that walks about in the dark. The business that walks about signifies the frequent repetition of sinning. For as a business is exercised with frequent repetition, thus the exercise of sin entices also the unknowing man to sin. Wherefore he also appropriately says, walks about in the dark, for the reason that we transgress also unknowingly. Of the invasion. The invasion signifies the onslaught of temptations; the noonday devil [signifies] evident temptation.)   That Pseudo-Haimo cannot, however, be dependent on Remigius alone for his account is evidenced by the fact that he incorporates the Augustinian characterizations of temptation as light or grave, which are not present in Remigius. Thus, despite its Remigian setting, the application of the Augustinian schema to the lemma ties Pseudo-Haimo securely to the rest of the group; it also shows that he is unlikely to be its immediate source. This brings us to the Gloss, which, intriguingly, glosses the first two objects using Augustine’s terms, and the last two using Remigius’s ‘occulta’ for the business, and the term 
                                                15 REMIG. in psalm.I 90,5–6. (Reims, BM, 132, fol. 151r; St. Gallen, SB, Cod. Sang. 312, p. 194.) Apart from one minor addition—irrelevant to our argument—there is no difference between the long and the short version of Remigius here. The long version, with the differences in bold, reads: “Non timebis, o vir iuste, a timore nocturno, id est ab occulta temptatione. A sagitta voante in die. Sagitta, negotium, incursus, et daemonium multiformes antiqui hostis designant temptationes, a quibus omnibus virum iustum, qui in protectione Dei commoratur, invulneratum et inlaesum protectione Domini clipei promittit futurum. A sagitta, inquit, volante. Sagitta volans in die significat temptatione elationis, aut cuiusqumque subreptionis in prosperitate, quae volando, id est subtiliter, et pertranseundo invisibiliter percutit et sauciat. Iuxta litteram sagitta appellatur quoddam genus serpentis, quod in Luncano iaculum vocatur. Id genus serpentis quicquid obvium habet velut sagitta citissime perforat. A negotio perambulante in tenebris. Negotium perambulans significat assiduitate[m] peccandi. Sic enim negotiatio assidue exercetur, ita usus peccandi hominem etiam nescientem ad peccatum illicit. Unde et bene dicit, perambulante in tenebris, pro [eo] videlicet quod etiam nesciendo delinquimus. Ab incursu. Incursus significat impetus temptationum; daemonium vero meridianum manifestam temptationem.” REMIG. in psalm.II 90,5–6. (BAV, Urb. lat. 85, fol. 128v.) 

--- [p.191] ---

 
 
184 ‘aperta’ (‘open, clear’) for the invasion and the noonday devil—a term which, as a synonym of ‘evident’, can be said to be Remigian. In other words, we have four accounts which are harmonized in so far as they use either the Augustinian terms or the Remigian, and then we have the Gloss, which uses both sets. This fact suggests, again, that the compiler of the Gloss is doing just that—compiling from two different sources. The scenario is straightforward and easy to imagine: the compiler looked at Augustine and abridged the passage that we saw above, excising snippet A4, “Temptationem quae fit in ignorantibus leuis, timorem nocturnum appelauit; et temptationem quae fit in scientibus leuis, sagittam uolantem per diem appellauit,” and inserting its constituent parts as interlinear glosses to the lemmata ‘timore nocturno’ and ‘sagitta volante’. He then also looked at Remigius and filled the two empty spaces at ‘negotio perambulante’ and ‘incursu’ etc. with his own combination of Remigius and Augustine, namely “gravi occulta” and “gravi aperta.”  At the same time, we must also take into account the fact that the Gloss is not the commentary that preserves the most original form of the Remigian terminology; Pseudo-Haimo is. It therefore becomes problematic to imagine that the Gloss itself is the point of departure for the two streams of the harmonized uses of the terminology—the Augustinian stream (Gilbert P, Remi M, and Pseudo-Bede) and the Remigian stream (Pseudo-Haimo). We must, instead, imagine that the Gloss is very closely related to the point of departure but not identical to it, and that there is, once again, something at work behind the extant commentaries which explains the patterns of agreement that we observe. And, once more, the indications suggest that Pseudo-Haimo would also occupy a place close to the point of departure, potentially explaining why the commentary was associated with Anselm. If we allow ourselves, furthermore, to posit for a moment that this suggestion is, in fact, correct, and that there is a shared, non-patristic intermediary source to which our commentaries 

--- [p.192] ---

 
 
185 are related in some way, this also bestows a new significance upon Pseudo-Haimo’s comment on “Guibert the mad.” This comment is made as an exemplification of the type of person that succumbs to the fourth form of temptation, namely grave and evident: … (PH6c) scilicet a gravissima et manifesta temptatione, (PH7a) qualis esset (PH7b) persecutio, si aliquis nefandus episcopus praedicaret et16 praeciperet fornicationem vel simoniam, sicut Guibertus17 demens.18  (… namely, of very grave and evident temptation, such as it would be if some reprobate bishop were to preach and teach fornication or simony, like Guibert the mad.)   Augustine, in A7, also describes these people, and, following him, so does Gilbert P, Remi M, and Pseudo-Bede. Here, Pseudo-Bede follows Augustine’s original most closely, saying: (PB16a) Sunt alii bene scientes rationem christianismi sui, (PB16b) quia intelligunt promissiones Dei non esse de hac terra, non esse de hac vita, et omnia terrena esse contemnenda, et coelestia appetenda; (PB16c) sed instat tortor, quia graviter urget, urit, torquet. (PB17) Instat incursus et daemonium meridianum, et ita vincitur.  (There are others who know the reason for their Christianity well, since they understand that God’s promises are not about this world, are not about this life, and that all earthly things are to be spurned and heavenly things desired; but the tormentor pursues, since he harries, burns, and wrenches severely. The invasion and the noonday devil pursue, and thus he is defeated.)  Remi M and Gilbert P are briefer, with the former also preserving some of the same Augustinian elements as Pseudo-Bede:  Remi M … (RM13b) id est ab instantia vehementis persecutionis quae fit scientibus se ad aeterna vocatos; (RM13c) cedunt tamen nimiis molestiis.19 (… that is, of the relentlessness of violent persecution, which happens to those who know that they are called to the eternal; nevertheless, they yield to too much distress.)                                                   16 praedicaret et] om. Troyes, BM, 904 17 Guibertus] om. Troyes, BM, 904 18 (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. 90,6. (Troyes, BM, 904, fol. 101v; PL Database vol. 116, col. 510AB.) 19 (PS.) REMIG. psalm. 90,6. (PL Database vol. 131, col. 627D.) 

--- [p.193] ---

 
 
186 Gilbert P … (GP11c) id est a gravi temptatione quae fit scienter peccantibus, (GP12a) sicut qui (GP12b) persecutore vehementius tormentis instante cedunt.20 (… that is, of grave temptation which happens to those who sin knowingly, like those who yield when the persecutor pursues more violently with torments.)   In light of these adaptations, Pseudo-Haimo’s comment about Guibert may be understood to be a substitution for this last description. If, then, the commentaries do indeed depend on a common source for their adaptation of Augustine, this means that, even if Pseudo-Haimo made his comment several decades after Guibert’s death, as Van den Eynde believes possible, the source he from which he made the substitution—and on which they all depend to some degree—would nevertheless have already had to be in existence, and this, if the Czech study is correct, well before 1130.21 We thus see that, even if Pseudo-Haimo may itself be a later text and a compilation of the opinions of other twelfth-century commentators, and thus not have the same author as the Gloss, the commentary’s attribution to Anselm may still not be a complete fiction: it is possible that Anselm’s teaching, preserved in some form, could be the source of the common core of material shared by our later commentaries. Additional evidence for the claim that our subgroup is connected by something beyond an independent use of the auctoritates is the fact that they all use the same excerpt from Cassiodorus to introduce Augustine’s theory. That this is the case, however, only becomes clear when one takes into account all of their renderings together. Cassiodorus writes:  
                                                20 GILB. PICT. in psalm. 90,6. (Paris, BnF, lat. 439, fol. 117r.) 21 This argument is not affected by the fact that Troyes, BM, 904 omits the name Guibertus: if it should be the case that the manuscript BAV, Reg. lat. 295 has added the name, the same reasoning still applies; the text to which the name was added must still have been in existence before 1130. As Pseudo-Haimo also seems to be particularly closely related to Gilbert P, we may note that the latter cannot have been a source of the former unless Gilbert’s commentary was in existence and available before the 1130s, when Pseudo-Haimo was copied at Olomouc.

187 Sicut dixit superius genera defensionum, ita nunc in his duobus uersibus denuntiat periculorum modos: quia multo gratior salus fit, cum euitata cognoscitur discriminum multitudo.22  (As he stated above the kinds of defences, thus he now in these two verses announces the types of dangers: because salvation becomes much more appreciated when the multitude of hazards which has been avoided is known.) (PL Database vol. 131, col. 627B.) 27 PS. BEDA psalm. 90,5–6. (PL Database vol. 93, col. 972D.) 

--- [p.195] ---

 
 
188 The Gloss follows Cassiodorus most closely, showing, as we have seen before, that it often reproduces the purest form of the patristic material, which indicates once again that, even if it may not be the direct source of our group of commentaries, the Gloss must occupy a place very close to the root of their shared tradition. Gilbert P reproduces the version most similar to the Gloss—only dropping the last part of the Cassiodorean quote—which accords with what we have so far concluded about the relationship between these two. Gilbert P is followed, in turn, by Pseudo-Haimo, who retains the original ‘notavit’ and ‘genera’ but has changed Cassiodorus’s ‘dangers’ to ‘temptations’, presumably to fit the Augustinian terminology; his account is thus more harmonized than the previous two, but we still recognize the Cassiodorean origins of the gloss. When we get to Remi M and Pseudo-Bede, however, the gloss is no longer recognizable as Cassiodorus, but through their similarity to Pseudo-Haimo we nevertheless see that these two commentaries are connected to the tradition in the same way: all three share the words ‘hic’ (“here”) and ‘quatuor’ (“four”), and the latter two, finally, also share (a form of) ‘ponit’ (“he lays down”). The example thus provides further support for the claim that our group is connected by something other than a direct dependence on the Fathers, and that their similarities cannot simply be explained as a shared dependence on the Gloss: once again, something else seems to be at work here of which we can only see indirect traces. Indications of a non-patristic connection underlying our group are also found in the Ordinaria’s continuation of this introductory Cassiodorean marginal gloss, namely the snippet “A timore nocturno, a similitudine periculi, unde timor tantum habetur.”28 (“Of the terror of the 
                                                28 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 90,5. (Laon, BM, 29, fol. 157r; Lund, UB, Med. 4, fol. 86v; Paris, BnF, lat. 105, fol. 119v; Paris, BnF, lat. 106, fol. 127v; Paris, BnF, lat. 442, fol. 113r; Paris, BnF, lat. 8846, fol. 163v; Paris, BnF, lat. 14403, fol. 128r; Paris, BnF, lat. 17213, fol. 133v; Reims, BM, 150, fol. 171v; Troyes 511, fol. 123v; Troyes, BM, 881, fol. 111r; Troyes, BM, 924, fol. 110v; Troyes, BM, 925, fol. 150v; BAV, Vat. lat. 85, fol. 131v; Vat. lat. 86, fol. 120v.) 

--- [p.196] ---

 
 
189 night, of the likeness of danger, wherefore only fear is felt.”) In Pseudo-Bede and Remi M we find, in turn, the following comments: Ps.-Bede … (PB6) per timorem vero levis, quia quamdiu malum aliquod timetur, non tantum patitur aliquis sicut quando infertur.29 (… by ‘fear’ [we understand] light [temptation], since a person, as long as some ill is feared, does not suffer as much as when [that ill] is brought on.)  Remi M (RM7) In timore notatur persecutio levis, quoniam timor solet praecedere factum.30 (By ‘fear’ is signified light persecution, since fear usually precedes the deed.)  Although rather differently worded, all three statements make the same point, namely that fear is a state of mind which occurs before the object of the fear has, in fact, materialized. I have not been able to identify any patristic source for this gloss, and it would thus appear that we have here another sign that our commentaries depend on a shared tradition of magisterial material (even though it is impossible to say which rendering would be the closest to the original source, or perhaps reflects the original itself).31 The variation that we see in the renderings of this material, may, furthermore, be an indication that this magisterial exegesis has its roots in oral lectures, which would further support the suggestion of the magisterial origin of the comment.  Finally, we may note the odd placement of this gloss in the Ordinaria: in all manuscripts except Lund, UB, Med. 4,32 this gloss on fear is attached to the Cassiodorean introductory phrase (“Sicut notavit genera …”).33 As there is no logical connection between these two snippets in                                                 29 PS. BEDA psalm. 90,5. (PL Database vol. 93, col. 972D.) 30 (PS.) REMIG. psalm. 90,5. (PL Database vol. 131, col. 627C.) 31 As we see, glosses like this one complicate Kraebel’s claim that it is Pseudo-Haimo, specifically, who is the Gloss’s magisterial source. 32 In Lund, UB, Med. 4, the gloss “A timore nocturno, a similitudine … habetur” has been separated from the Cassiodorean introductory phrase and placed as its own marginal gloss in the opposite margin. In Troyes, BM, 924, it has also been separated from Cassiodorus, but only by a pilcrow after which it follows directly. 33 It is a marginal gloss which, as a whole, consists of Augustine’s reading of the previous lemma, ‘His truth shall compass you with a shield’ (‘Scuto circumdabit te veritas eius’), followed by Cassiodorus’s reading of the same 

--- [p.197] ---

 
 
190 terms of their content, there is no obvious reason why the compiler of the Gloss would have attached the gloss on fear to Cassiodorus’s introductory phrase unless the source he was excerpting from had already done so. The implication of the placement of this gloss is thus that the compiler of the Ordinaria was using a magisterial source which included all of the material in this particular gloss, and which was also used by Remi M and Pseudo-Bede.  We find further evidence of the existence of a magisterial source, as well as support for the claim that the Gloss is not the shared intermediary source of our tradition, when we note that Pseudo-Bede and Remi M share two additional glosses of the same kind as the gloss on fear—that is to say, additional and slightly more mundane explanations of how the Augustinian exegesis fits the objects of the lemma. The first of these, explaining why the arrow represents something light, seems to have been inspired by Remigius, who expresses the idea that a flying arrow wounds lightly, “sagitta … quae volando, id est subtiliter, percutit et sauciat”34 (“the arrow … which by flying, that is, subtly, strikes and wounds”). In Remi M and Pseudo-Bede, we find the following glosses: Ps.-Bede … (PB8c) quia sagitta volatili non tantum quis laeditur quantum si cominus gladio feriatur.35 (… since a person is not wounded as much by a flying arrow as if he is struck up close by a sword.)   Remi M … (RM9b) quia sagitta levius vulnerat quam gladius.36 (… since an arrow wounds more lightly that a sword.)                                                 lemma and then his introduction of the next lemma: “Veritas Dei est quae non respicit personas nec miscet sperantes de se ipsis cum his qui sperant in Deo, sed discernit humiles et superbos. Vel veritas incarnatio Christi. Unde: Veritas de terra orta est [Ps. 84:12], quae et scapulis obumbrat et scuto defendit. Sicut notavit genera …” (“God’s truth is that which does not consider people nor mix those who rely on themselves with those who rely on God, but separates the humble from the proud. Or the truth is the incarnation of Christ. Whence: Truth is sprung out of the earth, which overshadows you with his shoulders and defends [you] with a shield. As he noted the kinds …”) 34 REMIG. in psalm. 90,5. (Reims, BM, 132, fol. 151r; St. Gallen, SB, Cod. Sang. 312, p. 194.) 35 PS. BEDA psalm. 90,5. (PL Database vol. 93, cols 972D–973A.) 36 (PS.) REMIG. psalm. 90,5. (PL Database vol. 131, col. 627C.) 

--- [p.198] ---

 
 
191 The comparison to the sword (gladius) unites the two beyond an independent reliance on Remigius,37 and, since only Pseudo-Bede preserves Remigius’s specific connection between lightly wounding and flying (“volando”/“volatili”), we may also conclude that his account does not depend directly on Remi M. For the third object of the lemma—the business—Remi M and Pseudo-Bede use an etymology found in Isidore, who writes, “Et dictum negotium quasi nec otium, id est sine otio.”38 (“And it is called business as if ‘nor leisure’, that is, ‘without leisure’”; the play on words here is lost in translation.) Our commentators incorporate this in the following way: Ps.-Bede … (PB10b) quia illa gravis temptatio quae tam acriter urget ignorantes (PB11a) dicitur bene negotium, (PB11b) id est “negans otium,” (PB11c) quia omnem quietem aufert, …39 (… because that grave temptation which harries the unknowing so fiercely is aptly called business, that is, “rejecting leisure,” because it takes away all peace, …   Remi M … (RM11b) id est a persecutione vehementi (RM11c), quae pro magnitudine negotium quasi “negans otium” dicitur, …40 (… that is, of the violent persecution, which because of its magnitude is called business, as if “rejecting leisure,” …)  Neither of these glosses appears in the Ordinaria and thus show that our continuous commentaries are linked by some other means.41

BEDA psalm. 90,6. (PL Database vol. 93, col. 973A.) 40 (PS.) REMIG. psalm. 90,6. 192 As it happens, Bruno, too, uses the Isidorean etymology for the same lemma:  (BC8a) Negotium enim actus aliquis dicitur, (BC8b) qui cum summo studio et labore perficitur, (BC8c) unde dicitur negotium quasi “negans otium.”42  (For the act of any person, which is performed with the greatest energy and labour, is called business, wherefore business is called as if “rejecting leisure.”)  If taken on its own, the incorporation of this etymology could, of course, have been done independently, but when we note that Bruno agrees with both Remi M and Pseudo-Bede—against the original Isidore—in their shared use of the present participle ‘negans’ for Isidore’s conjunction ‘nec’, the correspondence seems unlikely to be coincidental; instead, it appears to be one in a growing line of conspicuous agreements between our commentaries.43 Another indication that these agreements are more than coincidental is the fact that several commentaries in our group—this time, including also Bruno and Remi A (see 174n2)—associate the concept of business, via action, to the idea of hard work: Ps.-Haimo (PH4a) Per negotium accipimus quoscunque gravissimos actus ad alicuius seductionem, (PH4b) ut haeretici graviter laborant ut aliquem iustum decipiant.44  (By ‘business’ we understand whatever very grave acts [that are aimed] at leading someone astray, as the heretics work hard to deceive any just person.)   
                                                die, notat eam fieri in illis qui sunt quasi dies, scientes rationem reddere fidei suae; …”; “(RM14d) quia sciebant reddere rationem christianismi.” 42 (PS.) BRUNO CARTHUS. psalm. 90,6. (PL Database vol. 152, col. 1127B.) 43 We may compare our commentaries’ use of Isidore’s etymology with that of Gerhoch of Reichersberg, who uses Gilbert P extensively for his Psalms-commentary: Gerhoch does not use the etymology here, but at Psalm 70:15, where he comments on an alternative translation of ‘quoniam non cognovi litteraturam’ (‘because I have not known learning’), namely ‘quoniam cognovi negotiationem’ (‘because I have not known business’). Unlike our commentaries, Gerhoch includes here the original Isidore as an alternative reading to the one presented by our subsection: “quia negotium quasi ‘negans otium’ vel quasi ‘nec otium’ dicitur.” GERHOH. in psalm. 70,15. (PL Database vol. 194, col. 296C.) In other words, yes, any Psalms-commentator may have used the same etymology independently, but there would, in such a case, most likely be some differences, as Gerhoch’s use demonstrates. 44 (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. 90,6. (Troyes, BM, 904, fol. 101v; PL Database vol. 116, col. 510A.) 

--- [p.200] ---

 
 
193 Bruno  (BC8a) Negotium enim actus aliquis dicitur, (BC8b) qui cum summo studio et labore perficitur: …”45 (For the act of any person, which is performed with the greatest zeal and labour, is called business, …)  Remi A (RA3a) Et non timebis a negotio perambulante in tenebris, (RA3b) id est ab eadem sententia (RA3c) cum magno exercitio inventa, (RA3d) tamen penitus existente in ignorantia.46 (And you shall not be afraid of the business that walks about in the dark, that is, of the same interpretation which is devised with great effort, but is nevertheless deep down situated in ignorance.)  Pseudo-Haimo thus states that the heretics “work hard”, Bruno that business is an action that is performed “with the greatest zeal and labour,” and Remi A that the sententia he is speaking about is devised “with great effort.” Remi M contains what appears to be a vestige of this idea in his explanation, saying that the magnitude of the persecution is the reason why ‘business’ can be read as “rejecting leisure”: Remi M (RM11a) Non timebis etiam in tuis a negotio perambulante in tenebris, (RM11b) id est a persecutione vehementi, (RM11c) quae pro magnitudine negotium quasi negans otium dicitur; …47 (You shall not be afraid in [for] your people of the business that walks about in the dark, that is of the violent persecution, which because of the magnitude of the business is called as if “rejecting leisure; …”)  This connection between business and hard work has a precedent in Cassiodorus, who expounds the lemma as, “Negotium in tenebris, cum pravo studio perquiritur” (“The business in 
                                                45 (PS.) BRUNO CARTHUS. psalm. 90,6. (PL Database vol. 152, col. 1127C.) 46 PS. REMIG. I sup. psalt. 90,6. (Admont, SB, 99, fol. 116r.) 47 (PS.) REMIG. psalm. 90,5. (PL Database vol. 131, col. 627D.) As the leap from “magnitude of persecution” (as in “the magnitude of the work of persecution”) to “rejecting leisure” is a larger one than the one from simply “work” (labor/exercitium) to “rejecting leisure”—which concepts are, indeed, synonymous—Remi M’s explanation is, again, unlikely to be the source of any of the other three. 

--- [p.201] ---

 
 
194 the dark is sought with twisted zeal”).48 That several different commentators would cite for any particular lemma the exegesis of a Church Father on that very lemma is, of course, not surprising, but while Cassiodorus’s mention of zeal is not essential to his point, our commentaries share the fact that they are making an explicit connection between the concepts of business and hard work. And again, taken together with the rest of the agreements that we see in our group, it is becoming quite impossible to imagine that these are all coincidental.  All of these examples point in the same direction, namely that not only do our commentaries, in fact, belong together in a specific family tree, but that this family tree has its roots in some form of intermediary adaptation of the original patristic material. We have, furthermore, seen some indications that there is also magisterial material present in their shared tradition, and that this material in particular shows signs of having been transmitted orally or through different reportationes of the same teaching. In the following, we shall look at whether one of our commentaries could itself be this magisterial intermediary source.  ROOT OR BRANCH? A CASE FOR THE SHAPE OF THE FAMILY TREE By now it has become sufficiently clear that our eight commentaries belong together in a specific family tree of twelfth-century Psalms-exegesis. Several analyses of passages have, furthermore, been advanced in support of the argument that our commentaries all depend in some way on a shared non-patristic intermediary source. The question which naturally follows is whether one of our commentaries could itself be this shared non-patristic intermediary source. If so, a secure dating of this commentary would, of course, be of paramount importance to settling not only the question of Anselm’s potential authorship of the Gloss (or Pseudo-Haimo), but also of those                                                 48 CASSIOD. in psalm. 90, 6. (CCSL 98, 831.) 

--- [p.202] ---

 
 
195 attributions which have been proposed for our other commentaries, namely of Bruno to Bruno of Rheims, of Pseudo-Bede to Manegold of Lautenbach, and of Gilbert U to Gilbert the Universal.49  This question becomes even more pertinent considering that a case for such a scenario was, in fact, made by Van den Eynde in the follow-up to his 1954 article.50 Upon having discovered Remi A and its connection to some of the commentaries discussed in his original article, Van den Eynde revisits here the question of the textual relationships of the group, arguing, first, that Remi A is the direct source of Bruno, Remi M, and Gilbert U, and second, that Remi A must date to the middle of the twelfth century, thus retracting the arguments he made in 1954 for the earlier dates of these three proposed derivatives of Remi A and pushing them quite a bit further into the twelfth-century. As part of my attempt to outline the shape of our family tree, I shall thus address briefly Van den Eynde’s arguments and then advance the thesis that none of our commentaries can, in fact, be the shared intermediary source we have posited. In arguing this thesis, I shall pay particular attention to Remi A. It will also be argued that none of the commentaries can be the sole direct link of any other to the family tree and that, apart from Gilbert P and the Gloss, they are all more likely to be end-points rather than connection-points in our tree.  For each one of Remi M, Bruno, and Gilbert U, Van den Eynde presents a number of arguments for the priority of Remi A. Suffice it to say that, except for the formalist arguments made for Remi M,51 none of these are conclusive: most of them are based on his perception of                                                 49 See Introduction. 50 Van den Eynde, “Complementary Note.” 51 Contrary to Geiselmann’s theory that Remi M represents the original from Psalm 143:10 and onward, Van den Eynde argues that Remi A represents the original in both cases. For the first passage (Ps. 21:12–30), we have the undeniable proof, discovered by Geiselmann, that Remi M makes a “false reference” to “the aforementioned humility” (praedictam humilitatem) the first mention of which the commentator has, however, neglected to include. (Remi A, on the other hand, does include the original mention of the humility in question.) For the second passage (Ps. 143:10–150), Van den Eynde points out that only the favourite introductory formulae of Remi A (“Vel ita” and “Hoc est quod dicit”) occur, while those of Remi M (“Aliter” and “Ac si/quasi dicat”) are entirely lacking. Taken together with the evidence for the passage at Psalm 21, this argument seems convincing enough. This does not, however, mean that Remi A must itself be the direct source of Remi M. 

--- [p.203] ---

 
 
196 improvement, which, as we have seen, is something about which personal opinions may differ.52 Van den Eynde’s arguments for dating Remi A to the middle of the twelfth century, likewise, rely 
                                                52 See, for example, Chapter Three, 132n79. For his case for Remi A’s priority over Bruno, all of Van den Eynde’s arguments are of this nature: his view is, for example, that “several times, Bruno corrects the exaggerated theological statements of [Remi A].” There is, however, no reason why these could not be cases of Remi A exaggerating the correct theological statements of Bruno. As his coup de grâce, he points to a passage where the two commentaries quote a definition of virtue which he identifies as coming from Augustine: “Nunc ergo illud attende, utrum tibi videatur virtus aequalitas quaedam esse vitae, rationi undique consentientis.” (“Look now, therefore, to whether virtue seems to you to be a certain equanimity of a life which agrees in all respects with reason.”) AVG. quant. anim. 16, 27. (PL Database vol. 32, col. 1050.) While Remi A refers to the source of the quotation as “certain people” (quidam), Bruno tags the originators as “the philosophers” (philosophi), bespeaking clearly, according to Van den Eynde, “the ignorance and embarrassment of the plagiarist,” that is, Bruno. (Van den Eynde, “Complementary Note,” 154.) However, the quotation bears a certain resemblance to a passage of Seneca, whose Epistulae Morales, according to Marcia Colish, was a monastic favourite in the twelfth century. (Marcia L. Colish, The Stoic Tradition from Antiquity to the Early Middle Ages, vol. 1, Stoicism in Classical Latin Literature [Leiden: Brill, 1985], 19.) In his Letter 31 we find the definition, “Huc et illud accedat, ut perfecta virtus sit, aequalitas ac tenor vitae per omnia consonans sibi, quod non potest esse, nisi rerum scientia contingit et ars, per quam humana ac divina noscantur.” (“To this is added, in order that virtue be perfect, an equanimity and direction of life which is in harmony with itself through everything, which cannot be, unless knowledge of things is present, and the skill through which the human and divine are known.”) SEN. epist. 31:8. Although the shared quotation in question clearly is excerpted from Augustine, as Van den Eynde points out, and is preserved more fully—although not more accurately—by Remi A, one could, nevertheless, argue that Bruno is not entirely incorrect in his specification of the quote as coming from the philosophers. At the very least, it does not prove that Bruno must have borrowed from Remi A: as Van den Eynde himself notes Bruno’s rendering of the quotation is closer to Augustine’s original than is Remi A’s. However, Remi A’s adaptations he simply dismisses as scribal errors and concludes that Bruno had a different copy of Remi A before him, but, once again, the scenario of a shared intermediary source explains the patterns just as well. We should also note that Geiselmann finds places where Remi M and Bruno agree against Remi A, which speaks not for the priority of Remi A, but for the scenario of a shared source. (Geiselmann, “Zur frühmittelalterlichen Lehre,” 387.) For Gilbert U, Van den Eynde contents himself with arguing that, since “Remi A is a remarkably personal Commentary, [but Gilbert U] is a real mosaic of pieces taken word for word … from the Glossae of Anselm of Laon, Gilbert de la Porrée, Letbert of Lille and others,” the latter must necessarily be directly dependent on Remi A as well. (Van den Eynde, “Complementary Note, 157.”) For the actual parallels which he cites, however, he gives no arguments for why the dependence must go in that particular direction; for most of the parallels, this is, in fact, impossible to determine, as they are more or less identical. We may, however, mention one instance where Gilbert U must be said to make superior sense, complicating the idea that Remi A must necessarily represent the original: apropos of the four orders in the resurrection, Gilbert U writes, “Non iudicandi alii, sancti perfecti iudicantes falsos christianos, id est haereticos in comparatione sui.” (“Some [of those] not to be judged [are] the perfect saints who judge the false Christians, that is, the heretics compared to themselves.) GILB. UNIV. sup. psalt. 1,5. (Laon, BM, 17, fol. 3v.) Remi A, however, writes, “Non iudicandi alii sancti perfecti iudicantes falsos christianos et Iudeos in comparatione sui, …” (“Some [of those] not to be judged [are] the perfect saints who judge the false Christians and the Jews compared to themselves, …”) PS. REMIG. I sup. psalt. 1,5. (Admont, SB, 99, fol. 3r.) Gilbert U’s statement makes sense: a false Christian would be someone who claims to be Christian, but distorts Christian doctrine—that is to say, a heretic; and distortion—and hence heresy—is something that comes in degrees of comparison. (Or, if one wants to read, “false Christians—that is, heretics—in comparison to themselves,” and say that falsehood is what comes in degrees.) What Remi A says, however, is that the saints will judge those who are Jews in comparison to themselves, which, of course, makes no sense, as being a Jew is not a matter of degree. It seems perfectly reasonable that what could have happened is that Remi A has had a source in front of him which read, “the false Christians compared to themselves,” and that the copyist decided to insert “the Jews” into that otherwise intelligible sentence; Gilbert U’s source may have looked exactly the same, but he instead decided to clarify who the false Christians are with an “id est” explaining that they are the heretics. We thus see that Van den Eynde’s conclusions about the direction of influence are too hasty. (For his argument in the case of Remi M, see 195n51.) 

--- [p.204] ---

 
 
197 entirely on the doctrinal content of the commentary, which is a type of argument which he himself notes can easily be brought into question.53 But regardless of whether Van den Eynde’s claims about the advanced nature of the doctrinal content of these commentaries are correct—they may very well be—it is the contention that Remi A must be the source of three of the others which I shall raise objections to here. I shall, furthermore, argue that the same is true for the rest of our commentaries: I intend to show that none of them could have been the sole direct source of any one of the others—in other words, that none of our commentaries could be the shared intermediary source which would explain the affinity that exists between the others. Several points to this effect have already been made, chiefly in regard to Pseudo-Haimo, but also to the Gloss: we have seen, for example, that the Gloss does not reproduce the framework of the three deaths of the soul nor the same comprehensive version of the four orders at the resurrection; neither does, for that matter, Gilbert P, nor, in the case of the three deaths of the soul, Bruno. While Pseudo-Haimo does include these features, we have seen that he, in both cases, uses terminology that differs from the rest, indicating that he is not the shared intermediary source for the others of the material in question. In terms of terminology, we have also noted that Remi M and Pseudo-Bede show some differences from the others, indicating that neither of them could be responsible for transmitting the shared material to the others. 
                                                53 Van den Eynde, “Complementary Note,” 165. He refers here to Landgraf’s belief that certain “Abelardian” doctrines were being taught before Abelard himself embarked on his teaching career in the 1120s. Whether Landgraf’s belief about these “Abelardian” doctrines is correct or not is not of any consequence to our argument, as I am not trying to prove either the authenticity or the early date of any of the commentaries in our group—quite the contrary, as I am arguing here that all of them go back to a shared, non-patristic intermediary source which would, naturally, predate all of our commentaries. Any descendant of such a shared ancestor may have been produced at any later time, and about these dates I make no claims here. We may, however, point out that Van den Eynde himself concedes that, even though such doctrines as are found in Bruno and Remi A were not dreamt of in 1050–75, when master Bruno was still teaching at Rheims and had not yet become “the Carthusian,” they must, at the turn of the century, have gained footing in the cathedral schools. (Van den Eynde, “Complementary Note,” 165; my emphasis.) Geiselmann, however, places the sacramental doctrine of the Eucharist which is found in Remi A at the beginning to middle of the eleventh century (see Geiselmann, “Zur frühmitteralterlichen Lehre,” 387–88). In other words, to place a commentary squarely on one or the other side of 1150 on doctrinal grounds thus, again, seems rather precarious. 

--- [p.205] ---

 
 
198 If we turn, instead, to Remi A, we may point out that only this commentary, together with the Gloss and Pseudo-Haimo, leaves out the concept of delectatio in its account of the three stages of sin. (Pseudo-Haimo, however, includes voluntas as an overarching category, which connects him to Gilbert P.) For more or less every commentary, we have thus already had some indication that it could not responsible for transmitting the shared material in question to the others—the exception being Gilbert U. To strengthen this argument, and as this particular section of text provides a rare opportunity to bring all of the eight commentaries into the discussion at the same time, we shall look at the accessus and exegesis of Psalm 2:1–2, Why have the Gentiles roared, and the people devised vain things? The kings of the earth stood by, and the princes met together, against the Lord and against his Christ (Quare fremuerunt gentes, et populi meditati sunt inania? Astiterunt reges terrae, et principes convenerunt in unum adversus Dominum, et adversus Christum eius).54 We shall begin by examining the commentaries’ discussion of the accessus-item of order, ordo.55  The two Psalms-authorities that address the ordo of Psalm 2 are Jerome and Cassiodorus.56 Although there are some differences in which of these authorities the different commentators                                                 54 ‘Roared’ is a literal translation of ‘fremuerunt’, which is translated in the Douay-Rheims version as “raged”; this translation, however, obscures the point of our commentaries’ exegesis of the lemma. The translation of ‘gentes’ as “the Gentiles” has been kept even though it is also simply a synonym for ‘people’. ‘Stood by’ is a literal translation of ‘astiterunt’, which is translated in the Douay-Rheims version as “stood up’.” As this verb, however, also becomes a point of explanation in some of our commentaries, I have translated it according to its literal meaning. 55 Hunt divides the accessus into four types based on what headings they include; although there is great variety in which headings are included in different commentaries, and although these rarely can be sorted straightforwardly into any one of the types, our commentaries clearly display individual variations on the C type accessus, which includes the headings title (titulus), purpose (intentio), order (ordo), author (nomen auctoris), part of philosophy (pars philosophiae), and usefulness (utilitas; not included by any of our commentators); the C type also often includes subject matter (materia), and method (modus agendi; not included as such by our commentators, but found in Gilbert P as modus loquendi; furthermore, some of these headings are discussed only in the main prologue of our commentaries, and some—usually titulus, materia, and intentio—are taken up at each psalm). We may also note that none of our commentaries displays the distinction between ars extrinsecus (“the art outwardly”) and ars intrinsecus (“the art inwardly”), which, according to Hunt, emerges in the second half of the twelfth century, and which can be seen in an early form in the organization of the headings in Lombard’s Psalms-commentary (see Hunt, “Accessus,” 105–106). 56 The short version of Remigius repeats Cassiodorus’s point in abridged form (“Et nota quia hic psalmus primus est eorum qui habent titulos.” [“And note that this psalm is the first of those that have titles.”] REMIG. in psalm.I 2, 

--- [p.206] ---

 
 
199 make the most use of, there are a few details which reveal that they are connected to each other by some link other than the two Fathers independently or via the Gloss. Each one of the commentaries also contains some omission of an item or interpretation that is shared by all, or nearly all, of the rest of the group, showing that the commentary in question could not have been the shared source of the others.57 The question for the topic of the ordo is whether Psalm 2, Quare fremuerunt, should be counted as the first or the second psalm, as it appears to be the second in order but is, according to Cassiodorus—and, consequently, our commentaries—referred to in Acts as the first psalm. The passage referred to by Cassiodorus is Acts 13:33, where the early Greek manuscript Codex Bezae preserves the following reading: “For thus it is written in the first psalm, Thou art my Son; this day have I begotten thee.”58 (The Vulgate, however, has “in the second psalm.”) Cassiodorus solves the apparent contradiction in the following way: Quamuis in quibusdam codicibus nec iste psalmus habere titulum comprobetur, propter quod in Actibus apostolorum legitur: Sicut in primo psalmo dictum est: Quare fremuerunt gentes et reliqua., tamen si diligenter intendas, non uidetur esse contrarium. Dicitur enim in primo psalmo positum, sed qui per inscriptionem tituli primus habendus est, est enim ipse atque idem secundus in ordine, primus in titulo.59 (Even though this psalm in some manuscripts is attested to not have a title either [i.e. like Psalm 1]—for which reason it says in the Acts of the Apostles: as in the first psalm is written: Why have the Gentiles roared etc.—if you nevertheless pay close attention, it does not appear [to be] contradictory. For it is said that [this] is found in the first psalm, but [in] the one which should be considered [to be] the first due to its inscription of a title; for the one and the same is the second in order, [but] the first [to have] a title.)                                                  acces. [Reims, BM, 132, fol. 2r; St. Gallen, SB, Cod. Sang. 312, p. 5]), but neither Augustine nor Hilary addresses the topic. 57 The weakest case here is Gilbert U, who contains almost all of the shared items and makes only one rather minor omission (the specification of the book of Acts in the discussion of the ordo). We shall, however, return to his case below and show there that he cannot be the shared intermediary source of the others. 58 ACTS (Bezae), 70. On the codex Bezae, see J. Neville Birdsall, “The New Testament Text,” in The Cambridge History of the Bible, vol. 1, From the Beginnings to Jerome, ed. Peter R. Ackroyd and Christopher F. Evans, 308–377 (Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 1970), 355. 59 CASSIOD. in psalm. 2,1. (CCSL 97, 39–40.) 

--- [p.207] ---

 
 
200 This solution is referred to in various degrees of comprehensiveness by the Gloss, Gilbert P, Gilbert U, Bruno, and Pseudo-Bede: Glossa O ¶ Iste psalmus secundus in ordine, primus in titulo, unde in Actibus apostolorum dicitur primus.60 (¶ This psalm [is] the second in order, [but] the first [to have] a title, wherefore it is called the first in the Acts of the Apostles.)  Gilbert P (GP1a) Iste psalmus est secundus in ordine, primus in titulo, (GP1b) et tamen in Actibus apostolorum dicitur primus.61 (This psalm is the second in order, [but] the first [to have] a title, and it is, nevertheless, called the first in the Acts of the Apostles.)  Gilbert U (GU2a) Usus tamen obtinuit esse duos, minime in hoc contrarius beato Petro, qui vocat eum primum (GU2b) quantum ad titulum. (GU3) Intitulatur enim primus omnium aliorum Psalmus David. (GU4a) Beatus vir vero non vocat psalmum, quia est quasi titulus aliorum, …62 (Common practice, however, has maintained that they are two [different psalms], not at all, in this, in contradiction to blessed Peter, who calls it the first in regard to its title. For the first of all the other [psalms] is titled A Psalm of David. Indeed, [the psalmist] does not call Blessed is the man a psalm, since it is like a title of the others, …)  Bruno (BC2a) Dicitur enim (BC2b) in Actibus apostolorum secundus iste psalmus primus, quod facile solvitur: (BC3a) titulatorum enim psalmorum primus est, (BC3b) cum primus titulo careat. (BC4a) Quantum vero ad ordinem psalmorum secundus, …63 (For this second psalm is called the first in the Acts of the Apostles, which is easily solved: for it is the first of the psalms with a title, since the first psalm lacks a title. But in regard to the order of the psalms [it is] the second, …)  
                                                60 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 2. (Laon, BM, 29, fol. 2v; Lund, UB, Med. 4 deficit; Paris, BnF, lat. 105, fol. 2r; Paris, BnF, lat. 106, fol. 2r; Paris, BnF, lat. 442, fol. 3r; Paris, BnF, lat. 8846, fol. 7r; Paris, BnF, lat. 14403, fol. 2r; Paris, BnF, lat. 17213, fol. 6v; Reims, BM, 150, fol. 1v; Troyes, BM, 511, fol. 1v; Troyes, BM, 881, fol. 5r; Troyes, BM, 924, fol. 1v; Troyes, BM, 925, fol. 3v; BAV, Vat. lat. 85, fol. 1v; BAV, Vat. lat. 86, fol. 5v.) 61 GILB. PICT. in psalm. 2, acces. (Paris, BnF, lat. 439, fol. 2v.) 62 GILB. UNIV. sup. psalt. 2, acces. (Laon, BM 17, fol. 3v.) 63 (PS.) BRUNO CARTHUS. psalm. 2, acces. (PL Database vol. 152, col. 643CD.) 

--- [p.208] ---

 
 
201 Ps.-Bede (PB9b) quod in Actibus apostolorum (PB9c) hic psalmus a Petro primus dicitur. (PB10a) Nam primus dicitur, non quantum ad priorem, (PB10b) qui quasi titulus est omnium aliorum, (PB10c) et ideo titulo caret, (PB10d) sed quantum ad intitulatos psalmos sequentes, …64 (… that this psalm is called the first by Peter in the Acts of the Apostles. For it is called the first not in regard to the preceding [psalm], which is like a title of all the others, and therefore lacks a title, but in regard to the following, titled psalms, …)  What we may observe here is, first, that the Gloss and Gilbert P are closest to the original Cassiodorus, and could, as such, be dependent on him directly. The others are, however, neither getting their Cassiodorus directly from the original patristic source nor from the Gloss or Gilbert P: we see this in the fact that they all share the phrase ‘quantum ad’, “in regard to,” which suggests that they are getting their Cassiodorus from the same intermediary source rather than from Cassiodorus independently of each other. (It will soon be demonstrated that none of them can depend solely on one of the others.)65 Bruno is, however, the only one who mentions ordo explicitly, while the other two paraphrase (with slightly different outcomes in meaning).66 While this puts Bruno closer to the original Cassiodorus (or to the Gloss and Gilbert P), he can, nevertheless, not be the source of the other two: this is seen in the fact that he omits one conspicuous item which is shared by all the others except the Gloss and Gilbert P, namely the comment that it is Peter who calls Psalm 2 the first psalm in Acts (see also passages above): 
                                                64 PS. BEDA psalm. 2, acces. (PL Database vol. 93, col. 490B.) 65 As a couple of preliminary observations, we may mention that Gilbert U and Pseudo-Bede share the comment that Psalm 1 is like a title for the other psalms. This agreement is unique to the two and shows that neither can simply be directly dependent on Bruno. The possibility that one has borrowed directly from the other will seem more and more unlikely as the argument progresses. We may also note that Bruno and Pseudo-Bede share the comment that Psalm 1 lacks a title. A similar comment is found in Remi A (see 207n90), but in somewhat different wording. This is another indication of the less than straightforward relationships that exist between our commentaries. 66 While Pseudo-Bede preserves the idea that there is a difference between Psalm 1 and the rest due to the fact that Psalm 1 does not have a title—that is to say, between the order of the psalms and the psalms that have a title—Gilbert U interprets “quantum ad titulum” as meaning that Psalm 1 is like a title for the rest and therefore does not count as a psalm. We thus see how, even though they are working with the same pieces of text—such as the comment that Psalm lacks a title, and that it is like a title for the others—our commentators arrive at rather different solutions to the exegetical problems presented to them. 

--- [p.209] ---

 
 
202 Ps.-Haimo (PH10a) Quare fremuerunt gentes. Petrus loquens (PH10b) in Actibus apostolorum hunc psalmum dicit primum esse.67  (Why have the Gentiles roared. Peter speaking in the Acts of the Apostles calls this psalm the first.)  Remi A (RA8a) Multi volunt unum psalmum esse Quare fremuerunt cum superiori (RA8b) confirmantes id auctoritate sancti Petri, (RA8c) qui vocat eum primum in Actibus apostolorum.68  (Many want Why have [the Gentiles] roared to be one with the preceding [psalm] supporting this with the authority of blessed Peter, who calls it the first in the Acts of the Apostles.)  Remi M (RM1) Quidam a Beatus vir usque Beati omnes qui confidunt in eo unum psalmum dicunt esse. (RM2a) Hieronymus hanc rationem dat, quod sit unus, quia a beatitudine incipit et in beatitudine desinit, (RM2b) et beatus Paulus69 unum ostendit (RM2c) in Actibus apostolorum.70 (Some say that from Blessed is the man to Blessed are all they that trust in him is one psalm. Jerome gives the reason that it is one [psalm] because it begins in blessedness and ends in blessedness, and blessed Paul shows in the Acts of the Apostles that it is one.)  As mentioned above, the place in Acts where Psalm 2 is referred to (in certain versions of the early Bible) as the first psalm is Chapter 13, verse 33. However, the speaker here is Paul, not Peter;71 as we see, Remi M has caught the mistake and corrected it, which suggests that none of the other commentaries is dependent on him,72 but that they are instead connected to each other by some other relationship—one that, in this instance, as we have just seen, is not dependent on either Bruno, Remi M, the Gloss, or Gilbert P. 
                                                67 (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. 2,1 (Troyes, BM, 904, fol. 2v; PL Database vol. 116, col. 201B.) 68 PS. REMIG. I sup. psalt. 2, acces. (Admont, SB, 99, fol. 3v.) 69 See the following discussion for this correction. 70 (PS.) REMIG. psalm. 2,1. (PL Database vol. 131, cols 154D–155A.) 71 Peter is not mentioned in Chapter 13 at all. 72 As it is easier to explain why someone would have corrected ‘Peter’ to ‘Paul’ than introduced the error by changing ‘Paul’ to ‘Peter’, this is the more likely scenario. 

--- [p.210] ---

 
 
203 The association of Psalm 2 with Peter is something that our tradition may be getting from Jerome, who, in his introduction to this psalm, declares that Peter’s interpretation of it ought to be the only authoritative one:  Audacis est, hunc psalmum interpretari uelle post Petrum: immo de eo aliud sentire quam in Actis Apostolorum dixerit Petrus.73 (Only the audacious presumes to interpret this psalm after Peter; or indeed, to think anything about it other than what Peter said in Acts.)  Although Jerome does not specify what it is that Peter says about Psalm 2 in Acts, he must be referring to 4:23–30, where we do, indeed, find an exegesis of Psalm 2:1–2,74 and where Peter could be interpreted as at least one of the speakers: And being let go, [Peter and John] came to their own company, and related all that the chief priests and ancients had said to them. When they heard it, they with one accord lifted up their voice to God, and said: Lord, you are he that made heaven and earth, the sea, and all things that are in them. Who, by the Holy Ghost, by the mouth of our father David, your servant, has said: Why have the Gentiles roared, and the people meditated vain things? The kings of the earth stood by, and the princes assembled together against the Lord and his Christ.75 For truly there assembled together in this city against your holy child Jesus, whom you have anointed, Herod, and Pontius Pilate, with the Gentiles and the people of Israel, to do what your hand and your counsel decreed to be done. … And when they had prayed, the place was moved wherein they were assembled; and they were all filled with the Holy Ghost, and they spoke the word of God with confidence.76  Our tradition has thus conflated Jerome’s statement about Peter in Acts 4:23 with Paul’s statement in Acts 13:33. The conflation can be explained by the fact that Jerome, in his commentary on Psalm 1, refers to Acts 13:33 when explaining why Psalm 1 has no title (although without saying anything about Peter):                                                 73 HIER. in psalm. 2. (CCSL 72, 181.) 74 No exegesis is offered at Acts 13:33; Paul only quotes verse 7.  75 Ps 2:1–2. 76 Acts 4:23–31. 

--- [p.211] ---

 
 
204 Quidam dicunt hunc psalmum quasi praefationem esse Spiritus sancti, et ideo titulum non habere; alii, in eo quod primus sit ordinis sui, habere principium, et πλεονασµοῦ esse uitium, eum primum dicere, ante quem nullus sit. Aliter: apud Hebraeos et primus et secundus unus est psalmus, quod in apostolorum quoque Actibus conprobatur. Denique quia a beatitudine coeperat, in beatitudine desiuit, dicens: ‘beati omnes qui confidunt in eum’.77 (Some say that this psalm [i.e. Psalm 1] is like a preface of the Holy Spirit, and therefore has no title; others that, in the fact that it is the first in its order, it has a beginning, and that it is an error of repetition to call the one before which there is no one the first one. In another way: in the Hebrew [version], the first and the second psalm are one, which is attested in Acts. Lastly, because it began in blessedness [and] ended in blessedness, saying, Blessed are they that trust in him.)  What Jerome points out here is that, in the Hebrew version, Psalm 1 and 2 are presented as one unit, which is confirmed in Acts, by which he must be referring to 13:33. The point that Psalm 1 and 2 are viewed by some as one psalm is taken up by Bruno, Gilbert U, Remi A, Remi M, and Pseudo-Bede: Bruno (BC1) Psalmus autem qui hunc sequitur a quibusdam cum superiori dicitur unus, et a quibusdam alius.78  (And the psalm which follows this one [i.e. psalm 1] is said by some to be one with the preceding [psalm], and by others to be different.)  Gilbert U (GU1a) Multi volunt esse unum psalmum Quare fremuerunt cum superiori …79  (Many want Why have [the Gentiles] roared to be one with the preceding [psalm] …)  Remi A (RA8a) Multi volunt unum psalmum esse Quare fremuerunt cum superiori …80  (Many want Why have [the Gentiles] roared to be one with the preceding [psalm] …)  
                                                77 HIER. in psalm. 1. (CCSL 72, 178–179.) 78 (PS.) BRUNO CARTHUS. psalm. 2, acces. (PL Database vol. 152, col. 643C.) 79 GILB. UNIV. sup. psalt. 2, acces. (Laon, BM 17, fol. 3v.) 80 PS. REMIG. I sup. psalt. 2, acces. (Admont, SB, 99, fol. 3v.) 

--- [p.212] ---

 
 
205 Remi M (RM1) Quidam a Beatus vir usque Beati omnes qui confidunt in eo unum psalmum dicunt esse.81 (Some say that from Blessed is the man to Blessed are all they that trust in him is one psalm.)  Ps.-Bede (PB6a) Videtur autem hic psalmus esse idem cum superiori, …82 (For this psalm appears to be the same as the preceding one, …)  That our commentaries’ reliance on Jerome is not independent is clear not only from the fact that they all phrase their discussion in similar wording, but also from the fact that they have all incorporated this material from Jerome’s commentary on Psalm 1 into their exegesis of Psalm 2; their particular blend of Jerome, which results in an unfortunate mistake preserved by the majority of them (including Pseudo-Haimo, even though he does not include this Hieronymian exegesis), shows, once again, that our commentaries (excluding here the Gloss and Gilbert P) are all related through some form of shared, non-patristic ancestry.  So far in this chapter, we have seen indications that neither the Gloss nor Gilbert P, Bruno, or Remi M could be this shared source—the Gloss and Gilbert P on account of omitting ‘quantum ad’, Bruno on the grounds that he does not mention Peter, and Remi M on the grounds that he corrects ‘Peter’ to ‘Paul’. To these two we may add Pseudo-Haimo and Gilbert U—the former because he, although he includes the conflation of Peter and Acts 13:33, has abbreviated the discussion so far that it has lost its main gist, omitting the many verbal agreements we see among the others, and the latter because he is the only one who fails to mention Acts as the book where “Peter” calls Psalm 2 the first psalm. 
                                                81 (PS.) REMIG. psalm. 2,1. (PL Database vol. 131, col. 154D.) 82 PS. BEDA psalm. 2, acces. (PL Database vol. 93, col. 490A.) 

--- [p.213] ---

 
 
206 Pseudo-Bede presents a somewhat trickier case, as he includes all the items shared by the rest. Nevertheless, other commentaries share some verbatim agreements that are not present in this commentary, showing that they are related to each other independently of Pseudo-Bede: Bruno (BC5) Sive83 sit secundus, sive cum primo idem, semper cum superiori continuatur.84  (Whether it is the second or the same as the first, it is always connected to the preceding [psalm].)  Gilbert U (GU1a) Multi volunt esse unum psalmum Quare fremuerunt cum superiori (GU1b) confirmantes auctoritate beati Petri. (GU2a) Usus tamen obtinuit esse duos, minime in hoc contrarius beato Petro, qui vocat eum primum … (GU4b) sed sive sit idem cum superiori sive divisus, tamen pendet a superiori.85  (Many want Why have [the Gentiles] roared to be one with the preceding [psalm] supporting [this] with the authority of blessed Peter. Common practice, however, has maintained that they are two, not at all, in this, in contradiction with blessed Peter, who calls it the first … but whether it is the same as the preceding [psalm] or separate, it nevertheless depends on the preceding [psalm].)  Remi A (RA8a) Multi volunt unum psalmum esse Quare fremuerunt cum superiori (RA8b) confirmantes id auctoritate sancti Petri, (RA8c) qui vocat eum primum in Actibus apostolorum. (RA9a) Usus tamen obtinuit esse duos, minime in hoc contrarius beato Petro, qui vocat eum primum … (RA10) sed sive sit unus cum superiori sive non, tamen pendet a superiori.86  (Many want Why have [the Gentiles] roared to be one with the preceding [psalm] supporting this with the authority of blessed Peter, who calls it the first in the Acts of the Apostles. Common practice, however, has maintained that they are two, not at all, in this, in contradiction with blessed Peter, who calls it the first … but whether it is one with the preceding [psalm] or not, it nevertheless depends on the preceding [psalm].)  
                                                83 sive] si vero Migne 84 (PS.) BRUNO CARTHUS. psalm. 2, acces. (PL Database vol. 152, col. 643D.) 85 GILB. UNIV. sup. psalt. 2, acces. (Laon, BM 17, fol. 3v.) 86 PS. REMIG. I sup. psalt. 2, acces. (Admont, SB, 99, fol. 3v.)

207 Remi M (RM11a) Sive unus psalmus sit cum superiori, sive ab eo diversus, (RM11b) in voce Christi legitur, (RM11c) et continuatur cum superiori ita: …87 (Whether it is one with the preceding psalm, or different from it, it is read in the voice of Christ, and is connected to the preceding [psalm] thus: …)  Ps.-Bede (PB6a) Videtur autem hic psalmus esse idem cum superiori, … (PB9b) quod in Actibus apostolorum (PB9c) hic psalmus a Petro primus dicitur. (PB10a) Nam primus dicitur, non quantum ad priorem, (PB10b) qui quasi titulus est omnium aliorum, (PB10c) et ideo titulo caret, (PB10d) sed quantum ad intitulatos psalmos sequentes, … (PB17a) Continuatur autem hic psalmus superiori, (PB17b) si in voce Capitis ille legatur …88 (For this psalm appears to be the same as the preceding one, … that this psalm is called the first by Peter in the Acts of the Apostles. For it is called the first not in regard to the preceding [psalm], which is like a title of all the other psalms, and therefore lacks a title, but in regard to the following, titled psalms, … And this psalm is connected to the preceding [psalm], if it is read in the voice of the Head …)  Pseudo-Bede also displays several items which are each shared with only one of the other commentaries: he agrees with Remi M in pointing out that Psalm 2 should (or can) be read in the voice of Christ (PB17b, RM11b);89 with Gilbert U he shares the comment that Psalm 1 is like a title for the rest of the psalms (PB10b, GU4a), and with Bruno the comment that Psalm 1 lacks a title (PB10c, B3b).90 One possibility is thus that Pseudo-Bede is himself the source of each of these items for the other commentaries; the verbatim agreements that these display amongst themselves, which are not shared by Pseudo-Bede, would then have to be explained by some relationship between them that is independent of Pseudo-Bede. Another explanation would be that Pseudo-Bede has borrowed each item from each of the other commentaries; this, however,                                                 87 (PS.) REMIG. psalm. 2,1. (PL Database vol. 131, col. 155B.) 88 PS. BEDA psalm. 2, acces. (PL Database vol. 93, cols 490A–490D.) 89 Pseudo-Bede makes this a condition for reading Psalm 2 as a continuation of Psalm 1.  90 Remi A also comments on Psalm 1’s lack of a title, but his point is purely Hieronymian, pertaining only to the combination of Psalm 1 and 2 in the Hebrew tradition: “… (RA9b) ea ratione, quia praecedenti psalmo apud hebraeos (RA9c) sine titulo copulatur, …” (“… for the reason that [Psalm 2], in the Hebrew version, is attached to the preceding psalm without a title, …”) PS. REMIG. I sup. psalt. 2, acces. (Admont, SB, 99, fol. 3v.) 

--- [p.215] ---

 
 
208 seems somewhat unlikely, as we would have to imagine him to be using for his own compilation not only two texts which are already very similar, but at least three, and probably even more.91  The final and more likely scenario is that the items we see in Pseudo-Bede can be accounted for by one or maybe two sources from which our group of commentaries picked some of the same material. More evidence needs, however, to be gathered before we can decide between these scenarios with confidence. At this point we may only conclude that none of the other commentaries can be the sole direct source of Pseudo-Bede. If we finally turn to Remi A, we may make similar observations about this commentary as about Pseudo-Bede: although Remi A and Gilbert U display the largest amount of verbatim agreements, Gilbert U shares some similarities with Bruno and Pseudo-Bede that are not present in Remi A, namely their incorporation into their Hieronymian material of Cassiodorus’s solution of distinguishing between the order of the psalms as such and the order of the psalms which have a title. This suggests that these three commentaries are related to each other independently of Remi A. More information about the status of Remi A—and about and the general shape of the family tree, including the Gloss and Gilbert P—can be gathered from the continuation of their exegesis 
                                                91 Pseudo-Bede shares, for example, only with Pseudo-Haimo the comment that Christ is speaking of himself in the third person: “(PH24) Hic vero Christus de seipso quasi de tertia loquitur.” (“Indeed, here Christ speaks about himself as if about a third [person].”) (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. 2,2. (Troyes, BM, 904, fol. 3r; PL Database vol. 116, col. 201D.) And: “(PB17a) Continuatur autem hic psalmus superiori, (PB17b) si in voce Capitis ille legatur transmutatione de tertia persona in primam facta, …” (“For this psalm continues the previous [psalm], if it is read in the voice of Christ after a change from the third person to the first has been made, …”) PS. BEDA psalm. 2,1. (PL Database vol. 93, col. 490D.) Pseudo-Haimo also displays a similar paraphrase of 1 Kings 10:24, all the earth desired to see Solomon’s face, as both Pseudo-Bede and Gilbert U. The two latter, furthermore, combine the paraphrase with a quotation of Psalms 44:3, beautiful above the sons of men: “… (PB15c) et de quo dicitur, cuius vultum desiderat universa terra, (PB15d) et, speciosus forma prae filiis hominum.” (“… and about whom it is said, whose face all the earth desired to see, and, beautiful above the sons of men.”) PS. BEDA psalm. 2,1. (PL Database vol. 93, col. 490D.) And: “… (GU9c) de quo dicitur, Speciosus forma prae filiis hominum, (GU9d) cuius vultum desiderat universa terra.” (“… and about whom it is said, beautiful above the sons of men, whose face all the earth desired to see.”) GILB. UNIV. sup. psalt. 2,1. (Laon, BM, 17, fol. 3v.) And: “(PH5) Cuius visum desiderant adhuc etiam omnes sancti.” (“Whose face also all the saints still desire to see.”) (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. 2,1. (Troyes, BM, 904, fol. 2v; PL Database vol. 116, col. 201A.) 

--- [p.216] ---

 
 
209 of Psalm 2:1–2. Only a small part of the material present in our tradition comes here directly from Augustine, who has but the following to say about verses 1 and 2: 
Vtquid fremuerunt gentes, et populi meditati sunt inania? Astiterunt reges terrae, et principes conuenerunt in unum, aduersus Dominum, et aduersus Christum eius. Pro eo dictum est utquid, ac si diceretur: frustra; non enim impleuerunt quod uoluerunt, ut Christus exstingueretur. Dicitur hoc enim de persecutoribus Domini, qui et in Actibus Apostolorum commemorantur.92 (Why have the Gentiles roared, and the people devised vain things? The kings of the earth stood by, and the princes met together against the Lord and against his Christ. ‘Why’ is said for this reason, as if it were said, “in vain;” for they did not accomplish what they wanted, that Christ be destroyed. For this is said about the persecutors of the Lord, who are also remembered in Acts.)  From this passage, only the Ordinaria and Gilbert P pick up the following gloss:  
Glossa O (GO3) Quare/ quasi frustra, quia non impleverunt ut Christus extingueretur.93 ((GO3) Why/ as if in vain, since they did not accomplish that Christ be destroyed.)  Gilbert P (GP13) Quare fremuerunt, … (GP14a) Quasi frustra, (GP14b) quia non impleverunt quod intendebant, id est ut Christus exstingueretur.94 (Why … As if in vain, since they did not accomplish what they intended, that is, that Christ be destroyed.)  Here, the main authoritative source of our group is, instead, the long version of Remigius.95 In order to show the close relationship that exists between our commentaries, Remigius’s                                                 92 AVG. in psalm. 2,1. (CCSL 38, 3–4.) 93 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 2,1. (Laon, BM, 29, fol. 2v; Lund, UB, Med. 4 deficit; Paris, BnF, lat. 105, fol. 2r; Paris, BnF, lat. 106, fol. 2r; Paris, BnF, lat. 442, fol. 3r; Paris, BnF, lat. 8846, fol. 7r; Paris, BnF, lat. 14403, fol. 2r; Paris, BnF, lat. 17213, fol. 6v; Reims, BM, 150, fol. 1v; Troyes, BM, 511, fol. 1v; Troyes, BM, 881, fol. 5r; Troyes, BM, 924, fol. 1v; Troyes, BM, 925, fol. 3v; BAV, Vat. lat. 85, fol. 1v; BAV, Vat. lat. 86, fol. 5v.) 94 GILB.
