BnF, lat. 439, fol. 2v.) 95 Part of the Remigian passage incorporated by our commentaries is an adaptation of Cassiodorus: “Quare fremuerunt gentes, et populi meditati sunt inania? Haec figura graece dicitur erotema, latine interrogatio quae multis quidem modis fit, sed ut de his pauca ponantur, interrogamus quando aliquid scire uolumus quod nesciebamus, ut est illud in futuro psalmo: Quot sunt dies serui tui, quando facies de persequentibus me iudicium? [Ps. 118:84.] Interrogamus etiam quae certe nouimus, ut est illud: Numquid est in idolis gentium qui pluat, aut caeli dabunt pluvium, nisi tu uolueris? [Cf. Jer. 14:22.] Est quoque interrogatio arguendi, sicut hic posita est. Corripit enim populos propheta, cur fremuerint contra Dominum Saluatorem, cum causas iracundiae non haberent. Fremitus enim proprie ferarum est, qui iuste furentibus datus est, quando, ratione postposita, beluino furore 

--- [p.217] ---

 
 
210 introduction and exegesis of Psalm 2:1–2 have been included below in their entirety; as in the Augustinian example above, exegesis which is not picked up by any of our commentaries has been lowlighted. Ideas which are found in our tradition in conceptual but heavily paraphrased form have been left in plain text; words or synonyms found only in the subgroup represented by the Gloss and (/or) Gilbert P are underlined, and words or synonyms found only in the subgroup represented by the other six are in SMALL CAPS; words and synonyms found in both subgroups are in bold. The details of who reproduces what will be accounted for below:96  Titulus psalmus sequentis. ‘Finis’97 in sacro eloquio diversos habet sensus. Ponitur enim pro consummatione et perfectione. Pro consummatione sicut dicimus: “Finita est cena.” Pro termino sicut: Notum fac mihi Domine finem meum.98 Pro consummatione, id est perfectionis ponitur, sicut dicit apostolus: Legis finis Christus est,99 id est perfectio. In finem ergo hic psalmus scriptus est, id est in Christum, qui est perfectio et consummatio legis, cuius passionis continentiam per omnem hunc psalmum reperitur. David autem, qui interpretatur “manu fortis” vel “visu desiderabilis” significat Christum de quo apostolus ait, In quem desiderant angeli Dei prospicere.100 Quare fremuerunt. Interrogatio est prophetae cum increpatione. Interrogatio namque tribus modis fit. Interrogamus enim aliquem discere cupientes quae nescimus. Interrogamus etiam scire volentes utrum101 hoc sciat quem interrogamus. Tertius modus est, cum aliquem cum increpatione interrogamus, sicut hoc loco. Fremere bestiarum est, per quod ostenditur illos irrationabiliter et bestialiter contra Christum desaevisse. 
                                                succensi sunt.” (Why have the Gentiles roared, and the people devised vain things? This figure is called erotema in Greek, [and] questioning in Latin, which occurs in many ways, but in order to say a few things about these, we question when we want to know something that we do not know, as this [verse] in the psalm below exemplifies: How many are the days of your servant: when will you execute judgment on them that persecute me? We also inquire when we know something for certain, as this [verse] exemplifies: Is there any among the idols of the Gentiles that can send rain, or can the heavens give rain unless you have willed it? And there is also the questioning of accusing, as is employed here. For the prophet charges the people with why they roared against the Lord our Saviour when they had no cause for anger. For the roar is proper to wild animals, which was justly ascribed to the raging people when, with reason having been left behind, they were lit up by a bestial furore.) CASSIOD. in psalm. 2,2. (CCSL 97, 41.) 96 Translations of the relevant snippets will be given in the following. 97 finis] sinis cod.  98 Ps. 38:5. 99 Rom. 10:4. 100 1 Pet. 1:12. 101 utrum] utrum his cod. 

--- [p.218] ---

 
 
211 Gentes in hoc loco appellat romanos et Pilatum. Contra Christum in passione fremuerunt dicentes, Ave rex iudaeorum,102 et cetera ad illusionem pertinentia.  Populi meditati sunt inania. Populos ob dignitatem vocat iudaeos, quasi ratione utentes utpote qui Deum caeli adorabant et legem ac prophetias legebant. Hi meditabantur Christi nomen eradere et de terra delere. Sed haec meditatio inanis fuit, quia ad NULLUM EFFECTUM pervenit. Quo enim amplius obscurare voluerunt, eo amplius per universum mundum dilatatum est. Vel meditati sunt inania, quia legem et prophetias qui Christum pronuntiabant legentes meditabantur; ipsum vero venientem recipere noluerunt. Astiterunt reges terrae. Supple ‘quare’. Qui sunt reges et principes in Actibus apostolorum legimus: Convenerunt in hac civitate, Herodes et Pontius Pilatus, adversus puerum tuum QUEM UNXISTI facere quae manus tua et consilium decreverunt fieri,103 iuxta quem sensus dicit Beatus Beda reges et principes plurali numero esse descriptos pro singulari,104 regem scilicet Herodem filum maioris Herodes intelligere volens; principem vero Pilatum. Possumus vero reges pluraliter Herodem maiorem, sub quo Dominus natus est, et Herodem filium eius accipere. Quamvis enim ille maior Herodes Christum non interfecerit, eum INTER infantes necare VOLUIT. Ideoque etsi actu nequaquam tamen culpa caruit. Tali modo uterque Herodes Christi adstitit. Principes vero possumus intelligere Pilatum et scribas ac Phariseos.  Adversus Dominum. Supple ‘Deum Patrem’. Adversus Deum Patrem extiterunt, quia Filium inhonoraverunt. Pater enim et filius unum sunt, et QUI NON HONORAT PATREM, INHONORAT FILIUM.105  
                                                102 Matt. 27:29; Mark 15:18; John 19:3. 103 Acts 4:27–28. 104 BEDA in luc. VI, 23 (l. 1670–74): “… in psalmo etiam quod dictum est, Adsteterunt reges terrae et principes conuenerunt in unum, pluralem numerum pro singulari positum in actibus apostolorum inuenitur, nam reges propter Herodem principes propter Pilatum intellexerunt qui testimonium eiusdem psalmi adhibuerunt.” (“… that it is also said in the psalm, the kings of the earth stoody by and the princes met together against the Lord and against his Christ, is found in Acts [to be] a plural number used for a singular, for those who invoked the testimony of this psalm understood ‘kings’ as Herod and ‘princes’ as Pilate.”) 105 REMIG. in psalm.II 2, acces.–2. (BAV, Urb. lat. 85, fols 2v–3r.) The short version shares many similaritis with the long version, especially in the discussion of the types of questioning, but by its somewhat different rendering of Cassiodorus’s explanation about wild animals, we can see that it is the long version that our tradition depends on: “Quare fremuerunt gentes. ‘Fremuerunt’ translatio est ab animali ad animale, a fera ad hominem. Quare fremuerunt, id est beluino more insanierunt, … Sciendum quia tribus modis fit interrogatio: aut enim interrogamus discere volentes quae nescimus, aut scientes, sed experiri volentes utrum ipse sciat quem interrogamus. Nonnumquam increpando fieri solet interrrogatio, sic hic cum increpatione: Quare fremuerunt gentes, et cetera. Quare fremuerunt gentes et populi meditati sunt inania, id est quare iudaei consilium fecerunt ut Christum morti traderent, et nomen eius de terra viventium delerent. Sed hoc inane fuit, quia eo mortuo nominis eius gloria crevit.” (“Why have the Gentiles roared. ‘Have roared’ is a transferred sense from animal to animal, from wild beast to man. Why have [the Gentiles] roared, that is, [why] have they raged in a beastlike way, … It should be known that a questioning happens in three ways: for we question either when we want to learn what we do not know, or when we know but want to test whether the person whom we are questioning knows. Often a questioning happens in order to rebuke, as [it is said] here in rebuke: Why have the Gentiles roared, etc. Why have the Gentiles roared and the people devised vain things, that is, [why] did the Jews make a plan to hand Christ over to death and wipe out his name form the earth. But this was vain because, when he was dead, his name grew in glory.”) REMIG. in psalm.I 2,1. (Reims, BM 132, fol. 2rv; St. Gallen, SB, Cod. Sang. 312, p. 5.) 

--- [p.219] ---

 
 
212 For this passage, the idea that our commentaries may have borrowed independently from Remigius appears perhaps a little less unlikely than in the Augustinian example above, as we have more instances here of snippets that are borrowed by just one or two commentaries. Such is the case, for example, for Remigius’s quotation of Acts 4:27, of which we find a faint echo only in Remi A and Gilbert U: Remigius … in Actibus apostolorum legimus: Convenerunt in hac civitate, Herodes et Pontius Pilatus, adversus puerum tuum quem unxisti …106 (… we read in the Acts of the Apostles: There assembled together in this city against your child, whom you have anointed, Herod and Pontius Pilate …)  Gilbert U … (GU27b) Christum eius, id est quem inunxit in regem et sacerdotem.107 (… his Christ, that is, whom you have anointed to king and priest.)  Remi A … (RA16c) et adversus me, Christum eius, (RA16d) id est quem unxit in regem et sacerdotem.108 (… and against me, his Christ, that is, whom he has anointed to king and priest.)  These two renderings are, however, clearly more closely related to one another than independently to Remigius, and it may be said that referring to anointing when explaining Christ in a lemma would be quite natural. It may also be said that these two words are the only part of the biblical quote that Remi A and Gilbert U share with Remigius; they do not reproduce anything else that makes their comment identifiable as a quotation of Acts 4:27. It is thus not inconceivable that this agreement between the authority and the compilers is coincidental. 
                                                106 REMIG. in psalm.II 2,2. (BAV, Urb. lat. 85, fol. 2v.) 107 GILB. UNIV. sup. psalt. 2,2. (Laon, BM, 17, fol. 3v.) 108 PS. REMIG. I sup. psalt. 2,2. (Admont, SB, 99, fol. 3v.) 

--- [p.220] ---

 
 
213 We have also the case of Remigius’s quotation of Bede’s commentary on Luke, which is found only in Gilbert P:  Remigius … iuxta quem sensus dicit Beatus Beda reges et principes plurali numero esse descriptos pro singulari, regem scilicet Herodem filum maioris Herodes intelligere volens …109 (… according to which sense blessed Bede says that the kings and princes are described with the plural number for the singular, wanting to understand Herod the son of the greater Herod …)  Gilbert P Asititerunt reges terrae et principes, (GP21b) Herodes et Pilatus, plurale pro singulari; …110 (The kings of the earth and the princes stood by, Herod and Pilate, plural for singular; …)  We may note, however, that Gilbert’s explanation is much abbreviated, leaving out the mention of Bede as the source and the comment about which of the two Herods is to be understood, which could indicate that he is taking this not directly from Remigius, but from a source which had already done some excerpting and abbreviating. The final instance is Remigius’s paraphrase of John 5:23, which we find only in Pseudo-Bede: Remigius … qui non honorat Patrem, inhonorat Filium.111 (… he who does not honour the Father, dishonours the son.)  Ps.-Bede (PB31) Unde dictum est, Qui non honorificat Filium, non honorificat Patrem.112 (Whence it is said, He who does not honour the Son, does not honour the Father.)  However, unlike Remigius, Pseudo-Bede quotes John verbatim, and, as we find the same quotation in Cassiodorus, it is possible that Pseudo-Bede is relying independently on Cassiodorus here, and not on Remigius.                                                 109 REMIG. in psalm.II 2,2. (BAV, Urb. lat. 85, fol. 2v.) For Bede, see 211n104. 110 GILB. PICT. in psalm. 2,2. (Paris, BnF, lat. 439, fol. 2v.) 111 REMIG. in psalm.II 2,2. (BAV, Urb. lat. 85, fol. 2v.)  112 PS. BEDA psalm. 2,2. (PL Database vol. 93, col. 491B.) 

--- [p.221] ---

 
 
214 Other than this, our commentaries extract more or less the exact same snippets out of the longer Remigian passage and leave the same points untouched. The latter is the case for Remigius’s discussion of the meaning of ‘finis’, “goal,” his quotation of Matthew 27:29 et al., his explanation of why the Psalmist calls the Jews the people, and his interpretation of ‘the princes’ as the scribes and the Pharisees. Our commentaries also leave out his interpretation of the people devis[ing] vain things as the Jews trying to stamp out Christ’s name from the earth, where Remigius also explains that, the more they did this, the more his name spread across the world. The large majority of our commentaries have instead worked into their explanation of ‘inania’ a reference to John 12:19, Behold, the whole world is gone after him. Only Pseudo-Haimo leaves this out:113 Ps.-Haimo … inania, (PH17b) scilicet Christum non esse Messiam, (PH17c) vel se eum retinere posse in sepulcro.114 (… vain things, namely, that Christ is not the Messiah, or that they would be able to keep him in the grave.)  Glossa O (GO6) ¶ Inania. Detinere in morte vel ne mundus post eum abiret.115 ((GO6) ¶ Vain things. To keep [him] in death or that the world not go after him.  Gilbert P … inania, (GP18b) id est detinere in morte, (GP18c) et ne mundus post eum abiret,116  (GP18d) et in Scripturis de venturo adhuc Messia.117 
                                                113 The only one who, like John, expresses the statement as an already completed fact is, however, Remi M. The majority of the others phrase it as something which the Jews were trying to avoid, which, again, shows that their accounts are not simply related by an independent reliance on the Bible. (Gilbert U expresses the statement as something which will happen in the future, reading the lemma in the voice of Christ before the resurrection.) 114 (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. 2,1. (Troyes, BM, 904, fol. 3r; PL Database vol. 116, col. 201C.) 115 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 2,1. (Laon, BM, 29, fol. 2v; Lund, UB, Med. 4 deficit; Paris, BnF, lat. 105, fol. 2r; Paris, BnF, lat. 106, fol. 2r; Paris, BnF, lat. 442, fol. 3r; Paris, BnF, lat. 8846, fol. 7r; Paris, BnF, lat. 14403, fol. 2r; Paris, BnF, lat. 17213, fol. 6v; Reims, BM, 150, fol. 1v; Troyes, BM, 511, fol. 1v; Troyes, BM, 881, fol. 5r; Troyes, BM, 924, fol. 1v; Troyes, BM, 925, fol. 3v; BAV, Vat. lat. 85, fol. 1v; BAV, Vat. lat. 86, fol. 5v.) 116 Cf. John 12:19. 117 GILB. PICT. in psalm. 2,1. (Paris. BnF, lat. 439, fol. 2v.)

215 (… vain things, that is, to keep [Christ] in death, and that the world would not go after him, and in the Scriptures about the Messiah who is still going to come.)  Gilbert U … (GU16b) inania meditabuntur? (GU17a) Ac si dicat: “Inaniter,” (GU17b) id est “nullam efficaciam habebunt, (GU17c) quia resurgam (GU17d) et totus mundus post me abibit (GU17e) et ipsi amittent locum et gentem,118 (GU17f) quod post mortem meam putabant retinere.”119 (… [why] will they devise vain things? As if he should say: “Vainly,” that is, “they will have no efficacy, since I shall rise again and the whole world will go after me, and they will lose their land and nation, which they thought they would keep after my death.”)  Remi A … (RA18c) et propter eum locum et gentem amittere timebant, …120 (… and because of him they feared to lose their land and nation …)  Bruno … (BC21b) meditati sunt inania, id est meditabuntur (BC21c) me detinere in morte, (BC21d) ne mundus post me abeat, (BC21e) quod inane erit.121 (… have devised vain things, that is, they will devise to keep me in death, in order that the world not go after me, which will be vain.)  Remi M (RM19a) Inania haec fuerunt, (RM19b) quia illa mors Christi exaltatio fuit, (RM19c) et iam totus mundus post eum abiit.122 (These things were vain, since that death was the exaltation of Christ, and the whole world is now gone after him.)  Ps.-Bede (PB27a) Et quia putaverunt quod non amitterent locum et gentem, (PB27b) si ipsum interficerent, (PB27c) neque mundus post ipsum iret. (PB27d) Quod inane fuit, ut supra dictum est.123 (And since they thought that they would not lose their land and nation if they killed him, and [that] the world would not go after him. Which was vain, as was said above.)                                                 118 Cf. John 11:48. 119 GILB. UNIV. sup. psalt. 2,1. (Laon, BM, 17, fol. 3v.) 120 PS. REMIG. I sup. psalt. 2,1. (Admont, SB, 99, fol. 3v.) 121 (PS.) BRUNO CARTHUS. psalm. 2,1. (PL Database vol. 152, col. 644C.) 122 (PS.) REMIG. psalm. 2,1. (PL Database vol. 131, col. 155C.) 123 PS. BEDA psalm. 2,1. (PL Database vol. 93, col. 491AB.) 

--- [p.223] ---

 
 
216 In addition to this, Remi A, Gilbert U, and Pseudo-Bede, combine the quote of John 12:19 with a reference to John 11:48, If we let him alone so, all will believe in him, and the Romans will come and take away our place and nation,124 and Pseudo-Haimo, the Gloss, Gilbert P, and Bruno all envisage the vain things as trying to keep Christ in death/in the grave, of which there is also a faint echo in Gilbert U. That almost all of our commentaries have replaced the same point in Remigius with the same quotation of John seems very unlikely to be coincidental; the more likely scenario is that they all depend in some way on a post-Remigian stage of the tradition. Moving along through Remigius’s commentary, we may note that his discussion of the two Herods appears in similar form in our commentaries. However, the patristic source for it is Cassiodorus,125 who also seems to be the more likely source for our commentaries, as they share 
                                                124 The same reference is found in Letbert’s commentary; here, however, it is inserted into the middle of Remigius’s comment about Christ’s name which is not picked up by any of our commentaries, and which is the only thing Letbert takes from Remigius: “Quare fremuerunt gentes, id est gentiles, et populi, id est iudaei, meditati sunt inania? Et quare astiterunt reges terrae, Herodes et Pilatus, et principes, Annas et Caiphas, convenerunt in unum consilium et locum adversus Dominum Patrem et adversus Christum Filium eius? Tanta denique insania furoris eos invasit, tanta malitiae caecitas perculit, ut auctorem suum, quem credere et adorare debuissent, unanimiter etiam occidere non timerent. Dictum est autem, Quare, ac si diceretur, “frustra.” Non enim impleverunt quod voluerunt. Nam qui ideo Christum occiderunt, et ut nomen eius de terra delerent, et ne ipsi locum et gentem perderent: ideo quidem nomen eius auxerunt, ideo et gentem et locum perdiderunt, quia innocentem Dominum occiderunt. Miroque modo actum est, ut dum suum consilium implere se crederent, consilium Domini nolentes et nescientes implerent. Ergo meditati sunt inania. Non est enim sapientia, non est prudentia, non est consilium contra Dominum.” (“Why have the Gentiles roared, that is, the Gentiles, and the people, that is, the Jews, devised vain things? And why have the kings of the earth, Herod and Pilate, stood by, and the princes, Annas and Caiphas, met toegther in one plan and place against our Lord the Father and against his Son Christ? Finally, such great madness of furore came upon them, such great blindness of malice struck [them], that they unanimously did not fear to kill their maker, whom they ought to have believed and worshipped. And it is said, Why, as if it were said, “in vain.” For they did not accomplish what they wanted. For they killed Christ both in order to erase his name from the earth, and in order that they not lose their place and nation: [but] therefore, indeed, did they augment his name, and therefore did they lose their nation and their place, since they killed the innocent Lord. And it was done in an astounding way, so that while they thought they were fulfilling their plan, they unwittingly and unwillingly fulfilled the Lord’s. Therefore, they devised vain things. For there is no wisdom, no prudence, no plan [which goes] against God.”) LETBER. INS. in psalm. 2,1. (PL Database vol. 21, col. 650AB.) That our commentaries would have excised the quote of John 11:48 from Letbert, all managing to leave behind the Remigian material, and, what is more, that they would have done this independently of each other, seems highly unlikely. At the same time, it cannot be a coincidence that the quotation is found at the same lemma in Letbert as well; it is clear that there must be some intermediary connection between him and our group. 125 “Reges autem terrae Herodem uult intellegi, qui persequendo Dominum necauit infantes, alterum uero Herodem nepotem eius, qui Pontio Pilato in Saluatoris nece consensit.” (For [the Psalmist] wants to understand ‘kings of the earth’ as the Herod who killed infants when persecuting the Lord, [and] indeed, the other Herod, his nephew, who consented to Pontius Pilate regarding the killing.”) CASSIOD. in psalm. 2,2. (CCSL 97, 42.) 

--- [p.224] ---

 
 
217 with him the fact that they do not, as Remigius does, go into any discussion of justifying why Herod the Great should be included in the interpretation (as he was not the one involved Christ’s crucifixion). What the majority of our commentaries shares, however, and what indicates that they are more closely related to each other than to either of the authorities independently, is the fact that all except Pseudo-Haimo, Remi M, and Pseudo-Bede include Pilate under ‘reges’:126 Glossa O (GO7) reges/ Herodes et Pilatus127 ((GO7) the kings/ Herod and Pilate  Gilbert P … (GP21c) vel reges, Pilatus et Herodes prior, qui pueros occidit, et posterior, qui Pilato in morte Christi consensit.128  (… or the kings, Pilate and Herod the former, who killed the boys, and the latter, who consented to Pilate regarding the death of Christ.)  Gilbert U (GU25) Reges accipit Herodem qui in nativitate occisis pueris Dominum putavit occidere, et alium Herodem, qui in passione eum delusit cum exercitu, et etiam Pilatum.129 (He understands the kings as the Herod who thought to kill the Lord at his birth after having killed the boys, and the other Herod, who mocked him with the soldiers at his passion, and also Pilate.)  
                                                126 Pseudo-Haimo and Pseudo-Bede, like Remigius, place Pilate under ‘principes’ and Remi M has him under ‘gentes’. 127 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 2,2. (Laon, BM, 29, fol. 2v; Lund, UB, Med. 4 deficit; Paris, BnF, lat. 105, fol. 2r; Paris, BnF, lat. 106, fol. 2r; Paris, BnF, lat. 442, fol. 3r; Paris, BnF, lat. 8846, fol. 7r; Paris, BnF, lat. 14403, fol. 2r; Paris, BnF, lat. 17213, fol. 6v; Reims, BM, 150, fol. 2r; Troyes, BM, 511, fol. 1v; Troyes, BM, 881, fol. 5r; Troyes, BM, 924, fol. 1v; Troyes, BM, 925, fol. 3v; BAV, Vat. lat. 85, fol. 2r; BAV, Vat. lat. 86, fol. 5v.) 128 GILB. Prior qui infantes occidit Herodes; posterior qui Pilato consensit in morte,” which is closer to the wording of the Cassiodorean/Remigian original. (“Kings. The former Herod, who killed the infants; the latter, who consented to Pilate regarding the death [of Christ].”) GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 2,2. (Laon, BM, 29, fol. 2v; Lund, UB, Med. 4 deficit; Paris, BnF, lat. 105, fol. 2r; Paris, BnF, lat. 106, fol. 2r; Paris, BnF, lat. 442, fol. 3r; Paris, BnF, lat. 8846, fol. 7r; Paris, BnF, lat. 14403, fol. 2r; Paris, BnF, lat. 17213, fol. 6v; Reims, BM, 150, fol. 2r; Troyes, BM, 511, fol. 1v; Troyes, BM, 881, fol. 5r; Troyes, BM, 924, fol. 1v; Troyes, BM, 925, fol. 3v; BAV, Vat. lat. 85, fol. 2r; BAV, Vat. lat. 86, fol. 5v.) 129 GILB. UNIV. sup. psalt. 2,2. (Laon, BM, 17, fol. 3v.) 

--- [p.225] ---

 
 
218 Remi A (RA15) Hoc intelligendum est de Herode primo et secundo, necnon de Pilato, qui loco regis habebatur.130 (This should be understood as [being] about the first Herod and the second, and also about Pilate, who was there in place of a king.)  Bruno … (BC23b) sed etiam reges, … (BC23d) uterque scilicet Herodes—unus in nativitate, alter in passione—et Pilatus.131 (… but also the kings, … namely, each Herod—one at his birth, the other at his passion—and Pilate.)  That our commentaries co-vary so closely with each other in relation to Remigius’s account seems, once again, highly unlikely to have happened by chance. What seems more likely is that they would all be drawing on some form of intermediary adaptation of Remigius in combination with Cassiodorus.  If we look, instead, at what our commentaries do pick up from Remigius, we see that all except the Gloss include, first, the Hieronymian interpretations of the name ‘David’ as meaning “strong-handed” or “desirable in appearance”132 and of David as symbolising Christ; four of them also preserve in some form Remigius’s quotation of 1 Peter 1:12, on whom the angels desire to look, while three of them have replaced this with the same paraphrase of 1 Kings 10:24, And all the earth desired to see Solomon’s face (the common denominator of the two quotations being the word ‘desire’):133 
                                                130 PS. REMIG. I sup. psalt. 2,2. (Admont, SB, 99, fol. 3v.) 131 (PS.) BRUNO CARTHUS. psalm. 2,2. (PL Database vol. 152, col. 644C.) 132 HIER. nom. hebr. Lag. 35. Remigius takes these over from Cassiodorus, but adds the following quotation of 1 Peter 1:12, which is picked up by several of our commentaries. 133 Pseudo-Bede includes both quotations. Both of them are also found in Letbert’s commentary, including the same paraphrase of 1 Kings 10:24: “[Psalmus] est attribuendus David, id est Christo, qui verus David, id est, “visu desiderabilis” vel “manu fortis” interpretatur. 222 (‘David’ is interpreted as “strong-handed” or “desirable in appearance,” in whom Christ is signified, who is strong-handed, since he conquered the Devil, and distributed his spoils. … He is desirable, since not only men but also the angels desire to see him.)  Ps.-Bede David, (PB14d) id est Christo, (PB14e) qui vere manu fortis est, (PB14f) quia Diabolum alligavit, et spolia eius, … ei abstulit, et in ornamenta domus suae distribuit, … (PB15a) Est quoque Christus vere visu desiderabilis, (PB15b) cuius conspectu reficiuntur angeli, (PB15c) et de quo dicitur, cuius vultum desiderat universa terra, (PB15d) et, speciosus forma prae filiis hominum.148 (… David, that is Christ, who is truly strong-handed, since he bound the Devil, and took his spoils fom him, and distributed them as ornaments of his house, … Christ is also desirable in appearance, by the sight of whom the angels are refreshed, and about whom it is said, whose face all the world desires to see, and, beautiful above the sons of men.)  All our commentaries except the Gloss justify the application of the epithet ‘strong-handed’ to Christ by explaining that he defeated (or battled) someone; in the majority of the commentraries, this someone is the Devil. (Only Gilbert U appears to give a purely historical explanation, speaking only of “the strong, armed man”, presumably referring to Goliath.) This justification is not found in any of our Psalms-authorities, but comes instead from Haimo of Auxerre’s commentary on the Song of Songs:149 Hoc autem collum turri David comparatur. David, qui interpretatur “manu fortis” vel “visu desiderabilis,” Christum significat. Et tota quidem ecclesia civitas David, id est magni regis Christi est, qui est manu fortis, quoniam Diabolum devicit, iuxta quod psalmista dicit, Dominus fortis et potens, Dominus potens in praelio.150 151 (And this neck is compared to the tower of David. David, who is interpreted as “strong-handed” or “desirable in appearance,” signifies Christ. And the whole Church is the city of David, that is, of Christ the great king, who is 
                                                148 PS. BEDA psalm. 2,1. (PL Database vol. 93, col. 490CD.) 149 The commentary is printed twice in the PL, once among the works of Cassiodorus, to whom it is often attributed in the manuscripts (PL Database vol. 70, cols 1056–1106), and once under the name of Haimo of Halberstadt (PL Database vol. 117, cols 295–358). On the attribution to Haimo of Auxerre, see Alberto Vaccari, “L’editio princeps del commento di Aimone alla Cantica e la chiave di un problema letterario,” Biblica 5:2 (1924): 183–191. 150 Ps. 23:8. 151 HAIMO AUTISS. cant. 4,4. (PL Database vol. 117, col. 317C.)

223 strong-handed, since he defeated the Devil, according to what the Psalmist says, The Lord who is strong and mighty, the Lord strong in battle.)  The fact that this explanation is incorporated into our commentaries in the same place in their exegesis, when it does not itself come from a Psalms-commentary but from a commentary on the Song of Songs strongly, suggests that it cannot have been included here by coincidence. Once again, a more reasonable explanation is that our commentaries all ultimatley stem from a shared intermediary ancestor who is the one who excerpted the Haimonian justification—and this regardless of whether some of our commentaries are also borrowing from someone else in the family, as is also perfectly possible.152 Almost all our commentaries preserve also (in some way) Remigius’s interpretation of the psalm being an interrogatio cum increpatione, a “questioning with rebuke”:  Ps.-Haimo (PH12) Hic Dominus interrogatione loquitur, non propter inquisitionem, sed propter redargutionem, …153 (Here, the Lord is speaking by questioning, not for the sake of inquiry, but for the sake of reproof, …)  Glossa O (GO1) ¶ Primo increpat persequentes et minatur; praedicit quasi propheta.154                                                 152 We may note that also Letbert provides a similar justification, referring to “the prince of this world”: “[Psalmus] est attribuendus David, id est, Christo, qui verus David, id est “visu desiderabilis” vel “manu fortis” interpretatur. Quis enim adeo desiderabilis, sicut ille, de quo scriptum est, Cuius vultum desiderat universa terra, et in quem desiderant angeli prospicere. Et quis fortior eo, qui omnia quaecumque voluit, fecit [Ps. 113:11], qui principem huius mundi de regno suo expulit [cf. John 12:31], qui morte sua mortem nostram destruxit [cf. 2 Tim. 1:10].” (“[The psalm] should be attributed to David, that is, to Christ, who is the true David, that is, he is interpreted as “desirable in appearance,” or “strong-handed.” For who is as desirable as he of whom it is written, whose face all the world desired to see, and, on whom the angels desire to look. And who is stronger than him who has done all things whatsoever he wanted, who drove out the prince of this world from his kingdom, who destroyed our death with his own.”) LETBER. INS. in psalm. 2, acces. (PL Database vol. 21, col. 649D.) Our commentaries do not, however, appear to depend on Letbert, as they stick closer to Haimo’s original wording. This circumstance strengthens the suggestion that our commentaries share their magisterial ancestry with Letbert as well. 153 (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. 2,1. (Troyes, BM, 904, fols 2v–3r; PL Database vol. 116, col. 201B.) 154 GLOSSA (ord.) psalm. 2. --- [p.232] ---

 
 
225 replaced ‘increpatio’ with the respective synonyms ‘redargutio’ and ‘obiurgatio’, indicating that neither of these could have been the source of the others for this shared Remigian material. As further support for our theory of a shared intermediary ancestor, we may also note here that Remigius’s discussion of Cassiodorus’s three types of interrogatio, “questioning,” is preserved in Pseudo-Haimo, Gilbert U, and Remi A; here, however, only a twofold distinction is made, and in much abbreviated terms, suggesting, again, more than a coincidental occurrence of this feature. Here, we see also the particularly close relationship that exists between Gilbert U and Remi A. That the latter cannot, however, be the shared source of the group—or even of its closest relative Gilbert U—is seen if we look at how he renders this particular aspect of Remigius’s Cassiodorean material. Cassiodorus first subdivides questioning (interrogatio) into three types, explaining how the third type, questioning for the sake of accusing, is the one that applies to Psalm 2:1. This threefold distinction is reproduced in full in adapted form by Remigius. As mentioned, only a twofold division is found in our group: that Remi A and Gilbert U share a particular connection is evident from the fact that they both use the abverb ‘admirative’, “wonderingly,” to describe the first category, but while both Pseudo-Haimo and Gilbert U distinguish between questioning for the sake of inquiry/wondering (inquisitio/admiratio) and questioning for the sake of rebuke (redargutio/increpatio), Remi A conceives of the distinction as being one between questioning (interrogatio) and wondering (admiratio), between which concepts there is really no difference. Remi A has, in other words, lost the entire point of Cassiodorus’s distinction, where interrogatio is the overarching concept which is subdivided into types. This feature strongly suggests that Remi A cannot be the source for any of the others of this Remigian/Cassiodorean material.  If we turn, instead, to the Gloss and Gilbert P, we recall from Chapter Two that the Gloss precedes Gilbert P, but, at the same time, that Gilbert cannot have been relying solely on the 

--- [p.233] ---

 
 
226 Gloss for his material; the same is the case here, as the Gloss does not include the item ‘interrogatio’. Gilbert must thus either have known where this material came from and looked up the original source in order to expand on the Ordinaria-gloss, or himself have been the one who inserted the material into the Gloss. Considering that the addition of ‘interrogatio’ is a rather minor one, one wonders if the Ordinaria’s glosses really seemed so obscure and incomplete that Gilbert decided he needed to go through the trouble of tracking down the original source only to be able to make this addition. As has already been suggested, a scenario where Gilbert displays the more complete rendering of the patristic or Carolingian original because he is, in fact, the one who finds it and incorporates it into the Gloss (in shorter form—perhaps as a crib note) would make better sense of this state of affairs. Another item which the majority of our commentaries picks up in some form is Remigius’s Cassiodorean explanation of the verb ‘fremuerunt’, “have roared”:161 
Ps.-Haimo (PH13) Quare, id est, qua causa, fremuerunt gentes? (PH14a) Per ‘fremuerunt’ notat eos bestiales esse, (PH14b) quia fremere bestiarum est.162 (Why, that is, with what reason, have the Gentiles roared? By ‘roared’ he notes that they are beastlike, since roaring is proper to beasts.)  Glossa O (GO2) Quare/ qua utilitate sua?  ((GO2) Why/ with what use to themselves)  (GO3) Quare/ quasi frustra, quia non impleverunt ut Christus extingueretur. ((GO3) Why/ as if in vain, since they did not accomplish that Christ be destroyed.)  (GO4) fremuerunt/ ut ferae agentes sine ratione163

Remi A (RA17a) Merito gentes fremuisse dicit, (RA17b) quia sine omni causa (RA17b) eum tradiderunt morti.166  (He rightly says that the Gentiles have roared, since they handed him over to death without any cause whatsoever.) 228 Remi M … (RM13) quare fremuerunt gentes? Id est, ad quam utilitatem suam? (RM14a) Certe nullam, … (RM15c) fremuerunt, id est, sine ratione, (RM15d) ut leones, in me fremitum dederunt.168  (… why have the Gentiles roared? That is, to what use to them? Surely none, … have roared, that is, without [the faculty of] reason, they have sounded a roar at me like lions.)  Ps.-Bede … (PB1b) iudaeos et gentes obiurgans, (PB1c) quod non advertentes medicum salutis (PB1d) irrationabilem fremitum et impetum in ipsum facturi in eius passione erant. … (PB21) Quasi dicat: “Ob vilem, imo ob nullam causam hoc fecerunt.”169 (… reproaching the Jews and the Gentiles for the fact that, not heeding the physician of their salvation, they were to make an irrational roar and an assault upon him in his passion. … As if he should say: “They did this for a vile reason, nay, for no reason.”)  The same arguments that were made for the previous instance of our commentaries’ Remigian/Cassiodorean exegesis can be made here: once again, our commentaries have all picked out the very same Remigian snippets and Remi A missed the point of the patristic/Carolingian exegesis.  If we begin, however, with the Gloss and Gilbert P, we may first point out that the various forms of Remigius’s ‘irrationabiliter’ which we find in Gilbert U, Bruno, and Pseudo-Bede, and the variations on ‘nullum effectum’ which we find in Gilbert U, Bruno, and Remi M, are all closer to Remigius than is the Gloss’s and Gilbert P’s ‘sine ratione’ and ‘quasi frustra’ (the latter of which is pure Augustine). We also understand that neither the Gloss nor Gilbert P could be the transmitter of the Remigian/Cassiodorean snippet “fremere bestiarum est,”170 as this is found in forms closer to Remigius in Pseudo-Haimo, who preserves Remigius’s exact phrase, and Bruno, who has only substituted ‘leonum’, “[proper to] lions,” for ‘bestiarum’, “[proper to] wild beasts.” Remi M, in turn, seems in some way dependent on Bruno for his “ut leones,” “like lions,” while                                                 168 (PS.) REMIG. psalm. 2,1. (PL Database vol. 131, col. 155BC.) 169 PS. BEDA psalm. 2,1. (PL Database vol. 93, cols 489D–491A.) 170 Cassiodorus has, “Fremitus enim proprie ferarum est” (see 209n95). As ‘ferarum’ is, however, the word used by Cassiodorus, we may imagine that this exists as a variant of ‘bestiarum’ in the Remigian tradition.  

--- [p.236] ---

 
 
229 Pseudo-Bede is farthest from the original with ‘fremitus irrationabilis’, “irrational roar.” Gilbert U, for his part, agrees this time with the Gloss’s and Gilbert P’s “ut ferae agentes,” “acting like wild animals.”  If we turn to the logic of the explanation, we may note that, in line with Cassiodorus’s original, all of the accounts of our commentaries serve to justify why the Psalmist’s choice of the verb ‘fremere’, “to roar,” is appropriate. What Cassiodorus says in his passage is: For the prophet charges the people with why they roared against the Lord our Saviour when they had no cause for anger. For the roar is proper to wild animals, which was justly ascribed to the raging people when, with reason having been left behind, they were lit up by a monstrous furore.  Cassiodorus, in other words, first links the verb ‘fremere’, “to roar”, to wild beasts and then explains that this verb is appropriately chosen because, due to the fact that reason had been left behind, the fury of the people was beastlike, since beasts lack reason. The mediating concept which connects acting irrationally to roaring is thus the wild beasts.  Remigius preserves this connection between irrationality and roaring when he says, “Roaring is proper to beasts, through which it is shown that those people raged against Christ irrationally and bestially.” It is also preserved, more or less intact, by the majority of our commentaries;171 the exception is, once again, Remi A, who speaks neither of beasts nor irrationality, but only about cause: “He rightly says that the Gentiles have roared, since they handed him over to death without any cause whatsoever.” As no connection is made between roaring and doing things for no reason, and since there is no obvious connection between these two activities—or any obscure                                                 171 As in the case of the ordo of Psalm 2, Pseudo-Haimo has abridged the explanation of ‘fremuerunt’ to the extent that it has lost some of its logical force, saying only: “By ‘roared’ he notes that they are beastlike, since roaring is proper to beasts.” While this statement still makes sense, it does not explain the psalmist’s motivation for portraying the gentes as beastlike, since Pseudo-Haimo does not go into the issue of their acting irrationally, which is the reason why Cassiodorus brings the wild beasts into the explanation. As several of the others, on the other hand, reproduce this connection between beasts and irrationality, Pseudo-Haimo is not likely to be their shared source for this exegesis. 

--- [p.237] ---

 
 
230 one, for that matter—the choice of verb is not, in fact, given any explanation here at all. Remi A’s version thus seems more like the watered-down end-product of one of the better versions present elsewhere in the tradition than the direct source of any one of these. As a final case against Remi A, we note that he omits some additional, potentially magisterial material shared by the majority of the others: the first is the gloss “qua/m utilitate/m” found above in the Gloss, Gilbert P, Gilbert U, Bruno, and Remi M, and in the variation “qua causa” in Pseudo-Haimo; the second is the interpretation of ‘gentes’ as the Roman soldiers:172  
Ps.-Haimo (PH15a) Per ‘gentes’ milites romanos intelligit, …173 (By ‘Gentiles’ he understands the Roman soldiers, …)  Glossa O (GO5) gentes/ romani milites174 ((GO5) the Gentiles/ the Roman soldiers)  Gilbert P (GP16b) Gentes, id est romani milites crucifixores.175 (The Gentiles, that is, the Roman soldier crucifiers.)  Gilbert U … (GU15b) gentes, id est romani milites.176 (The Gentiles, that is, the Roman soldiers, …)  Bruno … (BC14c) gentes, id est romani milites, qui me crucifigent.177 (… the Gentiles, that is, the Roman soldiers, who crucified me, …)                                                 172 Neither of these two glosses appear in any of the Psalms-authorities. Apart from its evidence against Remi A, the only other thing that can be deduced from this gloss taken by itself is that Pseudo-Bede is not the source of it, since, while the renderings of the others are identical to each other, Pseudo-Bede expands the gloss to focus on the fact that the soldiers belonged to Pilate; their nationality is tacked on as an afterthought. 173 (PS.) --- [p.238] ---

 
 
231 Remi M (RM21a) Quasi dicat: Non solum gentes, scilicet minores (RM21b) milites romani, …178  (As if he should say: Not only the Gentiles, namely, the lesser Roman soldiers, …)  Ps.-Bede (PB22a) Gentes milites Pilati dicit, qui et romani et gentiles erant, …179 (He calls Pilate’s soldiers, who were both Romans and Gentiles, Gentiles, …)  In and of themselves, these pieces of “evidence” are small and could be dismissed as circumstantial. Taken together, however, the totality of pieces makes a compelling case: similarly to the case with the Augustinian passage above, it seems unlikely that our commentaries should all be borrowing independently from Remigius: they both borrow many of the same pieces and make many of the same adjustments to the pieces they borrow. That this should all have happened by coincidence seems highly unlikely.  The more likely explanation would be that they are all connected by some intermediary lying between the authoritative patristic and Carolingian sources and what is left of our tradition. At the same time, we have seen that more or less every commentary leaves something out or makes an adjustment of its own which disqualifies it from being the transmitter of the material in question to the others. The only commentary against which we have not found any conclusive evidence is Gilbert U, who includes more or less every item so far discussed.180 As it may thus appear that Gilbert U could be the shared intermediary source we are looking for, we shall examine the pattern of his agreements separately.  
                                                178 (PS.) REMIG. psalm. 2,1. (PL Database vol. 131, col. 155C.) 179 PS. BEDA psalm. 2,1. (PL Database vol. 93, col. 491A.) 180 The exception is the specification that Acts is the book where “Peter” calls Psalm 2 the first psalm. 

--- [p.239] ---

 
 
232 A CASE OF DEPENDENCE WITHIN THE FAMILY TREE What we find when we look closer at Gilbert U’s agreements is that more or less all of the material found in this commentary has a counterpart either in Remi A and/or Bruno on the one side, or with Gilbert P on the other. This strongly suggests that, rather than being the shared source of all the others, Gilbert U is splicing two sources together—on the one side, a source which is either related or identical to Gilbert P, and, on the other, either both Remi A and Bruno individually or a shared ancestor of all three. In order to demonstrate this pattern, Gilbert U’s text will be presented integrally below in one column with the corresponding pieces of Gilbert P juxtaposed on the left side and the corresponding pieces of Remi A and/or Bruno on the right. (The snippets of these commentaries will, naturally, be out of order; this can be seen in the snippet-numbers, but partial snippets taken out of order will also be preceded and/or followed by an ellipsis.) In order to give a fuller picture of Gilbert U’s source-splicing, the accessus to the psalm has also been included. Agreements with Gilbert U are in bold; this time, however, only exactly verbatim agreements are marked. This will show which rendering ought to have been the one present in Gilbert U’s direct source in those cases where more than one of the other commentaries includes the same general material. In order to make this even clearer, however, those boxes which contain a rendering which I do not believe is what Gilbert U’s source would have had have been lowlighted (as have empty boxes). In those cases where the relationship of dependence is indeterminable, all boxes have been left white. (Most often, this happens with the smaller pieces of common authoritative interpretations.) Finally, agreements which are shared among the other commentaries but not by Gilbert U are underlined:
