(BC28) Cum autem dicit: Dirumpamus, …  As we see from this table, there is very little material in Gilbert U which does not have a counterpart in either Gilbert P on the one side or Remi A and/or Bruno on the other. (The material that does not will be discussed below.) What is also immediately obvious is that, of the three relatives, Gilbert U agrees most closely with Remi A. (For the accessus, his agreement with Remi A is nearly complete; the disagreements will be discussed below.) Several things reveal, 

--- [p.247] ---

 
 
240 however, that Remi A cannot be the direct source of Gilbert U, but that the two must, instead, be relying on a shared intermediaty ancestor of their own.  To the evidence of Remi A’s inferior explanations mentioned above, we may add here his and Gilbert U’s shared use of the quotation of Augustine’s commentary on Psalm 63:7, “Sic omnia faciamus, ut nihil fecisse videamur” (“Let us do everything in such a way that we appear to have done nothing”; GU20b and RA18d). A comparison of the two accounts shows that Gilbert U’s version must more faithfully represent the intended sense of a common parent (or intermediary ancestor higher up in the tree). After having discussed Christ’s destruction of sacred Jewish traditions, which Remi A also mentions, Gilbert U prefaces the Augustine-quote with the following introduction: “And therefore he said of the Jews ‘to devise’, because they acted against him only with words.” (GU20a) He continues with, “as it is said elsewhere”—the “elsewhere” being Augustine’s commentary on Psalm 63:7—and then inserts the quote: “Thus we shall do everything in such a way that we appear to have done nothing.” The logic of Gilbert U’s explanation is that, by acting against Christ only in word, and not in deed, the Jews thought they would appear not to have acted at all. Remi A, however, includes no corresponding preface to the Augustine-quote; his full explanation reads simply:   For he attributed the plan [to have Christ killed] to the Jews for this reason, because they called him a destructor of the Law, and feared losing their land and people because of him, as it is said elsewhere: “Thus we shall do everything in such a way that we appear to have done nothing.”182   Without the idea of “acting only in word”, the Augustine-quote illuminates nothing about the reasoning of the Jews at all—rather the opposite: the quote seems like a non sequitur. Almost at every turn, we see that Remi A cannot have been the direct source even of its closest relative.                                                 182 “(RA18a) Consilium vero hac de causa attribuit iudaeis, (RA18b) quia eum legis destructorem dicebant, (RA18c) et propter eum locum et gentem amittere timebant, (RA18d) sicut alibi dicitur: “’Sic faciamus omnia, ut nihil fecisse videamur.’” PS. REMIG.II sup. psalt. 2,1. (Admont, SB, 99, fol. 3v.) 

--- [p.248] ---

 
 
241 That Bruno, in turn, is not a direct source of Gilbert U is seen, for example, at the same place in Gilbert U’s text, where, if this had been the case, the latter would be jumping between Bruno and his other source (his and Remi A’s common parent) at every other sentence, in the following sequence: GU18b = RA17b, GU18c-19a = BC18B-19a, GU19b = RA18b, GU20a = BC22, GU20b = RA18d, GU21 = BC20b, GU22 = RA12. Such erratic jumping back and forth does not seem to be the best explanation for the pattern in question; once again, the more reasonable conclusion is that the relationships which we see between these commentaries are only indirect and transmitted via routes which we do not (at least at the present moment) have access to.  Another small clue to this conclusion is the fact that Gilbert U uses the phrase ‘nullam efficaciam’ (GU17b), which echoes Remigius’s ‘nullum effectum’, where Bruno writes simply “nulla” (in answer to the question, “qua utilitate”). The fact that Gilbert U is the only one who preserves this Remigian phrase while not himself being the shared source of it indicates, once again, that he is borrowing from some source which is directly linked to the authoritative original, but that is not identical to it or to any one of our group of eight. Additional indications supporting the suggestion that all three are drawing on a shared intermediary ancestor are found, among other things, in the fact that there are instances both where Gilbert U and Bruno agree against Remi A and where Bruno and Remi A agree against Gilbert U. The first example of the former relationship is found at GU7a, “For titles may be considered in two ways, namely either according to the sequence of events [it describes; i.e. historically], or according to the interpretation of the words [i.e. allegorically], or according to both,” where Gilbert U and Bruno agree on the renderings ‘rei gestionem’ and ‘vocum/vocis interpretationem’ as opposed to Remi A’s ‘rem gestam’ and ‘solam interpretationem’; Gilbert U and Bruno also agree on the order in which these two alternatives are presented.  

--- [p.249] ---

 
 
242 We also have a few instances where Gilbert U and Bruno share biblical quotations, such as that of Genesis 49:10, he shall be the expectation of nations (GU9b and BC9b), of John 9:16, This man is not of God who keeps not the Sabbath (GU19a and BC19), and, together with Gilbert P, the reference to John 12:19, the whole world is gone after him (GU17d and BC21d). Both of these commentaries also include the same paraphrase of Jeremiah 11:4 (and others), namely, I will be your God, and you shall be my people183 (GU21 and BC20b), and, finally, they both make reference to the Hebrew version when explaining the verb ‘astiterunt’, “stood by” (GU23b and BC25). At the very least, these last two circumstances are very unlikely to have happened independently of each other. Although they are significantly fewer and often smaller, there are also some instances where Bruno and Remi A agree against Gilbert U. One such instance is the fact that both Remi A and Bruno use the gerundive to express that the psalm should be attributed to David/Christ (RA2b and BC7b); another is the fact that they both preserve Haimo of Auxerre’s mention of the Devil when explaining why the psalm should be attributed to Christ (RA3c and BC10b).184 A final detail is the fact that they both specify that ‘astiterunt’, “stood by,” should be understood as referring to the future, ‘astabunt’ (RA14 and BC24). Taken together, these patterns of agreement strongly support the suggestion that the connection seen between these three commentaries can only be explained by a shared intermediary ancestor (that is to say, an intermediary ancestor within the family tree, different from the suggested common magisterial source of the group as a whole). Some of the material in Gilbert U that does not have a counterpart in Gilbert P, Remi A, or Bruno provides even more support for this conclusion. First, we have the comment that the                                                 183 Cf. Jer. 11:4, 24:7, 30:22, 31:33, 32:38; Ezek. 11:20, 14:11, 36:28; Zech. 8:8; Heb. 8:10. 184 Note here that this is yet another instance where Remi A’s explanation falls short: the point about the Devil is an explanation of why Christ is called strong-handed, manu fortis—an item that, once again, Remi A has neglected to include. The example shows that both Gilbert U and Remi A have made adjustments which exclude them from being the transmitters of the material. (As mentioned above, Gilbert U seems to be referring historically to Goliath.) 

--- [p.250] ---

 
 
243 Psalmist does not call Beatus vir a psalm, since it functions as a title of the rest: “(GU4a) Beatus vir vero non vocat psalmum, quia est quasi titulus aliorum, …” (“For he does not call Blessed is the man a psalm, since it is like a title of the others, …”); the same comment is found in Pseudo-Bede (but in wording which is, in certain ways, closer to Bruno):185  Ps.-Bede (PB18a) Nam primus dicitur, non quantum ad priorem, (PB18b) qui quasi titulus est omnium aliorum, (PB18c) et ideo titulo caret, (PB19a) sed quantum ad intitulatos psalmos sequentes, …186 (For it is called the first, not in regard to the preceding [psalm], which is like a title of all the others, and therefore lacks a title, but in regard to the following psalms [which are] titled…)  After this, we have, in sequence, the quotation of Psalm 44:3, beautiful above the sons of men, and 1 Kings 10:24, And all the earth desired to see Solomon’s face, the first of which is shared only with Pseudo-Bede, and the second with both Pseudo-Bede and Pseudo-Haimo. The fact that all three reproduce the same paraphrase of 1 Kings 10:24 shows that their renderings are related beyond coincidence. Just like Gilbert U, Pseudo-Bede, furthermore, gives the two quotations as one chunk, introduced by the same phrase: Vulgate Et universa terra desiderabat vultum Salomonis …187 (And all the world desired to see Solomon’s face.)  Gilbert U … (GU9c) de quo dicitur, Speciosus forma prae filiis hominum, (GU9d) cuius vultum desiderat universa terra.188 
                                                185 Bruno’s account reads: “… (BC3a) titulatorum enim psalmorum primus est, (BC3b) cum primus titulo careat. (BC4a) Quantum vero ad ordinem psalmorum, secundus, …” (“… for it is the first of the titled [psalms], since the first [psalm] lacks a title. But in regard to the order, [it is] the second, …”) (PS.) BRUNO CARTHUS. psalm. 2, acces. (PL Database, vol. 152, col. 643CD.) 186 PS. BEDA psalm. 2, acces. (PL Database vol. 93, col. 490B.) 187 1 Kings 10:24. 188 GILB. UNIV. sup. psalt. 2, acces. (Laon, BM, 17, fol. 3v.) 

--- [p.251] ---

 
 
244 (… of whom it is said, Beautiful above the sons of men, whose face all the world desired to see.)  Ps.-Bede … (PB23c) et de quo dicitur, cuius vultum desiderat universa terra, (PB23d) et, speciosus forma prae filiis hominum.189 (… of whom it is said, whose face all the world desired to see, and, Beautiful above the sons of men.)  Ps.-Haimo (PH5) Cuius visum desiderant adhuc etiam omnes sancti.190 (Whose countenance also the saints still desire to see.)  One final detail illuminates the less than straightforward relationships that it is becoming undeniably clear exist between our commentaries; this is Gilbert U’s and Bruno’s comment on the Hebrew version of the verb ‘astiterunt’. Wanting to explain the choice of the verb ‘astare’, “to stand by,” they both point out that the Hebrew version has something closer to “they rose/stood up against.”191 Unlike Bruno, however, Gilbert U goes on to say that ‘astare’ is synonymous with ‘adiuvare’, “to help,” which we, hence, understand is the reason the verb needs explanation: this means that Bruno’s explanation misses the point somewhat, but, more importantly, we find the same interpretation of ‘astiterunt’ as ‘adiuverunt’ in Remi M:  (RM21a) Quasi dicat: Non solum gentes, (RM21b) scilicet minores milites Romani, (RM21c) sed etiam reges maiores astiterunt illis, (RM21d) id est adiuverunt illas adversum me.192 (As if he should say: Not only [have] the Gentiles, that is the lesser Roman soldiers, [roared], but the greater kings stood by them, that is, helped them against me.)   
                                                189 PS. BEDA psalm. 2, acces. (PL Database vol. 93, col. 490D.) 190 (PS.) HAIMO HALB. in psalm. 2, acces. (Troyes, BM, 904, fol. 2v; PL Database vol. 116, col. 201A.) 191 Their verbs are more or less synonymous. Jerome translates the Hebrew with ‘consurgent’—the ‘con’ and the future tense being a closer reflection of the original. I thank Andy Witt for assisting with the meaning of the Hebrew.  192 (PS.) REMIG. psalm. 2,1. (PL Database vol. 131, col. 155C.) 

--- [p.252] ---

 
 
245 That any one commentator would have used more than one source to put together his own biblical commentary is unsurprising; such is the nature of compiling. That he should have used more than two commentaries which already contain much of the exact same material seems a lot less likely.193 Such would be the case if we posited that Gilbert U borrowed material from not only the Gloss and/or Gilbert P and a common ancestor of his, Remi A’s and Bruno’s, but also, in addition to these, from Remi M and Pseudo-Bede. The explanation of some sort of intermediary ancestor on which all of these commentaries depend in some degree seems, once again, like a more satisfying explanation. Moving to the other side of the table, we may begin by noting that, whether or not he is also using the Gloss, Gilbert U is most definitely using Gilbert P, for he shares one sentence with him verbatim which is neither patristic nor Carolingian, is not reproduced by anyone else, and does not appear in the Gloss;194 this is GU10, “Huic attribuitur psalmus, quoniam ad eum spectat bonitas nostrae operationis” (“The psalm is attributed to him because the goodness of our work looks to him”). We may, therefore, imagine that also the other items shared on this side—namely, ‘qua utilitate’, ‘ut ferae agentes’, ‘sine ratione’, and ‘romani milites’—have been transmitted to Gilbert U via Gilbert P; this commentary is thus the second component of Gilbert U’s weave.  We see the weave clearly if we look at how these items are presented in Gilbert U; the discussion in question concerns the Psalmist’s choice of the verb ‘fremere’: 
                                                193 Peter Lombard, who uses both the Gloss and Gilbert P, is an exception here: we must remember that his objective, according to Herbert of Bosham, was only to elucidate the obscure brevity of the Gloss, and that he did not mean for his own commentary to become public (see Chapter One, 26n40). 194 A similar idea occurs in Letbert, from whom Gilbert U definitely also borrows material, but Letbert’s version is not in verbatim agreement with Gilbert P and Gilbert U, which shows that the relationship between the latter two must be independent of Letbert: “Psalmus ergo iste, id est, sequens exhortatio bonae operationis, est attribuendus David, …” (“Therefore, this psalm, that is, this exhortation to good works, should be attributed to David, …”) LETBER. INS. in psalm. 2, acces. (PL Database vol. 21, col. 649D.) 

--- [p.253] ---

 
 
246 Gilbert P Gilbert U Remi A Bruno  (GU18a) De gentibus dixit ‘fremuerunt’,  (RA17a) Merito gentes fremuisse dicit,  (BC18a) Ideoque gentes, id est gentiles, dicit in se in sui crucifixione fremuisse,   (GU18b) quia ipsi sine causa (RA17b) quia sine omni causa (BC18c) sine causae oppositione … (GP15b) sine ratione. (GU18c) et sine ratione  (BC18b) id est irrationabiliter  (GU18d) eum crucifixerunt. (RA17c) eum tradiderunt morti. (BC18d) eum crucifixisse.  The long version of Remigius uses for its explanation the adverb ‘irrationabiliter’, which is preserved in its original form in Bruno, but which both the Gloss and Gilbert P render as ‘sine ratione’. As mentioned above, Remi A botches this explanation by giving only ‘sine causa’ as the reason for the choice of verb; as Bruno, however, preserves both the full explanation with ‘irrationabiliter’ and the expression ‘sine causae oppositione’, we may conclude that both these ideas were present in their common ancestor. (We may even imagine that both phrases were present in the ancestor from which our group got its Remigius, and that the Gloss’s and Gilbert P’s phrase ‘sine ratione’ has been coloured by the expression ‘sine causa/causae oppositione’.) Gilbert U’s weaving together of his two sources can be clearly seen in the fact that the item ‘sine ratione’, excerpted from Gilbert P, is combined in his text together with the ‘sine causa’ from the common ancestor of his, Remi A’s, and Bruno’s; it is easy to imagine how Gilbert U simply replaced the ‘irrationabiliter’ of his one source with the synonymous expression of his other source at a point where these were using similarly sounding phrases. From this and the above analyses it should be sufficiently clear that none of our commentaries could itself be the shared intermediary source that I have suggested as the explanation for the pattern of agreements that exist between our commentaries. Only in the case of Gilbert P have 

--- [p.254] ---

 
 
247 we been able to establish that he is one of the direct sources of one of the other commentaries, namely Gilbert U. All the others appear, however, to be end points in our family tree of twelfth-century Psalms-exegesis.  CONCLUSION Taken together, the examples analysed in Chapter Three and Four strongly suggest that our eight commentaries are not only clearly closely related to one another, as several scholars have already observed, but that they also appear to be getting their shared material from the same adaptation(s) of the original patristic and Carolingian passages. In the previous chapter, we saw this in our commentaries’ incorporation of an adapted version of Remigius’s theory of the four orders at the resurrection and the application to Psalm 1:1 of the Augustinian theory of the three stages of sin conceived of as the deaths of the soul. In this chapter, we have seen that our commentaries display the same adaptation of Augustine’s exegesis of light and grave temptation which occurs in the knowing and the unknowing, introduced by the same snippet of Cassiodorus, and divided into two streams—an Augustinian and a Remigian one. Related to this, we also discovered that some of them, including the Gloss, share what appears to be some magisterial material. We have also seen the same type of pattern in their discussions of the ordo of Psalm 2 and in the exegesis of Psalm 2:1, with the irrationality of roaring lions and the Gentiles being understood as the Roman soldiers.  All of these agreements lend support to the theory that our commentaries ultimately stem from some shared, non-patristic intermediary source which is responsible for the adaptations. In those instances where we see many very closely verbatim agreements—such as between Gilbert U and Remi A, and between the Gloss and Gilbert P—the connection is most likely mediated textually. 

--- [p.255] ---

 
 
248 But we have also seen examples where there is more variation in the agreements—such as in the case of the concept of delectatio in the three deaths of the soul, or the explanation of fear preceding the realization of its object—suggesting the possibility that transmission may also have been oral or at least stem from different reportationes of the same teaching. It has also been made clear that none of our commentaries could itself be this shared intermediary source, as each one, at one point or another, leaves out some item or interpretation that the others—or several of the others—share. For the majority of our commentaries it has not, however, been possible to determine any strict relational hierarchy, the exception being Gilbert U’s relationship to Gilbert P. There is, however, no reason to expect this to be possible, since we must assume that there are points of connection—either in the form of texts or in the form of lectures—that we cannot see, either because they are no longer extant, or—in the case of texts—because they have simply not yet been discovered.  As for our subdivisions into branches, we have seen more evidence that the Gloss has an especially close connection to Gilbert P, while Remi M and Pseudo-Bede also seem to be particularly closely related. We have also seen evidence that Gilbert U, Bruno, and Remi A form a distinct subgroup, within which Gilbert U and Remi A appear to be the most closely related. The group consisting of Gilbert U, Bruno, Remi A, Remi M and Pseudo-Bede also often agrees internally against the group consisting of the Gloss, Gilbert P, and Pseudo-Haimo. Pseudo-Haimo’s exact place is, however, somewhat difficult to determine, as he sometimes sides with Gilbert P and the Gloss, and sometimes with Gilbert U, Bruno, Remi A, Remi M, and Pseudo-Bede, and at other times appears to stand off to his own side. We have also found that none of our commentaries could depend solely on one of the others for its material; in those cases where this has appeared to be a possibility—such as Remi M depending solely on Pseudo-Bede—it has turned out that the commentary in question can always 

--- [p.256] ---

 
 
249 be shown to have some connection to the group which must have been mediated via some route other than the suggested sole parent. While it is perfectly natural to imagine that each of our commentaries borrowed from multiple sources—in fact, the patterns of agreement indicate that some of our commentators must have borrowed from at least two of the others, such as Gilbert U from both Gilbert P and Remi A (or their near relatives) and Pseudo-Haimo from both Gilbert P and a shared or intermediary ancestor of Remi A’s group—the question is how many commentaries from this particular group it is reasonable to assume that any one compiler borrowed from, seeing as they all already share a large amount of material. Van den Eynde believes, for example, that Pseudo-Bede borrows from Letbert, Bruno, the Gloss, Remi M, and Pseudo-Haimo, but I would argue that such a scenario seems rather unlikely due to the fact that at least four of these commentaries already contain so much of the same material that looking at all of them would be highly redundant, and that the agreements are better explained by the hypothesis of a shared common source and the lost or invisible intermediaries which we can be certain must have existed, making the web of twelfth-century Psalms exegesis at the same time far more complex, but also more unified, than previously imagined. 

--- [p.257] ---

 
250 
CONCLUSION  Anselm of Laon and the Glossa Ordinaria on the Psalms   The Glossa ordinaria on the Bible is often said to epitomize the new attitudes to biblical learning, and to learning in general, that characterize the twelfth century.668 That the Middle Ages associated this work, and the Gloss on the Psalms in particular, with Anselm of Laon, the most celebrated theologian of the early twelfth century, is an undeniable fact. The truth of this assumption has rarely been questioned. At the same time, however, scholars have been puzzled by the lack of extant manuscripts of the Gloss on the Psalms dating to before the 1140s—especially considering that there do exist manuscripts of the Gloss on other biblical books which can be dated to the early 1130s and possibly even the mid-20s. This circumstance together with the very close textual relationships that exist between the Gloss and its two immediate successors, the Media glosatura of Gilbert of Poitiers and the Magna glosatura of Peter Lombard, has, however, led a few scholars to suggest alternative scenarios.  Furthermore, in light of these same circumstances, the results of some recent studies on the Gloss on other books of the Bible associated with Anselm become particularly interesting as they suggest that Anselm’s involvement in these other Gloss-books—the Gloss on Canticles, John, and Matthew—may have been of a more indirect nature than the standard narrative would have it. These studies have either shown, or shown it likely, that Anselm produced other materials—either in the form of his own written commentaries or in the form of lectures preserved in various reportationes—which served as sources for the Gloss, opening up the possibility that the                                                 668 Cf. Stirnemann’s comment: “Aucun livre manuscrit ne nous renseigne aussi bien que la Bible glosée sur la vie intellectuelle de XIIe siècle dans le nord de la France, sur les préoccupations, l’esprit novateur, les populations, la mobilité, et l’économique de ce temps et de ce milieu.” (Stirnemann, “Gilbert et les livres glosés,” 83.) 

--- [p.258] ---

 
 
251 compilation of the Gloss itself was done by his colleagues and/or students. The main question posed in this thesis has, therefore, been whether the same scenario might apply also to the Gloss on the Psalms.  Our first order of business was to examine the evidence for the various scenarios proposed for the priority of either the Media or Magna glosatura in order to settle this question definitively. By looking purely at the internal structure of the patristic material shared by the Gloss and the Media (and Magna) glosatura, we have been able to establish that the Gloss must, indeed, precede both of its successors in the chronology of their compilation. This is due to the fact that the Gloss presents the shared patristic material in “un-mixed” form, while both the Media and the Magna break the material in question up and weave it together with other material. Since it is highly unlikely that anyone would have had either the resources or, for that matter, the will to isolate and with surgical precision excise every excerpt from each of the Psalms-authorities found woven together in the Media or Magna in order to “purify” the authoritative material and restore it to its original form simply in order to, in turn, create the Gloss on the Psalms, we concluded that the standard account of the hierarchy of these three commentaries must hold. At the same time, however, we found that many of the points made by Gross-Diaz for the priority of the Media glosatura based on arguments claiming the superior coherence and sense of its exegesis are not unreasonable, and that, although it can be shown that what seemed like Gilbertine influences on the Gloss-text as it is presented in the Rusch edition only entered the tradition later, via Lombard’s Magna, the Gloss reads well as preparatory notes for Gilbert’s continuous commentary. Based on these observations, and in accordance with Stirnemann’s belief that Gilbert was instrumental in the dissemination of the Gloss following upon his time teaching at Chartres, I advanced a revised version of Gross-Diaz’s theory that Gilbert contributed to Anselm’s original purely patristic “little Gloss,” suggesting that the Gloss as we have it 

--- [p.259] ---

 
 
252 already in its earliest form may actually be more the result of Gilbert’s work than even Gross-Diaz believes.  Interestingly, at a late stage of writing this dissertation, I discovered that the manuscript Troyes, BM, 726, identified by Stirnemann as one of the earliest manuscripts of the Gloss on the Psalms,669 may, in fact, represent such a purely patristic version of the Gloss. This manuscript shares some similarities with the fifteen Gloss-manuscripts consulted here but also displays some substantial differences. A preliminary survey reveals that it mainly contains glosses from two of the Psalms-authorities, namely Augustine and Jerome. While I have not found any of its Hieronymian glosses in the ordinary Gloss-manuscripts, the Augustinian ones are all there, and this in the same adapted form as the Ordinaria and the rest of our group displays. In other words, whoever put together the Ordinaria used the same source for their Augustine as did   the compiler of this gloss. As far as I have been able to determine, this manuscript contains, however, none of the Cassiodorean glosses that we find in the Ordinaria nor any of the potentially magisterial material.  This manuscript thus appears to contain strong evidence that there existed in the twelfth century a source of adapted Augustinian glosses which was used by the compiler of the Gloss on the Psalms as we have it, and to which this compiler added other patristic, Carolingian, and magisterial material. As the analyses of the subsequent chapters also yielded some indications that Gilbert was, indeed, a key figure in transmitting what was believed to be Anselm’s exegesis on the Psalms, I believe that a comparison of his commentary, the Gloss, and Troyes, BM, 726 could produce some very interesting results and that this task, at the present moment, constitutes the most promising way forward to testing Gross-Diaz’s and my own hypotheses and reaching some potentially definitive answers to these questions.                                                 669 Stirnemann, “Où ont été fabriqués,” 267.  

--- [p.260] ---

 
 
253 The suggestion that Gilbert may, in fact, be the compiler of the Gloss on the Psalms, furthermore, accords well with the recent findings regarding other books of the Gloss, which show that Anselm’s association with the work, both in its separate parts and as a whole, can be explained by his authorship of other material, which was used as sources for the Gloss. Gilbert had been Anselm’s student at Laon, and would, as such, have been well acquainted with his master’s exegesis and also with the beginnings of the Gloss project. Even if we must imagine that Gilbert compiled the patristic parts of the Gloss before he wrote his own commentary—as evidenced by the “surgical precision”-argument and unlike what appears to be true at least for the Gloss on John—we must also take into account the fact that the Gloss project would have been well under way when Gilbert began his own teaching career at Paris and Chartres, and that Gilbert would thus have been in a position where he could have been consciously contributing to the Gloss project at the same time as creating his own lecture course on the Psalms, the result of which, we imagine, is his continuous commentary. The relationship between the Gloss on the Psalms and the Media glosatura would then be similar to that of the Gloss on John to the Glosae super Iohannem, even if reversed. (We may point out that the idea that there should be a continuous commentary other than Gilbert of Poitiers’s Media which served as a main source of the Gloss seems rather unlikely.) The above mentioned circumstances also made Wilmart’s suggestion that Anselm is the author of the work which we have designated here Pseudo-Haimo relevant once more, because although his suggestion had already been challenged, first by Landgraf and then by Van den Eynde, both of these scholars worked from the premise that the Gloss is the work of Anselm and that any continuous commentary also attributed to him must, therefore, agree in its exegetical interpretations with the Gloss. With this premise brought into question by recent scholarship, however, this objection loses its force. What became, instead, the new main question was 

--- [p.261] ---

 
 
254 whether Pseudo-Haimo could have been a source of the Gloss in a way that would fit the scenarios suggested for the Gloss on Canticles, John, and Matthew. We thus began, in Chapter Three, with a re-examination of Landgraf’s objections. What this showed us was that, although looking at the manuscripts rather than the early printed editions reveals that the differences between the Gloss and Pseudo-Haimo are smaller than what Landgraf believed, the renderings of their shared authoritative material are such that the Gloss could not have gotten this material from Pseudo-Haimo. At the same time, we saw that the two commentaries are still clearly related to one another, displaying some of the same renderings of their shared material. This could thus be explained either by Pseudo-Haimo borrowing from the Gloss or by the two borrowing from a shared source. What Landgraf’s examples also showed us is that some version of the Gloss had been interpolated with Lombardian additions already by the late twelfth century. As none of the fifteen manuscripts here consulted include these additions (except, in a couple of cases, as additions above the line by a second hand), this raises the question of the circulation of this interpolated version; in any case, it was clearly what the later twelfth-century theologians at Paris were using, and it ended up being the source of the early printed editions. Van den Eynde’s strategy for denying Anselm the authorship of Pseudo-Haimo rests mainly, in turn, on showing that the commentary in question must date to well after Anselm’s death in 1117. Whether or not he succeeds in doing this can be debated—we noted, for one, that his arguments of doctrinal improvement appear to be rendered invalid by external evidence showing that Pseudo-Haimo was in existence already before the 1130s; what an examination of his arguments showed us, however, is that Pseudo-Haimo is related in some way to Pseudo-Bede, but that, contrary to what Van den Eynde claims, it is not so clear that the former is the source of the latter. This introduced the possibility that the two are related by some third, shared source, 

--- [p.262] ---

 
 
255 which could, in turn, potentially be connected in some way to Anselm, explaining the attribution to him of Pseudo-Haimo that is found in the manuscript BAV, Reg. lat. 295. These implications were explored further within the framework of Flint’s arguments against Van den Eynde’s objections to the suggestion that Anselm may have authored Pseudo-Haimo. While Flint argues that Pseudo-Haimo and Pseudo-Bede were both used by Honorius of Autun, who wrote his Psalms-commentary sometime in the late eleven-teens or early twenties, a closer look at the evidence she advances showed us that it is not so clear that the relationship between these commentaries is so straightforwardly binary. It also showed us that the relationships of affinity extend far beyond these three commentaries, namely so far as to include more or less all of our group of eight. Most interestingly, however, we saw several signs that the similarities which we observe in our commentaries cannot simply be explained by a shared dependence on the Psalms-authorities directly, but that they appear to be connected to each other independently of these by some other means. Exploring this implication thus became the objective of Chapter Four. The passages analysed in Chapter Four turned out to support the suggestion that our commentaries all rely on some form of shared intermediary adaptation(s) of the original authoritative sources; we saw that this applies to material from Augustine, Jerome, Cassiodorus, and Remigius. At the same time, we saw that the Gloss cannot have been the source of these shared adaptations. This state of affairs thus raised the question of whether one of the other commentaries in our group of eight could itself be this shared intermediary source. We noted that such a claim had, in fact, been made by Van den Eynde in regard to Remi A’s relationship to Remi M, Bruno, and Gilbert U. Thus paying particular attention to Remi A and Gilbert U, which appeared to contain the least significant omissions of shared material, we examined the possibility of such a scenario, concluding, however, that none of our group of eight could itself be the source of the shared adaptations present in the tradition.  

--- [p.263] ---

 
 
256 In the case of Gilbert U, however, we found that his account appears to be a compilation of Gilbert of Poitiers’s Media glosatura and a separate ancestor of his, Remi A’s, and Bruno’s. It is possible, and even likely, that similar scenarios apply to other commentaries in our group—for example, Remi M and Pseudo-Bede appear to share a particular connection to each other, at the same time as neither can have been the sole direct source of the other. Pseudo-Haimo, in turn, often appears to depend on Gilbert P, but not exclusively: his inclusion of the three deaths of the soul and the four orders in the resurrection, for example, show that he is also dependent on the same intermediary tradition as the other commentaries in our group. As Pseudo-Haimo exhibits these same signs of dependence as do our other commentaries, however, and does not appear to be a possible candidate for our missing shared intermediary, it does not appear likely that this commentary could be the work of Anselm himself. We also saw in Chapter Four some signs that the shared intermediary source on which our commentaries depend in some way may ultimately be an oral one—perhaps in the form of different iterations of one and the same lecture course. All of these results align with Ballentyne’s observations for the Gloss on Matthew, namely that the texts which are related to it cannot be easily sorted into any straightforward chronological hierarchy, once again suggesting that there must be points of connection lying behind our extant texts which we cannot see. In light of the fact that no such text(s) have, however, been discovered, and knowing what we know about the nature of the transmission of knowledge in the early twelfth century, we have to accept that we may never find any such written proof of the existence of any such sources. This does not, however, mean that they have never existed nor preclude us from reconstructing approximations of their content by the type of comparison of related texts which has been done here. The results presented here are, however, all tentative; only small parts of the commentaries have been analysed. However, if a certain relationship can be proven to hold for part of a 

--- [p.264] ---

 
 
257 commentary, then the relationship must hold also for the whole. We may, therefore, invoke here the words of Lottin: Aucun d’eux n’est, à lui seul, une preuve, mais, comme en conviennent tous ceux qui s’occupent de critique interne, un faisceau d’indices favorables à une hypothèse rend celle-ci sérieusement probable.670  Nevertheless, more work needs to be done in order to settle these questions definitively. One of the lessons learned here is, however, that the printed editions of these texts are treacherous, and that anyone wanting to do a close textual comparison of these texts, or make any sort of formalist argument about them, must look at the manuscripts. Without any critical editions in existence, however, no result of any such comparison can be entirely conclusive. What we have done here is, rather, to map out a course of action for anyone wishing to accurately position these commentaries within the framework of twelfth-century Psalms-exegesis. What we have learned to this end is that nothing meaningful can be said about the relationships between these commentaries to each other unless one takes the whole group into account. From the results of the analyses offered in the preceding chapters, I shall, however, advance a tentative hypothesis which takes all of the parameters known so far into account; they are, once again, the following: the Middle Ages associated the Gloss, and the Gloss on the Psalms in particular, with Anselm; there are no manuscripts of the Gloss on the Psalms that can be dated to before the 1140s, which is more than 20 years after Anselm’s death and the time when the Media glosatura of Anselm’s student Gilbert of Poitiers began to circulate; recent Gloss-scholarship indicates that Anselm’s involvement in the compilation or the creation of the Gloss on other biblical books appears to be of a more indirect nature, namely in the form of texts or reportationes of lectures which served as sources for the Gloss; the relationship between the 
                                                670 Lottin, “La doctrine d’Anselme,” 288. 

--- [p.265] ---

 
 
258 Gloss and the Media glosatura is such that, while the Gloss contains the more original form of the authoritative excerpts, these appear to have been picked in such a way that they make the best sense when presented in continuous form in Gilbert’s commentary; what appears to be Gilbert’s original exegesis was sometimes credited to Anselm; there must have existed a source from which the compiler of the Gloss and the compiler of Troyes, BM, 726 got their Augustinian Psalms-glosses; our group of eight Psalms-commentaries appear to be related to each other by way of some shared, intermediary, non-patristic source which may have been oral or consist of various reportationes of lectures. The hypothetical scenario that thus presents itself is that Anselm, as Gross-Diaz suggests, compiled a purely patristic, mainly Augustinian gloss on the Psalms. This was then used and added to by his student Gilbert of Poitiers during his own teaching career at Chartres and Paris, whence it came into circulation along with Gilbert’s own continuous commentary in the form that we have it in the manuscripts. Anselm’s true “little gloss” never had a wide circulation of its own but has left a trace in the manuscript Troyes, BM, 726, and explains the association of the Gloss, via Gilbert, to Anselm. This would also explain the lack of Psalms-Gloss manuscripts dating to before the 1140s. It would also, in a sense, make the Media glosatura the continuous commentary which corresponds to the Gloss on the Psalms in a way similar (but not identical) to the Glosae to the Gloss on John. At some point, some influential theologian—perhaps Anselm, or perhaps Gilbert—lectured on the Psalms—perhaps with the use of the Gloss, or at least some of the same material—and these lectures are what we see the traces of in the shared material of our group of commentaries. As some of this material appears to have been available to Honorius of Autun in the late eleven-teens or early twenties, however, Gilbert seems like the less likely candidate for such a magisterial source than does Anselm. 

--- [p.266] ---

 
 
259 Once again, these suggestions are only hypothetical, but, as they make sense of all of the parameters that are known to us so far, they are attractive. Van den Eynde’s concluded his 1954 article by stating that “our long and tiresome inquiry into the earliest Scholastic commentaries on the Psalter has not been fruitless.”671 It is my hope that, even though we have not been able to prove Anselm’s authorship of any definitive work, the same can, nevertheless, be said of the inquiry at hand. Unfortunately, however, one of its results has been to make clear that we are not, in fact, in a position to claim Van den Eynde’s next assertion that “it has enabled us to disentangle their complicated relations and to settle the questions of chronology and authenticity of most of them.”672 What I have instead shown is that the question of these relationships is far more complex than anyone has previously recognized. What I hope to have achieved is to demonstrate a clear and promising path forward to settling once and for all the questions of authorship and kinship pertaining to this tangled web of texts that constitutes the corpus of early twelfth-century exegesis on the Psalms. 
                                                671 Van den Eynde, “Literary Note,” 149. 672 Van den Eynde, “Literary Note,” 149.
